1S, T and U are the aqueous chlorides of the elements X, Y and Z respectively. X, Y and Z are in the same period of the periodic table. U gives a white precipitate with NaOH but this white precipitate dissolves as more NaOH is added. When NaOH is added to T, a white precipitate forms which does not dissolve when more base is added. S does not give precipitate with NaOH. Which of the following statements are correct? (a) The three elements are metals (b) The electronegativity decreases from X to Y to Z (c) X, Y and Z could be sodium, magnesium and aluminium respectively (d) The first ionization increases from X to Y to ZAmphoteric hydroxidesAcidic, basic and amphoteric oxides/hydroxides across a period🟡 Similara, cmetals; Na, Mg, Al
Concept The behaviour of a metal hydroxide towards excess NaOH is a fingerprint of the metal's position in the period. A strongly basic hydroxide (group 1) is fully soluble and never precipitates at all; a purely basic hydroxide (group 2) precipitates and stays precipitated; an amphoteric hydroxide (group 13, Al) precipitates first and then redissolves as it is attacked by the excess base to give the aluminate ion. So the three tests given in the question read off the three columns of period 3 directly.
Key relation NaOH + NaOH → no precipitate Na⁺ has no insoluble hydroxide — S is NaCl, so X = Na. Mg²⁺ + 2OH⁻ → Mg(OH)₂↓ (stays) purely basic, unreactive towards more OH⁻ — T is MgCl₂, so Y = Mg. Al³⁺ + 3OH⁻ → Al(OH)₃↓ ; Al(OH)₃ + OH⁻ → [Al(OH)₄]⁻ (dissolves) amphoteric — U is AlCl₃, so Z = Al.
Solution
1. Assign the elements from the three NaOH tests: X = Na, Y = Mg, Z = Al — all in period 3, exactly as the question demands.
2. (a) Na, Mg and Al are all metals. ✓ Period 3 turns non-metallic only from Si onwards; the metal/non-metal staircase lies to the right of Al.
3. (b) Electronegativity Na 0.93 < Mg 1.31 < Al 1.61 — it increases from X to Z, so "decreases" is wrong. ✗
4. (c) The identification Na, Mg, Al is precisely what the precipitation data gave. ✓
5. (d) First ionisation enthalpies are Na 496, Mg 737, Al 577 kJ mol⁻¹ — not monotonic. Mg's filled 3s² makes it harder to ionise than Al's 3s²3p¹, so IE drops at Al. ✗
 element  chloride   + NaOH            + excess NaOH      class
 X = Na   NaCl       no precipitate    -                  strongly basic
 Y = Mg   MgCl2      Mg(OH)2 white     stays              basic
 Z = Al   AlCl3      Al(OH)3 white     dissolves          AMPHOTERIC

 EN     Na 0.93 < Mg 1.31 < Al 1.61     (b) says decreasing -> false
 IE1    Na 496  < Al 577  < Mg 737      (d) not monotonic  -> false
 ⇒ correct statements                                     <-- answer (a) and (c)
⚡ JEE Trick "White precipitate that redissolves in excess NaOH" is a three-word signature for Al³⁺, Zn²⁺, Pb²⁺, Sn²⁺, Cr³⁺ — the amphoteric set. If it does not redissolve it is Mg²⁺/Ca²⁺/Fe²⁺; if nothing precipitates it is an alkali metal. Trap: option (d) tempts you with "IE increases across a period", but the Mg → Al dip (and the N → O dip) are the two exceptions examiners test every year.
Answer: (a) and (c).
2The diagram shows part of the skeleton of the periodic table in which elements are indicated by letters which are not their usual symbols (J and B in the s-block region; K, H, L, E in the d-block; F, I, C, G on the right; A and D in the f-block rows). Answer on the basis of the modern periodic table: (I) Alkali metal(s) (II) An element with the outer configuration d8s2 (III) Lanthanoids (IV) Representative element(s) (V) Elements with incomplete f-subshell (VI) Halogen(s) (VII) s-block element(s) (VIII) Transition element(s) (IX) Noble gas(es) (X) Non-transition element(s)Blocks of the periodic tableFinding the group, period and block of any element🟡 Similar—I:B · II:H · III:A · IV:B,C,F,J,I · V:A · VI:I · VII:B,J · VIII:E,H,K · IX:G · X:L
Concept A blank periodic-table skeleton is answered purely by coordinates: the column fixes the group (and hence the outer configuration), the row fixes the period, and the two detached rows at the bottom are the lanthanoids and actinoids. Every label asked for here — alkali metal, halogen, noble gas, representative, transition, non-transition — is a statement about the column, so the whole question reduces to reading off which column each letter sits in.
Key relation group 1 → ns¹ (alkali) · group 2 → ns² · groups 3–12 → (n−1)d1–10ns1–2 · groups 13–17 → ns²np1–5 · group 18 → ns²np⁶ "Representative" = s-block + p-block excluding the noble gases; "transition" = an element with a partly filled d-subshell in the atom or in a common ion, which excludes the d¹⁰s² group-12 elements.
Solution
1. (I) Alkali metal → B. B sits at the far left of a period, column 1, outer ns¹.
2. (II) d⁸s² → H. H is the 8th box of the d-block row, so (n−1)d⁸ns² — the nickel column.
3. (III) Lanthanoids → A and (V) incomplete f-subshell → A. A lies in the upper of the two detached bottom rows, i.e. the 4f series.The same letter answers both because the lanthanoids are exactly the elements building up an incomplete 4f subshell.
4. (IV) Representative → B, C, F, J, I. These are the s- and p-block letters (B and J from the s-block; C, F, I from the p-block), excluding the noble gas G.
5. (VI) Halogen → I — column 17, ns²np⁵. (IX) Noble gas → G — column 18, ns²np⁶.
6. (VII) s-block → B, J. Both sit in the two left-hand columns.
7. (VIII) Transition → E, H, K — d-block letters with partly filled d. (X) Non-transition → L, the group-12 box: d¹⁰s² in the atom and d¹⁰ in the M²⁺ ion, so by definition not a transition element.
 letter  position                     answer to
 B       group 1                      (I) alkali, (IV) representative, (VII) s-block
 J       group 2                      (IV) representative, (VII) s-block
 K,E,H   d-block, partly filled d     (VIII) transition   [H = d8s2 -> (II)]
 L       group 12, d10s2              (X) NON-transition
 F,C     p-block, groups 13-16        (IV) representative
 I       group 17                     (VI) halogen, (IV) representative
 G       group 18                     (IX) noble gas
 A       4f row                       (III) lanthanoid, (V) incomplete f
 D       5f row                       (actinoid)                 <-- full match set
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the one-line definition that separates the two traps in this question: Zn, Cd, Hg (group 12) are d-block but NOT transition (d¹⁰s², d¹⁰ ion), and noble gases are p-block but NOT representative in the ICAD convention. Every "skeleton table" question hides at least one of these two. Also remember d⁸s² = group 10 = Ni/Pd/Pt — count the d-block boxes from the left starting at d¹s².
Answer: (I) B · (II) H · (III) A · (IV) B, C, F, J, I · (V) A · (VI) I · (VII) B, J · (VIII) E, H, K · (IX) G · (X) L.
3The diagram shows part of the skeleton of the periodic table in which elements are indicated by letters which are not their usual symbols (H and L at the left of period 3; Q in period 2 group 17; R in period 3 group 16; J in the period-4 d-block; T at the end of period 4; M at the left of period 5). Answer the following on the basis of the periodic table: (I) Element having greatest ionic character in its compound (II) Metal cation which is coloured in aqueous solution (III) Element(s) of which carbonate salt is/are water soluble (IV) Which element is monoatomic gas at room temperatureReading a periodic-table skeletonCarbonates of groups 1 and 2 — solubility and thermal stability🟡 Similar—I: M · II: J · III: H, L, M · IV: T
Concept Once the letters are converted into real elements by their coordinates, each of the four questions is a standard periodicity fact: ionic character tracks electropositivity (down a group ↑, across a period ↓); colour in aqueous solution needs a partly filled d-subshell; carbonate solubility is a group-1 versus group-2 lattice-energy/hydration-energy contest; and the only monoatomic gases at room temperature are the noble gases.
Key relation ionic character ∝ ΔEN ∝ electropositivity of the metal so the lowest-IE, largest metal in the diagram wins. colour ⇐ partly filled (n−1)d in the hydrated cation d–d transitions absorb visible light; d⁰ and d¹⁰ ions are colourless.
Solution
1. Read the coordinates: H = period 3 group 1 (Na), L = period 3 group 2 (Mg), Q = period 2 group 17 (F), R = period 3 group 16 (S), J = a period-4 d-block metal (Co/Fe-type), T = period 4 group 18 (Kr), M = period 5 group 1 (Rb).
2. (I) Greatest ionic character → M. M is the alkali metal furthest down the table, hence the largest and most electropositive; ΔEN with any non-metal is maximum, so its compounds are the most ionic.
3. (II) Coloured cation in aqueous solution → J. Only the d-block letter can give a hydrated ion with an incomplete 3d subshell (e.g. Co²⁺ pink, Fe³⁺ yellow); Na⁺, Mg²⁺, Rb⁺ are all closed-shell and colourless.
4. (III) Water-soluble carbonate → H, L, M. The alkali-metal carbonates Na₂CO₃ and Rb₂CO₃ are freely soluble (small charge, large hydration gain relative to lattice energy). Of the group-2 carbonates, MgCO₃ is by far the most soluble because Mg²⁺ is the smallest and most strongly hydrated cation of the group, so L is counted with them.
5. (IV) Monoatomic gas at room temperature → T. Group 18: a complete ns²np⁶ octet, no bonding tendency, so it exists as single atoms. F (Q) is a gas but diatomic, S (R) is a solid.
 letter  identity        property asked
 H       Na  (3,1)       carbonate soluble          (III)
 L       Mg  (3,2)       most soluble gp-2 CO3      (III)
 M       Rb  (5,1)       most electropositive       (I) and (III)
 J       3d metal        coloured aqueous cation    (II)
 Q       F   (2,17)      diatomic gas               -
 R       S   (3,16)      solid                      -
 T       Kr  (4,18)      MONOATOMIC gas             (IV)
⚡ JEE Trick "Coloured in aqueous solution" is never asked about the s-block — scan the diagram for the only d-block letter and stop. For solubility, memorise the one-liner: all group-1 carbonates soluble except Li₂CO₃; all group-2 carbonates insoluble, MgCO₃ least insoluble. Trap: candidates pick the halogen for "monoatomic gas" — halogens are X₂, only group 18 is monoatomic.
Answer: (I) M · (II) J · (III) H, L and M · (IV) T.
4The sum of IE1 and IE2, and of IE3 and IE4, for elements P and Q are: (P) IE1+IE2 = 2.45, IE3+IE4 = 8.82; (Q) IE1+IE2 = 2.85, IE3+IE4 = 6.11. Then according to the given information the correct statement(s) is/are: (a) P2+ is more stable than Q2+ (b) P2+ is less stable than Q2+ (c) P4+ is more stable than Q4+ (d) P4+ is less stable than Q4+Successive ionisation energiesPredicting an element and its stable ion from successive ionisation energies🟡 Similara, dP2+ more stable; P4+ less stable
Concept The energy needed to reach a given oxidation state is the running total of the ionisation energies up to that state. The smaller that total, the more easily the ion forms and the more stable it is in compounds. So to compare M²⁺ ions you compare (IE₁+IE₂); to compare M⁴⁺ ions you compare (IE₁+IE₂+IE₃+IE₄). Nothing else in the data matters.
Formula ΔE(M → M2+) = IE₁ + IE₂ and ΔE(M → M4+) = (IE₁+IE₂) + (IE₃+IE₄) the question hands you exactly these two bracketed sums, so no individual IE is ever needed. Lower total energy ⇒ more stable ion.
Solution
1. Cost of the +2 state: P needs 2.45, Q needs 2.85. P is cheaper by 0.40, so P²⁺ is more stable than Q²⁺ → (a) ✓, (b) ✗.
2. Cost of the +4 state: P needs 2.45 + 8.82 = 11.27; Q needs 2.85 + 6.11 = 8.96.P's third and fourth electrons are enormously expensive (8.82 against Q's 6.11), which flips the comparison.
3. Q's total is lower by 2.31, so P⁴⁺ is less stable than Q⁴⁺ → (d) ✓, (c) ✗.
4. The physical picture: P behaves like a group-2 metal (cheap +2, then a huge jump because the next electrons come from a noble-gas core), while Q behaves like a group-14 metal, for which +4 is still accessible.
 state    energy needed          P        Q        cheaper -> more stable
 M(2+)    IE1+IE2                2.45     2.85     P     <-- answer (a)
 M(4+)    (IE1+IE2)+(IE3+IE4)   11.27     8.96     Q     <-- answer (d)
          P: 2.45 + 8.82 = 11.27
          Q: 2.85 + 6.11 =  8.96
 ⇒ correct statements: (a) and (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Never compare a single IE when the question asks about the stability of an ion — always add up every step you have to climb to reach it. A large gap between one bracket and the next (here P's 2.45 → 8.82) marks the point where you break into a noble-gas core, which is exactly where the element's preferred oxidation state stops. Trap: options (a) and (c) are worded to look like a matched pair; the data deliberately reverses the answer between +2 and +4.
Answer: (a) and (d).
5Consider the value of all four quantum numbers of the last electron, the magnetic moment and the valence electrons of elements W, X, Y and Z in their ground state: W (3, 0, 0, +½; μ = 0; 2 valence e⁻), X (3, 1, +1, −½; μ = √3; 3), Y (3, 1, −1, +½; μ = √15; 5), Z (3, 1, 0, −½; μ = √8; 6). Then the correct statement(s) is/are: (a) I.E.1 of W > I.E.1 of X (b) I.E.1 of Y > I.E.1 of Z (c) I.E.2 of X > I.E.2 of W (d) I.E.2 of Z > I.E.2 of YQuantum numbers → identity → IEIdentifying an element from the four quantum numbers of its last electron🟡 Similara, b, c, dall four statements are correct
Concept Three independent clues are given for each element and they must agree: n and l fix the sub-shell, the magnetic moment fixes the number of unpaired electrons, and the valence-electron count fixes the group. Putting them together identifies four consecutive period-3 elements, after which the question is a pure ionisation-energy comparison — including the two famous anomalies of period 3.
Formula μ = √(n(n+2)) BM so μ = 0 ⇒ 0 unpaired; √3 ⇒ 1; √8 ⇒ 2; √15 ⇒ 3. All four have principal quantum number 3 ⇒ period 3.
Solution
1. W: n=3, l=0 → 3s; μ = 0 → no unpaired; 2 valence electrons → 3s². That is Mg (Z = 12).
2. X: n=3, l=1 → 3p; μ = √3 → 1 unpaired; 3 valence electrons → 3s²3p¹ = Al (13).
3. Y: 3p, μ = √15 → 3 unpaired, 5 valence electrons → 3s²3p³ = P (15).
4. Z: 3p, μ = √8 → 2 unpaired, 6 valence electrons → 3s²3p⁴ = S (16).
5. (a) IE₁: Mg 737 > Al 577 ✓ the classic Mg/Al anomaly — removing an electron from Al's singly-occupied 3p is easier than from Mg's filled 3s².
6. (b) IE₁: P 1012 > S 999 ✓ the P/S anomaly — P's half-filled 3p³ is extra stable, and S's 3p⁴ suffers electron–electron repulsion in the doubly-occupied orbital.
7. (c) IE₂: Al⁺ is 3s², a filled sub-shell (1817 kJ mol⁻¹); Mg⁺ is 3s¹, easy to strip (1451). So IE₂(Al) > IE₂(Mg) ✓ — the anomaly reverses on the second ionisation.
8. (d) IE₂: S⁺ is 3s²3p³, half-filled and stable (2252); P⁺ is 3s²3p² (1907). So IE₂(S) > IE₂(P) ✓ — again the reverse of the neutral-atom order.
 letter  n l  m   s     mu     val e   config      element
 W       3 0  0  +1/2    0       2     3s2          Mg
 X       3 1 +1  -1/2   √3       3     3s2 3p1      Al
 Y       3 1 -1  +1/2   √15      5     3s2 3p3      P
 Z       3 1  0  -1/2   √8       6     3s2 3p4      S

 IE1 (kJ/mol)   Mg 737 > Al 577          (a) TRUE
                P 1012 > S  999          (b) TRUE
 IE2 (kJ/mol)   Al 1817 > Mg 1451        (c) TRUE
                S  2252 > P  1907        (d) TRUE   <-- answer (a,b,c,d)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the pair of period-3 anomalies as a single rule — IE₁ dips at group 13 and again at group 16 (Mg>Al, P>S) — and then remember that both dips invert for IE₂, because ionising once shifts every configuration one place to the left. That single sentence answers all four options here. Trap: √8 is 2 unpaired, not 8 — always solve n(n+2) = μ² first.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d) — all four are correct.
6Consider the sequence X⁻ ←(ΔH4)— X —(ΔH1)→ X⁺ —(ΔH2)→ X2+ —(ΔH3)→ X3+, with ΔH5 the reverse of ΔH4 (X⁻ → X) and ΔH6 the reverse of ΔH1 (X⁺ → X). If the electronic configuration of element X is [Ne]3s1, which of the following orders is correct regarding the given enthalpies? (a) |ΔH4| = |ΔH5| (b) |ΔH2| > |ΔH1| (c) |ΔH2| > |ΔH3| (d) |ΔH1| = |ΔH6|IE / electron-gain enthalpy cycleIonisation potential IE₁, IE₂, IE₃ … and their relative magnitudes🟡 Similara, b, dequalities plus IE₂ > IE₁
Concept Every arrow on this diagram is either an ionisation (electron removed, always endothermic) or an electron gain (electron added). Two arrows that connect the same pair of species in opposite directions are exact negatives of each other, so their magnitudes are equal — that settles two options immediately. The remaining two are decided by the universal rule that successive ionisation energies always increase.
Key relation IE₁ < IE₂ < IE₃ < … each removal is from a smaller, more positively charged species, so it always costs more; there is no exception anywhere in the periodic table. ΔH(A → B) = −ΔH(B → A) Hess's law applied to a single step.
Solution
1. X = [Ne]3s¹ = Na. Identify the arrows: ΔH₁ = IE₁, ΔH₂ = IE₂, ΔH₃ = IE₃, ΔH₄ = ΔHeg (X + e⁻ → X⁻), ΔH₅ = X⁻ → X (the reverse of ΔH₄), ΔH₆ = X⁺ + e⁻ → X (the reverse of ΔH₁).
2. (a) ΔH₅ = −ΔH₄, so their magnitudes are equal ✓.
3. (d) ΔH₆ = −ΔH₁, so |ΔH₁| = |ΔH₆| ✓ — the electron-gain enthalpy of X⁺ is numerically the ionisation energy of X.
4. (b) IE₂ > IE₁ always; for Na the gap is spectacular (496 → 4562 kJ mol⁻¹) because the second electron comes out of the Ne core ✓.
5. (c) claims IE₂ > IE₃, which contradicts the monotonic rule — for Na, IE₃ = 6910 > IE₂ = 4562 ✗.
 arrow   process                 identity        Na value (kJ/mol)
 ΔH1     Na    -> Na+  + e-      IE1                496
 ΔH2     Na+   -> Na2+ + e-      IE2               4562
 ΔH3     Na2+  -> Na3+ + e-      IE3               6910
 ΔH4     Na + e- -> Na-          ΔHeg               -53
 ΔH5     Na-   -> Na  + e-       -ΔH4               +53   |ΔH4|=|ΔH5| (a) ✓
 ΔH6     Na+ + e- -> Na          -ΔH1               -496  |ΔH1|=|ΔH6| (d) ✓

 IE1 < IE2 < IE3  ⇒ (b) TRUE, (c) FALSE      <-- answer (a), (b), (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Two identities are worth memorising outright: ΔHeg(M⁺) = −IE₁(M) and IE(X⁻) = −ΔHeg(X). They convert half of every "cycle" question into a one-line answer. Trap: option (c) reverses the successive-IE inequality — no atom anywhere has IE₂ > IE₃, so it can be rejected without touching the data.
Answer: (a), (b) and (d).
7According to Slater's rule, the correct order of Zeff on the valence-shell electron is: (a) Fe > Fe2+ > Fe3+ (b) N3− < O2− < F (c) Na+ < Mg2+ < Al3+ (d) Ti2+ < V3+ < Mn5+Slater's rule, ZeffUsing Slater's rules to estimate the effective nuclear charge🟡 Similarb, c, dall except (a)
Concept Slater's rule says Z* = Z − σ, and σ depends only on how many electrons are present and where they sit — never on the charge of the ion directly. So for a set of isoelectronic species σ is identical and Z* simply follows Z. And for successive cations of the same element Z is fixed while σ falls as electrons are stripped, so Z* must rise with positive charge.
Formula σ = 0.35(others in same group) + 0.85(n−1 shell) + 1.00(n−2 and below) for an s/p valence electron; the electron in question is never counted. Consequence: remove electrons ⇒ σ↓ ⇒ Z*↑.
Solution
1. (a) is wrong. Fe, Fe²⁺, Fe³⁺ all have Z = 26 but 26, 24 and 23 electrons. Fewer electrons ⇒ less screening ⇒ larger Z*. The true order is Fe³⁺ > Fe²⁺ > Fe, the exact reverse of what is printed.
2. (c) is right. Na⁺, Mg²⁺, Al³⁺ are isoelectronic (10 e⁻ each), so σ = 0.35×7 + 0.85×2 = 4.15 for all three. Z* = 11−4.15 = 6.85, 12−4.15 = 7.85, 13−4.15 = 8.85 — increasing ✓.
3. (b) is right. N³⁻ and O²⁻ are also 10-electron species (σ = 4.15): Z* = 2.85 and 3.85. Neutral F has 9 electrons, σ = 0.35×6 + 0.85×2 = 3.80, Z* = 5.20. So 2.85 < 3.85 < 5.20 ✓.
4. (d) is right. Ti²⁺ (22−2), V³⁺ (23−3) and Mn⁵⁺ (25−5) all carry 20 electrons, so σ is common and Z* rises with Z: 22 < 23 < 25 ⇒ Ti²⁺ < V³⁺ < Mn⁵⁺ ✓.
 species    Z    e-    sigma    Z* = Z - sigma
 Fe        26    26    ~22.25       3.75
 Fe2+      26    24    ~21.55       4.45     order is Fe3+ > Fe2+ > Fe
 Fe3+      26    23    ~21.20       4.80     (a) printed backwards -> FALSE

 Na+       11    10     4.15        6.85 |
 Mg2+      12    10     4.15        7.85 |  isoelectronic, Z* follows Z  (c) ✓
 Al3+      13    10     4.15        8.85 |

 N3-        7    10     4.15        2.85 |
 O2-        8    10     4.15        3.85 |  (b) ✓
 F          9     9     3.80        5.20 |

 Ti2+/V3+/Mn5+  all 20 e-, same sigma ⇒ Z* ∝ Z      (d) ✓
 ⇒ correct orders                              <-- answer (b), (c), (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Two reflexes settle almost every Zeff question. (1) Isoelectronic set ⇒ σ cancels ⇒ just rank by atomic number. (2) For one element, Z* increases with positive charge (cation > atom > anion), which is also why cations are smaller and anions larger. Trap: option (a) exploits the common muddle between size (Fe > Fe²⁺ > Fe³⁺, correct) and Z* (exactly the opposite).
Answer: (b), (c) and (d).
8Match the column: Column-I — (A) Increasing order of I.E., (B) Increasing order of electron affinity, (C) Increasing order of atomic size. Column-II — (P) F < O < S < Se, (Q) O < N < F < Ne, (R) Na < Mg < Al < Si, (S) O2− < O− < O < O+. (a) (A)–(Q); (B)–(S); (C)–(P) (b) (A)–(R); (B)–(S); (C)–(Q) (c) (A)–(P),(Q); (B)–(R); (C)–(S) (d) (A)–(S); (B)–(P); (C)–(Q)IE, EA and size trendsElectron gain enthalpy and the periodic trends of IE and size🟡 Similara(A)–(Q); (B)–(S); (C)–(P)
Concept Each row of column II is a specific numerical sequence, so the match is decided by testing the sequence against the actual trend rather than by a general rule. Note in particular that (R) — Na < Mg < Al < Si — is a decoy: it is neither the IE order (because of the Mg/Al anomaly) nor the size order (size falls across a period), so it matches nothing.
Key relation IE: across a period ↑, down a group ↓ · size: across a period ↓, down a group ↑ · electron affinity of a species ↑ as its positive charge ↑ an O⁺ ion pulls in an electron eagerly; O²⁻ resists strongly because adding to an already doubly-charged anion is heavily endothermic.
Solution
1. (A) Increasing I.E. → (Q). O 1314 < N 1402 < F 1681 < Ne 2081 kJ mol⁻¹ — a genuine increasing sequence, with the O < N inversion built in.
2. Reject (R) for (A): Na 496 < Mg 737 but Al is only 577, so Na < Mg < Al is false.
3. (B) Increasing electron affinity → (S). O²⁻ (strongly resists another electron) < O⁻ (endothermic, ΔHeg2 ≈ +780 kJ mol⁻¹) < O (−141) < O⁺ (hugely exothermic, equal to −IE₁ of O) ✓.
4. (C) Increasing atomic size → (P). F 72 < O 73 < S 100 < Se 116 pm. F and O are period-2 (tiny), S and Se lie below in groups 16 ✓.
5. Reject (R) for (C) as well: across period 3 the size decreases, Na 186 > Mg 160 > Al 143 > Si 118 pm.
 Col-I                      test                              Col-II
 (A) increasing I.E.   O 1314 < N 1402 < F 1681 < Ne 2081     (Q)
 (B) increasing E.A.   O2- < O- < O < O+  (charge ↑ ⇒ EA ↑)   (S)
 (C) increasing size   F 72 < O 73 < S 100 < Se 116 pm        (P)
 (R) Na < Mg < Al < Si  fails IE (Al dips) and fails size (falls) — decoy
 ⇒ (A)-(Q), (B)-(S), (C)-(P)                          <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick In matrix-match questions, hunt first for the row that fits nothing — eliminating it usually kills three of the four options in one stroke. Here (R) is that row. Also memorise the electron-affinity ladder for one element: X⁺ ≫ X > X⁻ > X²⁻; it is asked repeatedly for oxygen. Trap: F is the smallest atom shown but has the highest EA magnitude? No — Cl does; but among these four F is smallest in size, which is what (P) tests.
Answer: (a) (A)–(Q); (B)–(S); (C)–(P).
9Match the column: Column-I — (A) F > Cl > Br > I, (B) Fe3+ > Fe2+ > Fe, (C) I− > I > I+, (D) O > C > B > N. Column-II — (P) Ionisation energy, (Q) Size, (R) Magnitude of ΔHeg, (S) Effective nuclear charge. (a) (A)-(P),(S); (B)-(P),(R),(S); (C)-(Q); (D)-(R) (b) (A)-(S); (B)-(Q); (C)-(R); (D)-(P) (c) (A)-(Q),(R); (B)-(S); (C)-(P); (D)-(Q),(R),(S) (d) (A)-(Q); (B)-(Q),(R); (C)-(S); (D)-(R),(S)Multi-match: IE, size, ΔHeg, ZeffPeriodicity for JEE — IE, size, electron gain enthalpy and Zeff together🟡 Similara(A)-(P),(S); (B)-(P),(R),(S); (C)-(Q); (D)-(R)
Concept This is a multiple-match: one sequence may satisfy several properties at once. The discipline is to take each sequence and ask, property by property, "is this order true?" — a sequence that decreases in size will usually increase in IE and Zeff simultaneously, so several ticks per row are expected.
Key relation down a group: size ↑, IE ↓, Zeff on valence ↓ · for one element: cation smaller, higher Z*, higher IE, more exothermic electron gain · |ΔHeg| is largest for a species that is positively charged or has a nearly complete octet.
Solution
1. (A) F > Cl > Br > I. Ionisation energy falls down group 17 (1681, 1251, 1140, 1008) ✓ (P). Zeff felt by the valence electron also falls down the group ✓ (S). It is not the size order (that is reversed) and not the |ΔHeg| order (Cl > F). → (P), (S).
2. (B) Fe³⁺ > Fe²⁺ > Fe. The energy to remove the next electron rises with charge ✓ (P). Z* rises as electrons are stripped ✓ (S). And a more positive ion accepts an electron far more exothermically, so |ΔHeg| also rises ✓ (R). → (P), (R), (S).
3. (C) I⁻ > I > I⁺. Adding an electron expands the species, removing one contracts it — this is a pure size order ✓ (Q). → (Q).
4. (D) O > C > B > N. Magnitudes of ΔHeg (kJ mol⁻¹): O 141 > C 122 > B 27 > N ≈ 0 (nitrogen's half-filled 2p³ actually resists an extra electron) ✓ (R). → (R).
5. Only option (a) reproduces all four assignments.
 Col-I sequence          P (IE)   Q (size)  R (|ΔHeg|)  S (Z*)
 (A) F > Cl > Br > I       ✓        ✗          ✗          ✓
 (B) Fe3+ > Fe2+ > Fe      ✓        ✗          ✓          ✓
 (C) I- > I > I+           ✗        ✓          ✗          ✗
 (D) O > C > B > N         ✗        ✗          ✓          ✗
 |ΔHeg| / kJ/mol : O 141 > C 122 > B 27 > N ≈ 0
 ⇒ (A)-P,S ; (B)-P,R,S ; (C)-Q ; (D)-R          <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Nitrogen's electron gain enthalpy is essentially zero (even slightly positive) because 2p³ is half-filled — that single fact is what makes (D) a ΔHeg row rather than an IE row, and it is the most-tested anomaly in this chapter. Trap: candidates match (A) to |ΔHeg| out of habit; remember Cl > F for electron gain enthalpy because F's 2p orbital is small and crowded.
Answer: (a) (A)-(P),(S); (B)-(P),(R),(S); (C)-(Q); (D)-(R).
10Match the column: Column-I (elements) — (A) F, (B) Cl, (C) Fe, (D) He. Column-II (periodic properties) — (P) Maximum ionization energy, (Q) Maximum electronegativity, (R) Maximum electron affinity, (S) Variable oxidation state. (a) (A)-(Q); (B)-(R); (C)-(S); (D)-(P) (b) (A)-(S); (B)-(Q); (C)-(P); (D)-(R) (c) (A)-(Q),(P); (B)-(S); (C)-(R); (D)-(S) (d) (A)-(P); (B)-(Q),(R); (C)-(P),(Q),(R); (D)-(S)Record-holders of the periodic tableElectronegativity on the Pauling scale and the periodic extremes🟡 Similara(A)-(Q); (B)-(R); (C)-(S); (D)-(P)
Concept Four "world records" of the periodic table are being tested, and three of them are commonly confused with one another. Electronegativity, ionisation energy and electron affinity are three different measures of an atom's pull on electrons, and their record-holders are three different elements: F, He and Cl respectively. Variable oxidation state, in contrast, is a d-block property.
Key relation Highest electronegativity = F (4.0) · Highest ionisation energy = He (2372 kJ mol⁻¹) · Most negative ΔHeg = Cl (−349 kJ mol⁻¹) · Variable oxidation states ⇐ partly filled (n−1)d the (n−1)d and ns levels lie so close in energy that different numbers of electrons can be lost.
Solution
1. (A) F → (Q). Fluorine is the most electronegative element on the Pauling scale (4.0), a consequence of its very small size and high Zeff.
2. (D) He → (P). Helium has the highest first ionisation energy of all elements: a 1s² closed shell with only two protons' worth of screening and the smallest radius in the table.
3. (B) Cl → (R). Chlorine, not fluorine, has the most negative electron gain enthalpy. F's 2p sub-shell is so compact that the incoming electron suffers severe inter-electronic repulsion, which offsets its high nuclear pull.
4. (C) Fe → (S). Iron shows +2, +3 (and +6 in ferrate) because 3d and 4s are nearly degenerate — the defining behaviour of a transition metal.
5. That combination is option (a).
 property                        record holder   value
 Maximum electronegativity (Q)   F               4.0 (Pauling)
 Maximum ionization energy (P)   He              2372 kJ/mol
 Maximum electron affinity (R)   Cl              -349 kJ/mol   (F is -328)
 Variable oxidation state  (S)   Fe              +2, +3, (+6)
 ⇒ A-Q, B-R, C-S, D-P                             <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Fix the three "most electron-hungry" records apart in one line: EN → F, IE → He, EA → Cl. Examiners rotate these three every session and the commonest error is giving fluorine all three. Trap: option (c) offers F both (P) and (Q) — attractive, but helium beats fluorine on ionisation energy by nearly 700 kJ mol⁻¹.
Answer: (a) (A)-(Q); (B)-(R); (C)-(S); (D)-(P).

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1Select the incorrect statement(s)/order(s): (a) d-orbital can accommodate 10 electrons (b) For Na (2s²2p⁶3s¹) → Na⁺ (2s²2p⁶) → Na²⁺ (2s²2p⁵) → Na³⁺ (2s²2p⁴), the order of successive I.E. is I.E.1 < I.E.2 > I.E.3 (c) Number of unpaired electrons in Co2+ cation > number of unpaired electrons of Co3+ cation (d) First ionization energy of Pt is greater than that of PdOrbitals, successive IE, unpaired e⁻d-block configurations, unpaired electrons and exceptions🟡 Similara, b, conly (d) is a correct statement
Concept Three separate traps are set here: the difference between an orbital and a sub-shell, the monotonic nature of successive ionisation energies, and the high-spin electron counts of Co²⁺ versus Co³⁺. The fourth option tests the 5d > 4d ionisation-energy inversion caused by lanthanoid contraction.
Key relation one orbital holds 2 electrons; a d sub-shell (5 orbitals) holds 10 · IE₁ < IE₂ < IE₃ < … always · μ ⇐ number of unpaired e⁻ in the free ion (Hund maximum multiplicity) · lanthanoid contraction ⇒ 5d elements are as small as 4d but far heavier-nucleused ⇒ higher IE.
Solution
1. (a) INCORRECT. A single d-orbital takes only 2 electrons; it is the d sub-shell, with its five orbitals, that takes 10. The wording is wrong, so (a) is one of the answers.
2. (b) INCORRECT. The printed order has I.E.₂ > I.E.₃, but successive ionisation energies never fall. For sodium: 496, 4562, 6910 kJ mol⁻¹, i.e. IE₁ < IE₂ < IE₃.
3. (c) INCORRECT. Co²⁺ is 3d⁷ → t↑↑↑↑↑ pairing gives 3 unpaired electrons; Co³⁺ is 3d⁶ → 4 unpaired electrons in the free (high-spin) ion. So Co²⁺ < Co³⁺, the reverse of the statement.
4. (d) CORRECT. IE₁(Pt) = 870 > IE₁(Pd) = 804 kJ mol⁻¹.Because of the lanthanoid contraction Pt is no bigger than Pd, yet its nuclear charge is 32 units higher, so the 6s electron is held far more tightly. This is the general 5d > 4d pattern.
 option  claim                                verdict
 (a)     "d-ORBITAL holds 10 e-"              WRONG (orbital = 2, subshell = 10)
 (b)     IE1 < IE2 > IE3                      WRONG (Na: 496 < 4562 < 6910)
 (c)     unpaired: Co2+ > Co3+                WRONG (3d7 = 3, 3d6 = 4)
 (d)     IE1(Pt) > IE1(Pd)                    RIGHT (870 > 804 kJ/mol)
 ⇒ incorrect statements                       <-- answer (a), (b), (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever an option says "orbital", silently check whether the number quoted belongs to an orbital (2), a sub-shell (2, 6, 10, 14) or a shell (2n²). Half the "select the incorrect statement" marks in this chapter come from that one substitution. Trap for (c): people quote the complex low-spin counts (Co³⁺ in [Co(NH₃)₆]³⁺ is diamagnetic) — for a bare gaseous cation always use Hund's rule, i.e. maximum unpaired.
Answer: (a), (b) and (c) are the incorrect statements.
2There are three elements A, B and C. Their atomic numbers are Z1, Z2 and Z3 respectively. If Z1 − Z2 = 2 and (Z1+Z2)/2 = Z3 − 2, and the electronic configuration of element A is [Ar]3d64s2, then the correct order of magnetic momentum is/are: (a) B⁺ > A2+ > C2+ (b) A3+ > B2+ > C (c) B > A > C2+ (d) B = A3+ > C3+Spin-only magnetic momentMagnetic moment and the number of unpaired electrons in transition-metal ions🟡 Similara, b, call except (d)
Concept Magnetic moment questions are really electron-counting questions. Identify the three metals from the algebra, write the 3d configuration of every species named in the options (remembering that 4s electrons leave first), count unpaired electrons by Hund's rule, and only then convert to μ. Because μ increases monotonically with n, you can usually compare unpaired counts and never evaluate the square root.
Formula μ = √(n(n+2)) BM where n = number of unpaired electrons: n = 1,2,3,4,5,6 → μ = 1.73, 2.83, 3.87, 4.90, 5.92, 6.93 BM memorising this six-value ladder turns every such question into arithmetic-free comparison.
Solution
1. A = [Ar]3d⁶4s² ⇒ Z₁ = 26, A = Fe. From Z₁ − Z₂ = 2, Z₂ = 24 ⇒ B = Cr.
2. (Z₁+Z₂)/2 = Z₃ − 2 ⇒ (26+24)/2 = 25 = Z₃ − 2 ⇒ Z₃ = 27 ⇒ C = Co.
3. Write the species: B⁺ = Cr⁺ 3d⁵ (5), A²⁺ = Fe²⁺ 3d⁶ (4), C²⁺ = Co²⁺ 3d⁷ (3), A³⁺ = Fe³⁺ 3d⁵ (5), B²⁺ = Cr²⁺ 3d⁴ (4), C = Co 3d⁷4s² (3), B = Cr 3d⁵4s¹ (6), A = Fe 3d⁶4s² (4), C³⁺ = Co³⁺ 3d⁶ (4).
4. (a) 5 > 4 > 3 ⇒ 5.92 > 4.90 > 3.87 ✓. (b) 5 > 4 > 3 ⇒ same ladder ✓. (c) 6 > 4 > 3 ⇒ 6.93 > 4.90 > 3.87 ✓.
5. (d) claims B = A³⁺: Cr atom has 6 unpaired (3d⁵4s¹, the exception configuration) while Fe³⁺ has 5, so 6.93 ≠ 5.92 ✗.
 species   config        unpaired   mu (BM)
 B  = Cr   3d5 4s1          6        6.93
 B+ = Cr+  3d5              5        5.92
 B2+= Cr2+ 3d4              4        4.90
 A  = Fe   3d6 4s2          4        4.90
 A2+= Fe2+ 3d6              4        4.90
 A3+= Fe3+ 3d5              5        5.92
 C  = Co   3d7 4s2          3        3.87
 C2+= Co2+ 3d7              3        3.87
 C3+= Co3+ 3d6              4        4.90
 (a) 5.92 > 4.90 > 3.87 ✓   (b) 5.92 > 4.90 > 3.87 ✓
 (c) 6.93 > 4.90 > 3.87 ✓   (d) 6.93 = 5.92 ? NO      <-- answer (a),(b),(c)
⚡ JEE Trick Two configurations are worth burning into memory because they carry the maximum unpaired count in the 3d series: Cr = 3d⁵4s¹ (6 unpaired, μ = 6.93 BM) and Cu = 3d¹⁰4s¹ (1 unpaired). Option (d) exists only to catch students who write Cr as 3d⁴4s². Trap: always remove 4s before 3d when forming the cation — Fe²⁺ is 3d⁶, never 3d⁴4s².
Answer: (a), (b) and (c).
3Consider the following values of I.E. (eV) for elements W and X — W: 10.5, 15.5, 24.9, 79.8; X: 8, 14.8, 78.9, 105.8. Other two elements Y and Z have outer electronic configuration ns²np⁴ and ns²np⁵ respectively. Then according to the given information which of the following compound(s) is/are not possible? (a) W₂Y₃ (b) X₂Y₃ (c) WZ₂ (d) XZ₂Valency from IE jumpsReading the group of an element from its successive ionisation energies🟡 Similarb, cX₂Y₃ and WZ₂ are impossible
Concept The position of the big jump in a list of successive ionisation energies tells you exactly how many valence electrons an atom has: the jump happens when you start pulling electrons out of the noble-gas core. Once the valencies of all four elements are fixed, each formula is checked for charge balance — a formula that does not neutralise is impossible.
Key relation jump after IEn ⇒ n valence electrons ⇒ common ion Mn+ · ns²np⁴ ⇒ Y²⁻ (group 16) · ns²np⁵ ⇒ Z⁻ (group 17).
Solution
1. W: 10.5, 15.5, 24.9, then 79.8. The jump comes after the third ionisation (24.9 → 79.8, a factor of 3.2), so W has 3 valence electrons ⇒ W³⁺, a group-13 element.
2. X: 8, 14.8, then 78.9. The jump comes after the second (14.8 → 78.9, a factor of 5.3), so X has 2 valence electrons ⇒ X²⁺, a group-2 element.
3. Y is ns²np⁴ ⇒ gains 2 electrons ⇒ Y²⁻. Z is ns²np⁵ ⇒ gains 1 ⇒ Z⁻.
4. Test each formula for electrical neutrality: W₂Y₃ = 2(+3) + 3(−2) = 0 ✓ possible. X₂Y₃ = 2(+2) + 3(−2) = −2 ✗ impossible (the real compound is XY).
5. WZ₂ = (+3) + 2(−1) = +1 ✗ impossible (the real compound is WZ₃). XZ₂ = (+2) + 2(−1) = 0 ✓ possible.
 element   IE pattern (eV)              valence e-   ion
 W         10.5 15.5 24.9 | 79.8            3        W3+
 X         8    14.8 | 78.9 105.8           2        X2+
 Y         ns2 np4                          -        Y2-
 Z         ns2 np5                          -        Z-

 formula   charge check                 possible?
 W2Y3      2(+3) + 3(-2) = 0            YES
 X2Y3      2(+2) + 3(-2) = -2           NO    <-- answer (b)
 WZ2       (+3)  + 2(-1) = +1           NO    <-- answer (c)
 XZ2       (+2)  + 2(-1) = 0            YES
⚡ JEE Trick Do not look at the absolute IE values — look for the point where the value roughly triples or more. Count how many ionisations came before it; that is the group valency (1 → group 1, 2 → group 2, 3 → group 13, …). Trap: X's IE₁ (8 eV) is lower than W's (10.5 eV), tempting you to call X the more metallic and therefore trivalent; the jump position, not the magnitude, fixes the valency.
Answer: (b) X₂Y₃ and (c) WZ₂ are not possible.
4Consider the Born–Haber cycle for 2B(s) + 3⁄2 X₂(g) —ΔH₁→ B₂X₃(s), with ΔH₂ = sublimation of B, ΔH₃ = dissociation of X₂, ΔH₄ = ionisation B(g) → B³⁺(g), ΔH₅ = electron gain X(g) → X²⁻(g), ΔH₆ = lattice enthalpy. Then according to the given information the correct statement is/are: (a) ΔH₂ and ΔH₃ are always positive (b) ΔH₁ = 2ΔH₂ + 3⁄2 ΔH₃ + 2ΔH₄ + 3ΔH₅ + ΔH₆ (c) Second electron gain enthalpy of X is negative (d) ΔH₁ must be negative for formation of B₂X₃(s)Born–Haber cycleBorn–Haber cycle: lattice energy, Hess's law and enthalpy of formation🟡 Similara, b, donly (c) is wrong
Concept A Born–Haber cycle is Hess's law drawn as a box: the direct route (ΔH₁) must equal the sum of the indirect route. The only subtleties are (i) getting the stoichiometric coefficients right, because ΔH₂…ΔH₅ are quoted per mole of atom, and (ii) knowing the sign each individual step is forced to carry.
Key relation ΔH₁ = 2ΔH₂ + 3⁄2 ΔH₃ + 2ΔH₄ + 3ΔH₅ + ΔH₆ 2 mol B must be sublimed and ionised, 3⁄2 mol X₂ must be dissociated to give 3 mol X, each of which gains 2 electrons. Signs: sublimation > 0, bond dissociation > 0, ionisation > 0, lattice enthalpy < 0, ΔHeg1 < 0 but ΔHeg2 > 0.
Solution
1. (a) TRUE. ΔH₂ (solid → gas) and ΔH₃ (breaking the X–X bond) both require energy input; no substance sublimes or dissociates exothermically.
2. (b) TRUE. Each per-mole quantity is scaled by how many moles the balanced equation needs: 2 for B-sublimation and B-ionisation, 3⁄2 for X₂ dissociation, 3 for the electron-gain of X, and one lattice term ✓.
3. (c) FALSE. ΔH₅ produces X²⁻, so it contains a second electron gain. Forcing an electron onto an already negative X⁻ requires work against electrostatic repulsion, so ΔHeg2 is always positive (for oxygen, +780 kJ mol⁻¹).
4. (d) TRUE. B₂X₃(s) exists as a stable solid, which means it lies below the elements in energy: ΔH₁ < 0. The large negative lattice enthalpy ΔH₆ is what pays for all the endothermic steps.
 step     process                          sign      coefficient
 ΔH2      B(s) -> B(g)                     +          x2
 ΔH3      X2(g) -> 2X(g)                   +          x3/2
 ΔH4      B(g) -> B3+(g) + 3e-             +          x2
 ΔH5      X(g) + 2e- -> X2-(g)             +/- (net +) x3   [eg1 < 0, eg2 > 0]
 ΔH6      2B3+(g) + 3X2-(g) -> B2X3(s)     -          x1
 ---------------------------------------------------------------
 ΔH1 = 2ΔH2 + (3/2)ΔH3 + 2ΔH4 + 3ΔH5 + ΔH6   and ΔH1 < 0
 ⇒ correct statements                         <-- answer (a), (b), (d)
⚡ JEE Trick ΔHeg1 can be negative, ΔHeg2 is never negative. That one line kills option (c) instantly and is the single most-tested fact about O²⁻ and S²⁻. Second trick: when writing a Born–Haber sum, count moles off the balanced equation, not off the arrows — the coefficients 2, 3⁄2, 2, 3 are exactly what the equation demands.
Answer: (a), (b) and (d).
5Passage (i)–(iii): The energy required to pull the most loosely bound electron from an atom is known as ionization potential, expressed in electron volts; it depends on (i) the nuclear charge, (ii) the atomic radius and (iii) the screening effect of inner shells. (i) Ionization potential of Na would be numerically the same as: (a) electron affinity of Na⁺ (b) electronegativity of Na⁺ (c) electron affinity of Na (d) ionization potential of Mg. (ii) Which of the following elements has the least ionization potential? (a) Lithium (b) Cesium (c) Magnesium (d) Calcium. (iii) Incorrect order of ionization energy is: (a) Pb(I.E.) > Sn(I.E.) (b) Na⁺(I.E.) > Mg⁺(I.E.) (c) Li⁺(I.E.) < O⁺(I.E.) (d) Be⁺(I.E.) < C⁺(I.E.)Ionisation potential (passage)Successive ionisation energies and what they reveal about structure🟡 Similari-a, ii-b, iii-cEA of Na⁺ · Cs · Li⁺ < O⁺
Concept Ionisation and electron gain are the same process run in opposite directions, so numerically IE(M) = −ΔHeg(M⁺). Within a group IE falls downwards because the valence electron sits in a higher shell and is better screened. And for cations, the decisive question is always what electronic configuration you are digging into — a noble-gas core is fantastically expensive to break.
Key relation M → M⁺ + e⁻ ; ΔH = +IE and M⁺ + e⁻ → M ; ΔH = −IE = ΔHeg(M⁺) identical species on both sides, arrow reversed ⇒ equal magnitude.
Solution
1. (i) The electron affinity of Na⁺ describes Na⁺ + e⁻ → Na — the exact reverse of Na → Na⁺ + e⁻. Its magnitude therefore equals the ionisation potential of Na → (a). Option (c), the electron affinity of neutral Na, refers to Na + e⁻ → Na⁻, a completely different (and small) quantity.
2. (ii) Of Li, Cs, Mg and Ca, ionisation potential is smallest for the element lowest in group 1: Cs (376 kJ mol⁻¹), against Li 520, Ca 590, Mg 737 → (b).
3. (iii)(a) IE₁(Pb) = 716 > IE₁(Sn) = 709 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓ true (inert-pair effect makes Pb's 6s² unusually tightly held).
4. (iii)(b) Ionising Na⁺ means breaking the [Ne] core (4562 kJ mol⁻¹); ionising Mg⁺ only removes its 3s¹ electron (1451). So Na⁺ > Mg⁺ ✓ true.
5. (iii)(c) Li⁺ is 1s², a helium core: ionising it costs 7298 kJ mol⁻¹. O⁺ is 1s²2s²2p³, only 3388. So Li⁺ > O⁺ — the statement writes "<", so (c) is the incorrect order ✓.
6. (iii)(d) Be⁺ (2s¹) 1757 < C⁺ (2s²2p¹) 2352 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓ true.
 (iii)  comparison               values (kJ/mol)          verdict
 (a)    Pb > Sn                  716 > 709                TRUE
 (b)    Na+ > Mg+                4562 > 1451              TRUE
 (c)    Li+ < O+                 7298 vs 3388 -> Li+ > O+ FALSE  <-- answer (c)
 (d)    Be+ < C+                 1757 < 2352              TRUE
⚡ JEE Trick When a cation appears in an IE comparison, immediately write its configuration. If it is a noble-gas core (Li⁺ = He core, Na⁺ = Ne core, Mg²⁺ = Ne core) the ionisation energy jumps into the thousands and beats almost anything else on the list. Trap in (i): "electron affinity of Na" and "electron affinity of Na⁺" differ by two orders of magnitude — read the charge.
Answer: (i) (a) · (ii) (b) · (iii) (c).
6Passage (i)–(iii): Nuclear charge actually experienced by an electron is the effective nuclear charge Z*, which depends on the type of shell and orbital; the penetrating power order is s > p > d > f for the same n. Penultimate-shell electrons screen the valence electrons, reducing the nuclear charge; more shielding ⇒ less Z* ⇒ less ionization energy. (i) Which of the following valence electrons experiences maximum effective nuclear charge? (a) 4s¹ (b) 4p¹ (c) 3d¹ (d) 2p³. (ii) Which of the following is not concerned with effective nuclear charge? (a) Higher ionization potential of carbon than boron (b) Higher ionization potential of magnesium than aluminium (c) Higher values of successive ionization energy (d) Higher electronegativity of higher oxidation state. (iii) Ionization energy is not influenced by: (a) Size of atom (b) Effective nuclear charge (c) Electron present in inner shell (d) Change in entropyEffective nuclear charge (passage)Slater's rule and the meaning of effective nuclear charge🟡 Similari-d, ii-b, iii-d2p³ · Mg vs Al · entropy
Concept Z* felt by a valence electron is governed above all by how close to the nucleus that electron's shell is: a 2p electron sits inside a tiny n = 2 shell with only two 1s electrons screening it, whereas a 4s or 4p electron is buried under three complete shells. Separately, the passage warns that not every ionisation trend is a Z* effect — some are sub-shell stability effects, and some quantities (entropy) have nothing to do with IE at all.
Key relation Z* = Z − σ with σ rising sharply as more complete inner shells are added each n−2 shell contributes 1.00 per electron, so deep-lying valence electrons feel a heavily reduced nucleus. IE = f(Z*, r, shielding, sub-shell stability) — a state function of the atom, independent of entropy.
Solution
1. (i) Compare shells first: 2p³ lies in n = 2, screened only by 1s². Slater gives Z*(N, 2p) = 7 − (0.35×4 + 0.85×2) = 3.90, whereas 4s¹ of K is 19 − 16.8 = 2.20 and 3d¹ of Sc is 21 − 18 = 3.00, and 4p¹ of Ga is 31 − 27.15 ≈ 3.85 but with a far larger radius. The compact, poorly-screened 2p³ wins → (d).
2. (ii) (a) C > B in IE is straightforwardly Z* increasing across period 2 — concerned. (c) Successive IEs rise because each removal raises Z*/electron — concerned. (d) A higher oxidation state means fewer electrons and a larger Z*, hence greater electronegativity — concerned.
3. But (b) Mg > Al is an anomaly against Z*: aluminium has the larger Z*, yet the lower IE. The cause is the extra stability of Mg's completely filled 3s² and the higher energy of Al's 3p orbital. So (b) is not a Z* effect ✓.
4. (iii) Ionisation energy depends on size, on Z*, and on the inner (shielding) electrons — all listed. It is a fixed energy difference between two states of the atom and has nothing to do with entropy → (d).
 (i)  electron   element   Z*  (Slater)       comment
      2p3        N         3.90               n=2, screened only by 1s2   <-- max
      3d1        Sc        3.00
      4p1        Ga        ~3.85 but r large  deep under 3 full shells
      4s1        K         2.20

 (ii) trend                                  Z*-driven?
      IP(C) > IP(B)                          yes
      IP(Mg) > IP(Al)                        NO - filled 3s2 stability  <-- (b)
      successive IE rise                     yes
      EN rises with oxidation state          yes

 (iii) size ✓ · Z* ✓ · inner electrons ✓ · entropy ✗   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Split every ionisation-energy trend into two boxes before answering: "Z*/size box" (normal trends) and "sub-shell stability box" (the Be>B, Mg>Al, N>O, P>S anomalies). Questions like (ii) are answered simply by spotting which box the option belongs to. Trap in (i): candidates pick 4s¹ because s penetrates best — penetration only matters within the same shell; a lower n always wins.
Answer: (i) (d) · (ii) (b) · (iii) (d).
7Passage (i)–(iii): Nine elements A to I are arranged in a Star-of-David figure (E top; A left and C right; G, H, I across the middle; D bottom-left and F bottom-right; B bottom). All belong to the p-block other than halogens. Z(B) is the average of Z(A) and Z(C); Z(E) is the average of Z(D) and Z(F); Z(H) is the average of Z(G) and Z(I). Atomic numbers of B, E and H are 7, 15 and 83; and Z(C), Z(I), Z(F) are greater than Z(A), Z(G), Z(D) respectively. (i) The incorrect order is: (a) F > E: Second ionization energy (b) B > C: Zeff on valence shell (c) I > H: First ionisation energy (d) C > F > E: Electronegativity. (ii) The correct statement is: (a) +5 oxidation state of H is more stable than its +3 (b) G2+ is a better oxidizing agent than G4+ (c) +3 oxidation state of E is more stable than its +5 (d) Ionisation energy of G is greater than that of tin. (iii) Which statement is incorrect? (a) B₂C₅ is acidic (b) AC₂ is acidic (c) FC₃ is basic (d) GC₂ is amphotericp-block identification + inert pairInert pair effect and the oxidation states of heavy p-block elements🟡 Similari-c ⚠, ii-d, iii-csee note
Concept The three "average" conditions plus the restriction "p-block, not a halogen" pin every letter to a unique element. Once A–I are named, the passage becomes three ordinary questions about second ionisation energies, the inert-pair effect and the acid/base character of oxides.
Key relation Z(B) = ½[Z(A)+Z(C)] = 7 ⇒ the pair (6, 8) = (C, O); (5, 9) is rejected because 9 is fluorine, a halogen. Z(E) = 15 ⇒ (14, 16) = (Si, S); (13, 17) rejected — Cl is a halogen. Z(H) = 83 ⇒ (82, 84) = (Pb, Po); (81, 85) rejected — At is a halogen.
Solution
1. Identities: A = C(6), B = N(7), C = O(8), D = Si(14), E = P(15), F = S(16), G = Pb(82), H = Bi(83), I = Po(84).
2. (i)(a) IE₂: S⁺ is 3s²3p³ (half-filled, hard to break) 2252 > P⁺ 3s²3p² 1907 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ F > E ✓ true.
3. (i)(d) Electronegativity O 3.44 > S 2.58 > P 2.19 ⇒ C > F > E ✓ true.
4. (i)(c) The key's answer. Bi (H) has the half-filled 6p³ configuration, so on the standard "extra-stability of a half-filled sub-shell" argument IE₁(Bi) > IE₁(Po) and the printed order I > H would be wrong.
5. (ii) H = Bi is stable in +3, not +5 (inert pair) ✗(a). G = Pb: it is Pb⁴⁺ that is the strong oxidant, not Pb²⁺ ✗(b). E = P is stable in +5, not +3 ✗(c). IE₁(Pb) = 716 > IE₁(Sn) = 709 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓ (d).
6. (iii) B₂C₅ = N₂O₅, acidic ✓; AC₂ = CO₂, acidic ✓; GC₂ = PbO₂, amphoteric ✓; FC₃ = SO₃, strongly acidic — not basic, so (c) is the incorrect statement ✓.
 letter  Z    element   letter  Z    element   letter  Z    element
 A        6   C         D       14   Si        G       82   Pb
 B        7   N         E       15   P         H       83   Bi
 C        8   O         F       16   S         I       84   Po

 (iii)  B2C5 = N2O5 acidic ✓ | AC2 = CO2 acidic ✓
        FC3  = SO3  ACIDIC (printed "basic")  <-- answer (iii) (c)
        GC2  = PbO2 amphoteric ✓
 (ii)   IE1: Pb 716 > Sn 709 kJ/mol           <-- answer (ii) (d)
⚠ Note For part (i) the printed key is (c), which requires IE₁(Bi) > IE₁(Po) from the half-filled-6p³ rule. Measured values are the other way round — Bi 703, Po 812 kJ mol⁻¹ — so (c) is actually a correct order. Meanwhile option (b) is definitely wrong: B = N and C = O are neighbours in period 2, and Slater gives Z* = 3.90 for N against 4.55 for O, so O > N and "B > C" is false. On the data, (b) is the incorrect order; the key's (c) is defensible only on the textbook half-filled heuristic. Both are flagged for verification.
⚡ JEE Trick In "Star of David" / averaging puzzles, use the excluded family (here "no halogens") to discard the alternative pair — every one of the three averages had exactly two candidate pairs and the halogen rule killed one each time. For the chemistry, the inert-pair ladder is worth memorising: Bi prefers +3, Pb prefers +2, Tl prefers +1, and their higher states (Bi⁵⁺, Pb⁴⁺, Tl³⁺) are strong oxidants.
Answer (as keyed): (i) (c) ⚠ · (ii) (d) · (iii) (c).
8Passage (i)–(iii): A fragment of the long form of the periodic table is shown, with rows for periods 2–6 and columns for groups 13–17: period 2 — A, (—), U, V, W; period 3 — B, (—), T; period 4 — C, D, X, S, R; period 5 — E (group 15), Q (group 17); period 6 — H, G, F, (—), P. The four quantum numbers of the last electron of X are n = 4, l = 1, m = 1, s = −½, and the spin multiplicity of X in its ground state is 4. (i) Which order is incorrect? (a) Magnetic moment: U > V > A (b) Atomic radius: E > X > T (c) Ionisation energy: R > X > B (d) Stability: F³⁺ < E³⁺ < X³⁺. (ii) The correct order is: (a) I.E. of V > I.E. of U (b) E.A. of X > E.A. of S (c) E.A. of X > I.E. of D (d) |ΔHEG| of T > |ΔHEG| of U. (iii) Which statement is incorrect: (a) Element P is radioactive (b) Elements B and C have almost similar size (c) Element G is more stable in +4 oxidation state (d) Element G has an electron with n = 4, l = 3, m = 0, s = +½p-block map + inert pairAtomic size, d-block and lanthanoid contraction across the p-block🟡 Similari-d, ii-d, iii-cBi³⁺>Sb³⁺>As³⁺ · P vs N · Pb is +2
Concept The quantum numbers fix one square of the map, and the rest of the fragment follows from its geometry. Spin multiplicity 2S + 1 = 4 ⇒ S = 3⁄2 ⇒ three unpaired electrons, and n = 4, l = 1 means the 4p sub-shell; the only 4p configuration with three unpaired electrons is 4p³. So X = arsenic, and every other letter is located relative to it.
Key relation multiplicity = 2S + 1 = nunpaired + 1 multiplicity 4 ⇒ 3 unpaired ⇒ half-filled 4p³. inert-pair effect: stability of the (group − 2) state increases down groups 13–15.
Solution
1. X = 4p³ = As (33), period 4, group 15. Reading the fragment: A = B(5), U = N(7), V = O(8), W = F(9); B = Al(13), T = P(15); C = Ga(31), D = Ge(32), S = Se(34), R = Br(35); E = Sb(51), Q = I(53); H = Tl(81), G = Pb(82), F = Bi(83), P = At(85).
2. (i)(a) Unpaired electrons N 3 > O 2 > B 1 ⇒ μ 3.87 > 2.83 > 1.73 ✓ true.
3. (i)(b) Radii Sb 141 > As 119 > P 110 pm ✓ true. (i)(c) IE Br 1140 > As 947 > Al 577 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓ true.
4. (i)(d) INCORRECT. Stability of the +3 state increases down group 15 (inert-pair effect), so As³⁺ < Sb³⁺ < Bi³⁺, i.e. X³⁺ < E³⁺ < F³⁺ — exactly the reverse of what is printed ✓.
5. (ii) (a) IE: O 1314 < N 1402 ✗. (b) EA: As 78 < Se 195 kJ mol⁻¹ ✗. (c) an electron affinity (78) can never exceed an ionisation energy (Ge 762) ✗. (d) |ΔHEG|: P 72 > N ≈ 0 ✓ (d).Nitrogen's half-filled 2p³ plus its very small size make its electron gain enthalpy essentially zero, while phosphorus accepts an electron readily — the classic 2nd-period anomaly.
6. (iii) (a) At is radioactive ✓. (b) Al (143 pm) ≈ Ga (135 pm) because the intervening 3d series contracts gallium ✓. (d) Pb is [Xe]4f¹⁴5d¹⁰6s²6p², so it certainly owns 4f (n = 4, l = 3) electrons ✓. (c) Pb is more stable in +2, not +4 — incorrect ✓.
 grid    gp13   gp14   gp15   gp16   gp17
 per 2   A=B     -     U=N    V=O    W=F
 per 3   B=Al    -     T=P     -      -
 per 4   C=Ga   D=Ge   X=As   S=Se   R=Br
 per 5    -      -     E=Sb    -     Q=I
 per 6   H=Tl   G=Pb   F=Bi    -     P=At

 (i)  stability of +3:  As3+ < Sb3+ < Bi3+   (printed reverse) <-- (d)
 (ii) |ΔHeg| : P 72 > N ~0 kJ/mol                              <-- (d)
 (iii) Pb: +2 stable, +4 oxidising (inert pair)                <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Convert "spin multiplicity" to unpaired electrons on sight: multiplicity = unpaired + 1 (singlet 0, doublet 1, triplet 2, quartet 3, quintet 4). Then use the sub-shell that matches. For the p-block map itself, remember the two "near-equal size" pairs created by contractions — Al ≈ Ga (d-block contraction) and Zr ≈ Hf (lanthanoid contraction).
Answer: (i) (d) · (ii) (d) · (iii) (c).
9Passage (i)–(ii): Consider the following elements with their electronegativity value (Pauling scale) — A: 3.77, B: 1.12, C: 2.25, D: 3.10. (i) Incorrect statement is: (a) AOH is more acidic than DOH (b) BOH is more basic than COH (c) 'AB' molecule is predominantly ionic (d) 'D—OH' bond is weaker than 'B—OH' bond in polar solvent. (ii) Select correct statement: (a) Oxide of element D is more acidic than that of A (b) Oxides of elements C and D are basic in nature (c) Oxide of element B is acidic in nature (d) BOH is more basic than H₂OElectronegativity → acidity/basicityPauling's scale, electronegativity trends and ionic vs covalent character🟡 Similari-d, ii-dD—OH is stronger · BOH more basic than water
Concept For an E–O–H unit, the electronegativity of E decides which bond breaks. If E is very electronegative it pulls electron density away from the O–H bond, weakening it, and the species ionises as an acid (E–O–H → E–O⁻ + H⁺). If E is very electropositive, the E–O bond itself is ionic and snaps in a polar solvent, releasing OH⁻ — a base. So one number, the electronegativity, orders every statement in this passage.
Key relation EN(E) high ⇒ EOH acidic ; EN(E) low ⇒ EOH basic · ΔEN > 1.7 ⇒ predominantly ionic bond · oxide character: metal (low EN) → basic, non-metal (high EN) → acidic.
Solution
1. Rank: B (1.12) < C (2.25) < D (3.10) < A (3.77). B is the metal; A is the most electronegative non-metal.
2. (i)(a) A is more electronegative than D, so AOH is more acidic than DOH ✓ true.
3. (i)(b) B is the least electronegative, so BOH is the most basic — more basic than COH ✓ true.
4. (i)(c) ΔEN(A–B) = 3.77 − 1.12 = 2.65 > 1.7, so AB is predominantly ionic ✓ true.
5. (i)(d) INCORRECT. B—OH, with ΔEN ≈ 2.4, is a highly ionic bond that ruptures readily in a polar solvent; D—OH (ΔEN ≈ 0.4) is essentially covalent and much stronger. The statement has the comparison backwards ✓.
6. (ii) (a) A (3.77) > D (3.10), so A's oxide is the more acidic ✗. (b) C and D are electronegative non-metals — their oxides are acidic, not basic ✗. (c) B is a metal — its oxide is basic ✗. (d) BOH is a metal hydroxide with a very ionic B–O bond, so it is a stronger base than water ✓ (d).
 element   EN     character        EOH behaves as
 B        1.12    metal            strong base   (B-OH ionic, breaks easily)
 C        2.25    weak non-metal   weakly acidic
 D        3.10    non-metal        acidic
 A        3.77    non-metal        most acidic

 ΔEN(A-B) = 3.77 - 1.12 = 2.65 > 1.7  ⇒ AB ionic          (i)(c) true
 D-OH covalent & STRONG vs B-OH ionic & weak              (i)(d) FALSE
 BOH (metal hydroxide) is a stronger base than H2O        (ii)(d) TRUE
⚡ JEE Trick Reduce every E–O–H question to a single question: "which bond is more ionic, E–O or O–H?" The ionic one is the one that breaks in a polar solvent, and that tells you acid or base. The same electronegativity number simultaneously predicts the acid/base character of the oxide, so parts (i) and (ii) are one calculation. Trap: option (i)(d) sounds plausible because "more electronegative = stronger pull"; but a strong pull on the O makes E–O covalent, i.e. harder to break.
Answer: (i) (d) · (ii) (d).
10Match the atomic numbers of the elements given in Column I with the periods given in Column II and mark the appropriate choice. Column I (Atomic number): (A) 31, (B) 50, (C) 56, (D) 14. Column II (Period): (i) 5, (ii) 3, (iii) 4, (iv) 6. (a) (A)→(i), (B)→(ii), (C)→(iii), (D)→(iv) (b) (A)→(ii), (B)→(i), (C)→(iv), (D)→(iii) (c) (A)→(iii), (B)→(iv), (C)→(i), (D)→(ii) (d) (A)→(iii), (B)→(i), (C)→(iv), (D)→(ii)Atomic number → periodFinding the period of an element from its atomic number🟡 Similard(A)→4, (B)→5, (C)→6, (D)→3
Concept The period number is simply the highest principal quantum number that carries electrons in the ground-state configuration — equivalently, the number of occupied shells. The fastest route is to remember where each period ends, because those boundaries are the noble gases; any Z between two boundaries belongs to the later period.
Key relation period ends at Z = 2, 10, 18, 36, 54, 86 He, Ne, Ar, Kr, Xe, Rn — the six numbers that partition the whole table. period = highest n in the configuration.
Solution
1. (A) Z = 31: lies between 18 (Ar) and 36 (Kr) ⇒ period 4. Configuration [Ar]3d¹⁰4s²4p¹ = Ga, highest n = 4 ✓ → (iii).
2. (B) Z = 50: between 36 (Kr) and 54 (Xe) ⇒ period 5. [Kr]4d¹⁰5s²5p² = Sn ✓ → (i).
3. (C) Z = 56: just past 54 (Xe) ⇒ period 6. [Xe]6s² = Ba ✓ → (iv).
4. (D) Z = 14: between 10 (Ne) and 18 (Ar) ⇒ period 3. [Ne]3s²3p² = Si ✓ → (ii).
5. The set (A)→(iii), (B)→(i), (C)→(iv), (D)→(ii) is option (d).
 Z    element   configuration            highest n   period   Col-II
 31   Ga        [Ar] 3d10 4s2 4p1            4          4      (iii)
 50   Sn        [Kr] 4d10 5s2 5p2            5          5      (i)
 56   Ba        [Xe] 6s2                     6          6      (iv)
 14   Si        [Ne] 3s2 3p2                 3          3      (ii)
 noble-gas boundaries: 2 | 10 | 18 | 36 | 54 | 86      <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the six noble-gas atomic numbers 2, 10, 18, 36, 54, 86 and you can place any element's period in two seconds without writing a configuration — just find which pair Z falls between. Trap: for d-block elements the period is not the shell being filled (3d) but the outermost ns shell, so Z = 31 is period 4, not period 3.
Answer: (d) (A)→(iii) 4, (B)→(i) 5, (C)→(iv) 6, (D)→(ii) 3.

Level-9

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1 (i)Four elements P, Q, R & S have ground state electronic configuration P → 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p³, Q → 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p¹, R → 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁶3d¹⁰4s²4p³, S → 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁶3d¹⁰4s²4p¹. Comment which of the following option represent the correct order of true (T) & false (F) statement. I. size of P < size of Q II. size of R < size of S III. size of P < size of R (appreciable difference) IV. size of Q < size of S (appreciable difference). (a) TTTT (b) TTTF (c) FFTT (d) TTFFAtomic size, d-block contractionPredicting atomic radius across periods and down groups🟡 SimilarbTTTF
Concept The whole passage collapses the moment you convert the four configurations into four real elements. Once you have them, statements I–III are ordinary periodic-trend statements, but statement IV is the famous d-block (scandide) contraction trap: the ten 3d electrons inserted between Al and Ga screen the nucleus very poorly, so Ga does not continue the "size increases down a group" pattern. Al and Ga are essentially the same size — Ga is in fact slightly the smaller of the two on covalent/metallic scales. Note also the word "appreciable": the examiner is testing whether you know how much the size changes, not only the direction.
Key relation radius ↓ across a period Zeff rises while n stays the same. radius ↑ down a group a new shell is added. but 3d¹⁰ insertion ⇒ poor screening ⇒ r(Ga) ≈ r(Al) σ for a d electron is far below 1, so Zeff jumps by ~10 × (1 − σ) and cancels the extra shell.
Solution
1. Identify. P: total e⁻ = 15 → P (phosphorus). Q: 13 → Al. R: 33 → As. S: 31 → Ga.
2. I. size of P < size of Q ⇒ P vs Al. Both period 3; Al (gp 13) lies to the left of P (gp 15), and radius falls left→right, so Al is bigger. r(Al) = 143 pm, r(P) = 110 pm. TRUE.
3. II. size of R < size of S ⇒ As vs Ga. Both period 4, Ga (gp 13) is left of As (gp 15). r(Ga) = 135 pm, r(As) = 121 pm. TRUE.
4. III. size of P < size of As, appreciable ⇒ going down group 15 from period 3 to period 4 adds a whole shell: 110 → 121 pm. The direction is right and the change is a real one. TRUE.
5. IV. size of Al < size of Ga, appreciable ⇒ FALSE. Between Al and Ga the entire 3d series is inserted. Ten d electrons shield badly, Zeff on the 4p electron shoots up, and the expected expansion is wiped out: r(Ga) = 135 pm is actually smaller than r(Al) = 143 pm. So neither "<" nor "appreciable" survives.This same contraction is why Ga has a higher IE₁ than Al — see part (ii).
6. Pattern = T T T F ⇒ option (b). (a) TTTT fails at IV; (c) FFTT gets the two easy period trends backwards; (d) TTFF wrongly kills III, where a genuine new shell is added.
 symbol  config                         Z    element   radius/pm
 P       1s2 2s2 2p6 3s2 3p3            15   P            110
 Q       1s2 2s2 2p6 3s2 3p1            13   Al           143
 R       [Ar] 3d10 4s2 4p3              33   As           121
 S       [Ar] 3d10 4s2 4p1              31   Ga           135

 I    P(110)  < Al(143)                        T
 II   As(121) < Ga(135)                        T
 III  P(110)  < As(121)   new shell            T
 IV   Al(143) < Ga(135)?  NO - 3d contraction  F   <-- answer (b) TTTF
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a question hops from period 3 to period 4 inside groups 13–17, stop and ask "did I cross the 3d block?" If yes, the normal down-the-group increase is cancelled: Al ≈ Ga, Si < Ge only slightly, and Ga even has a higher IE than Al. The trap here is option (a) TTTT, which you pick automatically if you apply "size increases down a group" without thinking.
Answer: (b) TTTF — statement IV is false because of the 3d (scandide) contraction.
1 (ii)(Same four elements P = 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p³, Q = 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p¹, R = [Ar]3d¹⁰4s²4p³, S = [Ar]3d¹⁰4s²4p¹.) Order of IE1 values among the following is (a) P > R > S > Q (b) P < R < S < Q (c) R > S > P > Q (d) P > S > R > QIonisation energy order, Ga > Al anomalyIonisation-energy trends and their exceptions for JEE🟡 SimilaraP > R > S > Q
Concept Ionisation energy is the mirror image of size: the smaller and the more tightly held the outermost electron, the larger the IE. Two extra effects decide this particular order. First, a half-filled p³ configuration (P and As) is extra stable, so those two sit above their group-13 neighbours. Second, the d-block contraction makes Ga marginally harder to ionise than Al — the one place where IE goes up on moving down a group.
Key relation IE ∝ Zeff/r² · np³ (half-filled) ⇒ extra exchange stability ⇒ IE anomalously high · IE₁(Ga) > IE₁(Al) Ga's 4p electron feels the badly-shielded 3d¹⁰ core.
Solution
1. Elements again: P = P, Q = Al, R = As, S = Ga.
2. Period 3 pair. P (3p³, half-filled, small) is far harder to ionise than Al (3p¹, and removing that lone p electron leaves a stable 3s² core): IE₁(P) = 1012, IE₁(Al) = 577 kJ mol⁻¹. So P is the maximum of the four.
3. Period 4 pair. Same logic: As (4p³) > Ga (4p¹): 947 vs 579 kJ mol⁻¹.
4. Cross-comparison. Down group 15, P (1012) > As (947) as expected. Down group 13, however, Ga (579) is very slightly greater than Al (577) — the d-block contraction anomaly. Either way Ga and Al are the two smallest values and Al is the smallest.So the position of Q at the far right of the order is safe even if you only remember "Ga ≈ Al".
5. Assemble: P (1012) > As (947) > Ga (579) > Al (577), i.e. P > R > S > Q = option (a).
6. Eliminate: (b) is the exact reverse; (c) puts As above P, contradicting the group trend; (d) puts Ga above As, ignoring the half-filled-p³ stability of As.
 element  valence config   IE1 / kJ mol-1
 P  (P)   3s2 3p3  (half)      1012      highest
 R  (As)  4s2 4p3  (half)       947
 S  (Ga)  4s2 4p1               579      > Al : d-block contraction
 Q  (Al)  3s2 3p1               577      lowest

 order :  P > R > S > Q                  <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Build the order in two strokes: first split the set by group (all p³ above all p¹, because half-filled beats "one lone p electron"), then order within each group. Only then worry about the Ga/Al tie-break. Trap: option (d) is designed for students who apply "IE decreases down the group" blindly to Ga vs As instead of comparing electron configurations.
Answer: (a) P > R > S > Q, i.e. P > As > Ga > Al.
2Match the various sequences with the appropriate orders: Sequences — (I) Na⁺ Mg²⁺ Al³⁺, (II) I⁻ S²⁻ N³⁻, (III) O O⁻ O²⁻, (IV) N F O (IE₂). Orders — (A) increasing size of ion in gaseous state, (B) decreasing size of ion in gaseous state, (C) increasing size of ions aqueous state, (D) decreasing size of ions aqueous state, (E) increasing order of IE₂, (F) decreasing order of electron affinity. (a) I-(B), II-(C), III-(E), IV-(F) (b) I-(C), II-(B), III-(A), IV-(F) (c) I-(B), II-(C), III-(F), IV-(E) (d) I-(B), II-(C), III-(A), IV-(E)Isoelectronic radii, hydrated size, EA, IE₂Ordering the radii of isoelectronic ions🟡 SimilarcI-(B), II-(C), III-(F), IV-(E)
Concept Four independent ideas are being matched: (i) among isoelectronic ions, size falls as nuclear charge rises; (ii) hydrated size runs opposite to bare ionic size, because a small, highly charged ion grips more water molecules; (iii) successive electron gain becomes ever more endothermic, so EA falls sharply from a neutral atom to an anion; (iv) IE₂ is decided by the configuration of the mono-cation, not of the neutral atom.
Key relation isoelectronic: r ∝ 1/Z · r(hydrated) ∝ charge density = q/r so the smallest bare ion is the biggest hydrated ion. · EA: X > X⁻ > X²⁻ adding e⁻ to an already-negative ion needs energy. · IE₂(X) = IE₁(X⁺)
Solution
1. (I) Na⁺, Mg²⁺, Al³⁺. All are 10-electron species; Z = 11, 12, 13. More protons pulling the same 10 electrons ⇒ 102 > 72 > 53.5 pm. The sequence as printed is therefore decreasing size in the gaseous state → (B).
2. (II) I⁻, S²⁻, N³⁻. Bare radii: 216 > 184 > 171 pm, so in the gas phase this is also decreasing — but (B) is used, and the list deliberately offers aqueous options. Charge density q/r: I⁻ smallest, N³⁻ largest, so the hydration sheath grows I⁻ < S²⁻ < N³⁻. As printed this is increasing size in aqueous state → (C).Exactly the same inversion that makes Li⁺ the slowest-moving alkali cation in water despite being the smallest bare ion.
3. (III) O, O⁻, O²⁻. ΔHeg(O) = −141 kJ mol⁻¹ (exothermic, favourable); ΔHeg(O⁻) = +780 kJ mol⁻¹ (you must force an electron onto a negative ion); O²⁻ would be worse still. So electron affinity falls steeply: decreasing order of electron affinity → (F).
4. (IV) N, F, O for IE₂. Remove the second electron from the cations: N⁺ = 2s²2p², F⁺ = 2s²2p⁴, O⁺ = 2s²2p³ (half-filled, extra stable). Hardest is O⁺. Values: N 2856, F 3374, O 3388 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ N < F < O, i.e. as printed increasing order of IE₂ → (E).
5. I-(B), II-(C), III-(F), IV-(E) = option (c). Options (a) and (d) both mis-handle sequence III (it is an EA list, not an IE₂ or size list), and (b) puts the isoelectronic cations in an aqueous box.
 (I)   Na+ 102 > Mg2+ 72 > Al3+ 53.5 pm   isoelectronic, Z up  -> (B)
 (II)  hydrated: I- < S2- < N3-           charge density up    -> (C)
 (III) dHeg: O -141 > O- +780 > O2- ++    ever more endothermic -> (F)
 (IV)  IE2 : N 2856 < F 3374 < O 3388     O+ = 2p3 half filled -> (E)
                                                       <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Two reflexes clear this in seconds. (1) If a list is isoelectronic, ignore everything except the atomic number. (2) If the word "aqueous" appears, flip the gaseous order — hydrated radius is inverse to bare radius. The trap is sequence (IV): students compute IE₂ from the neutral atoms (N > O > F thinking) instead of from the cations, where O⁺ is the half-filled one.
Answer: (c) I-(B), II-(C), III-(F), IV-(E).
3It is a data sufficiency problem. On the basis of the given statements, decide whether the following question can be answered or not (irrespective of whether the final answer is "Yes" or "No"). Will solid aluminium chloride, AlCl₃(s), behave as an ionic compound in aqueous solution? It is given that if ΔH(solution) < 0, the compound behaves as ionic in aqueous solution. Statement (1): The lattice enthalpy of AlCl₃ is 5137 kJ mol⁻¹. Statement (2): The hydration enthalpy of Al³⁺ ion is −4665 kJ mol⁻¹ and that of Cl⁻ ion is −381 kJ mol⁻¹. (a) Statement (1) alone is sufficient, but statement (2) alone is not sufficient. (b) Statement (2) alone is sufficient, but statement (1) alone is not sufficient. (c) Both statements together are sufficient, but neither statement alone is sufficient. (d) Both statements together are not sufficient.ΔHsoln = lattice + hydrationLattice energy vs hydration energy and solubility of ionic solids🟡 Similarcboth together sufficient, neither alone
Concept Dissolution of an ionic solid is a two-step thermodynamic cycle: first pull the lattice apart into gaseous ions (costs the lattice enthalpy, endothermic), then hydrate those ions (releases the hydration enthalpy, exothermic). The sign of the sum decides everything. A data-sufficiency question is not asking you to answer "yes/no" — it asks whether the numbers supplied are enough to compute ΔHsoln. Since the sum needs both terms, one term alone can never settle the sign.
Key relation ΔHsoln = ΔHlattice dissociation + ΔHhydration · ΔHhyd(AlCl₃) = ΔHhyd(Al³⁺) + 3 ΔHhyd(Cl⁻) one formula unit gives one Al³⁺ and three Cl⁻.
Solution
1. Statement (1) alone: gives +5137 kJ mol⁻¹ for breaking the lattice, but with no hydration data the sum is unknown — ΔHsoln could be either sign. Not sufficient.
2. Statement (2) alone: gives the hydration side, −4665 + 3(−381) = −5808 kJ mol⁻¹, but without the lattice enthalpy the sum is again unknown. Not sufficient.
3. Together: ΔHsoln = +5137 + (−5808) = −671 kJ mol⁻¹, which is < 0, so by the stated criterion AlCl₃ does behave ionically in water. Both statements together are sufficient.
4. Therefore option (c). (a) and (b) each claim one half of a two-term sum is enough — impossible. (d) is wrong because the combined data do yield a definite number.
5. Note the physical sense of the result: solid AlCl₃ is covalent (dimeric Al₂Cl₆), yet in water the huge hydration energy of the small, triply charged Al³⁺ ion overwhelms the lattice term and forces ionisation — which is why AlCl₃ solutions conduct and are strongly acidic.[Al(H₂O)₆]³⁺ is a genuine Brønsted acid, Ka ≈ 10⁻⁵.
 step                                        dH / kJ mol-1
 AlCl3(s)  ->  Al3+(g) + 3 Cl-(g)               +5137     [Stmt 1]
 Al3+(g)   ->  Al3+(aq)                         -4665     [Stmt 2]
 3 Cl-(g)  ->  3 Cl-(aq)     3 x (-381)         -1143     [Stmt 2]
 ------------------------------------------------------
 AlCl3(s)  ->  Al3+(aq) + 3 Cl-(aq)              -671  < 0  => ionic

 (1) alone : one term only  -> sign unknown
 (2) alone : one term only  -> sign unknown       <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For any data-sufficiency item, count the unknowns in the governing equation before doing arithmetic. ΔHsoln has exactly two inputs, each statement supplies exactly one, so (c) is forced without touching a calculator. Trap: multiply the Cl⁻ hydration enthalpy by 3 — forgetting the stoichiometric coefficient gives −4665 − 381 = −5046 and a wrong sign story for many similar problems.
Answer: (c) Both statements together are sufficient, but neither statement alone is sufficient (ΔHsoln = −671 kJ mol⁻¹).
4 (A)From the following information: A⁻(g) → A²⁺(g) + 3e⁻, ΔH₁ = 1400 kJ; A(g) → A²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻, ΔH₂ = 700 kJ; ΔHEG[A⁺(g)] = −350 kJ/mol; (IE₁ + IE₂) for A(g) = 950 kJ/mol. Find (A) IE₁ of A.IE₁ from ΔHeg of the cationElectron gain enthalpy and its relation to ionisation energy🟡 Similar350kJ/mol
Concept The single idea that unlocks all four parts is reciprocity: ionisation and electron gain are the same process run in opposite directions, so their enthalpies are equal and opposite. Attaching an electron to A⁺ gives back A — that is literally the reverse of the first ionisation of A. So a quantity labelled "electron gain enthalpy of the cation" is a disguised ionisation energy.
Key relation A(g) → A⁺(g) + e⁻   ΔH = IE₁ · A⁺(g) + e⁻ → A(g)   ΔH = ΔHEG[A⁺] ⇒ IE₁ = −ΔHEG[A⁺(g)] Hess's law: reversing a step reverses the sign of its enthalpy.
Solution
1. Write out what is given: ΔHEG[A⁺(g)] = −350 kJ mol⁻¹ describes A⁺(g) + e⁻ → A(g).
2. Reverse it: A(g) → A⁺(g) + e⁻, ΔH = +350 kJ mol⁻¹.
3. That reversed step is by definition the first ionisation energy. Hence IE₁ = 350 kJ mol⁻¹.
4. Sanity check with part (B): IE₁ + IE₂ = 950, so IE₂ = 600 > IE₁ = 350 — successive ionisation energies must increase, and they do.If your IE₁ had come out larger than IE₂ you would know the sign had been flipped.
 A+(g) + e-  ->  A(g)        dHEG[A+] = -350 kJ/mol   (given)
 reverse:
 A(g)  ->  A+(g) + e-        dH       = +350 kJ/mol = IE1

                                       <-- answer (A) 350 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Read the species in the bracket, not the name of the quantity. "ΔHeg of X⁺" = −IE₁(X); "ΔHeg of X²⁺" = −IE₂(X); "IE of X⁻" = −ΔHeg(X). The trap is copying the −350 straight into the answer box, because the number "looks like" an answer.
Answer: (A) IE₁ of A = 350 kJ/mol.
4 (B)(Same data as 4(A): ΔH₁ = 1400 kJ, ΔH₂ = 700 kJ, ΔHEG[A⁺(g)] = −350 kJ/mol, IE₁ + IE₂ = 950 kJ/mol.) Find (B) IE₂ of A.Successive ionisation energiesSuccessive ionisation energies explained🟡 Similar600kJ/mol
Concept Successive ionisation energies simply add: the energy to strip two electrons is IE₁ + IE₂, so once one of them is fixed the other follows by subtraction. Physically IE₂ must exceed IE₁ because the second electron is being pulled off a species that is already positively charged and has a smaller radius, so Zeff/r² is larger.
Key relation A(g) → A²⁺(g) + 2e⁻   ΔH = IE₁ + IE₂ = 950 kJ mol⁻¹ · IE₂ = (IE₁ + IE₂) − IE₁ · IE₁ < IE₂ < IE₃ … always, with no exceptions.
Solution
1. From part (A), IE₁ = 350 kJ mol⁻¹ (obtained by reversing ΔHEG[A⁺] = −350).
2. Given IE₁ + IE₂ = 950 kJ mol⁻¹.
3. Subtract: IE₂ = 950 − 350 = 600 kJ mol⁻¹.
4. Check the physics: 600 > 350, and the ratio IE₂/IE₁ ≈ 1.7 — a modest jump, so A is not an alkali metal; it loses two electrons comfortably, consistent with the problem's repeated use of A²⁺.A jump of 4–10× would have signalled that the second electron came from a noble-gas core.
 IE1 + IE2 = 950 kJ/mol      (given)
 IE1       = 350 kJ/mol      (from part A)
 -----------------------------------
 IE2       = 950 - 350 = 600 kJ/mol

 check: IE2 > IE1  (600 > 350) OK    <-- answer (B) 600 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick In Born–Haber style data sets, always chain the parts: solve the one with the fewest unknowns first (here (A) from the electron-gain datum), then every later part is one subtraction. Trap: reading "IE₁ + IE₂ = 950" as "IE₂ = 950".
Answer: (B) IE₂ of A = 600 kJ/mol.
4 (C)(Same data.) Find (C) ΔHEG of A.Electron gain enthalpy from a cycleWorking with electron gain enthalpy values🟡 Similar−450kJ/mol
Concept ΔH₁ describes stripping three electrons from the anion A⁻ all the way to A²⁺. Split that long jump into its three elementary one-electron steps; two of them are the ionisation energies you already know, and the leftover step is the reverse of electron gain by A. Hess's law does the rest — the total enthalpy of a route is path-independent.
Key relation A⁻ → A²⁺ + 3e⁻ = [A⁻ → A + e⁻] + [A → A⁺ + e⁻] + [A⁺ → A²⁺ + e⁻] · A⁻(g) → A(g) + e⁻ has ΔH = −ΔHEG(A) it is the reverse of A + e⁻ → A⁻.
Solution
1. Decompose ΔH₁: 1400 = [−ΔHEG(A)] + IE₁ + IE₂.
2. Substitute IE₁ + IE₂ = 950: 1400 = −ΔHEG(A) + 950.
3. So −ΔHEG(A) = 1400 − 950 = 450, giving ΔHEG(A) = −450 kJ mol⁻¹.
4. The negative sign is the expected one: A releases energy on accepting an electron, i.e. A is an electronegative, non-metallic element.−450 kJ mol⁻¹ is larger in magnitude than any real element (Cl is −349), but the numbers here are invented for the cycle.
5. Common error: treating ΔH₁ as if it started from neutral A, which would give ΔHEG = 1400 − 950 = +450 with the wrong sign.
 A-(g)  ->  A(g)   + e-        -dHEG(A)
 A(g)   ->  A+(g)  + e-          IE1
 A+(g)  ->  A2+(g) + e-          IE2
 -------------------------------------------
 A-(g)  ->  A2+(g) + 3e-       dH1 = 1400 kJ

 1400 = -dHEG(A) + (IE1 + IE2) = -dHEG(A) + 950
 dHEG(A) = -(1400 - 950) = -450 kJ/mol   <-- answer (C)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a reaction shows an anion on the left and a cation on the right, immediately write it as "reverse-EA + all the IEs". Counting the electrons released (3 here) tells you exactly how many one-electron steps you need. Trap: the sign of the reversed electron-gain step — write the arrow both ways on paper before substituting.
Answer: (C) ΔHEG of A = −450 kJ/mol.
4 (D)(Same data.) Find (D) ΔHHE of A²⁺(g).Hydration enthalpy from a cycleEnthalpy of hydration, lattice energy and enthalpy of solution🟡 Similar−250kJ/mol
Concept ΔH₂ takes A(g) all the way to the aqueous dication. That journey is two legs: ionise twice in the gas phase (IE₁ + IE₂), then dump the gaseous A²⁺ into water (hydration enthalpy, always exothermic). Because the overall change (700 kJ) is smaller than the ionisation cost alone (950 kJ), the hydration step must be releasing energy — which is exactly what a hydration enthalpy does.
Key relation A(g) → A²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ = [A(g) → A²⁺(g) + 2e⁻] + [A²⁺(g) → A²⁺(aq)] · ΔH₂ = (IE₁ + IE₂) + ΔHHE(A²⁺) · ΔHHE < 0 always ion–dipole attraction to water always releases energy.
Solution
1. Write the cycle: 700 = (IE₁ + IE₂) + ΔHHE(A²⁺) = 950 + ΔHHE(A²⁺).
2. Rearrange: ΔHHE(A²⁺) = 700 − 950 = −250 kJ mol⁻¹.
3. Sign check: negative, as every hydration enthalpy must be. A positive answer would mean water spontaneously pushes ions apart.
4. Magnitude check: hydration enthalpy scales as q²/r, so a dication should be several times more exothermic than a monocation of similar size — here the modest −250 kJ mol⁻¹ implies a fairly large A²⁺ ion.Compare real values: Mg²⁺ −1921, Ba²⁺ −1305 kJ mol⁻¹; the invented numbers keep the arithmetic clean.
