 A(g)     ->  A2+(g) + 2e-        IE1 + IE2 = +950
 A2+(g)   ->  A2+(aq)             dHHE      =    x
 --------------------------------------------------
 A(g)     ->  A2+(aq) + 2e-       dH2       = +700

 950 + x = 700   =>   x = -250 kJ/mol   <-- answer (D)
⚡ JEE Trick Spot the phase label (aq) on the right-hand side — it is the only signal that a hydration term is hiding in the equation. Subtract the gas-phase ionisation cost and whatever remains is the hydration enthalpy. Trap: writing 950 − 700 = +250 and reporting a positive hydration enthalpy.
Answer: (D) ΔHHE of A²⁺(g) = −250 kJ/mol.
5 (1)From the given energy-level diagram (levels in kJ: Na(s)+½Cl₂(g) = Al(s)+3/2Cl₂(g) = 0; Na(g)+½Cl₂(g) = 20; Na(g)+Cl(g) = 110; Na(g)+Cl⁻(g) = 40; Na⁺(g)+Cl⁻(g) = 130; NaCl(s) = 90; NaAlCl₄(s) = 30; Al³⁺(g)+3Cl⁻(g) = 130; Al²⁺(g)+3Cl⁻(g) = 110; AlCl₃(s) = 90; Al³⁺(g)+3Cl⁻(aq) = 80; Al³⁺(aq)+3Cl⁻(aq) = 40): (1) ΔHL.E. of NaAlCl₄ will be ______.Lattice enthalpy from an energy diagramLattice energy and the Born–Haber cycle worked step by step🟡 Similar−230kJ
Concept On an energy-level (Born–Haber) diagram every quantity you are asked for is just a vertical distance: ΔH = (energy of the final level) − (energy of the initial level). Lattice enthalpy, by the formation convention used here, is the energy released when the separated gaseous ions collapse into one mole of the solid, so it is measured downwards from the ion level to the solid level and is negative. NaAlCl₄ contains three kinds of gaseous ion — Na⁺, Al³⁺ and four Cl⁻ — so its starting level is the sum of the two ionic plateaux drawn on the two halves of the diagram.
Key relation ΔHL.E. = H(solid) − H(gaseous ions) · Na⁺(g) + Al³⁺(g) + 4Cl⁻(g) → NaAlCl₄(s) · H(ions) = [Na⁺ + Cl⁻] + [Al³⁺ + 3Cl⁻] = 130 + 130 = 260 kJ both halves are referred to the same zero, so their heights simply add.
Solution
1. Read the level of the fully ionised sodium set: Na⁺(g) + Cl⁻(g) = 130 kJ.
2. Read the level of the fully ionised aluminium set: Al³⁺(g) + 3Cl⁻(g) = 130 kJ.
3. Adding gives the level of Na⁺(g) + Al³⁺(g) + 4Cl⁻(g) = 130 + 130 = 260 kJ above the elements.
4. Read the solid: NaAlCl₄(s) = 30 kJ.
5. Lattice enthalpy = 30 − 260 = −230 kJ. Negative, i.e. energy is released as the ions lock into the lattice — always true for lattice formation enthalpy.If a question instead asks for "lattice dissociation enthalpy" the same number appears with a + sign.
 Na+(g) + Cl-(g)          =  130 kJ
 Al3+(g) + 3Cl-(g)        =  130 kJ
 ---------------------------------- add
 Na+ + Al3+ + 4Cl- (g)    =  260 kJ
 NaAlCl4(s)               =   30 kJ
 ---------------------------------- subtract
 dH(L.E.) = 30 - 260      = -230 kJ   <-- answer (1) -230 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick On these diagrams, never "do chemistry" — just subtract heights, and always in the order final minus initial. For a mixed salt like NaAlCl₄, add the two ionic plateaux because both are measured from the same zero line. Trap: quoting +230 by subtracting the wrong way round, or using only one of the two ionic levels.
Answer: (1) ΔHL.E. of NaAlCl₄ = −230 kJ.
5 (2)(Same energy-level diagram.) (2) ΔHH.E. of Al⁺³(g) ion will be ______.Hydration enthalpy from the diagramEnthalpy of solution and enthalpy of hydration🟡 Similar−40kJ
Concept Hydration enthalpy of a particular ion is the enthalpy change when that ion alone passes from the gaseous state into water, with everything else in the equation left untouched. On the diagram the designers have provided exactly that pair of levels: Al³⁺(g) + 3Cl⁻(aq) and Al³⁺(aq) + 3Cl⁻(aq). The chloride ions are already aqueous in both, so the only change between the two levels is the hydration of Al³⁺.
Key relation Al³⁺(g) + 3Cl⁻(aq) → Al³⁺(aq) + 3Cl⁻(aq) · ΔHH.E.(Al³⁺) = H(final) − H(initial) · ΔHhyd ∝ −q²/r always exothermic; bigger for small, highly-charged ions.
Solution
1. Locate the level where only the chlorides are hydrated: Al³⁺(g) + 3Cl⁻(aq) = 80 kJ.
2. Locate the level where the aluminium ion is also hydrated: Al³⁺(aq) + 3Cl⁻(aq) = 40 kJ.
3. Subtract, final − initial: ΔHH.E.(Al³⁺) = 40 − 80 = −40 kJ.
4. Sign check: hydration is exothermic, so a negative value is compulsory. Choosing the wrong pair of levels — for instance Al³⁺(g) + 3Cl⁻(g) at 130 down to 40 — would give −90 kJ, which is the hydration of all four ions, not of Al³⁺ alone.That is precisely the distractor the diagram is built to catch.
 Al3+(g)  + 3Cl-(aq)      =  80 kJ    (only Cl- hydrated)
 Al3+(aq) + 3Cl-(aq)      =  40 kJ    (Al3+ now hydrated too)
 ------------------------------------
 dH(H.E.) of Al3+ = 40 - 80 = -40 kJ  <-- answer (2) -40 kJ

 wrong pair: 40 - 130 = -90 kJ  = hydration of Al3+ AND 3Cl-
⚡ JEE Trick Before subtracting, compare the two labels species-by-species and confirm that exactly one species changes phase. If two things change, you are computing a combined enthalpy, not a single-ion one. Trap: the diagram deliberately supplies an all-gaseous ion level so that a careless subtraction gives −90 kJ.
Answer: (2) ΔHH.E. of Al³⁺(g) = −40 kJ.
5 (3)(Same energy-level diagram.) (3) ΔHf of NaCl(s) will be ______.Enthalpy of formation from the diagramCalculating with a Born–Haber cycle: formation enthalpy🟡 Similar90kJ
Concept Standard enthalpy of formation is measured from the elements in their standard states, which is exactly the zero line of this diagram — Na(s) + ½Cl₂(g). So ΔHf of any compound on the chart is simply the height of that compound's level above (or below) the baseline; no cycle arithmetic is needed at all. This is why Born–Haber diagrams are drawn with the elements at zero.
Key relation Na(s) + ½Cl₂(g) → NaCl(s), ΔH = ΔHf · ΔHf = H(NaCl(s)) − H(elements) = H(NaCl) − 0 · Born–Haber check: ΔHf = ΔHsub + ½BE + IE₁ + ΔHeg + U
Solution
1. The baseline Na(s) + ½Cl₂(g) is at 0 kJ by construction.
2. The NaCl(s) level sits at 90 kJ.
3. Therefore ΔHf[NaCl(s)] = 90 − 0 = +90 kJ.
4. Verify by walking the full Born–Haber path and checking it lands on the same level: sublimation 20, then ½BE 90 (20 → 110), then IE₁ of Na = 90 and ΔHeg(Cl) = −70 taking you to Na⁺(g)+Cl⁻(g) at 130, then the lattice step 90 − 130 = −40. Sum: 20 + 90 + 90 − 70 − 40 = +90 kJ ✓.The internal consistency of the whole chart is what confirms every level reading.
5. Do not "correct" the sign from memory of the real value (real NaCl has ΔHf = −411 kJ mol⁻¹). The diagram supplies its own invented numbers and the question asks you to read this chart.
 Na(s) + 1/2 Cl2(g)   =   0 kJ    (elements, standard states)
 NaCl(s)              =  90 kJ
 -----------------------------------
 dHf(NaCl) = 90 - 0   = +90 kJ    <-- answer (3) 90 kJ

 cross-check by the full cycle:
   sublimation Na          +20
   1/2 BE(Cl2)             +90
   IE1(Na)                 +90
   dHeg(Cl)                -70
   lattice (90 - 130)      -40
   ----------------------------
   total                   +90  OK
⚡ JEE Trick On any Born–Haber chart, ΔHf needs no calculation — it is the height of the compound above the element baseline. Reach for the five-term cycle only when the compound's level is not drawn. Trap: overriding the diagram with the memorised real value of ΔHf(NaCl).
Answer: (3) ΔHf of NaCl(s) = +90 kJ (as read from this diagram).
5 (4)(Same energy-level diagram.) (4) BECl₂ + ΔHeg[Cl(g)] will be ______.Bond energy + electron gain enthalpyBorn–Haber cycle exam question with bond and electron-affinity steps🟡 Similar110kJ
Concept The diagram only ever shows ½Cl₂ → Cl, because one formula unit of NaCl needs just one chlorine atom. That vertical step is therefore ½ × BE(Cl₂), and you must double it before adding the electron-gain term. The electron-gain step is the next rung down: Cl(g) + e⁻ → Cl⁻(g), read as the drop from the Na(g)+Cl(g) level to the Na(g)+Cl⁻(g) level (the sodium atom is a spectator in both).
Key relation ½Cl₂(g) → Cl(g), ΔH = ½BE(Cl₂) ⇒ BE(Cl₂) = 2 × (step) · Cl(g) + e⁻ → Cl⁻(g), ΔH = ΔHeg(Cl) < 0
Solution
1. Bond step. Na(g) + ½Cl₂(g) = 20 kJ; Na(g) + Cl(g) = 110 kJ. Rise = 110 − 20 = 90 kJ = ½BE(Cl₂).
2. Hence BE(Cl₂) = 2 × 90 = 180 kJ.
3. Electron-gain step. Na(g) + Cl(g) = 110 kJ; Na(g) + Cl⁻(g) = 40 kJ. Drop = 40 − 110 = −70 kJ = ΔHeg(Cl), correctly exothermic.
4. Add as asked: BE(Cl₂) + ΔHeg(Cl) = 180 + (−70) = 110 kJ.
5. Consistency: with ΔHeg = −70 and Na⁺(g)+Cl⁻(g) at 130, the ionisation step is 130 − 40 = 90 kJ = IE₁(Na), and the whole cycle then reproduces ΔHf = +90 kJ found in part (3).Every reading on this chart cross-checks against every other — that is how you confirm you read the grid lines correctly.
 Na(g) + 1/2 Cl2(g)  =  20 kJ
 Na(g) + Cl(g)       = 110 kJ   ->  1/2 BE = 90  =>  BE(Cl2) = 180
 Na(g) + Cl-(g)      =  40 kJ   ->  dHeg(Cl) = 40 - 110 = -70

 BE(Cl2) + dHeg(Cl)  = 180 + (-70) = 110 kJ   <-- answer (4) 110 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Any Born–Haber chart for an MX salt draws half a bond energy. Circle the "½" in the baseline label the moment you start, and double whatever you measure. Trap: answering 90 + (−70) = 20 kJ by forgetting the factor of two.
Answer: (4) BECl₂ + ΔHeg[Cl(g)] = 180 − 70 = 110 kJ.
5 (5)(Same energy-level diagram.) (5) ΔHeg₁ of [Al⁺³(g)] will be ______.Electron gain enthalpy of a cation = −IEReading each step of a Born–Haber energy-level diagram🟡 Similar−20kJ
Concept "First electron gain enthalpy of Al³⁺" sounds exotic but describes an elementary step: Al³⁺(g) + e⁻ → Al²⁺(g). That is precisely the reverse of the third ionisation of aluminium, so ΔHeg₁(Al³⁺) = −IE₃(Al). On the diagram both levels are drawn, so you can read the answer directly without ever mentioning IE₃.
Key relation Al³⁺(g) + e⁻ → Al²⁺(g), ΔH = ΔHeg₁(Al³⁺) · ΔHeg₁(Al³⁺) = −IE₃(Al) forward and reverse of the same one-electron step. · Adding an electron to a cation is always strongly exothermic.
Solution
1. Initial level: Al³⁺(g) + 3Cl⁻(g) = 130 kJ.
2. Final level after the electron lands on Al³⁺: Al²⁺(g) + 3Cl⁻(g) = 110 kJ. The three chloride ions are unchanged spectators.
3. Subtract, final − initial: ΔHeg₁(Al³⁺) = 110 − 130 = −20 kJ.
4. Equivalently IE₃(Al) = +20 kJ on this chart's scale — the reverse step read upwards.
5. Sign check: gaining an electron reduces the positive charge from 3+ to 2+, a strongly favourable electrostatic change, so a negative ΔH is compulsory.For real aluminium IE₃ = 2745 kJ mol⁻¹, so the true ΔHeg(Al³⁺) would be −2745; the chart uses scaled numbers.
 Al3+(g) + 3Cl-(g)   = 130 kJ
 Al2+(g) + 3Cl-(g)   = 110 kJ
 ----------------------------------
 Al3+(g) + e- -> Al2+(g)
 dHeg1 = 110 - 130 = -20 kJ  ( = -IE3 of Al )   <-- answer (5) -20 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Translate every "electron gain enthalpy of a cation" into "minus the corresponding ionisation energy" and the sign takes care of itself: ΔHeg(Xn+) = −IEn(X). Trap: reading the two aluminium chloride levels (AlCl₃(s) at 90) instead of the two gaseous ion levels — the spectator species must be identical on both sides.
Answer: (5) ΔHeg₁ of Al³⁺(g) = −20 kJ.
6 (i)Ionization energies of five elements in kcal/mol are given below — Atom (I, II, III): P (300, 549, 920); Q (99, 734, 1100); R (118, 1091, 1652); S (176, 347, 1848); T (497, 947, 1500). (i) Which element is a noble gas? (a) P (b) T (c) R (d) SIdentifying a noble gas from IE dataPredicting an element from its successive ionisation energies🟡 SimilarbT
Concept A table of successive ionisation energies is a fingerprint of the valence shell. Two features carry all the information: the absolute size of IE₁ (how tightly the outermost electron is held) and the position of the first big jump (where you break into a noble-gas core). A noble gas has a complete octet and no core to break into early, so its IE₁ is the largest of any element in its period and the sequence rises smoothly rather than leaping.
Key relation noble gas ⇒ IE₁ maximum, no early jump · alkali metal ⇒ IE₁ minimum, huge IE₂/IE₁ · jump after n electrons ⇒ group valency n
Solution
1. Compare the five IE₁ values: 300 (P), 99 (Q), 118 (R), 176 (S), 497 (T). T is by far the largest.
2. Check T's pattern: 497 → 947 → 1500. The ratio IE₂/IE₁ = 1.9 — a smooth rise with no sharp jump, exactly what a filled shell gives, because every removal is from an already very stable configuration.
3. Eliminate: Q (99) and R (118) have tiny IE₁ then a 7–9× leap — alkali metals. S leaps only after the second electron — an alkaline earth. P (300) is high but far below T and has no jump in the first three — a non-metal, not a noble gas.
4. Hence T is the noble gas, option (b).Reality check: He 567, Ne 497, Ar 363 kcal mol⁻¹ — 497 is exactly neon's value.
 atom   IE1   IE2   IE3    IE2/IE1   verdict
 P      300   549    920     1.8     non-metal
 Q       99   734   1100     7.4     alkali metal
 R      118  1091   1652     9.2     alkali metal
 S      176   347   1848     2.0     alkaline earth (jump at IE3)
 T      497   947   1500     1.9     NOBLE GAS - highest IE1

                                     <-- answer (b) T
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the IE₁ column first: the largest entry is the noble gas and the smallest is the most metallic element. Only then hunt for jumps to fix the valency of the rest. Trap: choosing P because 300 "looks big" — always compare against the maximum, not against your intuition.
Answer: (b) T is the noble gas (largest IE₁ = 497 kcal/mol, no early jump).
6 (ii)(Same IE table.) (ii) Which element form stable unipositive ion? (a) P (b) Q (c) R (d) TBig IE₁→IE₂ jump ⇒ M⁺Using successive ionisation energies to fix an element's valency🟡 SimilarbQ
Concept An element forms a stable Mn+ ion when removing exactly n electrons is cheap and removing the (n+1)th is prohibitively expensive — the jump marks the boundary of the noble-gas core. For a stable unipositive ion you therefore want a very low IE₁ followed by a very large IE₂. Among two candidates showing this alkali pattern, the one with the lower IE₁ parts with its single electron most readily and gives the more stable M⁺ in practice.
Key relation stable M⁺ ⇐ IE₁ small and IE₂ ≫ IE₁ · ease of forming M⁺ ∝ 1/IE₁ the smaller the first ionisation energy, the more readily the cation forms.
Solution
1. Locate the elements whose first jump comes after one electron: Q (99 → 734, ×7.4) and R (118 → 1091, ×9.2). Both are group-1 type.
2. Of the two options offered that are alkali-like, Q has the lowest first ionisation energy in the whole table (99 kcal/mol), so its single valence electron is the most loosely held of all five elements.
3. The lower IE₁ means Q⁺ is formed most easily and is the most stable unipositive ion — option (b).R also forms R⁺, but it costs 118 kcal/mol rather than 99; on this data Q is the better answer.
4. Eliminate the rest: P (300, no jump before IE₃) is a non-metal that does not form cations; T is the noble gas from part (i) and forms no ion at all; S leaps only after two electrons and so forms S²⁺.
 atom   IE1   IE2   ratio    first jump after
 Q       99   734    7.4     1 electron   -> M+   (lowest IE1)
 R      118  1091    9.2     1 electron   -> M+
 S      176   347    2.0     2 electrons  -> M2+
 P      300   549    1.8     none early   -> non-metal
 T      497   947    1.9     none         -> noble gas

 lowest IE1 among the M+ formers = Q     <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Read a successive-IE table as a staircase: count how many steps you climb before the wall. One step = M⁺, two = M²⁺, three = M³⁺. When two elements share the same staircase, the tie-break is the raw IE₁ value. Trap: picking T because its numbers are the largest — large IE means the element resists ionisation, the opposite of what is wanted.
Answer: (b) Q — lowest IE₁ (99 kcal/mol) with a 7.4× jump to IE₂, so Q⁺ is the stable ion.
6 (iii)(Same IE table.) (iii) The element having most stable oxidation state +2 is? (a) Q (b) R (c) S (d) TJump after the second electron ⇒ M²⁺Successive ionisation energies and group identification🟡 SimilarcS
Concept Same staircase logic as part (ii), shifted by one step. An element shows a stable +2 state when IE₁ and IE₂ are both modest and comparable, but IE₃ explodes — meaning the third electron would have to come out of a noble-gas core. That is the signature of a group-2 (alkaline earth) element.
Key relation stable M²⁺ ⇐ IE₂/IE₁ small, IE₃/IE₂ very large · ns² valence shell ⇒ two easy electrons, then the core
Solution
1. Compute the two ratios for each candidate. S: IE₂/IE₁ = 347/176 = 2.0 (small), IE₃/IE₂ = 1848/347 = 5.3 (huge).
2. That is a textbook alkaline-earth profile: two valence electrons leave easily, then the wall. So S forms a stable S²⁺.
3. Eliminate: Q and R both wall off after one electron (ratios 7.4 and 9.2 at IE₂), so their stable state is +1, not +2. T is the noble gas.
4. Answer (c) S.Compare magnesium: IE₁ 176, IE₂ 347, IE₃ 1848 kcal mol⁻¹ — the numbers in this table are magnesium's.
 atom   IE1   IE2   IE3    IE2/IE1   IE3/IE2   stable state
 Q       99   734  1100      7.4       1.5        +1
 R      118  1091  1652      9.2       1.5        +1
 S      176   347  1848      2.0       5.3        +2   <-- answer (c)
 T      497   947  1500      1.9       1.6        none (noble gas)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not compare IE values across elements — compare ratios within one element. The oxidation state equals the number of ionisations that occur before the biggest ratio. Trap: choosing R because its IE₂ (1091) is large in absolute terms; that large value is precisely why R does not reach +2.
Answer: (c) S — IE₃/IE₂ = 5.3 marks the core, so +2 is its stable oxidation state.
6 (iv)(Same IE table.) (iv) Which is a non-metal (excluding noble gas)? (a) P (b) Q (c) R (d) SMetal vs non-metal from IE₁Classifying elements as metals and non-metals🟡 SimilaraP
Concept Metallic character is defined by the ease of losing electrons, so it is inversely related to ionisation energy. Metals sit at the low-IE end and show an abrupt jump once their few valence electrons are gone; non-metals have high IE throughout and no early jump, because they have many valence electrons and no interest in forming cations at all.
Key relation low IE₁ + early jump ⇒ metal · high IE₁ + no early jump ⇒ non-metal · highest IE₁ of all ⇒ noble gas
Solution
1. Exclude T — it was identified as the noble gas in part (i) and the question rules noble gases out.
2. Of the remaining four, Q (99), R (118) and S (176) all have low IE₁ with a clear early jump ⇒ they are metals (two alkali, one alkaline earth).
3. P has IE₁ = 300 kcal/mol — two to three times higher than the metals — and its first three ionisations rise smoothly (300, 549, 920, ratios 1.8 and 1.7) with no jump, so P holds several valence electrons tightly. That is a non-metal.
4. Answer (a) P.The absence of a jump within the first three removals tells you P has at least four valence electrons — a p-block non-metal.
 atom   IE1(kcal/mol)   early jump?   class
 Q        99             yes (IE2)    metal (alkali)
 R       118             yes (IE2)    metal (alkali)
 S       176             yes (IE3)    metal (alkaline earth)
 P       300             no           NON-METAL      <-- answer (a)
 T       497             no           noble gas (excluded)
⚡ JEE Trick Rank the IE₁ column and split it: the bottom of the list is metallic, the top is non-metallic, and the single highest value is the noble gas. Once the noble gas is excluded, the next-highest IE₁ is the non-metal. Trap: assuming "highest IE = non-metal" without first removing T.
Answer: (a) P — IE₁ = 300 kcal/mol with no early jump, the only non-metal once the noble gas T is excluded.
6 (v)(Same IE table.) (v) If Q reacts with fluorine and oxygen, the molecular formula of fluoride and oxide will be respectively (a) QF₃, Q₂O₃ (b) QF, Q₂O (c) QF₂, QO (d) None of theseValency ⇒ formula of fluoride and oxideAlkali metals and the compounds they form🟡 SimilarbQF, Q₂O
Concept Once the IE table has fixed an element's stable oxidation state, its formulae follow mechanically from charge balance. Q was shown in part (ii) to be an alkali metal forming Q⁺. Fluorine is invariably F⁻ and oxygen in a normal oxide is O²⁻, so the subscripts are set by the criss-cross rule.
Key relation Q⁺ + F⁻ → QF · 2Q⁺ + O²⁻ → Q₂O · criss-cross: charge on one ion becomes the subscript of the other
Solution
1. From the IE data, Q's jump comes after one electron (99 → 734), so Q is monovalent: Q⁺.
2. Fluoride: F is always −1, so Q⁺F⁻ = QF (like NaF, KF).
3. Oxide: O is −2 in a normal oxide, so two Q⁺ are needed: Q₂O (like Na₂O, K₂O).
4. Eliminate: (a) QF₃/Q₂O₃ needs Q³⁺ (a group-13 element), (c) QF₂/QO needs Q²⁺ (that is element S, not Q). Option (b) is correct, so (d) is out.Real alkali metals also give peroxides (Na₂O₂) and superoxides (KO₂), but the question asks for the normal oxide.
 Q : jump after 1 electron  =>  Q+

 Q+  +  F-        ->  QF        (1 : 1)
 2 Q+  +  O2-     ->  Q2O       (2 : 1)

                                <-- answer (b) QF , Q2O
⚡ JEE Trick Never write formulae from the element's position if a data table is given — read the valency straight off the ionisation jump, then criss-cross. It is one step and immune to mis-remembering the periodic table. Trap: option (a), which is right for aluminium and therefore feels familiar.
Answer: (b) QF and Q₂O.
6 (vi)(Same IE table.) (vi) Which of the following pair represents elements of same group? (a) Q, R (b) P, Q (c) P, S (d) Q, SSame group ⇒ same IE jump patternDetermining an element's group from successive ionisation energies🟡 SimilaraQ, R
Concept Elements of the same group have the same number of valence electrons, so their successive-IE curves have the jump in the same place. The absolute values differ (they fall down a group as the atom gets bigger), but the shape of the pattern is a group fingerprint. So to match a pair, compare where the wall is, not how tall the numbers are.
Key relation same group ⇒ same number of valence e⁻ ⇒ same jump position · down a group: IE decreases larger n, greater shielding, weaker hold.
Solution
1. Jump positions: Q jumps at IE₂ (99 → 734), R jumps at IE₂ (118 → 1091), S jumps at IE₃ (347 → 1848), P has no jump in the first three, T none at all.
2. Only Q and R share a jump position — both after one electron, so both have a single valence electron: same group (group 1).
3. Their IE₁ values 99 and 118 differ as expected for two members of one group (the heavier, larger one has the lower value, so Q lies below R).
4. Eliminate: (b) P,Q pairs a non-metal with an alkali metal; (c) P,S pairs a non-metal with an alkaline earth; (d) Q,S pairs group 1 with group 2 — one electron versus two.
5. Answer (a) Q, R.
 atom   valence e- (from jump)   group
 P      >= 4 (no jump in 3)      p-block non-metal
 Q      1                        1
 R      1                        1     <-- same as Q
 S      2                        2
 T      8 (complete)             18

 pair with identical pattern = (Q, R)   <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Convert each row of the table into a single digit — the number of electrons removed before the jump — then just match digits. Two identical digits = same group. Trap: pairing on similar magnitudes (e.g. Q 99 with R 118 and S 176 all "look small"); magnitude tells you the period, the jump tells you the group.
Answer: (a) Q and R — both jump after one electron, so both are group-1 elements.
7 (i)If P, Q, R and S are elements of 3rd period of p-block in Modern Periodic Table, among these one element is metal and rest are non-metals and their order of electronegativity is given as P < Q < R < S. (i) In which of the following linkage release of H⁺ is relatively more easier: (a) P — O — H (b) S — O — H (c) Q — O — H (d) R — O — HE—O—H: acidity vs electronegativityElectronegativity on the Pauling scale and its consequences🟡 SimilarbS — O — H
Concept Every E—O—H unit can break in two ways: the E—O bond snaps (giving E⁺ + OH⁻, basic behaviour) or the O—H bond snaps (giving H⁺, acidic behaviour). Which one goes depends entirely on the electronegativity of E. A very electronegative E pulls electron density away from oxygen, oxygen compensates by pulling harder on the hydrogen, the O—H bond becomes strongly polar and H⁺ leaves easily. A weakly electronegative (metallic) E leaves the E—O bond ionic instead, and the compound behaves as a base.
Key relation EN(E) high ⇒ E—O covalent, O—H polar ⇒ acid · EN(E) low ⇒ E—O ionic ⇒ base · given P < Q < R < S ⇒ S is the most electronegative.
Solution
1. Identify the elements loosely: the 3rd-period p-block runs Al, Si, P, S, Cl. Exactly one of the four is a metal, and since electronegativity increases left to right, the metal must be the least electronegative one — P = Al. Q, R, S are the non-metals in increasing electronegativity.
2. The question asks where H⁺ leaves most readily, i.e. which hydroxide is the strongest acid.
3. Acid strength of E—O—H tracks EN(E). Since S has the highest electronegativity of the four, S—O—H ionises most easily.Compare the real series: Al(OH)₃ (amphoteric) → Si(OH)₄ (very weak acid) → H₃PO₄ (moderate) → H₂SO₄ (strong) → HClO₄ (super acid). Acidity climbs exactly with EN.
4. Eliminate: (a) P—O—H is the metal hydroxide, the most basic of the set; (c) Q—O—H and (d) R—O—H are intermediate.
5. Answer (b).
 EN order (given):   P  <  Q  <  R  <  S
                     |                  |
                   metal            most EN non-metal

 E-O-H behaviour:   P-O-H : E-O ionic  -> base
                    S-O-H : O-H polar  -> strongest acid

 easiest H+ release = S-O-H            <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick For any oxyacid/hydroxide comparison, ask one question only: "which bond is weaker, E—O or O—H?" High EN on E ⇒ O—H breaks ⇒ acid. The same single number then also settles the acidic/basic character of the oxide, which is exactly what part (ii) needs. Trap: choosing (a) because "P" is listed first and looks like the "main" element.
Answer: (b) S — O — H, because S is the most electronegative of the four.
7 (ii)(Same passage: P, Q, R, S are 3rd-period p-block elements, one metal, EN order P < Q < R < S.) (ii) Which element is expected to form amphoteric oxide. (a) P (b) Q (c) R (d) SAmphoteric oxidesAcidic, basic and amphoteric oxides across the periodic table🟡 SimilaraP
Concept Oxide character marches steadily across a period: strongly basic on the far left (Na₂O, MgO), amphoteric at the metal/non-metal frontier (Al₂O₃), then acidic and increasingly so towards the halogens (SiO₂, P₄O₁₀, SO₃, Cl₂O₇). An amphoteric oxide belongs to an element whose electronegativity is intermediate — metallic enough to react with acids, covalent enough to react with alkalis.
Key relation basic ← metal … amphoteric (borderline) … non-metal → acidic · Al₂O₃ + 6HCl → 2AlCl₃ + 3H₂O and Al₂O₃ + 2NaOH + 3H₂O → 2Na[Al(OH)₄] reacting with both acid and base is the definition of amphoteric.
Solution
1. The passage says exactly one of the four is a metal; since EN rises P → S, the metal is the least electronegative, P.
2. In the 3rd period the only p-block metal is aluminium, so P = Al.
3. Al₂O₃ is the classic amphoteric oxide — it dissolves in HCl to give AlCl₃ and in NaOH to give the aluminate ion.
4. Eliminate: Q, R and S are non-metals, so their oxides (SiO₂, P₄O₁₀/SO₃, Cl₂O₇ type) are purely acidic and grow more so with EN.SiO₂ is only very weakly acidic, but it is still not amphoteric.
5. Answer (a) P.
 3rd period p-block :  Al    Si     P      S      Cl
 EN                 : 1.61  1.90  2.19   2.58   3.16
 oxide character    : AMPH  weak   acid   acid   strongly
                             acid                  acid

 the single metal, lowest EN = P = Al  -> Al2O3 amphoteric
                                        <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the short amphoteric list — BeO, Al₂O₃, ZnO, SnO/SnO₂, PbO/PbO₂, As₂O₃, Sb₂O₃, Ga₂O₃, Cr₂O₃ — and you can answer this class of question instantly. In any "one metal, rest non-metals" passage the metal is always the amphoteric one. Trap: picking S because it is the "most reactive-looking"; high EN gives an acidic oxide, never an amphoteric one.
Answer: (a) P (= Al), whose oxide Al₂O₃ is amphoteric.
7 (iii)(Same passage.) (iii) Chloride compound of which element is hypovalent: (a) S (b) Q (c) R (d) PElectron-deficient (hypovalent) chloridesGroup 13: oxidation states, electron deficiency and chemical behaviour🟡 SimilardP
Concept "Hypovalent" (electron-deficient) means the central atom has fewer than eight electrons around it after bonding, so it still has a vacant orbital and behaves as a Lewis acid. Group-13 halides are the standard examples: AlCl₃ leaves aluminium with only six electrons, which is why it dimerises to Al₂Cl₆ through chloride bridges and why it is the workhorse catalyst of Friedel–Crafts chemistry. Elements to the right (Si, P, S, Cl) have enough valence electrons to complete or exceed the octet, so their chlorides are normal or hypervalent, not hypovalent.
Key relation hypovalent ⇒ central atom < 8 e⁻ ⇒ Lewis acid · AlCl₃: 3 + 3 = 6 e⁻ around Al · 2AlCl₃ ⇌ Al₂Cl₆ the octet is completed by dative bridge bonds from chlorine lone pairs.
Solution
1. From parts (i)–(ii), P is the metal of the set = Al.
2. Count electrons in its chloride: Al contributes 3 valence electrons and forms 3 bonds, giving 3 + 3 = 6 electrons around Al — two short of an octet. AlCl₃ is therefore hypovalent.
3. Check the others: SiCl₄ (8 e⁻, exact octet), PCl₃ (8, with a lone pair) and PCl₅ / SCl₆-type chlorides (10 and 12 e⁻) are hypervalent, not hypovalent.
4. Answer (d) P.Consistent evidence: AlCl₃ fumes in air, dimerises in the vapour and in non-polar solvents, and is a strong Lewis acid — all hallmarks of electron deficiency.
 chloride    e- around central atom      classification
 AlCl3 (P)     3 + 3 =  6                HYPOVALENT   <-- answer (d)
 SiCl4         4 + 4 =  8                normal octet
 PCl3          5 + 3 =  8 (+ lone pair)  normal octet
 PCl5          5 + 5 = 10                hypervalent
 SF6 / SCl-type 6 + 6 = 12               hypervalent
⚡ JEE Trick Count electrons, do not recall names: group valence electrons + number of bonds. Below 8 = hypovalent (BeCl₂, BCl₃, AlCl₃), exactly 8 = normal, above 8 = hypervalent (PCl₅, SF₆). Trap: reading "hypovalent" as "hypervalent" and choosing a period-3 chloride like PCl₅ that expands its octet.
Answer: (d) P (= Al) — AlCl₃ has only 6 electrons around Al, so it is hypovalent and dimerises to Al₂Cl₆.
8The ground state electronic configurations of the elements, U, V, W, X and Y (these symbols do not have any chemical significance) are as follow: U 1s²2s²2p³; V 1s²2s²2p⁶3s¹; W 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p²; X 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁶3d⁵4s²; Y 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁶3d¹⁰4s²4p⁶. Determine which sequence of elements satisfy the following statements: (i) Element forms a carbonate which is not decomposed by heating (ii) Element is most likely to form coloured ionic compounds (iii) Element has largest atomic radius (iv) Element forms only acidic oxide. (a) V W Y U (b) V X Y W (c) V W Y X (d) V X W UConfigurations → element → propertyReading s-, p-, d- and f-block identity from an electron configuration🟡 SimilarbV X Y W
Concept Convert each configuration to an atomic number, then to a real element, and every clue becomes a one-line fact. Four distinct pieces of chemistry are being tested: thermal stability of carbonates (only group-1 carbonates, except Li₂CO₃, survive heating), colour in ionic compounds (needs partly-filled d orbitals for d–d transitions), atomic radius (noble gases are quoted on the van der Waals scale and therefore come out largest in a period), and oxide character ("only acidic" excludes both metals and amphoteric borderline elements).
Key relation M₂CO₃ (M = Na, K …) stable to heat; MCO₃ (M²⁺) → MO + CO₂ a small, highly polarising cation destabilises the carbonate ion; Na⁺ is large and low-charged. · colour ⇐ partly filled d · r(van der Waals, noble gas) > r(metallic/covalent) of its period
Solution
1. Identify. U = 7 e⁻ → N. V = 11 → Na. W = 14 → Si. X = 25 → Mn. Y = 36 → Kr.
2. (i) Carbonate not decomposed by heating. Na₂CO₃ melts (1123 K) without losing CO₂; all group-2 and transition carbonates decompose. ⇒ V.
3. (ii) Coloured ionic compounds. Mn²⁺/Mn³⁺ have partly filled 3d shells (3d⁵, 3d⁴), giving the familiar pink, purple and green manganese salts. Na⁺ (2p⁶), Si⁴⁺, N and Kr have no partly-filled d level. ⇒ X.
4. (iii) Largest atomic radius. Among N, Na, Si, Mn, Kr the candidates are Na (metallic radius 186 pm) and Kr (van der Waals radius 202 pm). Since noble-gas radii are quoted as van der Waals radii, Kr is the largest. ⇒ Y.This is the standard convention in NCERT: the noble gas appears as the biggest atom of its period precisely because a different kind of radius is being reported.
5. (iv) Forms only acidic oxide. Na₂O is basic, Mn gives basic MnO and acidic Mn₂O₇ (so not "only"), Kr is inert; N gives acidic oxides but is already the leftover. SiO₂ is exclusively acidic — it reacts with NaOH but never with acids (except HF, which is a special fluoride reaction, not a base-like one). ⇒ W.
6. Sequence V, X, Y, W = option (b).
 symbol  config                       Z    element
 U       1s2 2s2 2p3                   7   N
 V       1s2 2s2 2p6 3s1              11   Na
 W       [Ne] 3s2 3p2                 14   Si
 X       [Ar] 3d5 4s2                 25   Mn
 Y       [Ar] 3d10 4s2 4p6            36   Kr

 (i)   carbonate stable to heat      Na2CO3        -> V
 (ii)  coloured ionic compounds      Mn2+ 3d5      -> X
 (iii) largest atomic radius         Kr 202 pm     -> Y
 (iv)  only acidic oxide             SiO2          -> W
                                     V X Y W   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Count the electrons in each configuration first and write the five element symbols in the margin — after that the question is four independent one-word recalls. Trap: statement (iii). Almost everyone answers Na, because Na is the biggest metal; the examiner is testing whether you know that noble-gas radii are van der Waals radii and therefore the largest in the period.
Answer: (b) V X Y W — i.e. Na, Mn, Kr, Si.
9Consider the following ionization reactions with the stated I.E. (kJ mol⁻¹): A(g) → A⁺(g) + e⁻, A₁; B(g) → B⁺(g) + e⁻, B₁; B⁺(g) → B²⁺(g) + e⁻, B₂; C(g) → C⁺(g) + e⁻, C₁; C⁺(g) → C²⁺(g) + e⁻, C₂; C²⁺(g) → C³⁺(g) + e⁻, C₃. If monovalent positive ion of A, divalent positive ion of B and trivalent positive ion of C have zero electron. Then incorrect order of corresponding I.E. is: (a) C₃ > B₂ > A₁ (b) B₁ > A₁ > C₁ (c) C₃ > C₂ > B₂ (d) B₂ > C₃ > A₁Hydrogen-like ions, IE ∝ Z²/n²Successive ionisation energies and how they scale🟡 SimilardB₂ > C₃ > A₁ is incorrect
Concept "Zero electron" is the key phrase — it means the ion is a bare nucleus. A⁺ bare ⇒ A had 1 electron ⇒ A = H. B²⁺ bare ⇒ B = He. C³⁺ bare ⇒ C = Li. Every ionisation asked about that leaves a bare nucleus, or starts from a one-electron species, is hydrogen-like, so Bohr's formula applies exactly and you can compute the numbers rather than guess them.
Key relation IE(one-electron species) = 13.6 Z²/n² eV exact for H, He⁺, Li²⁺ — any species with a single electron. · successive IE always increases · A = H, B = He, C = Li.
Solution
1. Identify. A⁺ has 0 e⁻ ⇒ A has 1 e⁻ ⇒ A = H. B²⁺ has 0 e⁻ ⇒ B has 2 ⇒ B = He. C³⁺ has 0 e⁻ ⇒ C has 3 ⇒ C = Li.
2. Compute the hydrogen-like ones. A₁ = 13.6 eV (H). B₂ = IE of He⁺ = 13.6 × 2² = 54.4 eV. C₃ = IE of Li²⁺ = 13.6 × 3² = 122.4 eV.
3. Look up the many-electron ones. B₁ = IE₁(He) = 24.6 eV; C₁ = IE₁(Li) = 5.4 eV; C₂ = IE₂(Li) = 75.6 eV.
4. Test each option. (a) C₃ > B₂ > A₁ → 122.4 > 54.4 > 13.6 ✓ correct. (b) B₁ > A₁ > C₁ → 24.6 > 13.6 > 5.4 ✓ correct. (c) C₃ > C₂ > B₂ → 122.4 > 75.6 > 54.4 ✓ correct.
5. (d) B₂ > C₃ > A₁ → 54.4 > 122.4 is false. C₃ (Li²⁺, Z = 3) must exceed B₂ (He⁺, Z = 2) because IE scales as Z². So (d) is the incorrect order.You do not even need numbers: both are 1s¹ species, so the larger Z always wins.
 A+ , B2+ , C3+ all have 0 electrons  =>  A = H , B = He , C = Li

 A1 = IE1(H)      = 13.6 eV      (Z=1, 1 e-)
 B1 = IE1(He)     = 24.6 eV
 B2 = IE(He+)     = 13.6 x 2^2   =  54.4 eV
 C1 = IE1(Li)     =  5.4 eV
 C2 = IE2(Li)     = 75.6 eV
 C3 = IE(Li2+)    = 13.6 x 3^2   = 122.4 eV

 (a) 122.4 > 54.4 > 13.6   TRUE
 (b)  24.6 > 13.6 >  5.4   TRUE
 (c) 122.4 > 75.6 > 54.4   TRUE
 (d)  54.4 > 122.4 ?       FALSE          <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick The phrase "ion has zero electrons" always means a bare nucleus — count backwards to get Z instantly. Then any ionisation of a species left with one electron is exactly 13.6 Z² eV, so H, He⁺ and Li²⁺ give the clean ratio 1 : 4 : 9. Trap: option (d) looks plausible because He is "more noble" than Li, but the last electron of Li²⁺ feels a nuclear charge of 3 with no shielding at all.
Answer: (d) B₂ > C₃ > A₁ is the incorrect order (C₃ = 122.4 eV > B₂ = 54.4 eV).
10The incorrect statement is: (a) The second ionization energy of Se is greater than that of second ionization energy of As (b) The first ionization energy of C²⁺ ion is greater than that of first ionization energy of N²⁺ ion (c) The third ionization energy of F is greater than that of third ionization energy of O (d) Halogens have highest I.E. in respective periodSuccessive IE anomalies; noble gas maximumPeriodic properties: ionisation energy trends and their exceptions🟡 Similardhalogens do not have the highest I.E.
Concept Three of these statements are "IE anomaly" statements that must be settled by writing the configuration of the species actually being ionised — never of the neutral parent. The recurring rule is that removing an electron from a half-filled or fully-filled shell costs extra because of the loss of exchange stability. The fourth statement is a plain factual claim about the periodic table, and it is the one that is wrong: within a period the ionisation energy peaks at the noble gas, not at the halogen.
Key relation "first IE of Xn+" = IEn+1 of X the labels shift by one — read the charge on the ion. · half-filled (p³) and filled (p⁶, s²) shells resist ionisation · IE maximum in a period = noble gas
Solution
1. (a) TRUE. IE₂ removes an electron from the mono-cation. Se⁺ = [Ar]3d¹⁰4s²4p³ (half-filled 4p³), As⁺ = [Ar]3d¹⁰4s²4p². Breaking a half-filled set costs more, so IE₂(Se) > IE₂(As): 2045 vs 1798 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓.
2. (b) TRUE. "First IE of C²⁺" means C²⁺ → C³⁺, i.e. IE₃ of carbon; likewise for N. C²⁺ = 1s²2s² (filled 2s), N²⁺ = 1s²2s²2p¹ (a lone, higher-energy 2p electron). Stripping the filled 2s is harder: IE₃(C) = 4620 > IE₃(N) = 4578 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓.
3. (c) TRUE. IE₃ removes an electron from the dication. F²⁺ = 1s²2s²2p³ (half-filled), O²⁺ = 1s²2s²2p². So IE₃(F) = 6050 > IE₃(O) = 5300 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓.
4. (d) FALSE ⇒ this is the answer. The highest ionisation energy in every period belongs to the noble gas, whose octet (or duplet for He) is complete: period 2 gives Ne 2081 > F 1681; period 3 gives Ar 1521 > Cl 1251. Halogens are second-highest, not highest.He 2372 kJ mol⁻¹ is the highest of any element — again a noble gas, not a halogen.
5. Answer (d).
 (a) IE2 : Se+ 4p3 (half) 2045  >  As+ 4p2   1798   TRUE
 (b) IE3 : C2+ 2s2 (full) 4620  >  N2+ 2p1   4578   TRUE
 (c) IE3 : F2+ 2p3 (half) 6050  >  O2+ 2p2   5300   TRUE
 (d) period maximum:  Ne 2081 > F 1681
                      Ar 1521 > Cl 1251             FALSE
                                             <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Translate the wording before you think: "n-th IE of X" ionises X(n−1)+, and "first IE of X2+" is the third IE of X. Write the configuration of that charged species and look for p³ or s²/p⁶ — if the species being ionised has one, its IE is anomalously high. Trap: statements (a)–(c) all sound like violations of the left-to-right trend, so students mark one of them and miss the plainly false statement (d).
Answer: (d) — the highest I.E. in a period belongs to the noble gas, not the halogen.

Level-10

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1 (i)Passage (i)–(iii). J. C. Slater proposed an empirical constant that represents the cumulative extent to which the other electrons of an atom shield (screen) any particular electron from the nuclear charge. Slater's screening constant σ is used as Z* = Z − σ; Z is the atomic number, and Z* is the effective nuclear charge, always smaller than Z because the electron in question is screened from Z by an amount σ. Where screening is small Z* is large; an electron that is well shielded experiences a small Z*. The value of σ for any one electron in a given electron configuration is calculated from Slater's empirical rules, the value of σ being the cumulative total contributed by the various other electrons of the atom. — The effective nuclear charge at the periphery of chromium atom [Z = 24]: (a) 4.25 (b) 2.60 (c) 3.60 (d) 1.21Slater's rules, Z* of Cr(4s)Step-by-step trick for Slater's screening constant and Z*🟡 Similarb ⚠2.60 (see note)
Concept "Periphery" means the outermost electron — for chromium that is the lone 4s electron, not a 3d electron. Slater's rules are a bookkeeping device: you sort the configuration into groups (1s)(2s,2p)(3s,3p)(3d)(4s,4p)(4d)…, then charge each other electron a shielding fee that depends on how far inside it sits. The whole point of the exercise is to show that an outer s electron of a d-block atom feels only a tiny net pull (≈ 3 units out of 24), which is exactly why 4s electrons are lost first in ionisation.
Formula Z* = Z − σ. For an s or p electron in shell n: other electrons in the same group contribute 0.35 each (1s uses 0.30), electrons in shell (n−1) contribute 0.85 each, and everything in shell (n−2) or deeper contributes 1.00 each. Nothing to the right of the electron (higher groups) shields it at all — outer electrons cannot screen inner ones. For a d or f electron the rule changes: every electron to its left counts a full 1.00, and same-group partners count 0.35.
Solution
1. Write chromium correctly: Cr = 1s² 2s²2p⁶ 3s²3p⁶ 3d⁵ 4s¹ — the half-filled exception, not 3d⁴4s².Cr and Cu are the two first-row exceptions; using 3d⁴4s² here changes the count of same-group electrons and corrupts the whole calculation.
2. Group the electrons for the target 4s electron: (1s)² | (2s,2p)⁸ | (3s,3p)⁸ | (3d)⁵ | (4s)¹.
3. Same group (4s): there are no other 4s or 4p electrons ⇒ contribution 0. An electron never shields itself — this is the single most common slip in this question.
4. Shell n−1 = 3 (that is 3s, 3p and 3d): 8 + 5 = 13 electrons × 0.85 = 11.05.
5. Shells n−2 and deeper (1s, 2s, 2p): 2 + 8 = 10 electrons × 1.00 = 10.00.
6. σ = 11.05 + 10.00 = 21.05 ⇒ Z* = 24 − 21.05 = 2.95. Of the four printed choices, 2.60 is the neighbouring value and is the one the key marks.
 target = 4s electron of Cr = [Ar]3d5 4s1

 group            n    count   factor   contribution
 (4s)  same        4     0       0.35      0.00   <-- self never counts
 (3s,3p) + (3d)    3    13       0.85     11.05
 (2s,2p)           2     8       1.00      8.00
 (1s)              1     2       1.00      2.00
                                 sigma  = 21.05
 Z* = 24 - 21.05 = 2.95        printed key = 2.60   <-- answer (b) as keyed
 (2.60 = 24 - 21.40, i.e. 21.05 + 0.35 : the 4s screening itself)
⚠ Note My derivation gives Z* = 2.95, not 2.60. The printed 2.60 can only be reached by adding an extra 0.35 to σ, i.e. by letting the single 4s electron screen itself — which Slater's rules forbid. The key letter (b) is displayed above as printed, but flagged.
⚡ JEE Trick For any 4s electron of a 3d-series atom, σ = 0.85 × (number of n = 3 electrons) + 1.00 × 10 + 0.35 × (other 4s electrons). Since the n = 3 shell fills from 8 to 18, Z* of the 4s electron creeps up only from about 2.2 (K) to 3.3 (Zn) across the whole series — that near-constant Z* is the real reason 3d-metals have such similar sizes and ionisation energies. Trap: the distractor 1.21 is what you get if you (wrongly) treat 3d as a d electron target, and 3.60/4.25 come from forgetting that 3d shields 4s at 0.85.
Answer: (b) 2.60 as keyed — strict Slater arithmetic gives 2.95.
1 (ii)Which of the following statement is correct? (a) A 4s-orbital is filled earlier than a 3d-orbital because Z* for 3d > Z* for 4s (b) A 4s-orbital is filled earlier than a 3d-orbital because Z* for 4s > Z* for 3d (c) The effective nuclear charge for 3d and 4s-orbital are same, but energy of 3d-orbital becomes higher (d) The effective nuclear charge for 3d and 4s-orbitals are same, but energy of 4s-orbital becomes higherWhy 4s fills before 3dSlater's rules applied to 4s versus 3d — penetration and orbital energy🟡 Similarb4s fills first because Z*(4s) > Z*(3d)
Concept An orbital is filled first because it is lower in energy, and orbital energy is set by how strongly the nucleus grips that electron, i.e. by Z*. The 4s orbital has a small but non-zero probability density right at the nucleus (it penetrates the argon core), whereas 3d is pushed outwards by its large centrifugal ℓ(ℓ+1) barrier and is almost perfectly screened by the 18 core electrons. Better penetration ⇒ less screening ⇒ larger Z* ⇒ lower energy ⇒ filled first. That is precisely the chain option (b) states.
Key relation penetration order within a shell: s > p > d > f · more penetration ⇒ smaller σ ⇒ larger Z* ⇒ lower E · (n + ℓ) rule: 4s (4+0 = 4) < 3d (3+2 = 5) the (n+ℓ) rule is just the empirical shorthand for the Z* argument, so the two must give the same verdict.
Solution
1. Take potassium, Z = 19, and compute Z* for a hypothetical 4s electron: σ = 8(0.85) + 10(1.00) = 16.8 ⇒ Z*(4s) = 19 − 16.8 = 2.20.
2. Now compute Z* for a hypothetical 3d electron of the same atom. For a d electron every one of the 18 electrons to its left counts a full 1.00: σ = 18(1.00) = 18 ⇒ Z*(3d) = 19 − 18 = 1.00.
3. So Z*(4s) = 2.20 > Z*(3d) = 1.00 — the 4s electron is held more than twice as tightly, sits lower in energy, and is therefore filled first. (b) is correct.
4. (a) reverses the inequality and is therefore wrong even though its conclusion (4s first) is right — in a "correct statement" question the reason must also be right.
5. (c) and (d) both assert Z*(3d) = Z*(4s). Step 1–2 shows they differ by more than one full unit of charge, so both fail at the first clause; the energy clause is never reached.
 K (Z = 19)          sigma                       Z*
 4s electron   8(0.85) + 10(1.00) = 16.80      2.20   <-- lower energy, fills first
 3d electron   18(1.00)           = 18.00      1.00
 => Z*(4s) > Z*(3d)                                    <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the two-line contrast: for an s/p target the (n−1) shell shields only 0.85; for a d target everything inside shields 1.00. That single asymmetry generates every "why 4s before 3d" answer, and also explains the reverse fact that once the 3d orbitals are occupied the 4s electrons are removed first on ionisation (by then 3d has dropped below 4s). Trap: options (c)/(d) sound sophisticated but violate Slater at the very first step.
Answer: (b) A 4s-orbital is filled earlier than a 3d-orbital because Z* for 4s > Z* for 3d.
1 (iii)According to Slater's rule, order of effective nuclear charge (Z*) for last electron in case of Li, Na and K. (a) Li > Na > K (b) K > Na > Li (c) Na > Li > K (d) K = Na > LiZ* of alkali-metal valence electronThree worked Slater's-rule examples on outer-shell electrons🟡 SimilardK = Na > Li (2.20 = 2.20 > 1.30)
Concept Everyone "knows" that Z* increases across a period and stays roughly constant down a group. Slater's rules make the second half of that statement exactly true for Na and K, and reveal that lithium is the odd one out. The reason is arithmetic: for Na and K the valence electron sees eight electrons at 0.85 plus a fully-1.00 deeper core, and the extra protons are cancelled unit-for-unit; for Li the only shield is the 1s pair at 0.85, which is a weaker screen than a full octet, so a smaller Z* survives.
Key relation Z*(valence ns¹) = Z − [0.85 × (electrons in shell n−1) + 1.00 × (electrons in shells ≤ n−2)] no same-group term, because an alkali metal has only one valence electron.
Solution
1. Li (Z = 3), 1s²2s¹. Target 2s. Shell n−1 = 1 holds 2 electrons ⇒ σ = 2(0.85) = 1.70. Z* = 3 − 1.70 = 1.30.
2. Na (Z = 11), 1s²2s²2p⁶3s¹. Target 3s. Shell 2 holds 8 ⇒ 8(0.85) = 6.80; shell 1 holds 2 ⇒ 2(1.00) = 2.00. σ = 8.80. Z* = 11 − 8.80 = 2.20.
3. K (Z = 19), [Ne]3s²3p⁶4s¹. Target 4s. Shell 3 holds 8 ⇒ 8(0.85) = 6.80; shells 1–2 hold 10 ⇒ 10(1.00) = 10.00. σ = 16.80. Z* = 19 − 16.80 = 2.20.
4. Na and K come out identical (2.20) and both exceed Li (1.30) ⇒ K = Na > Li, option (d).Going Na → K adds 8 protons and 8 electrons in the (n−1) shell; 8 × 0.85 = 6.80 shielding versus 8 units of charge would give +1.20, but the 8 electrons that were in shell n−1 for Na move to shell n−2 for K and their fee rises from 0.85 to 1.00, costing exactly 8 × 0.15 = 1.20. The two cancel.
5. Eliminate: (a) inverts the group trend, (b) claims K strictly greater than Na (the cancellation above forbids it), (c) puts K last, which is impossible since Li has the weakest core.
 atom  Z    target   sigma                              Z*
 Li     3    2s      2(0.85)                  = 1.70   1.30
 Na    11    3s      8(0.85) + 2(1.00)        = 8.80   2.20
 K     19    4s      8(0.85) + 10(1.00)       =16.80   2.20
 => K = Na > Li                                         <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the three alkali numbers Li 1.30, Na 2.20, K 2.20 (and Rb, Cs also 2.20) — after sodium the Slater Z* of a group-1 valence electron is frozen. That single fact instantly kills any option claiming a strict increase or decrease down group 1, and it also explains why ionisation energy falls down the group even though Z* does not: the electron simply sits in a larger, higher-n orbital. Trap: the exam wants Slater's rule answer (equal), not the real quantum-mechanical Z* (which does rise slightly).
Answer: (d) K = Na > Li.
2 (i)Passage (i)–(iii). The value of four quantum numbers for the last electron of atom of element 'X' are n = 7, ℓ = 1, m = +1, s = +1/2 or −1/2 and the value of spin magnetic momentum for element 'X' is zero. Element 'X' has two isotopes (I) ZAX and (II) ZBX. (Given: "B − A = B − 2Z = 18", where A and B are atomic masses and Z is atomic number.) — The incorrect statement regarding element 'X' is: (a) Element 'X' belongs to 18th group (b) Number of unpaired electrons in element 'X' is zero (c) Atomic number of element 'X' is 118 (d) 'X' is representative elementIdentifying element from quantum numbersThis exact passage worked out — last electron n=7, ℓ=1, zero spin moment🟢 Exactd'X' is representative element (it is a noble gas, not representative)
Concept Three pieces of data are given and each one nails down a different fact. n = 7, ℓ = 1 says the last electron enters a 7p orbital, so 'X' is in period 7 of the p-block. Spin magnetic moment = 0 says the number of unpaired electrons is zero, so the 7p subshell is not partly filled — it is completely filled. And m = +1 is the last magnetic quantum number available to a p subshell (m = −1, 0, +1), so the electron that just entered is the sixth and final 7p electron. Put together: the outer configuration is 7s² 7p⁶, which is a noble gas. Everything else in the passage follows from that single identification.
Key relation μs = √[n(n+2)] BM, so μs = 0 ⟺ nunpaired = 0. A half-filled 7p³ would give μ = √15 ≈ 3.87 BM, so "zero" rules out every partly-filled option and forces p⁶. Filling order for the last period: 7s → 5f → 6d → 7p · Rn(86) + 7s²(88) + 5f¹⁴(102) + 6d¹⁰(112) + 7p⁶(118)
Solution
1. From n = 7, ℓ = 1 the last electron is a 7p electron. From μs = 0 there are no unpaired electrons, so 7p must be full: 7p⁶.This is also why the passage says s = +1/2 or −1/2 — in a filled orbital both spins are present, so the sign is not determined.
2. Count up to the end of 7p using the Aufbau sequence: Rn = 86 → 7s² takes it to 88 → 5f¹⁴ to 102 → 6d¹⁰ to 112 → 7p⁶ to 118. So Z = 118 (oganesson). Statement (c) is true.
3. An outer configuration ns²np⁶ places the element in group 18. Statement (a) is true.
4. All 7p electrons are paired ⇒ zero unpaired electrons. Statement (b) is true — it is simply a restatement of the given μs = 0.
5. "Representative elements" (also called normal or main-group elements) are the s-block and p-block elements whose outermost shell is incompletely filled — groups 1, 2 and 13–17. A noble gas has a completely filled valence shell, so it is excluded. Statement (d) is therefore false, and the question asks for the incorrect one ⇒ (d).The trap is that 'X' is in the p-block, and many students equate "p-block ⇒ representative". Group 18 is the one p-block column that is not representative.
 last e-  : n = 7, l = 1, m = +1   -->  7p, sixth (last) p electron
 mu_s = 0 : zero unpaired          -->  7p(6), not 7p(1..5)

 Rn 86  +7s2 = 88  +5f14 = 102  +6d10 = 112  +7p6 = 118

 statement                                verdict
 (a) group 18                    ns2 np6   TRUE
 (b) unpaired electrons = 0      mu = 0    TRUE
 (c) Z = 118                     count     TRUE
 (d) representative element      noble gas FALSE  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Read "spin magnetic momentum = 0" as "the subshell is closed" and you have solved half the passage before touching the Aufbau count. Then remember the four end-of-period atomic numbers 2, 10, 18, 36, 54, 86, 118 — every group-18 question reduces to spotting one of these. Trap: options (a), (b) and (c) are all true, and a hurried reader who stops at the first true statement gets nothing; the question deliberately hides its one false claim, the "representative element" label, in the last position.
Answer: (d) 'X' is a representative element — false; oganesson (Z = 118) is a noble gas, group 18.
2 (ii)The value of A and B respectively are: (a) 118 and 136 (b) 218 and 236 (c) 236 and 254 (d) 226 and 244Mass numbers of two isotopesSuperheavy elements and element 118 — how Z fixes the isotope masses🟡 SimilarcA = 236, B = 254
Concept This part is pure arithmetic once part (i) has delivered Z = 118. The given string "B − A = B − 2Z = 18" is really two separate equations chained through a common value of 18, and the classic mistake is to read it as one. Isotopes of the same element share Z and differ only in mass number, so A and B are two different mass numbers of the same nucleus family; the difference B − A is simply the difference in neutron count.
Key relation B − A = 18 … (1) and B − 2Z = 18 … (2), with Z = 118 from part (i). Equation (2) is the more useful one because it contains only one unknown; solve it first, then feed B into (1). Neutron count N = mass number − Z.
Solution
1. Use equation (2): B = 2Z + 18 = 2(118) + 18 = 236 + 18 = 254.
2. Use equation (1): A = B − 18 = 254 − 18 = 236.Notice A = 2Z exactly, i.e. isotope (I) has equal numbers of protons and neutrons (118 each) — a neat internal check that the two equations are consistent.
3. So (A, B) = (236, 254) ⇒ option (c).
4. Eliminate (a): 118 is Z itself, not a mass number — this is the option for anyone who copies Z into the answer. Eliminate (b) 218 and 236: this pair does satisfy B − A = 18 but fails B − 2Z = 236 − 236 = 0 ≠ 18. Eliminate (d) 226 and 244: again B − A = 18, but B − 2Z = 244 − 236 = 8 ≠ 18.Three of the four options satisfy the first equation. Only equation (2) separates them, which is exactly why the paper-setter chained the two conditions.
 Z = 118   (from part (i))

 (2)  B - 2Z = 18   ->  B = 236 + 18 = 254
 (1)  B - A  = 18   ->  A = 254 - 18 = 236

 isotope (I)  236-X : p = 118, n = 118
 isotope (II) 254-X : p = 118, n = 136      (n difference = 18)

 option  A     B     B-A    B-2Z
 (a)     118   136    18    -100     x
 (b)     218   236    18       0     x
 (c)     236   254    18      18     <-- answer (c)
 (d)     226   244    18       8     x
⚡ JEE Trick When a "Given" line contains two equals signs, split it into two equations immediately and solve the one with a single unknown first — here B − 2Z = 18 hands you B in one line. Trap: every distractor except (a) already satisfies B − A = 18, so testing only that condition leaves you guessing among three; testing B − 2Z kills them instantly.
Answer: (c) A = 236 and B = 254.
2 (iii)The possible value of all four quantum numbers for 90th electron of atom of element 'X' is: (a) n → 6; ℓ → 2; m → 0; s → −1/2 (b) n → 5; ℓ → 2; m → −1; s → +1/2 (c) n → 6; ℓ → 0; m → 0; s → +1/2 (d) n → 5; ℓ → 3; m → −2; s → −1/2Quantum numbers of the 90th electronFinding the (n, ℓ, m, s) set of a specified electron by Aufbau counting🟡 Similardn = 5, ℓ = 3, m = −2, s = −1/2 (a 5f electron)
Concept "The 90th electron" means: fill the atom one electron at a time in Aufbau order and stop at number 90. The only skill being tested is knowing where each subshell starts and ends on the running total. Once you know which subshell electron 90 lands in, n and ℓ are fixed instantly; m and s then follow from Hund's rule, and in a four-option MCQ the (n, ℓ) pair alone is almost always enough to decide.
Key relation Aufbau / (n+ℓ) order with running totals: …6p⁶ → 86 · 7s² → 88 · 5f¹⁴ → 102 · 6d¹⁰ → 112 · 7p⁶ → 118. Electron 90 falls in the window 89–102, which is the 5f block. Subshell labels: ℓ = 0, 1, 2, 3 ⇒ s, p, d, f; for f, m = −3, −2, −1, 0, +1, +2, +3.
Solution
1. Build the running total: 1s²=2, 2s²=4, 2p⁶=10, 3s²=12, 3p⁶=18, 4s²=20, 3d¹⁰=30, 4p⁶=36, 5s²=38, 4d¹⁰=48, 5p⁶=54, 6s²=56, 4f¹⁴=70, 5d¹⁰=80, 6p⁶=86, 7s²=88, then 5f begins.
2. The 5f subshell therefore holds electrons 89 to 102. Electron 90 is the second 5f electron ⇒ n = 5, ℓ = 3. Only option (d) carries this pair, so (d) is the answer.
3. Fill 5f by Hund's rule: electron 89 goes to m = −3, electron 90 to m = −2, each singly occupied and with parallel spin. Option (d) quotes m = −2 ✓; the spin it quotes is −1/2, which is legitimate because the sign assigned to the first-filled set is pure convention — the question asks for a "possible" set, and both spin labels describe the same physical state provided all seven are parallel.Textbooks differ: some start Hund filling at s = +1/2, others at −1/2. Since m = −2 is fixed by Hund and (n, ℓ) is fixed by counting, the spin sign cannot change the answer.
4. Eliminate (a) n = 6, ℓ = 2 ⇒ 6d, which holds electrons 103–112, far past 90. Eliminate (b) n = 5, ℓ = 2 ⇒ 5d, electrons 71–80. Eliminate (c) n = 6, ℓ = 0 ⇒ 6s, electrons 55–56.
 running total after each subshell
 ... 6p6 -> 86
     7s2 -> 88
     5f  -> electrons 89 ... 102      <-- 90 lives here
     6d  -> electrons 103 ... 112
     7p  -> electrons 113 ... 118

 e- 89 : 5f, m = -3        e- 90 : 5f, m = -2
 => n = 5, l = 3, m = -2, s = +/-1/2   <-- answer (d)

 option  subshell   electrons it covers   contains 90?
 (a)     6d         103-112               no
 (b)     5d          71-80                no
 (c)     6s          55-56                no
 (d)     5f          89-102               YES
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the six "milestone" totals 2, 10, 18, 36, 54, 86 plus the three period-7 windows 7s 87–88, 5f 89–102, 6d 103–112, 7p 113–118. Any "which quantum numbers for the kth electron" question then takes about five seconds. Trap: the distractors are built from 5d and 6d, the two subshells adjacent to 5f in the (n+ℓ) list — students who remember "f comes somewhere near d" but not the exact window fall straight into (a) or (b).
Answer: (d) n = 5, ℓ = 3, m = −2, s = −1/2 — the second 5f electron.
3Considering the following representation based on the long form of the periodic table — a cross with P above, S to the left, X at the centre, Q to the right and R below. Here P, Q, R and S are the up, right, down and left elements with respect to the central element 'X' respectively. According to the above representation the correct match is/are: (X, P, Q, R, S) = (a) Ge, Si, As, Sn, Ga (b) Te, Se, I, Po, Sb (c) Sb, As, Te, Bi, Sn (d) In, Ga, Sn, Tl, Cd [printed as "Ti"; thallium Tl is meant]Neighbours in the long form of the periodic tableGroup-wise mnemonics for groups 1, 2, 13, 14, 15 — reading neighbours off the table🟡 Similara, b, c, dall four sets are correct
Concept A cross diagram on the periodic table encodes two rules at once. Moving up or down keeps you in the same group and changes the period by one; moving left or right keeps you in the same period and changes the group by one. So for a central element X in group g, period p: P = (g, p−1), R = (g, p+1), S = (g−1, p), Q = (g+1, p). The whole question is a test of whether you can place five p-block elements on the grid without a printed table — which is why the answer turns out to be "all of them".
Key relation P: same group, period − 1 · R: same group, period + 1 · S: same period, group − 1 · Q: same period, group + 1. Beware: in periods 4 and 5 the "left" neighbour of a group-13 element is a group-12 d-block metal (Zn, Cd, Hg), not a group-2 metal — the d-block sits between group 2 and group 13.
Solution
1. (a) X = Ge — group 14, period 4. Up = group 14, period 3 = Si ✓ · right = group 15, period 4 = As ✓ · down = group 14, period 5 = Sn ✓ · left = group 13, period 4 = Ga ✓. Correct.
2. (b) X = Te — group 16, period 5. Up = Se ✓ · right = group 17, period 5 = I ✓ · down = group 16, period 6 = Po ✓ · left = group 15, period 5 = Sb ✓. Correct.
3. (c) X = Sb — group 15, period 5. Up = As ✓ · right = group 16, period 5 = Te ✓ · down = group 15, period 6 = Bi ✓ · left = group 14, period 5 = Sn ✓. Correct.
4. (d) X = In — group 13, period 5. Up = group 13, period 4 = Ga ✓ · right = group 14, period 5 = Sn ✓ · down = group 13, period 6 = Tl ✓ · left = group 12, period 5 = Cd ✓. Correct.The book prints "Ti" for the down element. Titanium (Ti) is a period-4 group-4 metal and could not possibly sit below indium; the intended symbol is thallium, Tl. Read it as Tl and (d) is self-consistent.
5. Every one of the four rows survives all four tests, so the answer is (a), (b), (c) and (d) — a multiple-correct question where nothing is eliminated.
 rule : P = up (same group, period-1)   R = down (same group, period+1)
        S = left (same period, group-1) Q = right (same period, group+1)

 opt  X    grp/per   P(up)  Q(right)  R(down)  S(left)   verdict
 (a)  Ge   14 / 4     Si      As        Sn       Ga      all OK
 (b)  Te   16 / 5     Se      I         Po       Sb      all OK
 (c)  Sb   15 / 5     As      Te        Bi       Sn      all OK
 (d)  In   13 / 5     Ga      Sn        Tl       Cd      all OK  (Tl, not Ti)

 => a, b, c, d                                    <-- answer (a,b,c,d)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not try to redraw the periodic table. Convert each element to a (group, period) pair and check the four arithmetic conditions — four subtractions per option, no memory of neighbours needed. Memorising the three p-block columns 13–16 for periods 3–6 (Al Ga In Tl · Si Ge Sn Pb · P As Sb Bi · S Se Te Po) covers virtually every question of this type. Trap: the "left of group 13" step, where students reflexively write Ca/Sr instead of Zn/Cd because they forget the ten d-block columns wedged in between.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d) — all four matches are correct.
4Consider the following ionization steps: M(g) ⟶ M⁺(g) + e⁻ ; ΔH = + 100 eV and M(g) ⟶ M²⁺(g) + 2e⁻ ; ΔH = + 250 eV. Select correct statement(s): (a) I.E.1 of M(g) is 100 eV (b) I.E.1 of M⁺(g) is 150 eV (c) I.E.2 of M(g) is 250 eV (d) I.E.2 of M(g) is 150 eVSuccessive ionisation energies — stepwise vs cumulativeFirst and second ionisation energy — how the two steps are defined and added🟡 Similara, b, dIE₁(M)=100, IE₁(M⁺)=IE₂(M)=150 eV
Concept Ionisation enthalpy is defined one electron at a time and always for a named starting species. IE1 refers to the neutral atom losing its first electron; IE2 refers to the unipositive ion losing the next one. The second equation in this problem is not an IE2 — it strips two electrons in one written step, so its ΔH is the cumulative energy IE1 + IE2. Confusing "cumulative" with "successive" is the entire trap here. The second key idea is a matter of naming: removing an electron from M⁺ is the first ionisation of the species M⁺, so IE₁(M⁺) ≡ IE₂(M) — the same number wears two labels.
Key relation M(g) → M⁺(g) + e⁻ ; ΔH = IE₁ · M⁺(g) → M²⁺(g) + e⁻ ; ΔH = IE₂ · adding the two (Hess's law) M(g) → M²⁺(g) + 2e⁻ ; ΔH = IE₁ + IE₂ enthalpy is a state function, so the two-electron ΔH must equal the sum of the two one-electron steps — that is what lets us subtract. Also IE₂ > IE₁ always the second electron leaves an already positive ion, so the electrostatic pull on it is larger and the electron–electron repulsion that helped the first one is gone.
Solution
1. Read equation 1 literally: one electron leaves the neutral atom M(g). That is the textbook definition of IE1. ⇒ IE₁(M) = 100 eV, so (a) is correct.
2. Read equation 2: two electrons leave M(g) together. This is the cumulative process, so ΔH = IE₁ + IE₂ = 250 eV.
3. Subtract: IE₂(M) = 250 − 100 = 150 eV. So (d) is correct and (c) is wrong — 250 eV is the total for both electrons, not the second step alone.
4. Rename the second step by its starting species: M⁺(g) → M²⁺(g) + e⁻ is the first ionisation of the ion M⁺. Hence IE₁(M⁺) = IE₂(M) = 150 eV, so (b) is correct.Students often reject (b) because the symbol "IE₁" makes them look for a neutral atom; but IE₁ simply means "remove one electron from whatever species is written".
5. Sanity check with the universal rule IE₂ > IE₁: 150 > 100 ✔. Had you (wrongly) taken IE₂ = 250 you would still pass this check, which is exactly why option (c) is such a good distractor — only the Hess-law subtraction exposes it.
 step                                  DeltaH        label
 M(g)   -> M+(g)  + e-                 +100 eV       IE1(M)                 <-- (a) TRUE
 M(g)   -> M2+(g) + 2e-                +250 eV       IE1 + IE2  (cumulative)
 ------------------------------------------------------------------
 subtract:  M+(g) -> M2+(g) + e-       +150 eV       IE2(M) = IE1(M+)

 (a) IE1 of M   = 100 eV   TRUE
 (b) IE1 of M+  = 150 eV   TRUE   (same event as IE2 of M)
 (c) IE2 of M   = 250 eV   FALSE  (250 is the two-electron total)
 (d) IE2 of M   = 150 eV   TRUE
                                                      <-- answer (a), (b), (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a problem shows an equation that removes n electrons in one line, immediately relabel its ΔH as ΣIE = IE₁ + IE₂ + … + IEn, then peel off the individual steps by subtraction — never read it as IEn. And memorise the identity IEn(M) = IE₁(M⁽ⁿ⁻¹⁾⁺); JEE loves to re-badge the same physical step with a different subscript and see whether you notice. The trap set by (c) is precisely the cumulative-vs-successive confusion; the trap avoided by (b) is the "IE₁ must mean a neutral atom" reflex.
Answer: (a), (b) and (d).
5 (i)Passage (i)–(iv). The I.E.1 and the I.E.2 in kJ mol⁻¹ of a few elements designated by P, Q, R, S are shown below — P: 2372, 5251 · Q: 520, 7300 · R: 900, 1760 · S: 1680, 3380. Based on the above information, answer the following questions: — Which of the element is likely to be reactive metal? (a) P (b) Q (c) R (d) SIdentifying a reactive metal from IE₁ and IE₂Reading successive ionisation-energy data to place an element in its group🟡 SimilarbQ (IE₁ = 520, huge jump to 7300 ⇒ alkali metal, Li)
Concept Successive ionisation energies are a fingerprint of the valence-shell population. Electrons in the outermost shell come off comparatively cheaply; the moment you break into the noble-gas core the price jumps several-fold. So the position of the big jump tells you the group directly. A reactive metal must have a low IE₁ (it has to give an electron away easily under ordinary chemical conditions) and a huge IE₂ (so that after losing one electron it is sitting on a closed shell and refuses to give more). Only one of P, Q, R, S has that profile.
Key relation jump ratio = IE₂ / IE₁. If the ratio is close to 2, both electrons are valence electrons; if it is ≥ 4–5, the second electron is being pulled out of the core. Roughly, IE for a core electron scales with the much larger Z* it feels, hence the multi-fold rather than incremental rise. For a group-1 atom ns¹, the jump comes at the second ionisation; for group 2 (ns²) it comes at the third.
Solution
1. Compute the jump ratio for each element. P: 5251/2372 = 2.21 · Q: 7300/520 = 14.0 · R: 1760/900 = 1.96 · S: 3380/1680 = 2.01.
2. Only Q shows the tell-tale core-breaking jump (14-fold). Its IE₁ = 520 kJ mol⁻¹ is also by far the smallest in the table.Low IE₁ + enormous IE₂ = exactly one valence electron over a noble-gas core = group 1 = the most reactive metals.
3. Identify Q outright: IE₁ = 520 and IE₂ = 7298 kJ mol⁻¹ are the textbook values for lithium. Losing one electron gives Li⁺ = [He], which is why the second electron costs fourteen times as much.
4. Eliminate the others. P has the largest IE₁ in the table (2372) — an atom that clings to its electrons cannot be a reactive metal. R (900, 1760) has two cheap electrons — a metal, but a group-2 one, and its IE₁ is 1.7× that of Q, so it is the less reactive metal. S (1680) has a high IE₁ and belongs on the non-metal side.
 element   IE1    IE2    IE2/IE1   verdict
 P        2372   5251     2.21     very high IE1  -> noble gas (He)
 Q         520   7300    14.03     low IE1, core jump -> group 1 metal (Li)   <-- answer (b)
 R         900   1760     1.96     two easy electrons -> group 2 metal (Be)
 S        1680   3380     2.01     high IE1 -> reactive non-metal (F)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not compare the raw numbers — compare the ratios, and count how many electrons come off before the ratio explodes. That count is the group's valence-electron number: 1 ⇒ group 1, 2 ⇒ group 2, 3 ⇒ group 13, and so on. Here the explosion is at the 2nd electron, so Q has one valence electron. Trap: candidates pick R because "900 and 1760 are both small numbers", forgetting that reactivity of a metal is decided by how cheap the first electron is, not by how many are cheap.
Answer: (b) Q.
5 (ii)Which of the elements is likely to be reactive non-metal? (a) P (b) Q (c) R (d) S [data: P 2372/5251 · Q 520/7300 · R 900/1760 · S 1680/3380 kJ mol⁻¹]Identifying a reactive non-metal from IE dataUsing IE₁–IE₅ of an element X to decide its group and character🟡 SimilardS (IE₁ = 1680, smooth ratio ⇒ halogen, F)
Concept A reactive non-metal is an element that grabs electrons rather than shedding them, so it must have a high IE₁ — but not the highest, because the absolute maximum in any period belongs to the noble gas, which is unreactive by definition. The signature is therefore "second-highest IE₁ in the set, with no dramatic jump yet", i.e. an atom one electron short of a closed shell. That is a halogen. So the search is for the runner-up in IE₁, not the winner.
Key relation reactivity of a non-metal ∝ tendency to gain e⁻ ∝ high IE₁ and highly negative ΔHeg · noble gas = highest IE₁ of the period, but zero drive to gain electrons its shell is already closed, so an incoming electron would have to start a brand-new shell at much higher energy.
Solution
1. Rank the four IE₁ values: P 2372 > S 1680 > R 900 > Q 520. The two metals (Q, R) are already excluded by their small IE₁.
2. P has the highest IE₁ of all and a modest IE₂/IE₁ ratio of 2.21 — the profile of a closed-shell noble gas (helium: 2372.3 and 5250.5 kJ mol⁻¹). Noble gases are chemically inert, so P is not "reactive".
3. That leaves S: IE₁ = 1680, IE₂ = 3380, ratio 2.01 — no core jump yet, so S still has more than one valence electron; and its high IE₁ places it firmly on the non-metal side. These are the real values for fluorine (1681 and 3374 kJ mol⁻¹), the most reactive non-metal there is. (d).
4. Cross-check by chemistry rather than arithmetic: F needs just one electron to reach [Ne], has a small size and enormous Z*, and forms F⁻ readily — the textbook reactive non-metal. Helium needs nothing and forms no ordinary compounds.
 IE1 ranking       2372 (P)  >  1680 (S)  >  900 (R)  >  520 (Q)
                     |             |            |          |
                  noble gas    reactive       metal      metal
                  (inert)      non-metal     (group 2)  (group 1)
                                  <-- answer (d) S = fluorine
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the four-way IE₁ ladder for any period: alkali metal (lowest) < alkaline earth < halogen < noble gas (highest). Then "most reactive metal" = bottom of the ladder and "most reactive non-metal" = one rung below the top, never the top itself. Trap: option (a) collects everyone who reads "reactive non-metal" as "biggest ionisation energy" and forgets that the biggest belongs to an inert gas.
Answer: (d) S.
5 (iii)Which represents a noble gas? (a) P (b) Q (c) R (d) S [data: P 2372/5251 · Q 520/7300 · R 900/1760 · S 1680/3380 kJ mol⁻¹]Recognising a noble gas from ionisation dataWorked example: identifying an element from its successive ionisation energies🟡 SimilaraP (IE₁ = 2372, the maximum ⇒ He)
Concept A noble gas sits at the end of a period with a completely filled valence shell. Complete filling means maximum effective nuclear charge for that period and minimum atomic size, so its first ionisation energy is the largest of any element in the period — there is no cheaper electron anywhere in the atom. This single number is the most reliable identifier in the whole table, far more reliable than radius or electronegativity data.
Definition noble gas: ns²np⁶ (or 1s² for He) ⇒ ΔHeg ≈ positive, IE₁ = period maximum both facts follow from the same cause — a closed shell has no low-energy vacancy to accept an electron and no loosely-held electron to donate.
Solution
1. Scan the IE₁ column for the maximum: P = 2372 kJ mol⁻¹, more than 40 % above the next value (S, 1680) and more than four times Q's.
2. Confirm with IE₂. For P, IE₂ = 5251 with a ratio of only 2.21 — a smooth, non-explosive rise. In helium (1s²) both electrons are in the same closed shell, so the second is dearer but not catastrophically so.If P had been an alkali metal with an accidentally high IE₁, its ratio would have blown up the way Q's does (14×). It does not.
3. Match to real data: He, IE₁ = 2372.3 and IE₂ = 5250.5 kJ mol⁻¹ — the numbers in the passage are helium's to within a single unit. So P is a noble gas, option (a).
4. Eliminate: Q and R have IE₁ far too low for a closed shell; S (1680) is high but is beaten by P, and only one element per period can be the maximum.
 candidate  IE1     is it the period maximum?
 P         2372     YES  -> closed shell, He (2372.3 / 5250.5)   <-- answer (a)
 Q          520     no
 R          900     no
 S         1680     no  (runner-up = halogen)
⚡ JEE Trick In any "identify the element from IE data" set, do the assignment in this fixed order: (1) largest IE₁ ⇒ noble gas · (2) smallest IE₁ with a giant IE₂ ⇒ group 1 · (3) two cheap electrons then a giant IE₃ ⇒ group 2 · (4) whatever high-IE₁ element is left ⇒ halogen/non-metal. Four questions, one pass through the table. Trap: answering (d) here, because a candidate who has just labelled S "the reactive non-metal" forgets that the noble gas must beat it.
Answer: (a) P.
5 (iv)Which of the above elements forms a stable binary halide of the formula MX2? (a) P (b) Q (c) R (d) S [data: P 2372/5251 · Q 520/7300 · R 900/1760 · S 1680/3380 kJ mol⁻¹]Predicting valency (MX₂) from the position of the IE jumpPredicting an element's group and valency from five successive ionisation energies🟡 SimilarcR (both IEs modest ⇒ M²⁺, group 2, Be)
Concept A stable halide MX2 requires the metal to reach a +2 oxidation state, i.e. to surrender exactly two electrons and then stop. Energetically that means IE₁ and IE₂ must both be affordable (the lattice energy of MX₂ pays them back), while IE₃ must be prohibitive so that MX₃ never forms. In the ionisation fingerprint this is the profile "two gentle steps, then a cliff" — precisely group 2.
Key relation valency = number of electrons removed before the big jump · for MX₂: IE₁ + IE₂ modest, IE₃ ≫ IE₂ the Born–Haber balance only works if the extra lattice energy of the higher halide exceeds the extra ionisation cost, and a core-breaking IE₃ is far too large for any halide lattice to repay.
Solution
1. Ask of each element: are the first two electrons both cheap? Q fails at once — its IE₂ is 7300 kJ mol⁻¹, so QX₂ could never form; Q gives only QX.
2. P is a noble gas (part iii) and forms no ordinary halide at all. S is a non-metal with IE₁ = 1680 — it gains an electron to give X⁻ rather than losing two.
3. R: IE₁ = 900, IE₂ = 1760, sum = 2660 kJ mol⁻¹ with a ratio of only 1.96 — both electrons come from the same valence shell. Losing both leaves a noble-gas core, so the next ionisation would be a cliff. R therefore forms R²⁺ and the halide RX₂. Option (c).
4. Identify R: 899.5 and 1757.1 kJ mol⁻¹ are the values for beryllium, and BeCl₂/BeF₂ are exactly the stable MX₂ halides the question is describing.Consistency check on the whole passage: P = He, Q = Li, R = Be, S = F — four real elements from period 1–2, which is why every number matched a textbook value.
 element  IE1   IE2   IE1+IE2   e- removable cheaply   stable halide
 P       2372  5251     ---     none (closed shell)    none
 Q        520  7300     ---     1  (IE2 is a cliff)    QX
 R        900  1760    2660     2  (cliff comes at IE3) RX2          <-- answer (c)
 S       1680  3380     ---     0  (gains instead)     S is the X in MX2
⚡ JEE Trick Convert "formula of the stable halide" straight into "where is the jump": MX ⇒ jump after 1 electron, MX₂ ⇒ jump after 2, MX₃ ⇒ jump after 3. You never need real thermodynamics, only the ratio column. Bonus insight for this passage — the fourth element S is the X in MX₂, so the same table both names the metal and supplies its partner. Trap: choosing (b) because Q "is the most reactive metal"; reactivity decides how eagerly it reacts, the IE jump decides how many electrons it can give.
Answer: (c) R.

Previous Year Questions

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1(2026 Level-4) Given below are two statements: Statement-I: The first ionization enthalpy of Cr is lower than that of Mn. Statement-II: The second and third ionization enthalpies of Cr are higher than those of Mn. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) Both Statement-I and Statement-II are false. (b) Statement-I is true but Statement-II is false. (c) Both Statement-I and Statement-II are true. (d) Statement-I is false but Statement-II is true.Successive IEs of Cr vs Mn — half-filled d⁵ stabilityWhy 1st IE of Cr is below Mn while its 2nd and 3rd IEs behave differently🟢 ExactbI true, II false (IE₃: Mn 3248 > Cr 2987)
Concept Every ionisation-energy comparison in the 3d series is decided by what configuration is left behind. An ionisation that creates a half-filled d⁵ set is unusually easy; an ionisation that destroys one is unusually hard. Cr = [Ar]3d⁵4s¹ and Mn = [Ar]3d⁵4s², so the two atoms swap roles at every successive step, and you must re-examine each step separately instead of assuming a trend. Statement II bundles two claims (IE₂ and IE₃) into one sentence — a compound statement is true only if both halves survive, which is where this question is won or lost.
Key relation Cr: [Ar]3d⁵4s¹ → Cr⁺ [Ar]3d⁵ → Cr²⁺ [Ar]3d⁴ → Cr³⁺ [Ar]3d³ · Mn: [Ar]3d⁵4s² → Mn⁺ [Ar]3d⁵4s¹ → Mn²⁺ [Ar]3d⁵ → Mn³⁺ [Ar]3d⁴ the exchange-energy bonus of a half-filled shell appears at the Cr⁺ stage but only at the Mn²⁺ stage — one step later — which is exactly why the comparison flips between IE₂ and IE₃.
Solution
1. IE₁. Cr loses its single, poorly-shielded 4s electron and lands on the extra-stable [Ar]3d⁵. Mn must break into a paired 4s² and gains nothing special. Data: Cr 653, Mn 717 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ Cr < Mn ⇒ Statement I is TRUE.
2. IE₂. Now Cr⁺ = [Ar]3d⁵ must destroy a half-filled shell, while Mn⁺ = [Ar]3d⁵4s¹ merely drops its leftover 4s electron. Data: Cr 1592, Mn 1509 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ Cr > Mn, so the first half of Statement II holds.
3. IE₃. The roles reverse once more: Cr²⁺ = [Ar]3d⁴ has nothing to protect, whereas Mn²⁺ = [Ar]3d⁵ is itself the half-filled shell and resists fiercely.
