Data: Cr 2987, Mn 3248 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ Cr < Mn, so the second half of Statement II fails.This is the same fact that makes Mn²⁺ the most stable +2 ion of the whole first transition series and Mn³⁺ a strong oxidising agent.
4. Statement II claims "second and third … are higher"; since IE₃ is not, the compound statement is FALSE. Statement I true + Statement II false ⇒ (b).
5. Eliminate: (c) is the answer you get if you stop after IE₂; (d) reverses a fact that real data confirm; (a) contradicts both.
 kJ/mol      IE1     IE2     IE3        ion formed at each step
 Cr (24)     653    1592    2987        3d5      3d4      3d3
 Mn (25)     717    1509    3248        3d5 4s1  3d5      3d4
 ---------------------------------------------------------------
 IE1:  Cr < Mn   TRUE  (Cr+ = half-filled d5 is the reward)
 IE2:  Cr > Mn   TRUE  (Cr+ d5 must be broken; Mn+ just drops 4s)
 IE3:  Cr < Mn   so "2nd AND 3rd both higher" is FALSE
                                          <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Track the ion, not the atom: write the species that exists just before each ionisation and ask "is it d⁵ (or d¹⁰)?" If yes, that IE is anomalously high. Cr⁺ is d⁵ ⇒ IE₂(Cr) spikes; Mn²⁺ is d⁵ ⇒ IE₃(Mn) spikes. Trap: the examiner writes "second and third" as one clause hoping you will verify only the second; whenever a statement contains "and", test both halves before ticking it.
Answer: (b) Statement-I is true but Statement-II is false.
2(2026 Level-3) Which of the following represents the correct trend for the mentioned property? (A) F > P > S > B – First Ionization Energy (B) Cl > F > S > P – Electron Affinity (C) K > Al > Mg > B – Metallic character (D) K₂O > Na₂O > MgO > Al₂O₃ – Basic character. Choose the correct answer from the option given below. (a) A, B and D only (b) A, B, C and D (c) A and B only (d) B and C onlyFour periodic trends tested togetherJEE Main PYQs on periodic properties — IE, electron affinity, metallic and basic character🟡 SimilaraA, B and D only (C fails: Mg is more metallic than Al)
Concept Four independent trends are packed into one question, so the fastest safe route is to find the single false statement rather than prove three true ones. Three of the four lists mix elements from different periods and groups, which means you cannot use "left–right" or "top–bottom" reasoning alone; you need the actual anomalies — the half-filled p³ dip (P > S in ionisation energy), the small-size electron–electron repulsion in fluorine (Cl > F in electron affinity), and the fact that metallic character falls across a period so Mg must beat Al.
Key relation IE₁ / kJ mol⁻¹: B 801 · S 1000 · P 1012 · F 1681 · |EA| / kJ mol⁻¹: P 72 · S 200 · F 328 · Cl 349 · metallic character ↓ across a period, ↑ down a group · basicity of an oxide ↑ with the metallic (electropositive) character of the element a more electropositive metal gives a more ionic oxide, whose O²⁻ is more available to accept protons.
Solution
1. (A) F > P > S > B. Using the data above: 1681 > 1012 > 1000 > 801 ✔. Note the subtle P > S step — phosphorus has the extra-stable half-filled 3p³, sulphur has a paired 3p⁴ whose repulsion makes an electron easier to remove. TRUE.
2. (B) Cl > F > S > P. 349 > 328 > 200 > 72 ✔. Chlorine beats fluorine because F's 2p shell is so compact that adding an electron there costs a large repulsion penalty. TRUE.
3. (C) K > Al > Mg > B. K is certainly the most metallic and B the least, but Mg (group 2, period 3) is more metallic than Al (group 13, period 3) — metallic character decreases left to right. The true order is K > Mg > Al > B, so the printed order is FALSE. (Cross-check with IE₁: Mg 738 < Al 578? No — Al is 578, lower than Mg's 738, so use the more reliable markers: electronegativity Mg 1.31 < Al 1.61, and Mg(OH)₂ is a base while Al(OH)₃ is amphoteric.)
4. (D) K₂O > Na₂O > MgO > Al₂O₃. Basicity follows electropositivity: K > Na (down group 1) and Na > Mg > Al (across period 3). Chemically, K₂O and Na₂O are strongly basic, MgO is weakly basic, Al₂O₃ is amphoteric. TRUE.
5. Only C fails ⇒ the correct set is A, B and D only = (a). Option (b) needs C to be right; (c) drops the correct D; (d) keeps the wrong C.
 (A) IE1:   F 1681 > P 1012 > S 1000 > B 801                TRUE
 (B) |EA|:  Cl 349 > F 328 > S 200 > P 72                    TRUE
 (C) metallic:  printed K > Al > Mg > B
                actual  K > Mg > Al > B                      FALSE
 (D) basic: K2O > Na2O > MgO > Al2O3 (strong -> amphoteric)   TRUE
                                       A, B, D only  <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the two "anomaly pairs" that this paper recycles endlessly: P > S for ionisation energy and Cl > F for electron affinity. Statements containing them are usually the TRUE ones (the examiner is testing whether you know the exception), while a statement that merely looks like a smooth left-to-right march — such as K > Al > Mg — is where the error is hidden. Trap: candidates accept C because K and B sit at the right ends, never checking the middle pair.
Answer: (a) A, B and D only.
3(2026 Level-3) Given below are two statements: Statement-I: The correct order in terms of atomic/ionic radii is Al > Mg > Mg²⁺ > Al³⁺. Statement-II: The correct order in terms of the magnitude of electron gain enthalpy is Cl > Br > S > O. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are trueAtom vs cation radii; electron-gain-enthalpy orderIonic radii and isoelectronic species — comparing atoms with their cations🟡 SimilarbI false (Mg 160 pm > Al 143 pm); II true
Concept Statement I is a four-term chain, and a chain is true only if every consecutive link is true. Three of its links are obviously right — a neutral atom is always larger than its own cation, and among isoelectronic cations the higher charge is smaller — which is exactly why the examiner hides the error in the first link, Al vs Mg. Atomic radius decreases across a period, so Mg (group 2) must be larger than Al (group 13). Statement II tests the two independent anomalies of electron gain enthalpy: the second-row elements F and O are less exothermic than their third-row partners Cl and S because of compact-shell repulsion.
Key relation r(Mg) = 160 pm · r(Al) = 143 pm · r(Mg²⁺) = 72 pm · r(Al³⁺) = 53.5 pm · |ΔHeg| / kJ mol⁻¹: Cl 349 > Br 325 > S 200 > O 141 Cl beats F and S beats O for the same reason — the incoming electron enters a small, electron-dense 2p shell in F and O and suffers extra repulsion.
Solution
1. Statement I, link 1: Al > Mg? Both are in period 3; Z rises from 12 to 13 while the electron enters the same n = 3 shell, so Z* rises and the atom shrinks. Real values 160 pm (Mg) vs 143 pm (Al) ⇒ Mg > Al, the printed order is inverted. Statement I is FALSE at its very first comparison.
2. For completeness the rest of the chain is fine: Mg > Mg²⁺ (losing the whole 3s shell), and Mg²⁺ (72 pm) > Al³⁺ (53.5 pm) since both are isoelectronic with Ne and Al³⁺ has one more proton pulling the same ten electrons. A correct chain would read Mg > Al > Mg²⁺ > Al³⁺.
3. Statement II. Cl (349) > Br (325): within group 17, chlorine is the maximum because F is anomalously low and Br is larger and less attractive. Br (325) > S (200): a halogen needing one electron always releases more than a group-16 element needing two. S (200) > O (141): the classic second-row anomaly. All three links hold ⇒ TRUE.
4. Statement I false + Statement II true ⇒ (b). Options (c) and (d) require Statement I; (a) wrongly rejects Statement II.
 Statement I   printed : Al  > Mg  > Mg2+ > Al3+
               radii/pm: 143   160     72      53.5
               actual  : Mg(160) > Al(143) > Mg2+(72) > Al3+(53.5)
               => first link inverted                       FALSE

 Statement II  |dHeg|  : Cl 349 > Br 325 > S 200 > O 141    TRUE
                                                    <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick In any radius chain, check the neutral–neutral comparison first: cation-vs-atom and isoelectronic-cation links are almost always printed correctly, so the planted error is nearly always between two atoms of the same period. And keep the number pair Mg 160 / Al 143 handy — JEE has used the "Al > Mg" inversion in several papers. Trap: readers see the plausible tail "Mg²⁺ > Al³⁺" and approve the whole chain.
Answer: (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true.
4(2026 Level-3) Given below are two statements: Statement-I: C < O < N < F is the correct order in terms of first ionization enthalpy values. Statement-II: S > Se > Te > Po > O is the correct order in terms of the magnitude of electron gain enthalpy values. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) Statement-I is false but Statement-II is true. (b) Both Statement-I and Statement-II are true. (c) Both Statement-I and Statement-II are false. (d) Statement-I is true but Statement-II is false.N > O IE anomaly; group-16 electron gain enthalpyOrder of electron gain enthalpy for O, S, Se, Te and why oxygen is last🟡 SimilarbBoth true (C 1086 < O 1314 < N 1402 < F 1681)
Concept Both statements are built on the same physical idea seen from opposite sides. Half-filled and compact-shell effects distort the smooth periodic marches: nitrogen's 2p³ is extra stable, so N has a higher IE₁ than O even though O is further right; and oxygen's 2p shell is so small that an incoming electron is repelled, so O has the smallest |ΔHeg| in its own group. A student who knows only "IE increases across a period" and "electron gain enthalpy decreases down a group" will mark both statements false — and lose the marks.
Key relation IE₁ / kJ mol⁻¹: C 1086 · O 1314 · N 1402 · F 1681 N (2s²2p³, half filled) resists ionisation more than O (2s²2p⁴, one paired p orbital whose repulsion helps the electron leave). · |ΔHeg| / kJ mol⁻¹: S 200 · Se 195 · Te 190 · Po 174 · O 141 down group 16 the value falls smoothly from S onwards, but oxygen sits below all of them because of its very small size.
Solution
1. Statement I. Place the four values in increasing order: C 1086 < O 1314 < N 1402 < F 1681. That is precisely the printed chain C < O < N < F ⇒ TRUE.
2. Why O sits below N: removing an electron from O (…2px²2py¹2pz¹) relieves the pairing repulsion in the doubly-occupied 2px and leaves the stable half-filled 2p³, so it is cheaper than expected; N has no such incentive.The same argument, one period lower, is what makes P > S — the anomaly is systematic, not a one-off.
3. Statement II. Down group 16 the atom gets bigger, the added electron sits further from the nucleus, and |ΔHeg| falls: S 200 > Se 195 > Te 190 > Po 174. Oxygen breaks the pattern and lands at 141, below all of them, because its 2p shell is tiny and already electron-dense. So the chain S > Se > Te > Po > O is exactly right ⇒ TRUE.
4. Both true ⇒ (b). Option (d) is the trap for anyone who "corrects" S > Se > Te > Po > O to put O first; (a) for anyone who forces N < O; (c) for anyone who does both.
 Statement I   IE1/kJ mol-1
   C 1086  <  O 1314  <  N 1402  <  F 1681           TRUE
                     ^ N > O : half-filled 2p3 anomaly

 Statement II  |dHeg|/kJ mol-1
   S 200  >  Se 195  >  Te 190  >  Po 174  >  O 141  TRUE
                                              ^ O last: small, crowded 2p
                                                    <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Store two one-line exception rules and this whole family of questions collapses: (1) IE₁ — N > O and P > S (half-filled beats the trend). (2) ΔHeg — Cl > F and S > O (period-3 beats period-2). Rule 1 makes Statement I true; rule 2 makes Statement II true. Trap: both statements look wrong to a student reciting only the plain trends, and (c) is the seductive "both false" option placed exactly for them.
Answer: (b) Both Statement-I and Statement-II are true.
5(2026 Level-3) The correct trend in the first ionization enthalpies of the elements in the 3rd period of periodic table is: (a) Al < Si < S < P < Cl (b) Al < S < P < Si < Cl (c) Si < S < Al < P < Cl (d) S < Si < Al < P < ClFirst IE across period 3 with the P > S anomalyAssigning the correct ionisation-enthalpy values to Si, P, S and Cl🟡 SimilaraAl < Si < S < P < Cl (578 < 786 < 1000 < 1012 < 1251)
Concept Across period 3 the nuclear charge grows while the electron is added to the same n = 3 shell, so Z* rises, the atom contracts and IE₁ climbs — Al → Si → P → Cl obeys this perfectly. The one place the march breaks is at P → S: phosphorus has the half-filled 3s²3p³, sulphur has 3s²3p⁴ in which one 3p orbital is doubly occupied. That paired electron feels extra inter-electronic repulsion and is easier to remove, so IE₁(S) dips just below IE₁(P). The entire question is whether you place S before or after P.
Formula IE₁ ∝ Z*eff / r — rising Z* across the period is the main driver Al 578 → Cl 1251 is more than a doubling in five steps. Superposed on it: extra stability of a half-filled (p³) or filled (p⁶) sub-shell, which raises IE₁ for P (and for N, Mg, Be) above its neighbour on the right.
Solution
1. Write the real values (kJ mol⁻¹): Al 578 · Si 786 · P 1012 · S 1000 · Cl 1251.
2. Sort them into increasing order: 578 (Al) < 786 (Si) < 1000 (S) < 1012 (P) < 1251 (Cl). That reads Al < Si < S < P < Cl — exactly option (a).
3. Note that Al also sits below the "expected" line (Mg is 738, higher than Al's 578) because Al's electron leaves a 3p orbital while Mg's leaves the filled 3s² — but Mg is not in the option list, so this second anomaly does not affect the answer here.Being aware of it prevents the common error of "correcting" the sequence by moving Al upward.
4. Eliminate: (b) puts Si above P and S — impossible, Si is to the left of both. (c) and (d) place Al above Si or S, contradicting the basic left-to-right rise. Only (a) preserves the period trend and the single P/S inversion.
 element    Al     Si      S      P      Cl
 IE1        578    786   1000   1012   1251     kJ/mol
 sorted:    Al  <  Si  <  S   <  P   <  Cl
                             ^^^^^^^^
                             P > S : half-filled 3p3 of P
                                             <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick For period 3 remember the shape of the curve rather than the numbers: it rises steadily but has two dips, at Al and at S (mirroring B and O in period 2). So the sorted order is always Na < Al < Mg < Si < S < P < Cl < Ar. Read the required elements straight off that master chain and you never have to think again. Trap: the P/S gap is only 12 kJ mol⁻¹, so an option that puts S last among {Si, P, S} looks harmless — but it is the whole point of the question.
Answer: (a) Al < Si < S < P < Cl.
6(2026 Level-3) Elements X and Y belong to Group 15. The difference between the electronegativity values of 'X' and phosphorus is higher than that of the difference between phosphorus and 'Y'. 'X' & 'Y' are respectively (a) N & As (b) As & Bi (c) Bi & N (d) As & SbElectronegativity gaps down group 15Ordering electronegativity on the Pauling scale for N, P and their neighbours🟡 SimilaraN & As (|3.04−2.19| = 0.85 ≫ |2.19−2.18| = 0.01)
Concept Electronegativity falls down group 15, but not evenly. The drop from N to P is huge (0.85 units) because the valence shell jumps from the tiny 2p to the far larger 3p; from P downwards the values crawl (P 2.19, As 2.18, Sb 2.05, Bi 2.02) because the newly inserted 3d (and later 4f) electrons shield the nucleus poorly, so the extra protons almost exactly offset the extra size. The question is therefore a test of whether you know that the first step of a p-block group is always the big one, and that P–As is essentially a zero-gap pair.
Key relation Pauling EN: N 3.04 · P 2.19 · As 2.18 · Sb 2.05 · Bi 2.02 · the condition required is |EN(X) − EN(P)| > |EN(P) − EN(Y)| the gaps are compared in magnitude, so X should be the element far from phosphorus and Y the element nearest to it.
Solution
1. Test (a) X = N, Y = As. |3.04 − 2.19| = 0.85; |2.19 − 2.18| = 0.01. Since 0.85 > 0.01 the condition is satisfied comfortably — this is the answer.
2. Test (b) X = As, Y = Bi. |2.18 − 2.19| = 0.01; |2.19 − 2.02| = 0.17. Here 0.01 < 0.17 — the inequality is the wrong way round. ✗
3. Test (c) X = Bi, Y = N. 0.17 vs 0.85 ⇒ 0.17 < 0.85. ✗ (This is option (a) with X and Y swapped — the examiner's mirror trap.)
4. Test (d) X = As, Y = Sb. 0.01 vs |2.19 − 2.05| = 0.14 ⇒ 0.01 < 0.14. ✗
5. Only (a) survives. Qualitatively: X must be nitrogen because nitrogen is the only group-15 element far removed from phosphorus in electronegativity, and Y must be arsenic because As is the element that is practically identical to P.The near-equality of P and As is a real chemical fact — it is why arsenic mimics phosphate in biology, which is the basis of arsenate toxicity.
 EN (Pauling)   N 3.04   P 2.19   As 2.18   Sb 2.05   Bi 2.02

 option   |EN(X) - EN(P)|      |EN(P) - EN(Y)|     X-gap > Y-gap ?
 (a) N,As     0.85                  0.01                YES     <-- answer (a)
 (b) As,Bi    0.01                  0.17                no
 (c) Bi,N     0.17                  0.85                no
 (d) As,Sb    0.01                  0.14                no
⚡ JEE Trick You do not need the decimals — you need two facts: the N→P drop is the largest gap in group 15, and P ≈ As (the 3d-contraction pair, just like Ga ≈ Al and Ge ≈ Si). Any option pairing "far from P" with "next to P" wins instantly. Trap: option (c) contains the same two elements as the answer but with the roles reversed, so a candidate who computes the numbers correctly can still lose the mark by not tracking which symbol is X.
Answer: (a) N & As.
7(2026 Level-3) Given below are two statements: Statement I: The second ionisation enthalpy of Na is larger than the corresponding ionisation enthalpy of Mg. Statement II: The ionic radius of O²⁻ is larger than that of F⁻. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below. (a) Both statement I and statement II are true (b) Both statement I and statement II are false (c) Statement I is false but statement II is true (d) Statement I is true but statement II is falseIE₂ of Na vs Mg; isoelectronic anion sizesWhich ion is larger, O²⁻ or F⁻ — reasoning for isoelectronic anions🟡 SimilaraBoth true (IE₂: Na 4562 ≫ Mg 1451; r: O²⁻ 140 > F⁻ 133 pm)
Concept Both statements are consequences of one principle: what matters is the shell the electron sits in, not the raw atomic number. For Statement I, Na⁺ is already a neon core, so its "second" electron must be dragged out of a complete n = 2 shell, whereas Mg⁺ still has a loose 3s electron — the difference is not a few per cent but a factor of three. For Statement II, O²⁻ and F⁻ are isoelectronic (10 electrons each); the one with fewer protons holds those ten electrons less tightly, so it is bigger. Both are standard results, and the question simply checks that you do not "correct" either of them.
Key relation IE₂(Na): Na⁺ [Ne] → Na²⁺ [He]2s²2p⁵ = 4562 kJ mol⁻¹ · IE₂(Mg): Mg⁺ [Ne]3s¹ → Mg²⁺ [Ne] = 1451 kJ mol⁻¹ · for isoelectronic species r ∝ 1 / (Z/e⁻ ratio), i.e. radius falls as nuclear charge rises O²⁻ (Z = 8) and F⁻ (Z = 9) both carry 10 electrons, so F⁻'s extra proton contracts the cloud.
Solution
1. Statement I. Write the species being ionised. Na⁺ = 1s²2s²2p⁶, a closed noble-gas shell: removing an electron here means breaking into the core ⇒ enormous cost, 4562 kJ mol⁻¹.
2. Mg⁺ = [Ne]3s¹ still has one outer-shell electron; its removal actually produces the stable [Ne] core, so it is cheap — 1451 kJ mol⁻¹.This is why sodium's chemistry stops at +1 while magnesium routinely reaches +2: the second ionisation is affordable only for Mg.
3. 4562 > 1451 by more than a factor of three ⇒ Statement I is TRUE.
4. Statement II. O²⁻: 8 protons for 10 electrons (Z/e = 0.80). F⁻: 9 protons for 10 electrons (Z/e = 0.90). Weaker pull per electron in O²⁻ ⇒ larger cloud. Values: O²⁻ 140 pm > F⁻ 133 pm ⇒ TRUE. (The full isoelectronic order is N³⁻ > O²⁻ > F⁻ > Na⁺ > Mg²⁺ > Al³⁺.)
5. Both true ⇒ (a). Option (d) traps anyone who thinks "F is more electronegative so F⁻ must be bigger"; (c) traps anyone who compares Na and Mg as neutral atoms instead of as +1 ions.
 Statement I     species ionised          config change          IE2 kJ/mol
   Na            Na+  [Ne]         -> Na2+ [He]2s2 2p5             4562
   Mg            Mg+  [Ne]3s1      -> Mg2+ [Ne]                    1451
   => Na >> Mg                                                    TRUE

 Statement II    isoelectronic, 10 e- each
   O2-  Z = 8   Z/e = 0.80   r = 140 pm
   F-   Z = 9   Z/e = 0.90   r = 133 pm
   => O2- > F-                                                    TRUE
                                                         <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Two reflexes settle almost every question of this family. (1) For any IEn comparison, first write the ion that is about to lose the electron and check whether it is a noble-gas core — if it is, that IE is huge. (2) For isoelectronic species, line them up by nuclear charge and read the radius order backwards. Trap: candidates compare "IE₂ of Na" with "IE₂ of Mg" using the periodic trend for atoms (Mg > Na) and pick (c); the trend for atoms says nothing about ions with different cores.
Answer: (a) Both statement I and statement II are true.
8(2026 Level-3) Given below are two statements: Statement I: K > Mg > Al > B is the correct order in terms of metallic character. Statement II: Atomic radius is always greater than the ionic radius for any element. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (d) Statement I is true but Statement II is falseMetallic-character order; atomic vs ionic radiusCorrect order of metallic character for B, Al, Mg and K🟡 SimilardI true; II false (anions are larger than their atoms)
Concept Statement I is the plain trend read in both directions at once: metallic (electropositive) character increases down a group and decreases across a period. Statement II is a sweeping generalisation containing the word "always" — and in periodic chemistry an "always" is almost always the planted error. It is true for cations (a metal loses its whole outer shell and shrinks) but flatly false for anions, where the added electron increases repulsion and expands the cloud. One counter-example is enough to demolish it.
Key relation metallic character ∝ ease of losing e⁻ ∝ 1/IE₁ ∝ 1/EN — EN: K 0.82 < Mg 1.31 < Al 1.61 < B 2.04 the exactly reversed electronegativity list is the metallic-character list. · r(cation) < r(atom) < r(anion) · counter-examples: Cl 99 pm → Cl⁻ 181 pm · O 66 pm → O²⁻ 140 pm
Solution
1. Statement I. K (period 4, group 1) is the most electropositive of the four. Then within period 3, Mg (group 2) > Al (group 13) because metallic character falls left-to-right. B (period 2, group 13) is the least metallic — it is in fact a metalloid. Order K > Mg > Al > B ⇒ TRUE.Chemical cross-check: KOH and Mg(OH)₂ are bases, Al(OH)₃ is amphoteric, and B(OH)₃ is a weak acid — a perfect metallic-to-non-metallic gradient.
2. Compare with Q2 of this same PYQ set, where the order printed was K > Al > Mg > B and was false. The examiner is testing the identical Mg/Al pair twice, once correctly and once inverted.
3. Statement II. Take chlorine: atomic (covalent) radius 99 pm, but Cl⁻ is 181 pm — nearly double. Oxygen: 66 pm vs O²⁻ 140 pm. The anion is far larger than the atom, because adding electrons without adding protons lowers the effective nuclear charge per electron and increases mutual repulsion.
4. Therefore the word "always" fails and Statement II is FALSE. The correct general rule is r(cation) < r(neutral atom) < r(anion).
5. Statement I true + Statement II false ⇒ (d). Option (a) accepts the "always"; (b) and (c) wrongly reject the metallic order.
 Statement I   metallic character
   K (EN 0.82) > Mg (1.31) > Al (1.61) > B (2.04)             TRUE
   hydroxide test: KOH base > Mg(OH)2 base > Al(OH)3 amph. > H3BO3 acid

 Statement II  "atomic radius ALWAYS > ionic radius"
   cations : Na 186 -> Na+  102 pm     smaller  (rule holds)
   anions  : Cl  99 -> Cl-  181 pm     LARGER   (rule broken)
             O   66 -> O2-  140 pm     LARGER
   => counter-examples exist                                   FALSE
                                                       <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Treat the words "always", "never", "for any element" as red flags: hunt for one counter-example instead of trying to verify the claim. Here a single anion destroys Statement II in two seconds. For metallic character, memorise the reversed-electronegativity trick — write the electronegativities, then read the list right-to-left. Trap: the paper deliberately prints the Mg/Al pair correctly here and incorrectly in Q2, so answers cannot be carried across.
Answer: (d) Statement I is true but Statement II is false.
9(2026 Level-3) The correct order of C, N, O and F in terms of second ionisation potential is (a) F < N < C < O (b) C < O < N < F (c) C < N < F < O (d) C < F < N < OSecond ionisation potential — half-filled O⁺ anomalyThe correct order of second ionisation potential of C, N, O and F — full solution🟢 Exactb ⚠C < O < N < F (see note — derivation gives (c))
Concept A second ionisation potential is a property of the unipositive ion, not of the atom, so the first thing to do is redraw all four configurations after one electron has already gone. Doing that shifts the half-filled anomaly one place to the right: in neutral atoms it is N (2p³) that is extra stable, but among the cations it is O⁺ (2p³) that is extra stable. Consequently the familiar "N > O" of first ionisation energy becomes "O > F" at the second ionisation — that single displacement is the entire content of this question.
Key relation C⁺ = 1s²2s²2p¹ · N⁺ = 1s²2s²2p² · O⁺ = 1s²2s²2p³ (half filled) · F⁺ = 1s²2s²2p⁴ · IE₂ / kJ mol⁻¹: C 2352 · N 2856 · F 3374 · O 3388 (in eV: 24.38, 29.60, 34.97, 35.12) O⁺ must break a half-filled 2p³, F⁺ only has to remove one of a paired 2p⁴ — so despite fluorine's larger Z, O⁺ resists slightly more.
Solution
1. Write the cations: C⁺ 2p¹, N⁺ 2p², O⁺ 2p³, F⁺ 2p⁴.
2. Baseline trend: across the period Z rises, so IE₂ should climb C⁺ < N⁺ < O⁺ < F⁺. That gives C < N < O < F as a first guess.
3. Apply the half-filled correction. O⁺ is the 2p³ species, so its ionisation is anomalously expensive and it jumps above F. Meanwhile F⁺ is 2p⁴, with one doubly-occupied 2p orbital whose repulsion assists removal, so it drops. The O/F pair therefore swaps.
4. Final order: C (2352) < N (2856) < F (3374) < O (3388), i.e. O > F > N > C — that is option (c).
5. Eliminate: (a) puts F lowest, contradicting the rise in Z; (b) puts O below N, but O⁺ (2p³) must beat N⁺ (2p²) on both Z and stability grounds; (d) puts F below N for the same untenable reason.
 element   ion after 1st IE   2p occupancy   IE2 kJ/mol   IE2 eV
 C         C+  1s2 2s2 2p1        p1            2352       24.38
 N         N+  1s2 2s2 2p2        p2            2856       29.60
 F         F+  1s2 2s2 2p4        p4            3374       34.97
 O         O+  1s2 2s2 2p3        p3 half-full  3388       35.12
 -----------------------------------------------------------------
 sorted:   C  <  N  <  F  <  O          = option (c)   [my derivation]
 printed key : (b) C < O < N < F                       <-- answer (b) as keyed
⚠ Note My derivation gives C < N < F < O — option (c) — from the standard second-ionisation data (C 2352, N 2856, F 3374, O 3388 kJ mol⁻¹; equivalently 24.38, 29.60, 34.97 and 35.12 eV). Option (b), which the printed key gives, requires IE₂(O) < IE₂(N), i.e. it asks the 2p³ half-filled cation O⁺ to ionise more easily than the 2p² cation N⁺ — impossible, since O⁺ has both the greater nuclear charge and the extra exchange stabilisation. The key letter (b) is displayed above as printed, but flagged; the defensible answer is (c).
⚡ JEE Trick Shift the anomaly by the charge: for IE₁ the stable species are N (2p³) and Ne (2p⁶); for IE₂ they move one place right, to O⁺ and Na⁺; for IE₃ one further, to F²⁺ and Mg²⁺. So "the element whose IEn is anomalously high" is simply "the one that is (group of N) + (n − 1)". Trap: reciting the neutral-atom result N > O and forcing it into a second-ionisation question — which is exactly the reasoning that produces option (b).
Answer: (b) C < O < N < F as keyed — re-derivation from IE₂ data gives (c) C < N < F < O.
10(2026 Level-3) In period 4 of the periodic table, the elements with highest and lowest atomic radii are respectively. (a) Na & Cl (b) K & Se (c) K & Br (d) Rb & BrExtremes of atomic radius within period 4Atomic-radius trends across a period and down a group🟡 SimilarcK & Br (K 227 pm is the largest, Br 114 pm the smallest)
Concept Two filters solve this. First, membership: period 4 runs from K (Z = 19) to Kr (Z = 36), so any option containing an element from another period is dead on arrival — Na is period 3 and Rb is period 5. Second, the trend: within a period the atomic radius falls steadily from left to right as Z* grows while the shell number stays fixed, so the group-1 element is the largest and the element nearest the right-hand end is the smallest. Only one option passes both filters.
Key relation r ∝ n² / Z* — n is constant across a period, Z* rises ⇒ r falls the 3d electrons inserted in the middle of period 4 shield very poorly, so the contraction continues right through the p-block. · covalent radii / pm: K 227 · Ca 197 · Ga 122 · Ge 122 · As 119 · Se 120 · Br 114
Solution
1. Reject by period. (a) contains Na — period 3. (d) contains Rb — period 5. Both are out without any radius reasoning.
2. That leaves (b) K & Se and (c) K & Br; both agree that K is the largest, which is correct — potassium is the group-1 element that opens the period, with a brand-new 4s electron very far from the nucleus (227 pm).
3. Decide the minimum. Br (Z = 35) lies to the right of Se (Z = 34), so it has one more proton acting on the same n = 4 shell and must be smaller: Br 114 pm < Se 120 pm. ⇒ (c).
4. Why not krypton, the last element of the period? Kr forms no ordinary bonds, so it has no covalent radius; its quoted van der Waals radius (≈ 202 pm) is measured on a different scale and always looks anomalously large. Comparisons of atomic radius across a period therefore stop at the halogen — and in any case Kr is not among the options.This is the same convention that makes the halogen, not the noble gas, the "smallest atom of the period" in every JEE answer key.
 period 4 :  K  Ca  Sc ... Zn  Ga  Ge  As  Se  Br  (Kr)
 r/pm      227 197            122 122 119 120 114   vdW 202

 (a) Na  -> period 3         reject
 (d) Rb  -> period 5         reject
 (b) K & Se : K right, but Se is not the smallest
 (c) K & Br : largest 227, smallest 114            <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick On "highest and lowest in period n" questions, always run the membership check first — examiners routinely slip in a neighbour from the period above or below, and it eliminates half the options for free. Then remember the working convention: largest = the group-1 metal, smallest = the halogen (noble gases are excluded because their radii are van der Waals values). Trap: option (b), which is right about the maximum and wrong about the minimum, catches anyone who stops checking after the first symbol matches.
Answer: (c) K & Br.
11(2026 Level-3) Consider the elements N, P, O, S, Cl and F. The number of valence electrons present in the elements with most and least metallic character from the above list is respectively. (a) 7 and 5 (b) 5 and 6 (c) 5 and 7 (d) 6 and 7Metallic character within a p-block set → valence-electron countRanking elements by metallic character using periodic position🟡 Similarc5 and 7 (most metallic P has 5 e⁻, least metallic F has 7 e⁻)
Concept This is a two-stage question: identify the extremes of metallic character, then count valence electrons — and the second stage is trivial once the first is right. Metallic character grows down a group and shrinks across a period, so within the given set the winner must be the element that is furthest left and furthest down, and the loser the element that is furthest right and furthest up. Laying the six elements on a mini-grid makes both extremes obvious in one glance.
Key relation valence electrons = group number − 10 for p-block groups 13–18: group 15 (N, P) ⇒ 5 · group 16 (O, S) ⇒ 6 · group 17 (F, Cl) ⇒ 7. metallic character ↑ down a group, ↓ across a period down a group the valence shell is further out and more easily lost; across a period Z* rises and the atom holds its electrons harder.
Solution
1. Grid the six elements: period 2 has N (15), O (16), F (17); period 3 has P (15), S (16), Cl (17).
2. Most metallic = furthest left and lowest = P (group 15, period 3). It beats N because it is one period lower, and it beats S and Cl because it is further left. Chemically, phosphorus is the only element here with a genuinely metallic-looking allotrope (black phosphorus, a semiconductor) and the least negative electron gain enthalpy of the set.
3. Least metallic = furthest right and highest = F (group 17, period 2) — the most electronegative element in the entire periodic table (EN 3.98). It beats Cl by being one period up and beats O and N by being further right.
4. Count valence electrons: P is 3s²3p³ ⇒ 5; F is 2s²2p⁵ ⇒ 7. Answer 5 and 7 = (c).
5. Eliminate: (a) is (c) with the two numbers swapped — the classic "respectively" trap; (b) picks O or S as the least metallic; (d) picks a group-16 element as the most metallic.
                  group 15   group 16   group 17
 period 2            N          O          F      <-- least metallic = F (7 e-)
 period 3            P          S          Cl
                     ^
                 most metallic = P (5 e-)

 direction of increasing metallic character: right -> left, up -> down
 most metallic P : 3s2 3p3  => 5 valence electrons
 least metallic F: 2s2 2p5  => 7 valence electrons
                                        5 and 7   <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Draw the two-row, three-column grid before answering — the extremes then sit at diagonally opposite corners (bottom-left = most metallic, top-right = least metallic), and you never have to weigh "further left" against "further down". Then read the group numbers off the corners. Trap: the word "respectively". Option (a) contains exactly the right pair of numbers in the wrong order and collects every candidate who solves the chemistry correctly but reads the sentence carelessly.
Answer: (c) 5 and 7.
12(JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan. Shift 2, Level-5) Given below are two statements: Statement (I): The first ionization energy of Pb is greater than that of Sn. Statement (II): The first ionization energy of Ge is greater than that of Si. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (b) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are falseGroup-14 ionisation energies — poor d/f shieldingStatement I: first ionization energy of Pb is greater than that of Sn — worked answer🟢 ExactbI true (Pb 716 > Sn 709); II false (Si 786 > Ge 762)
Concept Down group 14 the ionisation energy does not fall smoothly, because two opposing effects fight each other: increasing size (which lowers IE) versus increasingly ineffective shielding by the newly inserted 3d, 4d and 4f sub-shells (which raises Z* and hence IE). Where a d or f block has just been crossed, the shielding effect wins and the IE goes up instead of down. This happens twice in group 14 — at Si → Ge (after the 3d insertion) and at Sn → Pb (after the 4f + 5d insertion, the lanthanoid contraction). The examiner has written one of these two anomalies correctly and the other backwards.
Key relation IE₁ / kJ mol⁻¹: C 1086 · Si 786 · Ge 762 · Sn 709 · Pb 716 the sequence falls, falls, falls, then rises — Pb is the only element in the group whose IE₁ exceeds that of the element above it. · poor shielding: f < d < p < s, so 4f¹⁴5d¹⁰ before Pb leaves its 6s² electrons unusually exposed (the same effect that produces the inert pair effect and makes Pb²⁺ more stable than Pb⁴⁺).
Solution
1. Statement I: Pb vs Sn. Pb = [Xe]4f¹⁴5d¹⁰6s²6p². Between Sn and Pb the periodic table inserts fourteen 4f and ten 5d electrons, all of which shield very badly. The nuclear charge rises by 32 while the screening rises by much less, so Z* at the 6s²6p² shell increases. Data: Pb 715.6 > Sn 708.6 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ Statement I is TRUE.
2. Statement II: Ge vs Si. The 3d insertion before Ge also shields poorly, but here the size increase from n = 3 to n = 4 still dominates, so the IE falls: Si 786.5 > Ge 762 kJ mol⁻¹. The statement asserts the reverse ⇒ Statement II is FALSE.Note the effect is not absent — Ge's IE is only 24 kJ mol⁻¹ below Si's, whereas the C → Si drop is 300. The d-contraction has flattened the fall almost to nothing, which is exactly why Ge and Si are so chemically similar.
3. Statement I true + Statement II false ⇒ (b).
4. Eliminate: (c) requires Ge > Si, contradicting the data; (a) requires Pb < Sn, denying the only genuine anomaly in the group; (d) denies both and is the choice of anyone who applies the naive "IE always decreases down a group".
 group 14   IE1/kJ mol-1     step        cause of the step
 C            1086
 Si            786          -300         normal size increase
 Ge            762          - 24         3d insertion nearly cancels it
 Sn            709          - 53         normal
 Pb            716          +  7         4f + 5d insertion WINS  <-- anomaly

 Statement I  : Pb > Sn   716 > 709      TRUE
 Statement II : Ge > Si   762 < 786      FALSE
                                                    <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the single sentence "in group 14 only Pb breaks the downward trend" and you can answer every Si/Ge/Sn/Pb ionisation question instantly. The physical tag is the inert pair effect: the badly-shielded 6s² pair of Pb is both hard to ionise (high IE₁) and reluctant to bond (Pb²⁺ ≫ Pb⁴⁺ in stability) — one cause, two examinable consequences. Trap: statements I and II look symmetric, tempting you to mark them both true or both false; in fact only the heavier pair is anomalous.
Answer: (b) Statement I is true but Statement II is false.
13An element 'E' has the ionisation enthalpy value of 374 kJ mol⁻¹. 'E' reacts with elements A, B, C and D with electron gain enthalpy values of −328, −349, −325 and −295 kJ mol⁻¹, respectively. The correct order of the products EA, EB, EC and ED in terms of ionic character is: (JEE Main 2025, 29 Jan Shift 1, Level-5) (a) ED > EC > EB > EA (b) EA > EB > EC > ED (c) EB > EA > EC > ED (d) ED > EC > EA > EBIonic character from IE and ΔegHWhy the electron gain enthalpy order is Cl > F > Br > I🟡 SimilarcEB > EA > EC > ED
Concept An ionic bond is born from a complete electron transfer: the metal pays an ionisation enthalpy to lose the electron and the non-metal returns an electron gain enthalpy for accepting it. Because the metal 'E' is the same in all four compounds, the IE term (374 kJ mol⁻¹) is a constant and cancels out of every comparison. What is left is a single variable — the electron gain enthalpy of the partner. The more energy released when the partner accepts the electron (i.e. the more negative ΔegH), the more thermodynamically favourable and hence the more complete the transfer, and the more ionic the product.
Key relation Net energy of transfer ΔHtransfer = IE(E) + ΔegH(X). With IE fixed at +374, the ranking of ΔHtransfer is decided entirely by ΔegH; the most negative ΔegH gives the lowest (most favourable) ΔHtransfer. Ionic character then follows more negative ΔegH ⇒ easier E⁺X⁻ formation ⇒ higher ionic character.
Solution
1. Compute the net transfer energy for each partner, IE = +374 throughout:
EA: 374 + (−328) = +46 · EB: 374 + (−349) = +25 · EC: 374 + (−325) = +49 · ED: 374 + (−295) = +79 kJ mol⁻¹.
2. Lower net cost ⇒ more complete transfer ⇒ more ionic. Ranking from cheapest to dearest: EB (25) < EA (46) < EC (49) < ED (79).
3. Therefore ionic character runs EB > EA > EC > ED ⇒ option (c).
4. Identify the players for reassurance: IE = 374 kJ mol⁻¹ is caesium (the lowest IE of any stable element), and −328, −349, −325, −295 are the standard ΔegH values of F, Cl, Br, I respectively. So A = F, B = Cl, C = Br, D = I, and the question is really "rank CsF, CsCl, CsBr, CsI on this energetic criterion".Chlorine, not fluorine, has the most negative ΔegH — the tiny 2p shell of F makes the incoming electron feel strong inter-electronic repulsion. That single anomaly is the whole point of the question.
5. Eliminate (b) EA > EB > …: this is the answer you get if you assume F beats Cl, i.e. if you ignore the fluorine anomaly. (a) and (d) both start with ED, the least exothermic partner, and invert the criterion entirely.
 IE(E) = +374 kJ/mol   (constant -- cancels in every comparison)

 product  partner  eg-enthalpy   net = 374 + eg    rank
 EB       B (Cl)     -349          +25            1  most ionic
 EA       A (F)      -328          +46            2
 EC       C (Br)     -325          +49            3
 ED       D (I)      -295          +79            4  least ionic

 => EB > EA > EC > ED                        <-- answer (c)
⚠ Note On a pure electronegativity-difference criterion CsF would be the most ionic halide of caesium. This question deliberately defines ionic character through the given energetics, and on that basis Cl wins because its ΔegH is the most negative. Answer the question with the data it supplies.
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever one reactant is common to all products, delete its contribution and rank on the varying term alone — here you never actually need the number 374. Memorise the four halogen ΔegH values F −328, Cl −349, Br −325, I −295; almost every "electron gain enthalpy order" question in JEE is a rearrangement of exactly these four numbers. Trap: the setter supplies F's value first (as element A) so that the alphabetical order A, B, C, D looks like it should be the answer order.
Answer: (c) EB > EA > EC > ED.
14Match List-I with List-II. List-I: (A) Al³⁺ < Mg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻ (B) B < C < O < N (C) B < Al < Mg < K (D) Si < P < S < Cl. List-II: (i) Ionisation Enthalpy (ii) Metallic character (iii) Electronegativity (iv) Ionic radii. Choose the correct answer: (JEE Main 2025, 22 Jan Shift 1, Level-4) (a) (A)-(iv), (B)-(i), (C)-(ii), (D)-(iii) (b) (A)-(iv), (B)-(i), (C)-(iii), (D)-(ii) (c) (A)-(iii), (B)-(iv), (C)-(ii), (D)-(i) (d) (A)-(ii), (B)-(iii), (C)-(iv), (D)-(i)Matching four periodic trendsJEE Main 2025 periodic-table PYQs, including match-the-trend questions🟡 SimilaraA-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii
Concept Four different orderings are given and each is the fingerprint of exactly one property. The fastest route is not to test every pairing but to look for the tell-tale species in each row: charged ions can only be an ionic-radius list; a row that breaks the smooth left-to-right rise must be ionisation enthalpy (the N/O anomaly); a row that runs up a group and right-to-left across a period must be metallic character; and a smooth left-to-right rise among non-metals of one period is electronegativity.
Key relation isoelectronic ions: larger Z ⇒ smaller radius · IE rises across a period, but N > O because 2p³ is half-filled · metallic character rises down a group and falls across a period · electronegativity rises across a period. Only ionisation enthalpy and electron gain enthalpy show the group-15/16 inversion; atomic radius and electronegativity do not.
Solution
1. (A) Al³⁺ < Mg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻. All four are isoelectronic (10 electrons each). Nuclear charge falls 13 → 12 → 11 → 9, so the pull per electron weakens and the radius grows. This is an ionic radii list ⇒ (A)-(iv).
2. (B) B < C < O < N. The presence of an inversion — O placed below N even though O is to the right of N — is the signature of ionisation enthalpy: IE₁ = B 801, C 1086, O 1314, N 1402 kJ mol⁻¹. ⇒ (B)-(i).Removing an electron from O (2p⁴) relieves the pairing repulsion in one 2p orbital, while N (2p³) is a stable half-filled set, so IE(N) > IE(O).
3. (C) B < Al < Mg < K. B → Al goes down group 13, Al → Mg goes left in period 3, Mg → K goes down-and-left. Every step is in the direction of increasing electropositivity, so this is metallic character ⇒ (C)-(ii).
4. (D) Si < P < S < Cl. Four consecutive period-3 non-metals in strict left-to-right order, no inversion: electronegativity 1.90 < 2.19 < 2.58 < 3.16 on the Pauling scale ⇒ (D)-(iii).
5. Combining: A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii ⇒ option (a). Option (b) is the near-miss that swaps only C and D — it would claim B < Al < Mg < K is an electronegativity order, but electronegativity decreases B → Al → Mg → K, so (b) fails.
 list-I row              signature                        property
 (A) Al3+<Mg2+<Na+<F-    all 10-electron ions             ionic radii   (iv)
 (B) B < C < O < N       inversion at N/O (801,1086,       ionisation
                          1314,1402 kJ/mol)                enthalpy     (i)
 (C) B < Al < Mg < K     down a group AND leftwards       metallic
                                                           character    (ii)
 (D) Si < P < S < Cl     smooth L-to-R, period 3          electro-
                          (1.90,2.19,2.58,3.16)            negativity   (iii)

 => A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii                       <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the four rows for the one that contains ions and the one that contains an inversion — those two pin themselves down instantly, and in a four-way match that usually leaves only one surviving option. Trap: (b) differs from (a) only in the C/D assignment, betting that you will match the "smooth" rows carelessly; always confirm the direction of travel in the row you assign to metallic character.
Answer: (a) (A)-(iv), (B)-(i), (C)-(ii), (D)-(iii).
15The successive 5 ionisation energies of an element are 800, 2427, 3658, 25024 and 32824 kJ/mol, respectively. By using the above values predict the group in which the above element is present: (JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 2, Level-3) (a) Group 13 (b) Group 14 (c) Group 2 (d) Group 4Group from successive IE jumpThis exact data set — 800, 2427, 3658, 25024, 32824 kJ/mol🟢 ExactaGroup 13
Concept Successive ionisation energies always increase, because each removal leaves a more positively charged ion. But the increase is gentle while you are stripping valence electrons and violent the moment you break into the noble-gas core. So the position of the single huge jump tells you exactly how many valence electrons the atom had, and therefore its group. This is the cleanest experimental proof that electrons really are arranged in shells.
Key relation number of valence electrons = number of IE values before the big jump. For a p-block element, group number = 10 + (valence electrons); for the s-block, group number = valence electrons. A "big jump" means roughly a five- to ten-fold rise, not the routine 1.3–1.6× rise between successive valence removals.
Solution
1. Form the ratio of each IE to the one before it: 2427/800 = 3.0 · 3658/2427 = 1.5 · 25024/3658 = 6.8 · 32824/25024 = 1.3.
2. The one dramatic jump is between IE₃ and IE₄ (3658 → 25024, a factor of nearly 7, an absolute rise of over 21 000 kJ mol⁻¹). Everything before it is comparatively smooth.
3. Hence the atom parts with three electrons easily and the fourth only under extreme duress ⇒ 3 valence electrons ⇒ configuration ns²np¹ ⇒ group 13, option (a).These are in fact the ionisation energies of aluminium: IE₁ 577… the given 800 fits boron's neighbourhood, but either way the third-to-fourth jump fixes the group at 13.
4. Eliminate the rest by asking where their jump would have to be: group 14 needs the jump after the 4th IE (not seen — IE₄→IE₅ rises by only 1.3×); group 2 needs it after the 2nd (but IE₂→IE₃ rises by only 1.5×); group 4 is a d-block group whose first four IEs rise smoothly with no core break at all in this range.
 IE(n)  value (kJ/mol)   ratio to previous
 IE1      800              -
 IE2     2427             3.0      still valence
 IE3     3658             1.5      still valence
 IE4    25024             6.8      <== CORE BROKEN
 IE5    32824             1.3      core

 valence electrons removed easily = 3   ->  ns2 np1
 group = 10 + 3 = 13                          <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not read absolute numbers — read ratios. Count how many values sit before the ratio blows past ~4; that count is the valence-electron number, and adding 10 converts it to a p-block group number. Trap: option (b) Group 14 is chosen by students who mis-locate the jump by one place, and option (d) Group 4 by those who write the valence count as the group number without the +10 correction for the p-block.
Answer: (a) Group 13.
16Consider the following elements In, Tl, Al, Pb, Sn and Ge. The most stable oxidation states of elements with highest and lowest first ionisation enthalpies, respectively, are: (JEE Main 2025, 28 Jan Shift 1, Level-3) (a) +4 and +1 (b) +1 and +4 (c) +4 and +3 (d) +2 and +3IE₁ ranking + inert pair effectInert pair effect and the oxidation states of group 13 and 14 elements🟡 Similarc+4 (Ge, highest IE₁) and +3 (In, lowest IE₁)
Concept This is a two-stage question. Stage one is a size/IE ranking among six heavy p-block metals; stage two is the inert pair effect, which says that as you descend groups 13 and 14 the ns² pair becomes progressively reluctant to take part in bonding, so the lower oxidation state (+1 for group 13, +2 for group 14) grows more stable at the expense of the group state (+3 and +4). The two stages are linked: the element with the largest IE₁ is the lightest one present, which is also the one that still happily uses its ns² pair.
Key relation IE₁ (kJ mol⁻¹): In 558 · Tl 589 · Al 577 · Pb 715 · Sn 708 · Ge 762. The values are irregular because of poor d- and f-shielding: Tl > In and Pb > Sn even though they lie lower in their groups. Ge is nevertheless the largest and In the smallest of the six. Inert pair effect: stability of lower state ↑ down the group.
Solution
1. Rank the six first ionisation enthalpies: Ge 762 > Pb 715 > Sn 708 > Tl 589 > Al 577 > In 558. So highest = Ge, lowest = In.Ge sits highest in group 14 among those listed (period 4) and follows the 3d block, so it is small with a high Z*. In is a large period-5 group-13 atom and gives up its 5p electron most easily.
2. Most stable oxidation state of Ge: germanium is early in group 14, the inert pair effect has barely begun, and the 4s² pair is still fully available ⇒ +4 (GeO₂, GeCl₄ are the ordinary compounds; Ge(II) is a reducing species).
3. Most stable oxidation state of In: indium is in group 13 where the group state is +3. The inert pair effect is significant only at thallium, so indium is still predominantly +3 (In₂O₃, InCl₃); In(I) exists but is the minor, easily oxidised state.
4. Answer pair = +4 and +3 ⇒ option (c).
5. Eliminate: (a) "+4 and +1" would be right only if the lowest-IE element were Tl, where the inert pair really does make +1 the stable state. (b) reverses the two requested elements. (d) "+2 and +3" would need Pb or Sn as the highest-IE element, but both are below Ge.
 element  group  period  IE1 (kJ/mol)
 Ge        14      4       762    <-- HIGHEST
 Pb        14      6       715
 Sn        14      5       708
 Tl        13      6       589
 Al        13      3       577
 In        13      5       558    <-- LOWEST

 Ge : group 14, inert pair not yet operative  ->  most stable = +4
 In : group 13, inert pair only strong at Tl  ->  most stable = +3

 => +4 and +3                                     <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the two inert-pair "switch points": in group 13 the lower state takes over only at Tl (Tl⁺ > Tl³⁺), and in group 14 only at Pb (Pb²⁺ > Pb⁴⁺). Everything above those two still prefers the group oxidation state. Trap: the list mixes both groups on purpose, so a student who assumes "heavier ⇒ lower IE" picks Tl as the lowest-IE element and lands on (a) — but In, not Tl, has the smallest IE₁ because of the d-block contraction acting on thallium.
Answer: (c) +4 and +3.
17The incorrect decreasing order of atomic radii is: (JEE Main 2025, 28 Jan Shift 1, Level-2) (a) Si > P > Cl > F (b) Be > Mg > Al > Si (c) Al > B > N > F (d) Mg > Al > C > OAtomic radius trend — spotting the false orderPeriodic trends in atomic radius, across periods and down groups🟡 SimilarbBe > Mg > Al > Si is wrong (Mg > Be)
Concept Atomic radius is governed by two competing counters: the principal quantum number of the outermost shell (bigger n ⇒ bigger atom, dominant when you move down a group) and the effective nuclear charge (bigger Z* ⇒ smaller atom, dominant when you move across a period). Unlike ionisation enthalpy, atomic radius has no anomalies in the main-group s and p blocks — the trend is perfectly monotonic. That makes "spot the wrong order" questions purely mechanical: convert every element to (group, period) and check that each step either moves right in a period or up a group.
Key relation radius increases down a group (n ↑) · radius decreases across a period (Z* ↑, same n). Reference values (pm): Be 111 · Mg 160 · Al 143 · Si 118 · P 110 · Cl 99 · F 72 · B 85 · N 75 · C 77 · O 73.
Solution
1. (a) Si > P > Cl > F. Si → P → Cl all move rightwards along period 3, so each is smaller than the last; Cl → F moves up group 17, so F is smaller still. 118 > 110 > 99 > 72 ✓ correct.
2. (b) Be > Mg > Al > Si. The very first step is wrong: Be is in period 2 and Mg directly below it in period 3, so Mg > Be (160 > 111). The claimed order is therefore false ⇒ this is the answer. (The remaining steps Mg > Al > Si are fine.)Going down group 2 adds a whole new shell; the extra proton count cannot compensate for a jump in n. Down-a-group always wins over across-a-period.
3. (c) Al > B > N > F. Al → B moves up group 13 (smaller), B → N → F move rightwards along period 2 (smaller each time). 143 > 85 > 75 > 72 ✓ correct.
4. (d) Mg > Al > C > O. Mg → Al is rightwards in period 3; Al → C moves up a period and rightwards, so C is much smaller; C → O is rightwards in period 2. 160 > 143 > 77 > 73 ✓ correct.
5. Only (b) contains a step that runs against the trend, so the incorrect order is (b).
 option  chain                radii (pm)                  verdict
 (a)  Si > P > Cl > F     118 > 110 > 99 > 72          correct
 (b)  Be > Mg > Al > Si  111 < 160 ...                 WRONG at step 1
 (c)  Al > B > N > F     143 > 85 > 75 > 72            correct
 (d)  Mg > Al > C > O    160 > 143 > 77 > 73           correct

 Be (period 2) vs Mg (period 3, same group) : Mg is LARGER
 => the incorrect order is (b)                    <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Check the first pair of every option before doing any other work — three of these four options fail or pass on step one alone, and (b) collapses immediately. Remember the one-line rule "n beats Z*": any comparison that changes the period is settled by the period, whatever the groups are doing. Trap: options (a) and (c) both end with F, tempting you to hunt for an anomaly at fluorine; there is none — atomic radius is anomaly-free in the main group.
Answer: (b) Be > Mg > Al > Si — incorrect, because Mg is larger than Be.
18Which of the following electronegativity order is incorrect? (JEE Main 2025, 22 Jan Shift 1, Level-1) (a) Mg < Be < B < N (b) S < Cl < O < F (c) Al < Si < C < N (d) Al < Mg < B < NElectronegativity — spotting the false orderElectronegativity trends across periods and down groups🟡 SimilardAl < Mg < B < N is wrong (Al > Mg)
Concept Electronegativity is the pull an atom exerts on a shared pair of electrons. It rises across a period (Z* increases while the shell stays the same) and falls down a group (the bonding pair sits further from the nucleus and behind more shielding). Like atomic radius, and unlike ionisation enthalpy, the Pauling electronegativity scale has no half-filled-shell anomaly in the main group — so once again the test is purely directional, and any option that increases while moving left in a period is dead on the spot.
Key relation EN increases → across a period · EN decreases ↓ down a group. Pauling values: Mg 1.31 · Al 1.61 · Be 1.57 · B 2.04 · Si 1.90 · C 2.55 · N 3.04 · S 2.58 · Cl 3.16 · O 3.44 · F 3.98. Note Be (1.57) < Al (1.61) < B (2.04): the diagonal Be/Al pair have nearly equal electronegativity, which is exactly why they show a diagonal relationship.
Solution
1. (a) Mg < Be < B < N. Mg → Be goes up group 2 (EN rises), Be → B → N goes rightwards along period 2 (EN rises). 1.31 < 1.57 < 2.04 < 3.04 ✓ correct.
2. (b) S < Cl < O < F. S → Cl is rightwards in period 3; Cl → O jumps up to period 2 (much more electronegative); O → F is rightwards in period 2. 2.58 < 3.16 < 3.44 < 3.98 ✓ correct.
3. (c) Al < Si < C < N. Al → Si rightwards in period 3; Si → C up group 14; C → N rightwards in period 2. 1.61 < 1.90 < 2.55 < 3.04 ✓ correct.
4. (d) Al < Mg < B < N. The first step demands Mg to be more electronegative than Al, but Mg (1.31) sits to the left of Al (1.61) in the same period 3, so Al > Mg. The order is false ⇒ answer (d). (Its later steps Mg < B < N happen to be true, which is what makes the option look plausible.)
5. Sanity check on the whole set: F is the most electronegative element (3.98) and appears last wherever it occurs; every option that keeps F or N terminal is consistent on that count, so the decision genuinely rests on the first pair in each chain.
 element  Mg    Al    Be    B     Si    C     N     S     Cl    O     F
 EN      1.31  1.61  1.57  2.04  1.90  2.55  3.04  2.58  3.16  3.44  3.98

 option  chain                 numbers                       verdict
 (a)  Mg < Be < B < N     1.31 < 1.57 < 2.04 < 3.04     correct
 (b)  S < Cl < O < F      2.58 < 3.16 < 3.44 < 3.98     correct
 (c)  Al < Si < C < N     1.61 < 1.90 < 2.55 < 3.04     correct
 (d)  Al < Mg < B < N     1.61 > 1.31 ...                WRONG at step 1

 => the incorrect order is (d)                    <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Only three Pauling numbers really need memorising — F 4.0, O 3.5, N 3.0 — and then the rule "each step left or down subtracts roughly 0.3–0.5". That is enough resolution for every JEE ordering question. Trap: options (a) and (d) both contain Mg, B and N; they differ only in whether Be or Al is the fourth member, and Be (1.57) < Al (1.61) is such a narrow gap that guessing on magnitude fails — decide it by direction of travel instead, which is unambiguous.
Answer: (d) Al < Mg < B < N — incorrect, since aluminium is more electronegative than magnesium.
19Given below are two statements: Statement (I): An element in the extreme left of the periodic table forms acidic oxides. Statement (II): Acid is formed during the reaction between water and oxide of a reactive element present in the extreme right of the periodic table. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2025, 22 Jan Shift 2, Level-1) (a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (d) Statement I is false but Statement II is trueAcidic vs basic character of oxidesThis exact statement pair worked out — oxides at the two ends of the table🟢 ExactdStatement I false, Statement II true
Concept The acid–base character of an oxide is decided by the nature of the E–O bond. A strongly electropositive element on the far left gives up electrons completely, so the oxide is ionic and contains discrete O²⁻ ions; water attacks it to liberate OH⁻, making it basic. A strongly electronegative element on the far right shares electrons, so the oxide is covalent; the highly polarised E=O framework pulls electron density away from the O–H bond formed with water and releases H⁺, making it acidic. Acidic character therefore increases from left to right across a period and decreases down a group, with amphoteric oxides (Al₂O₃, ZnO, BeO) forming the diagonal boundary.
Key relation metal oxide + H₂O → base, e.g. Na₂O + H₂O → 2NaOH. non-metal oxide + H₂O → acid, e.g. Cl₂O₇ + H₂O → 2HClO₄ and SO₃ + H₂O → H₂SO₄. "Extreme right" in this context means the halogens (and the group-16 non-metals); the noble gases are excluded because they are essentially unreactive — the question says "a reactive element present in the extreme right", which is precisely how it sidesteps group 18.
Solution
1. Statement I. The extreme left of the periodic table is group 1 — Li, Na, K, Rb, Cs. Their oxides (Na₂O, K₂O) are the most strongly basic oxides known; dissolved in water they give the strong alkalis NaOH and KOH. The statement claims they are acidic, so Statement I is false.
2. Statement II. The extreme right (excluding the inert group 18) holds the halogens. Their oxides react with water to give oxo-acids: Cl₂O₇ + H₂O → 2HClO₄ (perchloric acid), Cl₂O + H₂O → 2HOCl. Moving one column left, SO₃ + H₂O → H₂SO₄ and N₂O₅ + H₂O → 2HNO₃. So Statement II is true.
3. I false, II true ⇒ option (d).
4. Eliminate the others: (a) inverts both verdicts; (b) would require alkali-metal oxides to be acidic; (c) would require halogen oxides not to give acids with water, contradicting the standard reactions above.A quick physical check on Statement I: aqueous Na₂O turns red litmus blue and has pH ≈ 13–14 — unmistakably basic.
 position in table   example oxide   + H2O gives        character
 extreme LEFT        Na2O            NaOH  (pH ~ 14)    BASIC
 (group 1, 2)        CaO             Ca(OH)2            basic

 middle/diagonal     Al2O3           reacts both ways   amphoteric

 extreme RIGHT       SO3             H2SO4              ACIDIC
 (groups 16, 17)     Cl2O7           HClO4              acidic

 Statement I  : "extreme left forms acidic oxides"   -> FALSE
 Statement II : "extreme right oxide + water = acid" -> TRUE
 => I false, II true                              <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Compress the whole trend into one sentence: "metals give bases, non-metals give acids, and the metalloid diagonal gives amphoterics." For assertion–reason pairs, evaluate each statement in complete isolation and only then combine — students lose this question by letting the plausible Statement II colour their reading of Statement I. Trap: the phrase "extreme right" makes many candidates think of noble gases and conclude "no oxides, so false"; the qualifier "reactive" is in the statement precisely to exclude them.
Answer: (d) Statement I is false but Statement II is true.
20The element that does not belong to the same period of the remaining elements (modern periodic table) is: (JEE Main 2025, 23 Jan Shift 1, Level-1) (a) Iridium (b) Platinum (c) Osmium (d) PalladiumLocating the period of a d-block elementFinding period, block and group of an element from its atomic number alone🟡 SimilardPalladium (period 5; the others are period 6)
Concept The period number of any element is simply the highest principal quantum number occupied in its ground-state configuration — equivalently, the value of n for its outermost s subshell. For a d-block element the d electrons carry the quantum number (n−1), one less than the period, which is the single fact this question tests. The four names given all belong to the platinum-group metals, so they look interchangeable; only their atomic numbers separate them.
Key relation Period boundaries by atomic number: period 4 = 19–36 · period 5 = 37–54 · period 6 = 55–86. So any element with Z between 37 and 54 is in period 5; between 55 and 86, period 6. Just placing Z on this ruler answers the question. Atomic numbers here: Pd 46 · Os 76 · Ir 77 · Pt 78.
Solution
1. Write the atomic numbers: Iridium Ir = 77, Platinum Pt = 78, Osmium Os = 76, Palladium Pd = 46.
2. Place them on the period ruler. 76, 77 and 78 all lie in the range 55–86 ⇒ period 6. 46 lies in the range 37–54 ⇒ period 5.
3. Hence palladium is the odd one out ⇒ option (d).
4. Confirm by configuration: Os [Xe]4f¹⁴5d⁶6s², Ir [Xe]4f¹⁴5d⁷6s², Pt [Xe]4f¹⁴5d⁹6s¹ — all have n = 6 as their highest shell. Pd is [Kr]4d¹⁰, highest n = 5 (and famously has no 5s electron at all, the only element in the table whose outermost s subshell is empty).Even with the empty 5s, Pd's highest occupied principal quantum number is 5, so it is unambiguously a period-5, group-10 element sitting directly above Pt.
5. Structural cross-check: Os, Ir, Pt are three consecutive members of the 5d series (groups 8, 9, 10 of period 6); Pd is the group-10 member of the 4d series. Three consecutive neighbours plus one from the row above — the pattern makes the odd one obvious once the numbers are written down.
 element      symbol   Z    highest n   period
 Osmium        Os      76      6          6
 Iridium       Ir      77      6          6
 Platinum      Pt      78      6          6
 Palladium     Pd      46      5          5      <-- odd one out

 period 5 : Z = 37 to 54       period 6 : Z = 55 to 86
 => answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the six period-ending atomic numbers 2, 10, 18, 36, 54, 86. Any "which element is in a different period" question is then answered by bracketing each Z between two consecutive milestones — no configurations needed. Trap: all four options are precious platinum-group metals, and Pd is the one most often assumed to sit with Pt because they are chemically alike and share group 10; being in the same group is not being in the same period.
Answer: (d) Palladium — Z = 46, period 5, while Os, Ir and Pt are all period 6.
21Which of the following statements are NOT true about the periodic table? A. The properties of elements are function of atomic weights. B. The properties of elements are function of atomic numbers. C. Elements having similar outer electronic configurations are arranged in same period. D. An element's location reflects the quantum numbers of the last filled orbital. E. The number of elements in a period is same as the number of atomic orbitals available in energy level that is being filled. Choose the correct answer: (JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 1, Level-1) (a) A, C and E only (b) A and E only (c) B, C and E only (d) D and E onlyModern periodic law and period structureHistorical development of the periodic table — Mendeleev to Moseley🟡 SimilaraA, C and E only
Concept Three separate ideas are being probed. First, the modern periodic law: after Moseley's 1913 X-ray work the organising variable changed from atomic weight to atomic number, which is why the Ar/K and Te/I "anomalies" of Mendeleev's table disappeared. Second, the group vs period distinction: identical outer configurations define a group, while a common highest principal quantum number defines a period. Third, the counting rule for period length: each orbital holds two electrons, so a period contains twice as many elements as there are orbitals being filled, not the same number.
Key relation Modern periodic law: properties are a periodic function of atomic number, not atomic weight. Period lengths: 2, 8, 8, 18, 18, 32 from orbital counts 1, 4, 4, 9, 9, 16. Period 2 fills 2s + 2p = 1 + 3 = 4 orbitals and holds 8 elements; period 4 fills 4s + 3d + 4p = 1 + 5 + 3 = 9 orbitals and holds 18. In every case elements = 2 × orbitals.
Solution
1. A — NOT true. This is Mendeleev's old law. The modern table is ordered by atomic number; had weight remained the criterion, Ar (39.9) would have to precede K (39.1) in the wrong group. Include A.
2. B — true. This is the modern periodic law verbatim, established by Moseley's relation √ν ∝ (Z − b). Exclude B.
3. C — NOT true. Elements with similar outer electronic configurations occupy the same group (vertical column), not the same period. Na 3s¹ and K 4s¹ share a configuration pattern but sit in periods 3 and 4. Include C.
4. D — true. The last filled orbital fixes n (⇒ period), ℓ (⇒ block) and the electron count within that subshell (⇒ group). Exclude D.
5. E — NOT true. Period 1 fills one orbital (1s) but holds 2 elements; period 2 fills four orbitals (2s, 2p×3) but holds 8. The correct statement is "the number of elements in a period is twice the number of orbitals available". Include E.The factor of two is the Pauli exclusion principle: each orbital accommodates two electrons of opposite spin.
6. Not-true set = {A, C, E} ⇒ option (a). Option (c) wrongly indicts B (the modern law itself) and (d) wrongly indicts D while missing A and C entirely; (b) is a subset that overlooks C.
 stmt  claim                                        true?   include?
 A     properties are function of atomic WEIGHT     NO      yes
 B     properties are function of atomic NUMBER     YES     no
 C     similar outer config -> same PERIOD          NO      yes
       (it is the same GROUP)
 D     location reflects quantum numbers of the
       last filled orbital                          YES     no
 E     no. of elements in a period = no. of         NO      yes
       orbitals being filled  (it is 2 x orbitals)

 period   orbitals filled            elements
   1      1s            = 1              2
   2      2s,2p         = 4              8
   4      4s,3d,4p      = 9             18
   6      6s,4f,5d,6p   = 16            32       elements = 2 x orbitals

 NOT-true = A, C, E                               <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick In "which are NOT true" questions, mark each statement T/F in the margin first and only afterwards look at the options — reading the options early biases you toward whichever combination appears most often. Keep the period-length table 2, 8, 8, 18, 18, 32 and its orbital counts 1, 4, 4, 9, 9, 16 memorised as a pair; the factor of 2 between them is tested repeatedly. Trap: statements A and B are deliberately placed adjacent and differ by one word (weights/numbers), so a skimmer will tick both or neither.
Answer: (a) A, C and E only.
22The large difference between the melting and boiling points of oxygen and sulphur may be explained on the basis of: (JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 1, Level-1) (a) Atomicity (b) Electron gain enthalpy (c) Electronegativity (d) Atomic sizeWhy O₂ and S₈ differ so sharplyThis exact question solved — melting/boiling points of oxygen versus sulphur🟢 ExactaAtomicity (O₂ diatomic vs S₈ octatomic)
Concept Melting and boiling do not break covalent bonds — they overcome the intermolecular forces holding separate molecules together. For non-polar elements those forces are London dispersion forces, whose strength scales with the size and polarisability of the whole molecular unit. Oxygen and sulphur are in the same group, yet oxygen exists as a small diatomic O₂ molecule while sulphur exists as a puckered crown-shaped S₈ ring roughly four times heavier and vastly more polarisable. The difference in the number of atoms per molecule — the atomicity — is therefore the controlling factor.
Key relation London dispersion force ∝ polarisability ∝ number of electrons per molecule. O₂ carries 16 electrons per molecule; S₈ carries 128. An eightfold heavier, far more polarisable unit needs far more thermal energy to separate, which is why S₈ is a solid at room temperature (m.p. 388 K, b.p. 718 K) while O₂ is a gas (m.p. 54 K, b.p. 90 K). Physical data: O₂: 54 K / 90 K · S₈: 388 K / 718 K.
Solution
1. Note the molecular formulae, not just the element symbols: oxygen is O₂ (atomicity 2); the stable allotrope of sulphur is S₈ (atomicity 8), a crown-shaped ring.Oxygen forms pπ–pπ double bonds readily because its 2p orbitals are small and overlap sideways well; sulphur's diffuse 3p orbitals overlap poorly sideways, so sulphur prefers eight single σ bonds in a ring rather than S=S. This is the deeper reason the atomicities differ.
2. Melting/boiling requires only that these molecular units be pulled apart, so the energy needed tracks the strength of the dispersion forces between them.
3. S₈ has 128 electrons spread over a large ring, giving a far larger instantaneous dipole and hence far stronger dispersion attraction than the small 16-electron O₂. Result: S₈ melts at 388 K and boils at 718 K, versus 54 K and 90 K for O₂ — a gap of hundreds of kelvin.
4. The property responsible is therefore atomicity ⇒ option (a).
5. Eliminate: (b) electron gain enthalpy describes the energy of adding an electron to a gaseous atom — irrelevant to a phase change of a neutral element. (c) electronegativity governs bond polarity; both O₂ and S₈ are homonuclear and therefore perfectly non-polar, so it cannot distinguish them. (d) atomic size does matter, but only through its effect on polarisability — and a mere size difference between two like-structured molecules could never produce a 600 K gap; the change in molecular structure from diatomic to octatomic is what does.
 element   formula   atomicity   e- per molecule   m.p. (K)   b.p. (K)
 oxygen      O2          2             16             54         90
 sulphur     S8          8            128            388        718

 phase change breaks only London forces, and
 London force grows with molecular size / polarisability
 O2 (small, 2 atoms)  ->  weak forces  ->  gas at RT
 S8 (large, 8 atoms)  ->  strong forces -> solid at RT

 controlling property = ATOMICITY               <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a question compares physical constants (m.p., b.p., density, volatility) of two elements in the same group, ask "are their molecular units the same shape?" before reaching for any periodic trend. The classic pairs are O₂ vs S₈ and N₂ vs P₄ — both governed by atomicity, both explained by the failure of pπ–pπ bonding below period 2. Trap: options (c) and (d) are genuine periodic trends and feel like the "chemistry" answer, but neither survives the fact that both species are non-polar and in the same group.
Answer: (a) Atomicity — O₂ is diatomic while sulphur exists as octatomic S₈.
23Given below are two statements: Statement (I): According to the Law of Octaves, the elements were arranged in the increasing order of their atomic number. Statement (II): Meyer observed a periodically repeated pattern upon plotting physical properties of certain elements against their respective atomic numbers. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2025, 28 Jan Shift 2, Level-1) (a) Both Statement (I) and Statement (II) are false (b) Both Statement (I) and Statement (II) are true (c) Statement (I) is false but Statement (II) is true (d) Statement (I) is true but Statement (II) is falseNewlands' octaves and Meyer's curveDöbereiner's triads and Newlands' law of octaves explained🟡 SimilarcStatement I false, Statement II true
Concept Every pre-Mendeleev classification used atomic weight, for the simple reason that the atomic number had not been discovered — Moseley's X-ray experiments that defined Z came in 1913, half a century after Newlands (1865) and Meyer (1864–70). The chronology alone settles Statement I. Meyer's contribution was different in kind from Newlands': instead of listing elements he plotted a physical property (atomic volume) and found a repeating wave of peaks at the alkali metals — the first graphical demonstration of periodicity, and the reason his name survives beside Mendeleev's.
Key relation Newlands' Law of Octaves (1865): arrange by increasing atomic weight; every eighth element repeats the properties of the first — valid only up to calcium. Meyer's atomic-volume curve: atomic volume = atomic mass ÷ density, plotted against the ordering variable, gives sharp maxima at Li, Na, K, Rb, Cs. The peaks fall exactly on the alkali metals because they are the largest, least dense atoms of each row — the same periodicity Mendeleev expressed as a table.
Solution
1. Statement I. Newlands ordered the elements by increasing atomic weight, not atomic number. Atomic number did not exist as a concept in 1865. Statement I is therefore false.
2. Statement II. Meyer did exactly what the statement describes in substance: he plotted physical properties (atomic volume, melting point, boiling point) of the elements and observed a periodically repeating pattern of maxima and minima. Taken as the description of Meyer's contribution — that a graph of physical properties against the elements' ordering repeats periodically — Statement II is true.
3. I false, II true ⇒ option (c).
4. Eliminate: (d) would require Newlands to have used atomic number; (b) would make Statement I true for the same reason; (a) would deny Meyer's curve altogether, which is a documented and famous result.
 worker      year    ordering variable used    contribution
 Doebereiner 1829    atomic WEIGHT             triads (middle = mean)
 Newlands    1865    atomic WEIGHT             law of octaves (upto Ca)
 Meyer       1864-70 atomic WEIGHT             atomic-volume curve:
                                               periodic maxima at Li,
                                               Na, K, Rb, Cs
 Mendeleev   1869    atomic WEIGHT             periodic table + gaps
 Moseley     1913    atomic NUMBER             modern periodic law

 Statement I  : octaves used atomic NUMBER        -> FALSE
 Statement II : Meyer saw periodic repetition on
                plotting physical properties      -> TRUE
 => answer (c)
⚠ Note Strictly, Meyer plotted atomic volume against atomic weight, since atomic number was still unknown in 1870; the statement's phrase "atomic numbers" is a loose modernisation. The examiner's intent (and the printed key) is that Statement II correctly captures Meyer's discovery of a periodically repeated pattern, while Statement I misattributes the atomic-number criterion to Newlands. Marked as keyed, (c).
⚡ JEE Trick Anchor everything to one date: 1913, Moseley. Any claim that a pre-1913 worker used atomic number is automatically false — that single filter answers most history-of-the-periodic-table questions without recalling any other detail. Trap: Statement I mentions "Law of Octaves" and "increasing order", both of which are genuinely Newlands' words; only the final two words are swapped, which is exactly the kind of one-word substitution that survives a fast read.
Answer: (c) Statement (I) is false but Statement (II) is true.
24Given below are two statements: Statement (I): The radii of isoelectronic species increases in the order Mg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻ < O²⁻. Statement (II): The magnitude of electron gain enthalpy of halogen decreases in the order Cl > F > Br > I. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2025, 29 Jan Shift 1, Level-1) (a) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct (b) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are correctIsoelectronic radii + halogen ΔegHIncreasing order of ionic radii among isoelectronic species🟡 SimilardBoth statements are correct
Concept Two independent standards are being checked. Isoelectronic species all carry the same number of electrons, so the electron–electron repulsion and the shielding are essentially identical; the only variable left is the nuclear charge Z. More protons pulling on the same ten electrons means a tighter, smaller ion — so radius falls as Z rises, i.e. radius rises as Z falls.
