The second statement tests the single most famous anomaly of the periodic table: fluorine, despite being the most electronegative element, releases less energy than chlorine when it accepts an electron, because its 2p shell is so compact that the incoming electron suffers severe repulsion from the electrons already there.
Key relation For isoelectronic ions: r ∝ 1/(Z/e⁻ ratio), so larger Z ⇒ smaller radius. Ionic radii (pm): Mg²⁺ 72 · Na⁺ 95 · F⁻ 136 · O²⁻ 140. Halogen ΔegH (kJ mol⁻¹): F −328 · Cl −349 · Br −325 · I −295. Magnitudes: Cl 349 > F 328 > Br 325 > I 295 — the order Statement II claims.
Solution
1. Statement I. All four species have 10 electrons (Mg²⁺ 12−2, Na⁺ 11−1, F⁻ 9+1, O²⁻ 8+2). Nuclear charges are 12, 11, 9, 8 respectively — falling in exactly that sequence.
2. Falling Z on a fixed electron count means a weaker per-electron pull and a larger ion: 72 < 95 < 136 < 140 pm. The claimed order Mg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻ < O²⁻ matches. Statement I is correct.A useful shorthand: among isoelectronic species, cations are always smaller than the neutral parent and anions always larger, and within each set the higher the positive charge the smaller the ion.
3. Statement II. Compare the magnitudes: |Cl| 349 > |F| 328 > |Br| 325 > |I| 295, so the order Cl > F > Br > I holds. Statement II is correct.
4. The physical reason F falls below Cl: the incoming electron enters the small, compact 2p subshell where inter-electronic repulsion is unusually high, cancelling much of the energy released. Chlorine's larger 3p subshell accommodates the extra electron with far less repulsion. Below chlorine the ordinary size trend resumes, giving Br and then I progressively smaller magnitudes.
5. Both statements correct ⇒ option (d).
 STATEMENT I  -- all species have 10 electrons
 species    Z    e-    radius (pm)
 Mg2+      12    10        72     smallest (highest Z)
 Na+       11    10        95
 F-         9    10       136
 O2-        8    10       140     largest  (lowest Z)
 => Mg2+ < Na+ < F- < O2-                      CORRECT

 STATEMENT II -- electron gain enthalpy, kJ/mol
 F  -328     Cl -349     Br -325     I  -295
 magnitudes: 349 (Cl) > 328 (F) > 325 (Br) > 295 (I)
 => Cl > F > Br > I                            CORRECT

 both correct                                  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick For isoelectronic sets, rank by charge and never by position in the table: the sequence always runs highest cation charge → lower cation charge → neutral → anion → higher anion charge, i.e. 3+ < 2+ < 1+ < 0 < 1− < 2−. For ΔegH, remember the exception in one line — "Cl beats F, and O is beaten by S" — the two small period-2 elements both lose to their period-3 partners. Trap: Statement II is written in terms of "magnitude", so a student who compares the raw signed values (−349 < −328 < −325 < −295) may declare the order reversed and pick (b); read the word "magnitude" carefully.
Answer: (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct.
25The type of oxide formed by the element among Li, Na, Be, Mg, B and Al that has the least atomic radius is : (JEE Main 2025, 29 Jan Shift 2, Level-1) (a) A2O (b) A2O3 (c) AO2 (d) AOAtomic radius trend → oxide stoichiometryThis exact JEE 2025 question solved: smallest radius among Li, Na, Be, Mg, B, Al and its oxide🟢 ExactbA2O3
Concept This is a two-stage question wearing a single-stage disguise. Stage one is pure periodicity — pick the smallest atom out of a set that spans two periods and three groups. Stage two is valency — once the element is named, its oxide formula follows from the group-valence rule and you simply have to recognise which of the four generic templates (A2O, A2O3, AO2, AO) it fits. The six elements Li, Be, B (period 2) and Na, Mg, Al (period 3) are deliberately chosen so that they form a neat 2×3 rectangle of the s- and p-blocks: groups 1, 2 and 13.
Key relation radius decreases → across a period (Zeff rises, same shell n) · radius increases ↓ down a group (new shell added, n rises) both effects push in the same direction here: the smallest atom must be the one furthest RIGHT in the HIGHEST period, i.e. the top-right corner of the rectangle. Oxide stoichiometry: valence of group 13 element = 3, of O = 2 ⇒ criss-cross ⇒ A2O3.
Solution
1. Sort the six elements into their grid. Period 2: Li (gr 1), Be (gr 2), B (gr 13). Period 3: Na (gr 1), Mg (gr 2), Al (gr 13).
2. Within period 2, radius falls left→right: Li 152 > Be 111 > B 88 pm. Within period 3: Na 186 > Mg 160 > Al 143 pm.Across a period the added electron enters the same shell while Z rises by one each step, so the poorly-shielded valence electrons are pulled in harder; the shell does not change, so the atom simply contracts.
3. Every period-2 member is smaller than the period-3 member directly below it (Li < Na, Be < Mg, B < Al), so the winner must come from period 2, and inside period 2 the smallest is the right-most, boron (88 pm).
4. Boron is in group 13 → valence 3. Oxygen is divalent. Criss-crossing the valencies gives B2O3 (boron trioxide, an acidic oxide that dissolves in water to give H3BO3). In the generic notation of the options this is A2O3.
5. Eliminate the rest: A2O would need a group-1 metal (Li2O, Na2O); AO would need a group-2 metal (BeO, MgO); AO2 would need a tetravalent element (CO2, SiO2) — none of which is in the list at all.
 element   group   period   atomic radius (pm)
 Na          1       3          186   largest
 Li          1       2          152
 Mg          2       3          160
 Be          2       2          111
 Al         13       3          143
 B          13       2           88   SMALLEST  <-- pick this

 B is group 13  =>  valence 3
 B(III) + O(II)  --criss-cross-->  B2O3   i.e.  A2O3   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick For any "smallest/largest atom in this mixed list" question, do not memorise radii — just plot the elements on a mental grid and travel to the corner: smallest = top-right, largest = bottom-left. Here the top-right corner is B, and you are done in three seconds. The trap the paper-setter builds is that Al is written last in the list "Li, Na, Be, Mg, B and Al", so a hurried student reads it as "furthest right = smallest" and answers A2O3 for the wrong reason (right letter, wrong element) — or worse, notices Al is in period 3 and switches to AO2. The other trap is confusing "least atomic radius" with "least atomic number" (that would be Li → A2O, option a).
Answer: (b) A2O3 — boron has the least atomic radius and forms B2O3.
26Match List I with List II. List I (Elements): (A) Cl, S · (B) Ge, As · (C) Fr, Ra · (D) F, O. List II (Properties in their respective groups): (i) Elements with highest electronegativity · (ii) Elements with largest atomic size · (iii) Elements which show properties of both metals and non-metal · (iv) Elements with highest negative electron gain enthalpy. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2025, 8 April Shift 1, Level-1) (a) A-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii (b) A-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv (c) A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i (d) A-ii, B-iii, C-iv, D-iGroup extremes: ΔegH, metalloids, size, ENMetals, non-metals and metalloids — where Ge and As sit on the staircase🟡 SimilarcA-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i
Concept Read the List-II heading with care: every property is qualified by "in their respective groups". That single phrase converts what looks like a whole-table comparison into four separate, easy, one-group comparisons — and it is exactly what makes the Cl/S versus F/O pair distinguishable. F and O are the champions of electronegativity in groups 17 and 16; Cl and S are the champions of electron gain enthalpy in those same two groups. That inversion between an atom's pull on a shared electron (electronegativity) and the energy released when it captures a free electron (ΔegH) is the whole point of the question.
Key relation Electronegativity ↑ across a period, ↓ down a group ⇒ F is the most electronegative element of all (4.0), O second (3.5) · |ΔegH| is maximum at period 3, not period 2: Cl (−349) > F (−328); S (−200) > O (−141) kJ mol⁻¹ the 2p subshell of F and O is so compact that the incoming electron suffers huge inter-electronic repulsion, cancelling much of the energy released. · atomic size ↑ down a group ⇒ the bottom member of a group is the biggest · metalloids lie on the B–Si–Ge–As–Sb–Te staircase.
Solution
1. (A) Cl, S. Cl is group 17, S is group 16. In group 17, |ΔegH| runs Cl 349 > F 328 > Br 325 > I 295; in group 16 it runs S 200 > Se 195 > Te 190 > O 141 kJ mol⁻¹. Both are the most negative in their own group → A-iv.
2. (B) Ge, As. Both lie on the diagonal staircase separating metals from non-metals; they are lustrous like metals but brittle semiconductors with non-metallic chemistry → metalloids → B-iii.
3. (C) Fr, Ra. Fr is the last alkali metal (group 1), Ra the last alkaline-earth (group 2). Being at the bottom of their groups, they have the highest principal quantum number and hence the largest atomic size in those groups → C-ii.
4. (D) F, O. Pauling electronegativities: F 4.0 (highest of any element, hence highest in group 17) and O 3.5 (highest in group 16) → D-i.
5. Assemble: A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i ⇒ option (c). Options (a) and (d) both send Cl, S to "largest atomic size" (absurd — Cl and S are period-3, not bottom-of-group) and are dead on arrival; option (b) makes Fr, Ra the most electronegative, which is the exact opposite of the truth (Fr is the least electronegative element).
 List-I    identity                     property IN THEIR OWN GROUP     List-II
 Cl, S     gr-17 & gr-16, period 3      most negative delta_eg H         (iv)
 Ge, As    on the metal/non-metal       metalloid character              (iii)
           staircase
 Fr, Ra    gr-1 & gr-2, period 7        largest atomic size              (ii)
 F, O      gr-17 & gr-16, period 2      highest electronegativity        (i)

 A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i                                    <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Burn one sentence into memory: "F wins electronegativity, Cl wins electron gain enthalpy; O wins electronegativity, S wins electron gain enthalpy." It resolves half the period-2 anomaly questions in this chapter, and it is precisely what separates the (A) row from the (D) row here. The trap is that a student who only remembers "F is the most reactive/most electron-hungry element" will pair F, O with (iv) and Cl, S with (i), producing a matching that is not even in the option list — which then panics them into choosing (d) by elimination.
Answer: (c) A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i.
27Match List I with List II. List I (Element): (A) N · (B) S · (C) Br · (D) Kr. List II (Electronic configuration): (i) [Ar]3d104s24p5 · (ii) [Ne]3s23p4 · (iii) [He]2s22p3 · (iv) [Ar]3d104s24p6. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 2, Level-1) (a) A-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv (b) A-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii (c) A-i, B-iv, C-iii, D-ii (d) A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-iNoble-gas-core electronic configurationsWriting s, p, d, f electronic configurations with noble-gas cores🟡 SimilaraA-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv
Concept Every configuration in List II is written in noble-gas-core shorthand, so the core symbol alone ([He], [Ne], [Ar]) already tells you the period of the element: [He] core ⇒ period 2, [Ne] core ⇒ period 3, [Ar] core ⇒ period 4. That single observation splits the four options into pairs before you count a single electron. Then the number of electrons written outside the bracket fixes the group. You never need to add up total electrons — but doing so is a fast check.
Key relation Z = (electrons in the noble-gas core) + (electrons written outside) with He = 2, Ne = 10, Ar = 18 · Aufbau filling order for period 4: 4s → 3d → 4p so a period-4 p-block element must carry a complete 3d10 before its 4p electrons — that is why (i) and (iv) both show 3d10.
Solution
1. (A) N, Z = 7. Core [He] = 2, so 5 electrons remain: 2s22p3. That is (iii). Check: 2 + 5 = 7 ✔ (half-filled 2p3, three unpaired electrons — the extra-stable configuration behind nitrogen's famously high IE₁).
2. (B) S, Z = 16. Core [Ne] = 10, so 6 remain: 3s23p4. That is (ii). Check: 10 + 6 = 16 ✔
3. (C) Br, Z = 35. Core [Ar] = 18, so 17 remain: 3d104s24p5. That is (i). Check: 18 + 10 + 2 + 5 = 35 ✔ (one electron short of the krypton octet — hence Br's halogen chemistry).
4. (D) Kr, Z = 36. Core [Ar] = 18, so 18 remain: 3d104s24p6. That is (iv). Check: 18 + 18 = 36 ✔ (closed 4p6 — the noble gas itself).
5. Assemble: A-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv ⇒ option (a). The single fastest discriminator: only (i) and (iv) have an [Ar] core, and only Br and Kr are period-4, so A and B must map to (ii)/(iii) — which instantly kills options (b), (c) and (d).
 element  Z    core   electrons outside core        List-II
 N        7    [He]=2   2s2 2p3            (5)      (iii)
 S       16    [Ne]=10  3s2 3p4            (6)      (ii)
 Br      35    [Ar]=18  3d10 4s2 4p5      (17)      (i)
 Kr      36    [Ar]=18  3d10 4s2 4p6      (18)      (iv)

 A-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv                             <-- answer (a)
⚠ Note The printed entry (i) in the source book reads "[Ar]3d104s24p5AR" — the trailing "AR" is a typographical artefact of the scan/typesetting and carries no meaning. The intended configuration is [Ar]3d104s24p5 (bromine), which is how it is treated above and which is consistent with the keyed answer.
⚡ JEE Trick In any match-the-configuration question, read the bracket, not the exponents. [He] → period 2, [Ne] → period 3, [Ar] → period 4, [Kr] → period 5. One glance sorts the list into periods and usually leaves exactly one surviving option. The trap here is the 3d10: students who write bromine as "[Ar]4s24p5" (forgetting the d-block sandwiched between) count Z = 25 and conclude nothing matches. Remember that after argon the 3d subshell must be filled and paid for before you reach the 4p block.
Answer: (a) A-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv.
28Match List I with List II. List I: (A) Melting Point / K · (B) Ionic Radius [M3+/pm] · (C) ΔiH1 [kJ mol⁻¹] · (D) Atomic Radius / pm. List II: (i) Tl > In > Ga > Al > B · (ii) B > Tl > Al ≈ Ga > In · (iii) Tl > In > Al > Ga > B · (iv) B > Al > Tl > In > Ga. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 2, Level-1) (a) A-ii, B-iii, C-iv, D-i (b) A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii (c) A-i, B-ii, C-iii, D-iv (d) A-iii, B-iv, C-i, D-iiGroup-13 anomalies: m.p., rionic, IE, ratomicTrends in the properties of group-13 elements (B, Al, Ga, In, Tl)🟡 SimilarbA-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii
Concept Group 13 is the classic "no trend is smooth" family, and this question deliberately picks the four properties whose orders all look different. Two of them (ionic radius M3+) behave perfectly — a clean monotonic increase down the group. Two of them are broken by d-block (scandide) and f-block (lanthanoid) contractions: Ga is smaller than Al even though it sits below it, and Tl's ionisation enthalpy is anomalously high. The fourth, melting point, is broken for a completely different, structural reason: gallium's peculiar Ga₂ dimeric metallic lattice makes it melt in your hand at 303 K. Match the property to its cause and the four orders sort themselves out.
Key relation r(M3+)/pm: B 27 < Al 53.5 < Ga 62.0 < In 80.0 < Tl 88.5 a bare M3+ has lost its whole valence shell, so only the noble-gas-like core is left and the size grows monotonically with the core's principal quantum number — no anomaly survives. · r(atomic)/pm: B 85, Al 143, Ga 135, In 167, Tl 170 Ga < Al because the ten poorly-shielding 3d electrons inserted between them raise Zeff more than the new shell expands the atom (scandide contraction). · ΔiH₁/kJ mol⁻¹: B 801, Al 577, Ga 579, In 558, Tl 589 · m.p./K: B 2453, Al 933, Ga 303, In 430, Tl 576.
Solution
1. (B) Ionic radius M3+ is the only property with a perfectly regular down-the-group increase, so it must take the perfectly regular option: Tl > In > Ga > Al > B = (i). Fix this first — it is the anchor.
2. (D) Atomic radius. Same general increase, but Ga slips below Al: Tl (170) > In (167) > Al (143) > Ga (135) > B (85) = (iii).The only difference between (i) and (iii) is that Al and Ga swap. That swap is the scandide contraction, and it survives in the neutral atom (where the 4s/4p electrons feel the poor 3d shielding) but vanishes in M3+ (where those electrons are gone).
3. (C) First ionisation enthalpy. B is by far the highest (801, small period-2 atom). Then Al 577, Ga 579, In 558, Tl 589 — a near-flat, zig-zag set in which Tl is the highest of the four heavy members (6s inert-pair / lanthanoid-contraction effect) and Al and Ga are within 2 kJ mol⁻¹ of each other. Order: B > Tl > Al ≈ Ga > In = (ii). The "≈" sign in option (ii) is the give-away — no other property has two members that are numerically almost equal.
4. (A) Melting point. Boron is a giant covalent solid (2453 K), aluminium a normal close-packed metal (933 K), then the metallic bonding weakens and gallium's odd Ga₂-dimer lattice collapses to a liquid at 303 K, with In (430 K) and Tl (576 K) recovering. Order: B > Al > Tl > In > Ga = (iv).
5. Assemble: A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii ⇒ option (b).
 property            B      Al     Ga     In     Tl     resulting order        List-II
 melting point / K  2453    933    303    430    576   B>Al>Tl>In>Ga            (iv)
 r(M3+) / pm          27   53.5   62.0   80.0   88.5   Tl>In>Ga>Al>B            (i)
 IE1 / kJ mol-1      801    577    579    558    589   B>Tl>Al~Ga>In            (ii)
 r(atomic) / pm       85    143    135    167    170   Tl>In>Al>Ga>B            (iii)

 A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii                                          <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Use the "≈" fingerprint. Whenever one option in a match-list contains an approximate-equality sign, look for the property whose data genuinely has a near-tie — here Al 577 vs Ga 579 kJ mol⁻¹ — and lock that pairing in immediately. Combine it with the rule "the ionic-radius column is always the well-behaved one" and you have two of four matches for free, which uniquely determines the answer. The classic trap is D: students who assume atomic radius must increase strictly down the group pick option (i) for it, land on (c), and never notice that Ga is smaller than Al.
Answer: (b) A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii.
29Consider the following elements, arranged with Group running down and Period running across: A′ B′ in one period, and C′ D′ in the period directly below (C′ under A′, D′ under B′). Which of the following is/are true about A′, B′, C′ and D′? A. Order of atomic radii: B′ < A′ < D′ < C′. B. Order of metallic character: B′ < A′ < D′ < C′. C. Size of the element: D′ < C′ < B′ < A′. D. Order of ionic radii: B′⁺ < A′⁺ < D′⁺ < C′⁺. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2024, 31 Jan Shift 2, Level-5) (a) A only (b) A, B and D only (c) A and B only (d) B, C and D onlyCombined period/group trends on a 2×2 blockHow metallic and non-metallic character vary across a period and down a group🟡 SimilarbA, B and D only
Concept The four elements form a 2 × 2 block of the periodic table: A′ and B′ are neighbours in one period (A′ on the left), C′ and D′ sit immediately below them in the next period. Every property asked about — atomic radius, metallic character and cationic radius — is governed by the same pair of competing influences: adding a new shell (n ↑, size ↑, electron easier to lose) versus raising the effective nuclear charge within a shell (Zeff ↑, size ↓, electron harder to lose). Because all three properties respond in the same direction, one single ordering must serve all three, and the only question is whether the diagonal step A′ → D′ increases or decreases the size.
Key relation across a period (→): r ↓, metallic character ↓ · down a group (↓): r ↑, metallic character ↑ · the diagonal rule: going down one period beats going right one group adding a whole new principal shell changes n, which dominates the ~1-unit rise in Zeff from a single step to the right. This is exactly the reason diagonal relationships (Li–Mg, Be–Al, B–Si) exist: the two effects nearly cancel along a diagonal, but the shell effect wins slightly.
Solution
1. Fix the horizontal comparisons. A′ and B′ share a period with A′ to the left, so B′ < A′. Likewise D′ < C′.
2. Fix the vertical comparisons. C′ is directly below A′ and D′ directly below B′, so A′ < C′ and B′ < D′.
3. Fix the diagonal. A′ (period n, left column) versus D′ (period n+1, right column): the extra shell in D′ outweighs the one-step increase in Zeff, so A′ < D′. Chaining everything: B′ < A′ < D′ < C′.Concrete example — take A′ = Na (186 pm), B′ = Mg (160), C′ = K (227), D′ = Ca (197): 160 < 186 < 197 < 227 ✔.
4. Statement A quotes exactly this order for atomic radii → TRUE.
5. Statement B. Metallic character means the ease of losing an electron; it rises with size and falls with Zeff, i.e. it follows the size order verbatim. So B′ < A′ < D′ < C′ for metallic character too → TRUE. (K is more metallic than Ca, which is more metallic than Na, which is more metallic than Mg.)
6. Statement C claims D′ < C′ < B′ < A′, i.e. the whole lower period is smaller than the whole upper period. That directly contradicts A′ < C′ and B′ < D′ → FALSE. (A and C cannot both be true, which already tells you at least one is wrong.)
7. Statement D. All four cations carry the same +1 charge, so no charge effect intervenes and the isoelectronic complication never arises; the cations inherit the parent ordering: B′⁺ < A′⁺ < D′⁺ < C′⁺ → TRUE. (Mg⁺ < Na⁺ < Ca⁺ < K⁺.)
8. True statements: A, B and D ⇒ option (b).
              period n   :   A'   B'        (A' left of B')
              period n+1 :   C'   D'        (C' under A',  D' under B')

 rule 1  across a period ->  radius DOWN     =>  B' < A'   and   D' < C'
 rule 2  down a group    ->  radius UP       =>  A' < C'   and   B' < D'
 rule 3  diagonal A'->D' ->  new shell wins  =>  A' < D'

 chain            B' < A' < D' < C'
 worked example   Mg 160 < Na 186 < Ca 197 < K 227  (pm)

 A  radii            B'<A'<D'<C'         TRUE
 B  metallic char.   B'<A'<D'<C'         TRUE  (tracks size exactly)
 C  size             D'<C'<B'<A'         FALSE (contradicts A)
 D  ionic radii M+   B'+<A'+<D'+<C'+     TRUE  (same charge, same trend)

 A, B and D only                                 <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a question hides behind primed symbols A′, B′, C′, D′, substitute real elements immediately — Na, Mg, K, Ca is the cheapest 2×2 block there is, and every statement becomes a number comparison you already know. Also exploit the internal contradiction: statements A and C assert opposite orders, so exactly one of them is true; any option containing both (or neither) can often be struck out at a glance. The trap in D is the word "ionic": students reflexively invoke the isoelectronic rule ("higher charge ⇒ smaller ion"), but all four ions here have the same +1 charge, so that rule has nothing to bite on and the ordinary size trend simply carries over.
Answer: (b) A, B and D only.
30The correct order of the first ionization enthalpy is (JEE Main 2024, 4 Apr Shift-2, Level-5) (a) Al > Ga > Tl (b) Ga > Al > B (c) Tl > Ga > Al (d) B > Al > GaAnomalous IE₁ order in group 13This exact JEE 2024 question: the correct order of the first ionization enthalpy🟢 ExactcTl > Ga > Al
Concept Every option in this question is a three-element slice of group 13, so the naive rule "ionisation enthalpy decreases down a group" would make (a) and (d) look right and (b) and (c) look absurd. In fact group 13 is the standard counter-example to that rule. After aluminium the values stop falling and start creeping back up, because each new row inserts a block of poorly-shielding electrons: Ga sits after the 3d10 block (scandide contraction) and Tl sits after both the 5d10 block and the 4f14 lanthanoid series. Those inner electrons screen the valence np electron badly, so Zeff at the valence shell rises and the electron is held more tightly than the plain group trend predicts.
Key relation ΔiH₁ / kJ mol⁻¹ : B 801, Al 577, Ga 579, In 558, Tl 589 the only big drop is B → Al (one whole new shell, 224 kJ mol⁻¹); after that the curve is essentially flat with a shallow zig-zag, and Tl finishes highest of the four heavy members. · Zeff ↑ when a d or f block is inserted, because d and f electrons shield poorly (s > p > d > f in screening power).
Solution
1. Write the five values down before touching the options: B 801, Al 577, Ga 579, In 558, Tl 589 kJ mol⁻¹.
2. (a) Al > Ga > Tl needs 577 > 579, which is false at the very first step. Rejected.Ga exceeds Al by 2 kJ mol⁻¹ precisely because the ten 3d electrons interposed between them shield the 4p electron poorly.
3. (b) Ga > Al > B has the first inequality right (579 > 577) but then demands Al > B, i.e. 577 > 801, which is badly false. Rejected.
4. (c) Tl > Ga > Al requires 589 > 579 > 577. Both inequalities hold. Accepted.Tl beats Ga because the 6p electron of thallium is shielded by an entire 4f14 set as well as 5d10; the lanthanoid contraction leaves Tl no larger than In while its nuclear charge is 32 units higher.
5. (d) B > Al > Ga starts correctly (801 > 577) but then needs Al > Ga, i.e. 577 > 579 — false by the same 2 kJ mol⁻¹ margin that killed (a). Rejected. This is the option most students choose.
 element   IE1 (kJ/mol)   comment
 B            801         period-2, tiny atom, no inner d/f
 Al           577         one extra shell -> big drop
 Ga           579         after 3d10  -> poor shielding, creeps UP
 In           558         normal size increase pulls it down
 Tl           589         after 4f14 + 5d10 -> highest of the heavies

 (a) Al > Ga > Tl   577 > 579 ?   NO
 (b) Ga > Al > B    577 > 801 ?   NO
 (c) Tl > Ga > Al   589 > 579 > 577   YES        <-- answer (c)
 (d) B > Al > Ga    577 > 579 ?   NO
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise group 13 as the "W-shape": B (high) → Al (deep drop) → Ga (up a hair) → In (down) → Tl (up again). Three of the four options here are destroyed by the single fact Ga > Al, so if you remember only that one inequality you can answer in five seconds. The distractor (d) B > Al > Ga is deliberately built to reward the student who correctly knows B is the highest — the paper-setter is betting that having got the first comparison right, you will not check the second. Whenever an option chain contains Al and Ga (or In and Tl) adjacent, check that pair first.
Answer: (c) Tl > Ga > Al.
31Given below are two statements : Statement I : The correct order of first ionization enthalpy values of Li, Na, F and Cl is Na < Li < Cl < F. Statement II : The correct order of negative electron gain enthalpy values of Li, Na, F and Cl is Na < Li < F < Cl. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below : (JEE Main 2024, 4 Apr Shift-2, Level-5) (a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are trueIE₁ and ΔegH of Li, Na, F, ClStatement question on the electron gain enthalpy of fluorine versus chlorine🟡 SimilardBoth Statement I and Statement II are true
Concept The same four elements are ranked twice, once by how hard it is to remove an electron and once by how much energy is released when one is added. Two alkali metals (Li, Na) and two halogens (F, Cl) are chosen so that each statement contains one ordinary comparison and one anomaly. The ordinary part is that alkali metals sit far below halogens on both scales. The subtle part is what happens within each pair: for ionisation enthalpy the normal group trend survives (Li > Na, F > Cl), but for electron gain enthalpy the group-17 trend inverts (Cl beats F), because the incoming electron has to squeeze into fluorine's tiny, electron-dense 2p subshell.
Key relation ΔiH₁ / kJ mol⁻¹ : Na 496, Li 520, Cl 1251, F 1681 alkali metals have one loosely held ns¹ electron whose removal produces a noble-gas core, so their IE₁ is minimal; halogens are one electron short of a closed shell and hold their valence electrons hardest in the period. · |ΔegH| / kJ mol⁻¹ : Na 53, Li 60, F 328, Cl 349 both trends fall down a group, except that the period-2 member of group 17 (F) is anomalously low because of inter-electronic repulsion in the compact 2p shell.
Solution
1. Statement I — ionisation enthalpy. Within group 1, IE₁ falls down the group, so Na (496) < Li (520). Within group 17, IE₁ also falls down the group, so Cl (1251) < F (1681). Metals sit far below non-metals. Chaining: Na < Li < Cl < F. This is exactly the printed order ⇒ Statement I is TRUE.
2. Statement II — negative electron gain enthalpy. For the alkali metals the magnitudes are tiny but non-zero, and again the smaller atom releases more: Na (53) < Li (60). For the halogens, the anomaly applies: F (328) < Cl (349). Chaining: Na < Li < F < Cl. This is exactly the printed order ⇒ Statement II is TRUE.
3. Note carefully that the two statements are not the same order with F and Cl swapped by accident — the swap is real chemistry. In Statement I fluorine finishes on top; in Statement II chlorine does. A student who assumes "F is always the extreme element" will call Statement II false and pick (a).
4. Why F loses to Cl on ΔegH: fluorine's 2p subshell is very small, so the seven electrons already there repel the incoming eighth strongly. Much of the energy released by nuclear attraction is spent overcoming that repulsion. Chlorine's 3p subshell is roomier, the repulsion penalty is smaller, and more energy survives as a net release.
5. Both statements true ⇒ option (d).
 STATEMENT I -- first ionisation enthalpy, kJ/mol
 Na  496  <  Li  520  <  Cl 1251  <  F 1681
 printed order  Na < Li < Cl < F                      TRUE

 STATEMENT II -- magnitude of negative electron gain enthalpy, kJ/mol
 Na   53  <  Li   60  <  F   328  <  Cl  349
 printed order  Na < Li < F < Cl                      TRUE
                              ^^^^^^^ the group-17 inversion

 both true                                             <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Keep two separate one-line rules and never mix them: "for IE₁, F beats Cl" (normal group trend) but "for ΔegH, Cl beats F" (period-2 anomaly). Nine out of ten statement-pair questions in this chapter turn on exactly that contrast. The trap here is symmetry: both printed chains start "Na < Li < …", so the eye reads them as identical and the brain applies one rule to both. Read the last two symbols of each chain before deciding anything — they are the only place the two statements differ.
Answer: (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are true.
32Evaluate the following statements related to group 14 elements for their correctness. (A) Covalent radius decreases down the group from C to Pb in a regular manner. (B) Electronegativity decreases from C to Pb down the group gradually. (C) Maximum covalence of C is 4 whereas other elements can expand their covalence due to presence of d orbitals. (D) Heavier elements do not form pπ–pπ bonds. (E) Carbon can exhibit negative oxidation states. Choose the correct answer from the options given below : (JEE Main 2024, 6 Apr Shift-2, Level-5) (a) (A), (B) and (C) Only (b) (C), (D) and (E) Only (c) (C) and (D) Only (d) (A) and (B) OnlyGroup-14 anomalous properties of carbonThis exact JEE 2024 question: evaluating the five statements on group-14 elements🟢 Exactb(C), (D) and (E) Only
Concept Five statements, of which two are ordinary trend statements written with a deliberately wrong direction or a wrong adverb, and three are statements about the anomalous behaviour of carbon — the theme of the whole group-14 chapter. Carbon is anomalous for exactly two structural reasons: (i) it has no d orbitals in its valence shell, so it can never exceed a covalence of four; and (ii) it is small, so its 2p orbitals overlap sideways efficiently and it alone forms strong pπ–pπ multiple bonds. Once you see that (C), (D) and (E) are all restatements of those two facts, the answer is forced.
Key relation covalent radius / pm : C 77, Si 118, Ge 122, Sn 140, Pb 146 — it INCREASES down the group · electronegativity (Pauling) : C 2.5, Si 1.8, Ge 1.8, Sn 1.8, Pb 1.9 there is one big drop from C to Si and then essentially no change — the word "gradually" makes statement (B) false. · max covalence: C = 4 (no 2d orbitals exist); Si, Ge, Sn, Pb = up to 6 (vacant nd available) e.g. [SiF₆]²⁻ · pπ–pπ overlap requires small, compact p orbitals ⇒ only C does it well (C=C, C≡C, C=O).
Solution
1. (A) FALSE. Covalent radius increases from C (77 pm) to Pb (146 pm) — each step adds a principal shell. The statement reverses the direction, and it also fails the word "regular" (the jump C → Si is 41 pm, but Si → Ge is only 4 pm because of the intervening 3d contraction).
2. (B) FALSE. Electronegativity does fall from C (2.5) to Si (1.8), but from Si onwards it is practically constant and even ticks up slightly at Pb (1.9). NCERT records the values for Si to Pb as almost the same, so the claim of a gradual decrease all the way from C to Pb is not correct.
3. (C) TRUE. Carbon's valence shell is n = 2, which contains only 2s and 2p — there is no 2d subshell in existence — so carbon is capped at four bonds. Silicon and its heavier congeners have empty 3d (4d, 5d…) orbitals and routinely reach a covalence of six: [SiF₆]²⁻, [GeCl₆]²⁻, [Sn(OH)₆]²⁻.
4. (D) TRUE. Effective pπ–pπ sideways overlap needs small atoms with compact p orbitals close enough together. Beyond carbon the atoms are large and their p orbitals diffuse, so Si, Ge, Sn and Pb do not form stable pπ–pπ multiple bonds — this is why CO₂ is a discrete O=C=O molecule while SiO₂ is a giant network of Si–O single bonds.
5. (E) TRUE. Carbon shows the full range −4 to +4: it is −4 in CH₄, −3 in C₂H₆, −2 in CH₃OH / C₂H₄, and +4 in CO₂ and CCl₄. Negative states are precisely what carbon shows when bonded to less electronegative hydrogen.
6. True statements = (C), (D), (E) ⇒ option (b). Options (a) and (d) both include the reversed-trend statement (A), and (c) drops the perfectly correct (E).
 stmt  claim                                              verdict
 (A)   covalent radius DECREASES C -> Pb, regularly       FALSE (77 -> 146 pm, it INCREASES,
                                                                and not regularly)
 (B)   electronegativity decreases C -> Pb gradually      FALSE (2.5 -> 1.8 then flat, Pb 1.9)
 (C)   max covalence C = 4; heavier expand via d          TRUE  ([SiF6]2-, no 2d for carbon)
 (D)   heavier elements do not form p-pi--p-pi bonds      TRUE  (CO2 molecular vs SiO2 network)
 (E)   carbon can exhibit negative oxidation states       TRUE  (-4 in CH4)

 correct set = (C), (D), (E) only                         <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick In "evaluate the statements" questions, hunt for the direction word first — increases/decreases — and for weasel adverbs like regular, gradual, always. Statement (A) dies on the direction word and (B) dies on the adverb, and killing those two alone leaves only option (b) standing, because every remaining option except (b) contains (A) or omits (E). The trap in (E) is that students associate carbon with +4 (CO₂, CCl₄) and forget CH₄, where hydrogen is the less electronegative partner and carbon is therefore −4.
Answer: (b) (C), (D) and (E) Only.
33Given below are two statements : one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: The first ionisation enthalpy decreases across a period. Reason R: The increasing nuclear charge outweighs the shielding across the period. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate from the options given below: (JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 1, Level-4) (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A (b) A is true but R is false (c) A is false but R is true (d) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of AAssertion–Reason: IE₁ trend across a periodThis exact assertion–reason question on first ionisation enthalpy across a period🟢 ExactcA is false but R is true
Concept The examiner has taken a perfectly correct Reason and bolted it onto a deliberately reversed Assertion. This is the commonest construction in assertion–reason chemistry: if you check only whether the two sentences "sound related", you will mark them consistent and choose (a). The correct method is to evaluate each sentence independently, on its own truth value, before ever asking about the explanatory link. Here R is textbook-accurate and A is textbook-backwards, which is a combination that only option (c) allows.
Key relation ΔiH₁ INCREASES left → right across a period · Zeff = Z − σ across a period each step adds one proton (Z rises by 1) but the extra electron enters the SAME shell, where it screens the nucleus only weakly (σ rises by roughly 0.35 for a same-shell electron by Slater's rules). So Zeff climbs by about 0.65 per step: the nuclear charge genuinely outweighs the shielding. · higher Zeff and smaller radius ⇒ valence electron more tightly bound ⇒ larger IE₁.
Solution
1. Test the Assertion. Period 2 values (kJ mol⁻¹): Li 520, Be 899, B 801, C 1086, N 1402, O 1314, F 1681, Ne 2081. Ignoring the two well-known dips (Be→B and N→O, caused by the 2s→2p subshell change and by p⁴ pairing repulsion), the trend is strongly upward. Assertion A says "decreases" ⇒ FALSE.
2. Test the Reason. Moving right, Z increases by 1 per element while the added electron goes into the same principal shell and shields poorly. Net effect: Zeff rises steadily — Li ≈ 1.30, Be ≈ 1.95, B ≈ 2.60, C ≈ 3.25, N ≈ 3.90, O ≈ 4.55, F ≈ 5.20 by Slater's rules. So the increasing nuclear charge really does outweigh the shielding ⇒ Reason R is TRUE.
3. Notice the irony: R is precisely the reason why A is wrong. If nuclear charge outweighs shielding, the valence electron is held more tightly and IE₁ must rise, not fall. A correct assertion would read "The first ionisation enthalpy increases across a period."
4. A false, R true ⇒ option (c). Options (a) and (d) both require A to be true; option (b) requires R to be false, which contradicts step 2.
 period-2 first ionisation enthalpies, kJ/mol
 Li   Be    B     C     N     O     F     Ne
 520  899  801  1086  1402  1314  1681  2081
  |____^ overall the numbers RISE left to right
       (the two dips Be->B and N->O are subshell/pairing effects, not a downtrend)

 Slater Z_eff for the valence electron
 Li 1.30  Be 1.95  B 2.60  C 3.25  N 3.90  O 4.55  F 5.20    -> rises steadily

 Assertion : IE1 decreases across a period          FALSE
 Reason    : nuclear charge outweighs shielding     TRUE

 A false, R true                                    <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Grade assertion–reason questions in a strict order: truth of A → truth of R → link, and never let a convincing-sounding R persuade you that A must be true. A useful sanity check is to ask "if R is true, what does it predict?" — here R predicts a rising IE₁, which flatly contradicts A, and that contradiction is itself the proof that A is false. The trap is that the paper-setter has written R in impressive, correct-sounding language so that the pair reads like a matched statement-and-explanation; roughly a third of JEE A–R questions in periodicity use exactly this true-R / false-A pattern.
Answer: (c) A is false but R is true.
34Given below are two statements: Statement I: Fluorine has most negative electron gain enthalpy in its group. Statement II: Oxygen has least negative electron gain enthalpy in its group. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate from the options given below. (JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 2, Level-4) (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (b) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (d) Statement I is false but Statement II is truePeriod-2 anomaly in ΔegH (groups 16 & 17)Why the electron gain enthalpy of fluorine is less negative than that of chlorine🟡 SimilardStatement I is false but Statement II is true
Concept Both statements test the same anomaly — that the period-2 head of a p-block group has an unexpectedly small electron affinity — but they phrase it in opposite directions, so exactly one of them survives. F and O are both very small atoms whose 2p subshell is already crowded; forcing an extra electron in costs a lot of inter-electronic repulsion energy, which cancels much of the energy released by nuclear attraction. The consequence is that F loses to Cl in group 17, and O loses to all of S, Se and Te in group 16. So fluorine is not the group-17 champion, while oxygen genuinely is the group-16 wooden-spoon holder.
Key relation Group 17 ΔegH / kJ mol⁻¹ : F −328, Cl −349, Br −325, I −295 ⇒ most negative is Cl. Group 16 ΔegH / kJ mol⁻¹ : O −141, S −200, Se −195, Te −190 ⇒ least negative is O. In both groups the ranking is anomalous only at the period-2 member; from period 3 downwards the ordinary "smaller atom ⇒ more negative ΔegH" trend resumes (Cl > Br > I, S > Se > Te).
Solution
1. Statement I. In group 17 the most negative electron gain enthalpy belongs to chlorine (−349), not fluorine (−328). Fluorine is only second. Statement I is FALSE.The 2p orbitals of F are so compact that adding a ninth electron into an already seven-electron 2p set costs a large repulsion penalty. Chlorine's 3p subshell has a bigger volume, so the same addition is far less costly and more energy is released overall.
2. Statement II. In group 16 the values are O −141, S −200, Se −195, Te −190. The least negative — i.e. the smallest magnitude — is oxygen. Statement II is TRUE.Same physical cause as above, but in group 16 the penalty is even more severe because oxygen already carries four 2p electrons; the anomaly is therefore large enough that O falls below Te as well as below S.
3. Do not confuse the two anomalies. In group 17 fluorine is displaced by one position (Cl > F > Br > I). In group 16 oxygen is displaced to last place (S > Se > Te > O). Both are consequences of small size, but the magnitudes differ, and the question exploits that asymmetry.
4. Statement I false, Statement II true ⇒ option (d).
 GROUP 17 -- electron gain enthalpy, kJ/mol
 F  -328    Cl -349    Br -325    I  -295
 most negative = Cl, NOT F                     Statement I  FALSE

 GROUP 16 -- electron gain enthalpy, kJ/mol
 O  -141    S  -200    Se -195    Te -190
 least negative = O                            Statement II TRUE

 I false, II true                              <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Compress both anomalies into one slogan: "Cl is the king of group 17; O is the pauper of group 16." That single line answers this question, and it also answers the very common variants "which element has the highest electron affinity in the periodic table?" (Cl, not F) and "arrange O, S, Se, Te by ΔegH" (S > Se > Te > O). The trap in Statement I is the word "most": students who have learnt that F is the most electronegative and most reactive halogen transfer that superlative to electron gain enthalpy, mark I true, and land on (a) or (b). Electronegativity and electron gain enthalpy are different properties and their group-17 champions are different elements.
Answer: (d) Statement I is false but Statement II is true.
35Given below are the two statements: one is labeled as Assertion (A) and the other is labeled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): There is a considerable increase in covalent radius from N to P. However from As to Bi only a small increase in covalent radius is observed. Reason (R): Covalent and ionic radii in a particular oxidation state increase down the group. In the light of the above statement, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2024, 30 Jan Shift 1, Level-4) (a) (A) is false but (R) is true (b) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A) (c) (A) is true but (R) is false (d) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)Group-15 covalent radii; d/f-contractionThis exact assertion–reason: considerable increase in covalent radius from N to P, small increase thereafter🟢 ExactbBoth (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
Concept This is the "true-and-true but unrelated" pattern, and it is the hardest of the four assertion–reason outcomes to spot. The Reason states the general rule (radii grow down a group). The Assertion states an anomaly within that rule — that the growth is large at the top of group 15 and small at the bottom. A general rule can never explain a deviation from itself. The real explanation of the Assertion is the insertion of poorly-shielding completely filled d and f subshells in the heavier members (As comes after 3d¹⁰, Sb after 4d¹⁰, Bi after 4f¹⁴ and 5d¹⁰), which drags the outer electrons in and cancels much of the expected size increase.
Key relation Group-15 covalent radii N 70, P 110, As 121, Sb 141, Bi 148 pm ⇒ increments N→P = +40, P→As = +11, As→Sb = +20, Sb→Bi = +7 pm the very first step is by far the biggest; every later step is damped. · shielding power: s > p > d > f ⇒ a filled d¹⁰ or f¹⁴ set raises Zeff on the valence shell and shrinks the atom relative to expectation.
Solution
1. Test the Assertion. N → P jumps by about 40 pm — a genuinely large step, because P adds a whole new shell with nothing but s and p electrons in between. From As to Bi the total increase is only about 27 pm spread over three elements, i.e. small per step. The Assertion reproduces the standard NCERT sentence for group 15 and is TRUE.
2. Test the Reason. "Covalent and ionic radii in a particular oxidation state increase down the group" is the plain, correct group trend for group 15 (and for main groups generally). It is TRUE.
3. Test the link. R asserts only that the radii increase. The Assertion is not about whether they increase — it takes that for granted — it is about why the size of the increase is uneven. R contains no information about d/f-orbital shielding, so it cannot account for the damping observed from As to Bi. The link fails.
4. The genuine explanation, for completeness: As sits just after the first 3d¹⁰ block, Sb just after 4d¹⁰, and Bi just after the 4f¹⁴ lanthanoids plus 5d¹⁰. Those inner electrons shield the nucleus poorly, so the effective nuclear charge felt by the valence np electrons rises sharply and offsets most of the shell-by-shell expansion.
5. A true, R true, R not the explanation ⇒ option (b).
 group-15 covalent radii (pm) and step sizes
 N  70
        +40   <-- considerable  (no d or f block in between)
 P 110
        +11
 As 121                                        As sits after 3d10
        +20
 Sb 141                                        Sb sits after 4d10
        +7    <-- only small increases
 Bi 148                                        Bi sits after 4f14 + 5d10

 Assertion : big jump N->P, small increases As->Bi        TRUE
 Reason    : radii increase down the group                TRUE
 does R explain the UNEVENNESS ?                          NO
   (R explains only THAT they increase, not by HOW MUCH;
    the real cause is poor d/f shielding -> higher Z_eff)

 both true, R not the explanation                <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever an Assertion describes an irregularity and the Reason quotes the regular trend, the answer is almost always "both true but R is not the correct explanation". Ask one question: does R mention the specific mechanism the Assertion needs? If the Assertion needs "d and f electrons shield poorly" and R only says "radii increase down the group", the link is broken however true both sentences are. The trap is that R sounds like the topic sentence of the very NCERT paragraph the Assertion is quoted from, which nudges students straight to (d).
Answer: (b) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A).
36Number of elements from the following that CANNOT form compounds with valencies which match with their respective group valencies is _____ . B, C, N, S, O, F, P, Al, Si (JEE Main 2024, 4 Apr Shift-1, Level-4) (a) 7 (b) 3 (c) 5 (d) 6Group valence vs. maximum covalenceValency and oxidation-state trends across the periodic table, and where they break down🟡 Similarb3 (namely N, O and F)
Concept "Group valency" here means the valence an element would show if it used all its valence electrons in bonding — the valence with respect to oxygen in the NCERT table, which equals the group number for groups 1, 2 and 13–17 counted as 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7. Whether an element can actually reach that number depends on one structural fact: does its valence shell contain empty d orbitals? Period-2 elements (n = 2) have only 2s and 2p — there is no such thing as a 2d subshell — so they are hard-capped at four bonds and can never expand their octet. Period-3 elements and below have empty 3d (4d, …) orbitals available and can promote electrons into them, reaching their full group valence.
Key relation group valence = number of valence electrons (valence w.r.t. O) · maximum covalence of a period-2 element = 4 (only four orbitals: one 2s + three 2p) · period-3 and beyond: nd orbitals available ⇒ octet expansion possible this is exactly why PCl₅ and SF₆ exist but NCl₅ and OF₆ do not, and why F is invariably univalent while Cl reaches +7 in Cl₂O₇.
Solution
1. Tabulate group and group valence for all nine elements: B (13 → 3), C (14 → 4), N (15 → 5), S (16 → 6), O (16 → 6), F (17 → 7), P (15 → 5), Al (13 → 3), Si (14 → 4).
2. B, C, Al, Si — no problem. Their group valences are 3, 4, 3, 4, all ≤ 4, so no octet expansion is required at all: BF₃ (3), CH₄/CCl₄ (4), AlCl₃ (3), SiCl₄ (4). All four CAN.
3. P and S — no problem either. They are period-3, so 3d orbitals are available. P reaches valence 5 in PCl₅ and P₂O₅; S reaches valence 6 in SF₆ and SO₃. Both CAN.
4. N — cannot. Nitrogen's group valence is 5, but with no 2d subshell it is stuck at a maximum covalence of 4 (as in NH₄⁺). NCl₅ and NF₅ do not exist, and although nitrogen reaches oxidation state +5 in HNO₃ it does so with only four σ/π bonds plus formal charge, never with five bonds. CANNOT.
5. O — cannot. Group valence 6, but oxygen is normally divalent and can be pushed no further than four bonds; OF₆, OCl₆ and the like are unknown. CANNOT.
6. F — cannot. Group valence 7, yet fluorine is rigidly monovalent: it is the most electronegative element, has no accessible d orbitals, and shows no positive oxidation state at all. There is no FCl₇ or F₂O₇. CANNOT.
7. Count the failures: N, O, F = 3 elements ⇒ option (b). Note that all three are period-2 and all three sit to the right of carbon — exactly the region where the group valence first exceeds 4.
 element  group  group valence  d orbitals in valence shell   can it reach it?
 B         13         3               no (period 2)   3 <= 4    YES  BF3
 C         14         4               no (period 2)   4 <= 4    YES  CH4
 N         15         5               NO              5 >  4    NO   <-- fails
 S         16         6               yes (3d)                  YES  SF6
 O         16         6               NO              6 >  4    NO   <-- fails
 F         17         7               NO              7 >  4    NO   <-- fails
 P         15         5               yes (3d)                  YES  PCl5
 Al        13         3               no (period 2*)  3 <= 4    YES  AlCl3
 Si        14         4               yes (3d)                  YES  SiCl4
 * Al is period 3, but its group valence of 3 needs no expansion anyway.

 count of CANNOT = N, O, F = 3                        <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Reduce the whole question to one test: is the element in period 2 AND is its group valence greater than 4? Only then does it fail. Period-2 elements with group valence ≤ 4 (Li, Be, B, C) are fine, and every period-3-and-below element is fine because nd orbitals are available. Scanning the list that way, only N (5), O (6) and F (7) qualify — three, in five seconds. The trap is option (d) 6: students who read "cannot form compounds with valencies matching group valency" as "cannot show variable valency" start ticking off B, C, Al, Si as well and arrive at 6 or 7. A second trap is nitrogen in HNO₃: its oxidation state is +5, but its covalence is still 4, and this question is about valence/covalence, not oxidation number.
Answer: (b) 3 — nitrogen, oxygen and fluorine.
37The correct order of first ionization enthalpy values of the following elements is: (A) O (B) N (C) Be (D) F (E) B. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) E < C < A < B < D (b) C < E < A < B < D (c) B < D < C < E < A (d) A < B < D < C < E [JEE Main 2024 (4 Apr Shift-1)]IE₁ order across period 2Increasing order of first ionization energy of Be, B and their period-2 neighbours🟡 SimilaraE < C < A < B < D  (B < Be < O < N < F)
Concept Across period 2 the first ionisation enthalpy rises from Li to Ne because Zeff grows while the electrons keep entering the same n = 2 shell. The rise is not monotonic: it dips twice. The first dip (Be → B) happens because boron's outgoing electron is a 2p electron, which is higher in energy and better shielded by the filled 2s² pair. The second dip (N → O) happens because oxygen's 2p⁴ forces one pair into a single p orbital, and the extra inter-electron repulsion in that doubly-occupied orbital makes the electron easier to pull out. Both dips are asked in this question at once, so a candidate who only remembers "IE increases left to right" gets it wrong.
Key relation IE₁ ∝ Zeff/n² along a period, modified by two structural corrections: 2s more penetrating than 2p ⇒ IE(Be) > IE(B) the 2s electron in Be sees the nucleus more directly, so removing it costs more than removing B's 2p electron. and half-filled 2p³ is extra stable ⇒ IE(N) > IE(O) N has px¹py¹pz¹ with zero pairing energy; O must pay the price of one paired orbital, and that pairing energy is refunded on ionisation.
Solution
1. Write the standard NCERT values (kJ mol⁻¹): Be 899, B 801, N 1402, O 1314, F 1681.
2. Rank them from smallest to largest: B (801) < Be (899) < O (1314) < N (1402) < F (1681).
3. Translate elements back into the question's letters: B = (E), Be = (C), O = (A), N = (B), F = (D).
4. Substituting, the increasing order reads E < C < A < B < D, which is option (a).
5. Eliminate the rest. (b) puts Be below B — it ignores the 2s/2p penetration dip. (c) starts with N (B) as the smallest, which is absurd since N has one of the highest IE₁ in the period. (d) is simply option (a) reversed and would make F the easiest to ionise, contradicting F being the most electronegative element.
 letter  element  config      IE1 (kJ/mol)   rank (low to high)
 (E)     B        [He]2s2 2p1      801             1
 (C)     Be       [He]2s2          899             2
 (A)     O        [He]2s2 2p4     1314             3
 (B)     N        [He]2s2 2p3     1402             4
 (D)     F        [He]2s2 2p5     1681             5
 order:  E < C < A < B < D                        <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the period-2 "zig-zag" as a shape, not as numbers: Li ↑ Be ↓ B ↑ C ↑ N ↓ O ↑ F ↑ Ne. Only two elements break the rise — B and O — and both breaks are downward immediately after a specially stable configuration (2s² and 2p³). The distractors here are built exactly on forgetting one dip at a time: option (b) forgets the Be/B dip, and any option that puts O above N forgets the N/O dip. If an option honours both dips, it is almost certainly the answer.
Answer: (a) E < C < A < B < D, i.e. B < Be < O < N < F.
38The statement(s) that are correct about the species O2−, F−, Na+ and Mg2+. (A) All are isoelectronic (B) All have the same nuclear charge (C) O2− has the largest ionic radii (D) Mg2+ has the smallest ionic radii. Choose the most appropriate answer: (a) (B), (C) and (D) only (b) (C) and (D) only (c) (A), (C) and (D) only (d) (A), (B), (C) and (D) [JEE Main 2024 (5 Apr Shift-1)]Isoelectronic series, ionic radiiRadii of the isoelectronic species Mg²⁺, Na⁺, F⁻, O²⁻ compared🟡 Similarc(A), (C) and (D) only
Concept "Isoelectronic" is a statement about electrons only — the species must have the same total electron count and hence the same electronic configuration. It says nothing whatever about the nucleus. In fact the whole point of an isoelectronic series is that the electron cloud is held constant so the only variable left is the nuclear charge Z. More protons pulling on the same ten electrons means a tighter, smaller ion; so within an isoelectronic set the radius falls steadily as Z rises, i.e. the most negative anion is the biggest and the most positive cation is the smallest.
Key relation isoelectronic ⇒ same number of e⁻, NOT same Z · rion ∝ 1/Zeff at constant electron count · equivalently r increases as the ratio (e⁻/protons) increases O²⁻ has 10 electrons for only 8 protons (ratio 1.25) so its cloud is loosely held and swollen; Mg²⁺ has 10 electrons for 12 protons (ratio 0.83) so its cloud is squeezed.
Solution
1. Count electrons: O²⁻ = 8 + 2 = 10, F⁻ = 9 + 1 = 10, Na⁺ = 11 − 1 = 10, Mg²⁺ = 12 − 2 = 10. All four carry ten electrons with configuration 1s²2s²2p⁶ (neon core). Statement (A) is TRUE.
2. Nuclear charges are 8, 9, 11 and 12 respectively — four different values. Statement (B) is FALSE.This is the deliberate trap: candidates who read "isoelectronic" as "identical in every way" tick (B) and land on option (d).
3. Lowest Z (8) belongs to O²⁻, so it holds the ten electrons least tightly and is the largest (1.40 Å). Statement (C) is TRUE.
4. Highest Z (12) belongs to Mg²⁺, so it is the smallest (0.65 Å). Statement (D) is TRUE.
5. Correct set = (A), (C), (D) ⇒ option (c). Option (a) wrongly includes (B) and drops (A); option (b) drops the true statement (A); option (d) includes the false statement (B).
 species   e-   protons  e-/p    ionic radius (A)
 O2-       10      8     1.25         1.40   <-- largest  (C true)
 F-        10      9     1.11         1.33
 Na+       10     11     0.91         0.95
 Mg2+      10     12     0.83         0.65   <-- smallest (D true)
 same e- count = isoelectronic (A true); Z differs 8,9,11,12 (B false)
 correct statements: (A), (C), (D)                  <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For any isoelectronic set, just write the charges in order −2, −1, 0, +1, +2 — size falls monotonically left to right, no data needed. And treat the phrase "same nuclear charge" as an automatic red flag: nuclear charge is fixed by the element, so two different elements can never share it. Recognising that one line kills options (a) and (d) instantly and leaves only the (A)-in-or-out decision.
Answer: (c) (A), (C) and (D) only.
39Given below are two statements: Statement I: The metallic radius of Na is 1.86 Å and the ionic radius of Na⁺ is lesser than 1.86 Å. Statement II: Ions are always smaller in size than the corresponding elements. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (b) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is true (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (d) Statement I is correct but Statement II is false [JEE Main 2024 (5 Apr Shift-2)]Metallic vs ionic radiusWhy cations shrink and anions swell relative to the parent atom🟡 SimilardStatement I is correct but Statement II is false
Concept Two different ideas are being tested. First, that a cation is always smaller than its parent atom: removing an electron (for Na, the entire 3s shell) both strips off an outer shell and raises the proton-to-electron ratio, so the remaining cloud contracts sharply. Second — and this is where the question bites — the sweeping generalisation that "ions are always smaller than atoms" is false, because anions are always larger than their parent atoms: the added electron increases inter-electronic repulsion in the valence shell while Z stays fixed, so the cloud expands.
Key relation r(Mn+) < r(M) and r(Xn−) > r(X) · for sodium Na (metallic) = 1.86 Å → Na⁺ ≈ 0.95–1.02 Å Na is 1s²2s²2p⁶3s¹; Na⁺ is 1s²2s²2p⁶, so an entire principal shell (n = 3) has vanished and 11 protons now hold only 10 electrons. · counter-example for Statement II: Cl = 0.99 Å but Cl⁻ = 1.81 Å.
Solution
1. Check Statement I numerically. The metallic radius of sodium is indeed 1.86 Å (half the internuclear distance in sodium metal), and the ionic radius of Na⁺ in its crystals is about 0.95 Å — comfortably less than 1.86 Å. Statement I is CORRECT.
2. Check Statement II as written: "ions are always smaller". One counter-example is enough to destroy it. O²⁻ (1.40 Å) is far larger than the O atom (0.66 Å); Cl⁻ (1.81 Å) is nearly double Cl (0.99 Å). Statement II is FALSE.The correct general rule is charge-dependent, not ion-dependent: cations shrink, anions swell.
3. Correct ⇒ I true, II false ⇒ option (d).
4. Eliminate: (a) and (b) both call Statement I wrong, contradicting the tabulated radii; (c) endorses the false universal claim in Statement II.
 species         config          radius (A)   vs parent atom
 Na  (metal)     [Ne]3s1           1.86           --
 Na+             [Ne]              0.95        SMALLER  (I correct)
 Cl  (covalent)  [Ne]3s2 3p5       0.99           --
 Cl-             [Ar]              1.81        LARGER   (II false)
 rule: cations shrink, anions swell            <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick In assertion/statement questions, hunt for absolute words — "always", "never", "all", "only". A single counter-example demolishes them, and examiners plant them deliberately. Here "ions are always smaller" is refuted by every halide ion you know. Also remember that metallic, covalent and van der Waals radii of the same element differ (Na: metallic 1.86 Å), so never compare a metallic radius with a covalent one; compare only like with like, or with the ionic radius as the question does.
Answer: (d) Statement I is correct but Statement II is false.
40Given below are two statements: Statement (I): The 4f and 5f-series of elements are placed separately in the Periodic table to preserve the principle of classification. Statement (II): s-block elements can be found in pure form in nature. In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer: (a) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are false [JEE Main 2024 (27 Jan Shift 1)]f-block placement; reactivity of s-blockStatement I: the 4f and 5f series are placed separately in the periodic table — worked🟢 ExactcStatement I is true but Statement II is false
Concept The long form of the periodic table is built on one organising principle: elements in the same group must share the same valence-shell configuration and hence similar chemistry. The fourteen lanthanoids all differ only in a buried 4f electron; chemically they are near-identical trivalent metals. If they were written into the main body they would need fourteen extra columns whose members would not be genuinely "different groups" at all, wrecking both the shape of the table and the group principle. So they are footnoted below the table — a presentational device that preserves the classification. The second statement tests something quite different: whether s-block metals occur free in nature. They do not — they are the most reactive metals known.
Statement 4f (La–Lu) and 5f (Ac–Lr) = inner-transition series, differentiating e⁻ enters (n−2)f the (n−2)f shell is two shells below the valence shell, so its filling barely changes the chemistry — the very reason these elements cannot form fourteen distinct groups. · s-block metals: very low IE₁, very high reducing power ⇒ found only as compounds Na, K, Ca etc. react instantly with air and water; they are stored under kerosene precisely because "pure form in nature" is impossible.
Solution
1. Statement (I). Lanthanoids and actinoids are placed in two separate rows at the foot of the table so that the main body keeps 18 columns and so that elements with genuinely different valence configurations are not forced into the same period-row. This is exactly "to preserve the principle of classification". TRUE.
2. Statement (II). The s-block contains the alkali and alkaline-earth metals. Their ionisation enthalpies are the lowest in each period, so they are powerful reducing agents that oxidise on contact with air and react violently with water. Consequently they occur in nature only as halides, carbonates, silicates and sulfates — never free. FALSE.Contrast the noble metals Au, Pt and Ag, which do occur native precisely because their ionisation enthalpies are high.
3. I true + II false ⇒ option (c).
4. Eliminate: (a) inverts both truth values; (b) endorses the false claim about s-block metals; (d) wrongly rejects the standard textbook reason for the f-block footnote.
 statement                                              verdict
 (I)  4f/5f footnoted to preserve classification         TRUE
      -- differentiating electron enters (n-2)f,
         chemistry nearly identical across the series
 (II) s-block elements occur in pure form in nature      FALSE
      -- lowest IE1 in each period; occur only as
         combined salts (NaCl, KCl, CaCO3, ...)
 I true, II false                                <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick A one-line memory hook for occurrence: the less reactive the metal, the more likely it is found native — Au, Pt, Ag (native) ≫ Cu (both) ≫ Fe, Zn (ores) ≫ Na, K, Ca (never native). Any statement claiming an alkali or alkaline-earth metal is found free is automatically false. On the f-block half, remember the standard NCERT wording "to preserve the principle of classification" verbatim — JEE recycles it, and here it is quoted word-for-word in the true statement.
Answer: (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false.
41Total number of ions from the following with noble gas configuration is _____ : Sr2+ (Z = 38), Cs+ (Z = 55), La2+ (Z = 57), Pb2+ (Z = 82), Yb2+ (Z = 70) and Fe2+ (Z = 26) [JEE Main 2024 (27 Jan Shift 2)]Noble-gas configuration of ionsTotal number of ions from the given list with noble-gas configuration — full solution🟢 Exact2only Sr²⁺ and Cs⁺
Concept An ion has a "noble gas configuration" only if its entire electron count equals that of a noble gas and the configuration terminates in a closed ns²np⁶ (or 1s² for He). Subtracting the charge from Z gives the electron count in one step, so the whole problem reduces to asking: does N = Z − charge equal 2, 10, 18, 36, 54 or 86? Where it does not, the leftover electrons sit in a d or f sub-shell — a pseudo-noble-gas core (ns²np⁶nd¹⁰) or an inert-pair core, which the question deliberately does not accept.
Key relation electrons in ion = Z − (charge) · noble-gas electron counts: He 2, Ne 10, Ar 18, Kr 36, Xe 54, Rn 86 any other count means the outermost sub-shell is d or f, not p, so the ion has a pseudo-noble-gas or inert-pair configuration instead. · remember that in cations the ns electrons leave before (n−1)d and before (n−2)f.
Solution
1. Sr²⁺: 38 − 2 = 36 e⁻ = [Kr]. ✓ noble gas.
2. Cs⁺: 55 − 1 = 54 e⁻ = [Xe]. ✓ noble gas.
3. La²⁺: 57 − 2 = 55 e⁻ = [Xe]5d¹ — one electron beyond xenon. ✗ (La³⁺ would have qualified; the question gives the 2+ ion on purpose.)
4. Pb²⁺: 82 − 2 = 80 e⁻ = [Xe]4f¹⁴5d¹⁰6s². ✗ — the inert 6s² pair is retained, so this is not a closed p⁶ shell.The inert-pair effect explains why Pb prefers +2 over +4: the 6s pair is poorly shielded by 4f and 5d electrons and is reluctant to ionise.
5. Yb²⁺: 70 − 2 = 68 e⁻ = [Xe]4f¹⁴. ✗ — a stable filled f configuration, which is why Yb²⁺ exists at all, but it is not a noble-gas configuration.
6. Fe²⁺: 26 − 2 = 24 e⁻ = [Ar]3d⁶. ✗.
7. Count the ticks: Sr²⁺ and Cs⁺ ⇒ 2.
 ion     Z    charge   e- left   configuration        noble gas?
 Sr2+    38     +2       36      [Kr]                    YES
 Cs+     55     +1       54      [Xe]                    YES
 La2+    57     +2       55      [Xe]5d1                  no
 Pb2+    82     +2       80      [Xe]4f14 5d10 6s2        no (inert pair)
 Yb2+    70     +2       68      [Xe]4f14                 no (filled f)
 Fe2+    26     +2       24      [Ar]3d6                  no
 count of YES                                     <-- answer (2)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not write configurations at all on the first pass — just compute Z − charge and compare against the six-number list 2, 10, 18, 36, 54, 86. Five seconds per ion. Only for a near-miss do you need the configuration, and then the three classic traps are: d¹⁰ pseudo-noble gas (Zn²⁺, Ag⁺, Cd²⁺, Cu⁺ — 18-electron shell, NOT accepted), inert pair (Tl⁺, Pb²⁺, Bi³⁺ — keep ns²), and filled f (Yb²⁺, Eu²⁺). Each of those three traps appears in this single question.
Answer: 2 — only Sr²⁺ and Cs⁺ have true noble-gas configurations.
42The element having the highest first ionization enthalpy is: (a) Si (b) Al (c) N (d) C [JEE Main 2024 (29 Jan Shift 2)]Highest IE₁ among Si, Al, N, CCorrect order of first ionization energy of Na, Mg, Al and Si🟡 SimilarcN (1402 kJ mol⁻¹)
Concept Two independent trends decide this in one glance. Going down a group IE₁ falls (the valence electron is farther out and better screened), and going right along a period IE₁ rises (Zeff grows at constant n). Of the four elements, N and C are period-2 while Al and Si are period-3, so both period-3 elements are already out of the running. Between C and N, nitrogen additionally enjoys the extra stability of a half-filled 2p³ sub-shell, so it wins comfortably.
Key relation IE₁ ↑ across a period, IE₁ ↓ down a group · exactly half-filled p³ ⇒ extra exchange stability ⇒ anomalously high IE removing an electron from N's px¹py¹pz¹ set destroys a symmetric, maximally exchange-stabilised arrangement, so it costs extra energy. · standard values (kJ mol⁻¹): Al 577 < Si 786 < C 1086 < N 1402.
Solution
1. Place the elements: C (group 14, period 2), N (group 15, period 2), Al (group 13, period 3), Si (group 14, period 3).
2. Compare within group 14: C is above Si, so IE₁(C) > IE₁(Si) — 1086 > 786. Option (a) is out.
3. Compare within period 3: Al (577) < Si (786) because Al's 3p¹ electron is shielded by the filled 3s² pair. So Al is the lowest of all four; option (b) is out.
4. Compare C and N, both in period 2: N lies further right (higher Zeff) and has the half-filled 2p³ bonus, so IE₁(N) = 1402 > IE₁(C) = 1086. Option (d) is out.
5. Highest is therefore N ⇒ option (c).
 element  group  period  config          IE1 (kJ/mol)
 Al        13      3     [Ne]3s2 3p1        577      lowest
 Si        14      3     [Ne]3s2 3p2        786
 C         14      2     [He]2s2 2p2       1086
 N         15      2     [He]2s2 2p3       1402      <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For "highest IE₁" questions, first cross out every element that is not in the topmost period present — going down a group is the single biggest destroyer of ionisation enthalpy. That alone removed Al and Si here. Then, among survivors, the one that is furthest right wins, unless a half-filled/fully-filled anomaly (Be vs B, N vs O) intervenes. Here the anomaly works for N, not against it, so N is doubly safe.
Answer: (c) N.
43If IUPAC name of an element is "Unununnium" then the element belongs to nth group of periodic table. The value of n is _____ . [JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 1)]IUPAC nomenclature of Z > 100"Unununnium" — decoding the IUPAC name and locating the group🟢 Exact11Z = 111, roentgenium (Rg), group 11
Concept Elements with Z > 100 are given provisional systematic names built digit-by-digit from the atomic number, so that priority disputes over discovery do not hold up nomenclature. Each digit is replaced by its numerical root, the roots are strung together in order, and the suffix -ium is added; the symbol is the three initial letters of the roots. Decode the name to get Z, then place Z in the periodic table by writing its configuration — that second step is what the question actually wants.
Key relation IUPAC numerical roots: 0 nil, 1 un, 2 bi, 3 tri, 4 quad, 5 pent, 6 hex, 7 sept, 8 oct, 9 enn · name = roots of the digits + "ium" an "i" or "n" is dropped where it would double up, which is why "un-un-un-ium" is written Unununnium. · group from configuration: (n−1)d¹⁰ns¹ ⇒ group 11.
Solution
1. Split the name: Un | un | un | nium → digits 1, 1, 1 ⇒ Z = 111, symbol Uuu.
2. Element 111 is now officially roentgenium, Rg.
3. Write its configuration by continuing the 6d series: Rn core (86) + 5f¹⁴ (100) + 6d⁹7s² … Rg = [Rn]5f¹⁴6d¹⁰7s¹, the heavier congener of Cu, Ag and Au.Like Cu/Ag/Au it adopts the d¹⁰s¹ arrangement rather than d⁹s², since a filled d sub-shell is extra stable.
4. Counting valence electrons for group number in the d-block: (n−1)d + ns = 10 + 1 = 11.
5. Cross-check by position: 111 − 100 = 11 columns into the 7th period beyond the actinoid footnote, landing directly under Au (Z = 79, group 11). So n = 11.
 name        Un   un   un   + ium
 digits       1    1    1          => Z = 111  (Uuu = Rg, roentgenium)
 config      [Rn] 5f14 6d10 7s1
 group    = (n-1)d + ns = 10 + 1 = 11   (below Cu, Ag, Au)
 n                                          <-- answer (11)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the roots by the mnemonic nil–un–bi–tri–quad–pent–hex–sept–oct–enn and remember that the symbol has exactly three letters taken from those roots (Uuu, Unp, Uus …). Then never guess the group from the last digit of Z — that only works by accident. Convert to a configuration: Z = 111 sits eleven places after the last noble gas core plus the f-block, so d¹⁰s¹ ⇒ group 11. The same route gives Z = 112 (Uub, Cn) group 12 and Z = 118 (Uuo, Og) group 18.
Answer: n = 11 (element 111, roentgenium, lies in group 11 below gold).
44The correct sequence of electron gain enthalpy of the elements listed below is: A. Ar B. Br C. F D. S. Choose the most appropriate from the options given below: (a) C > B > D > A (b) A > D > B > C (c) A > D > C > B (d) D > C > B > A [JEE Main 2024 (31 Jan Shift-1)]Electron gain enthalpy orderDecreasing order of electron gain enthalpy (magnitude only) for S and its neighbours🟡 Similara ⚠F > Br > S > Ar (by magnitude)
Concept Electron gain enthalpy ΔegH is the enthalpy change when one electron is added to a neutral gaseous atom. It is negative when the process releases energy (halogens, chalcogens) and positive when energy must be supplied (noble gases, and the alkaline-earth metals). Two structural facts control the four elements here: a noble gas has a closed ns²np⁶ shell so the incoming electron must start a brand-new shell (endothermic, positive); and within group 17, F is anomalously less exothermic than Cl but still slightly more exothermic than Br, because the tiny 2p sub-shell of fluorine is already crowded.
Key relation Standard values (kJ mol⁻¹): F −328, Cl −349, Br −325, I −295, O −141, S −200, Ar +96 Cl beats F because F's compact n = 2 valence shell makes electron–electron repulsion severe for the incoming electron; the same small-size argument explains why S is more exothermic than O. · noble gas ⇒ ΔegH > 0.
Solution
1. Assign values: A = Ar (+96), B = Br (−325), C = F (−328), D = S (−200).
2. Rank by the amount of energy released, i.e. by how strongly the atom wants the electron: F (328) > Br (325) > S (200) > Ar (releases nothing at all). That reads C > B > D > A ⇒ option (a), the keyed answer.
3. Why F edges out Br: F and Br are both one electron short of a closed shell, but F's extra electron enters a small n = 2 shell with high repulsion, so the two values are almost tied (328 vs 325). Sulphur, needing two electrons for a closed shell, releases much less (200), and argon already has a closed shell so it releases none.
4. Eliminate (c) and (d): (c) puts F below Br, and (d) makes S the most exothermic of all — both contradict the tabulated data outright.
⚠ Note Option (b), A > D > B > C, is the order you get if the four signed ΔegH values are compared algebraically: +96 > −200 > −325 > −328, i.e. Ar > S > Br > F. Read strictly as "electron gain enthalpy", that is the mathematically correct decreasing sequence, and (b) and the keyed option (a) are exact reverses of each other. The printed key takes the sequence to mean the tendency to gain an electron (magnitude of the negative ΔegH), which gives (a). The key letter (a) is shown above, flagged, because the two readings genuinely give different letters — if the paper's wording is read with signs, the answer is (b).
 letter  element  ΔegH (kJ/mol)   |ΔegH|
 (C)     F           -328           328   most exothermic
 (B)     Br          -325           325
 (D)     S           -200           200
 (A)     Ar           +96            --   endothermic (closed shell)
 by magnitude / tendency : C > B > D > A     <-- answer (a)  [key]
 by signed value         : A > D > B > C     <-- option (b)  [strict]
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a question mixes a noble gas into an electron-gain-enthalpy list, stop and ask which convention the options assume: put the noble gas at the top and the paper means signed values; put it at the bottom and the paper means magnitude. Only one of the two orders will appear among the options in most papers — here, unusually, both appear, which is exactly the trap. The safe data to carry in: Cl > F > Br > I and S > O in magnitude, with the second-period anomaly (F, O) caused by small size and high electron–electron repulsion.
Answer: (a) C > B > D > A, i.e. F > Br > S > Ar (ordering by magnitude of ΔegH; see the ⚠ note above).
45In case of isoelectronic species the size of F⁻, Ne and Na⁺ is affected by: (a) Principal quantum number n (b) None of the factors because their size is the same (c) Electron-electron interaction in the outer orbitals (d) Nuclear charge z [JEE Main 2024 (01 Feb Shift 1)]What controls isoelectronic sizeSize of isoelectronic species — which one is smaller and why🟡 SimilardNuclear charge z
Concept Atomic and ionic size is governed by three things: the principal quantum number of the outermost shell, the nuclear charge, and the mutual repulsion of the electrons. In an isoelectronic set two of those three are frozen — every species has the same number of electrons in the same shells, so n is identical and the total electron–electron repulsion is essentially identical too. That leaves exactly one variable free, the nuclear charge, and so the whole spread of sizes must be attributed to it. This is why isoelectronic series are the cleanest possible demonstration of the effect of Z on size.
Key relation Zeff = Z − σ, and with the electron count fixed, σ is fixed, so Zeff ∝ Z · r ∝ 1/Zeff ⇒ r falls as Z rises all three species are 1s²2s²2p⁶, so the screening constant σ for a 2p electron is the same number in each; only the proton count changes.
Solution
1. Configurations: F⁻ (9 + 1 = 10 e⁻), Ne (10 e⁻), Na⁺ (11 − 1 = 10 e⁻) — all 1s²2s²2p⁶.
2. Option (a): the valence shell is n = 2 in all three, so n cannot be the discriminating factor. Rejected.
3. Option (c): the number of electron pairs and the shells they occupy are identical, so the outer-orbital repulsion term is the same in each. Rejected.
4. Option (b): the sizes are demonstrably not equal — F⁻ 1.33 Å, Ne ≈ 1.12 Å (van der Waals-corrected estimate), Na⁺ 0.95 Å. Rejected.
5. Option (d): the nuclear charges are 9, 10 and 11. As Z rises, the same ten electrons are pulled in harder and the radius shrinks. This is the only factor left and it explains the data exactly. Correct.
 species   e-   Z    outer shell   radius trend
 F-        10    9       n=2        largest
 Ne        10   10       n=2        intermediate
 Na+       10   11       n=2        smallest
 n same, e--e- repulsion same; only Z varies
 size controlled by nuclear charge z      <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Turn every isoelectronic problem into a single ratio: electrons ÷ protons. Larger ratio ⇒ larger ion (F⁻ 10/9 = 1.11 > Ne 1.00 > Na⁺ 10/11 = 0.91). It reproduces both the ordering and the reason in one line, and it makes it obvious that the answer to "what causes the difference" can only be the proton count.
