Distractor (b) preys on the common misreading that isoelectronic means identical — it never means identical size.
Answer: (d) Nuclear charge z.
46Given below are two statements: Statement (I): Both metal and non-metal exist in the p-block and d-block elements. Statement (II): Non-metals have higher ionisation enthalpy and higher electronegativity than the metals. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are true [JEE Main 2024 (01 Feb Shift 2)]Metal/non-metal distribution; IE and ENPeriodic trends in electronegativity, valency and metallic character🟡 SimilarbStatement I is false but Statement II is true
Concept The metal/non-metal boundary is a staircase that runs through the p-block only. To its left every p-block element is a metal (Al, Ga, Sn, Pb, Bi …), to its right a non-metal (C, N, O, F, Cl …), with the metalloids B, Si, Ge, As, Sb, Te sitting on the line. The d-block is different: every d-block element is a metal without exception, because in each of them the outermost electrons are one or two loosely held ns electrons over a partly filled (n−1)d shell — the classic recipe for metallic bonding. So a claim that the d-block contains non-metals is false. The second statement is simply the definition of non-metallic character expressed through two periodic properties.
Statement p-block: metals + metalloids + non-metals · d-block: ALL metals (Sc → Zn, Y → Cd, La/Hf → Hg) even mercury, a liquid, is a metal; there is no non-metallic d-block element. · non-metals: high IE₁, high electronegativity, high (negative) ΔegH they hold their own electrons tightly and attract shared electrons strongly — the two properties are two faces of the same high Zeff.
Solution
1. Statement (I). The p-block half of the claim is true (Al is a metal, S is a non-metal). But the d-block half is false — all d-block elements are metals. A conjunction with a false half is false. Statement I is FALSE.
2. Statement (II). Compare across period 3: IE₁ Na 496 → Cl 1251 kJ mol⁻¹, and electronegativity Na 0.9 → Cl 3.0. The non-metals sit at the high end of both scales, the metals at the low end. Statement II is TRUE.Both trends follow from the same cause: along a period Zeff rises while n stays fixed, so the valence electrons are gripped more tightly.
3. I false + II true ⇒ option (b).
4. Eliminate: (c) and (d) both accept the false d-block claim; (a) wrongly rejects the standard IE/electronegativity contrast, which is the very definition of metallic vs non-metallic character.
 block   metals?   non-metals?   verdict for Statement I
 p        YES         YES        consistent
 d        YES         NO         breaks the claim   => I FALSE
 property        typical metal (Na)   typical non-metal (Cl)
 IE1 (kJ/mol)          496                 1251        II TRUE
 electronegativity      0.9                  3.0
 I false, II true                             <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the one-line block census: s-block — all metals (except H); p-block — metals, metalloids and non-metals; d-block — all metals; f-block — all metals. Any statement asserting a non-metal in the s, d or f block is instantly false, and this single fact settles a whole family of JEE statement questions without any further reasoning.
Answer: (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true.
47The electron affinity value are negative for: A. Be → Be⁻ B. N → N⁻ C. O → O2− D. Na → Na⁻ E. Al → Al⁻. Choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: (a) D and E only (b) A, B and C only (c) A and D only (d) A, B, D and E only [JEE Main 2024 (6 Apr Shift-1)]Sign of electron affinityThe electron affinity value are negative for Be→Be⁻, N→N⁻, O→O²⁻, Na→Na⁻, Al→Al⁻🟢 ExactbA, B and C only
Concept Electron affinity (EA) and electron gain enthalpy (ΔegH) carry opposite signs: EA = −ΔegH. EA is defined as the energy released when an electron is added, so a favourable, exothermic addition gives a positive EA and an unfavourable, endothermic addition gives a negative EA. The question therefore asks: for which of the five processes must energy be supplied? Three situations force energy in — adding an electron to a filled s² sub-shell, adding one to an exactly half-filled p³ sub-shell, and adding a second electron to an already negative ion.
Key relation EA = −ΔegH · endothermic addition ⇒ EA < 0 · the three destabilising cases: ns² (Be, Mg) — incoming e⁻ must enter the higher np orbital, np³ (N, P) — incoming e⁻ must pair up in a half-filled set, X⁻ + e⁻ → X²⁻ — a negative ion repels the incoming electron for oxygen the two steps are O + e⁻ → O⁻ (−141) and O⁻ + e⁻ → O²⁻ (+780), so the overall O → O²⁻ is strongly endothermic at about +639 kJ mol⁻¹.
Solution
1. A. Be → Be⁻. Be is [He]2s². The extra electron has to go into 2p, which is higher in energy and well shielded by the filled 2s². Endothermic ⇒ EA negative ✓.
2. B. N → N⁻. N is [He]2s²2p³, an exactly half-filled p sub-shell of maximum exchange stability. The incoming electron must pair inside an occupied p orbital, costing pairing energy. ΔegH is about +7 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ EA negative ✓.
3. C. O → O²⁻. The second step adds an electron to the already negative O⁻, against strong electrostatic repulsion; the net process is highly endothermic ⇒ EA negative ✓. (In ionic solids this cost is repaid by the lattice enthalpy, which is why O²⁻ exists in MgO but not as a free ion.)
4. D. Na → Na⁻. Na is [Ne]3s¹; the electron simply completes the 3s pair. ΔegH = −53 kJ mol⁻¹, exothermic ⇒ EA positive ✗.
5. E. Al → Al⁻. Al is [Ne]3s²3p¹; the electron enters a vacant 3p orbital with no pairing penalty. ΔegH = −43 kJ mol⁻¹, exothermic ⇒ EA positive ✗.
6. Negative EA for A, B and C ⇒ option (b). Option (a) lists exactly the two exothermic cases (the sign trap), (c) mixes one of each, and (d) wrongly includes D and E.
 process        config change             ΔegH (kJ/mol)   EA = -ΔegH
 A Be -> Be-    [He]2s2 -> 2s2 2p1           ~ +66          NEGATIVE
 B N  -> N-     2p3 -> 2p4 (pairing)         ~  +7          NEGATIVE
 C O  -> O2-    O(-141) then O-(+780)        ~ +639         NEGATIVE
 D Na -> Na-    3s1 -> 3s2                      -53         positive
 E Al -> Al-    3s2 3p1 -> 3s2 3p2              -43         positive
 negative EA: A, B, C                          <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Write "EA = −ΔegH" at the top of the page before touching the options — half the marks lost on this question are pure sign errors, and option (a) is placed first precisely to catch that slip. Then screen the processes by configuration alone: any addition to ns², np³, np⁶ or to an existing anion is endothermic; everything else is exothermic. No numerical data are needed.
Answer: (b) A, B and C only.
48The correct order of metallic character is: (a) K > Be > Ca (b) Be > Ca > K (c) Ca > K > Be (d) K > Ca > Be [JEE Main 2023 (10 April Shift 2)]Metallic character orderArranging Mg, Ca, K, Ge, Ga in increasing order of metallic character🟡 SimilardK > Ca > Be
Concept Metallic character means the ease with which an atom gives up its valence electrons — it is essentially the inverse of ionisation enthalpy. It therefore increases down a group (the valence electron moves into higher shells and is screened by more inner electrons, so it is lost easily) and decreases across a period (Zeff grows at constant n, so the atom clings to its electrons). Ranking K, Ca and Be is a matter of applying both trends once each.
Key relation metallic character ∝ 1/IE₁ ∝ electropositivity · ↑ down a group, ↓ across a period · supporting data (kJ mol⁻¹): IE₁: K 419 < Ca 590 < Be 899 the element that surrenders its electron most cheaply is the most metallic, so the metallic order is exactly the reverse of the IE₁ order.
Solution
1. Locate each element: K = group 1, period 4; Ca = group 2, period 4; Be = group 2, period 2.
2. K vs Ca (same period 4): K lies to the left, so K is more metallic — and indeed K needs to lose only one electron from a diffuse 4s¹ orbital. K > Ca.
3. Ca vs Be (same group 2): Ca is two periods lower with its valence electrons in n = 4 rather than n = 2, so Ca is far more metallic. Ca > Be.Be is so weakly metallic that it is amphoteric-leaning — BeO reacts with both acids and alkalis, unlike the strongly basic CaO.
4. Chaining: K > Ca > Be ⇒ option (d). The IE₁ data confirm it: 419 < 590 < 899.
5. Eliminate: (a) and (b) both place Be above Ca, contradicting the down-a-group trend; (c) places Ca above K, contradicting the across-a-period trend.
 element  group  period  IE1 (kJ/mol)   metallic character
 K          1      4         419              highest
 Ca         2      4         590              middle
 Be         2      2         899              lowest
 order: K > Ca > Be                      <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Read metallic character straight off the periodic table as "distance from fluorine": the further down-and-left an element sits, the more metallic it is (Cs/Fr the most, F the least). K is down-and-left of Ca, and Ca is far below Be — so the chain writes itself with no data at all. Use the IE₁ numbers only as a cross-check, remembering that they run in exactly the opposite direction to metallic character.
Answer: (d) K > Ca > Be.
49Which of the following represents the correct order of metallic character of the given elements? (JEE Main 2023, 25 Jan. Shift 2, Level-4) (a) Si < Be < Mg < K (b) Be < Si < Mg < K (c) K < Mg < Be < Si (d) Be < Si < K < MgMetallic character — periodic trendOrdering Si, Be, Mg and K by metallic character🟢 ExactaSi < Be < Mg < K
Concept Metallic character is simply the ease with which an atom gives up its valence electron — it is electropositivity, the mirror image of ionisation enthalpy. Anything that makes IE small (large radius, small effective nuclear charge, valence electron far from the nucleus) makes the element more metallic. Therefore metallic character falls left→right across a period (Zeff rises, radius shrinks) and rises down a group (a new shell each time, so the valence electron is farther out and better shielded). The only skill needed is to place the four elements on the grid and read off the two trends.
Key relation metallic character ∝ 1/IE₁ ∝ 1/electronegativity ∝ atomic size all four properties are driven by the same quantity, Zeff/r², so once you know one trend you know all of them. Positions: Be (Gp-2, Period-2), Mg (Gp-2, Period-3), K (Gp-1, Period-4), Si (Gp-14, Period-3).
Solution
1. Si vs Be. Si is a metalloid — the least metallic of the four. Even though Be sits in the metal block, Be is notoriously the least metallic group-2 element (its compounds are covalent). Comparing IE₁: Si 786, Be 899 kJ mol⁻¹, so on IE alone Be would look "less metallic". But metallic character is judged from the periodic position/electropositivity, and Si is a p-block metalloid that forms only covalent oxides (SiO₂ is purely acidic) while BeO is amphoteric — Be is genuinely more metallic. This is the intended NCERT-level reading: the group-2 element beats the group-14 metalloid.
2. Be vs Mg. Same group, Mg is one period lower ⇒ bigger radius, lower IE (737 vs 899), MgO strongly basic vs BeO amphoteric ⇒ Mg > Be.
3. Mg vs K. K is further left and further down (Period-4, Group-1), with IE₁ = 419 kJ mol⁻¹ — the smallest of all four — and K₂O is a strongly basic oxide ⇒ K is the most metallic.
4. Assemble the increasing chain: Si < Be < Mg < K, i.e. option (a).
5. Eliminations: (b) puts Si above Be — wrong, a metalloid cannot beat an alkaline-earth metal. (c) is the exact reverse (decreasing order) — the classic "read the direction" trap. (d) places K below Mg — impossible, K is both lower and further left.
 element  position        IE1 (kJ/mol)   oxide          metallic character
 Si       Gp-14, P-3          786        SiO2 acidic    least (metalloid)
 Be       Gp-2,  P-2          899        BeO amphoteric next
 Mg       Gp-2,  P-3          737        MgO basic      next
 K        Gp-1,  P-4          419        K2O strongly basic  most
 increasing order  Si < Be < Mg < K              <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not rank metallic character by IE₁ numbers alone — Be (899) has a higher IE₁ than Si (786), so a pure-IE argument gives the wrong answer here. Use the nature of the oxide as the tie-breaker instead: basic > amphoteric > acidic ⇒ K₂O > MgO > BeO > SiO₂, which reproduces the required order immediately. The distractor set always contains the reversed chain (option c) precisely because candidates forget whether "correct order" meant increasing or decreasing — the stem here shows "<" signs, so it is increasing.
Answer: (a) Si < Be < Mg < K
50Given below are two statements: Statement I: The decrease in first ionization enthalpy from B to Al is much larger than that from Al to Ga. Statement II: The d orbitals in Ga are completely filled. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below (JEE Main 2023, 29 Jan. Shift 2, Level-4) (a) Statement I is incorrect but statement II is correct (b) Both the statements I and II are correct (c) Statement I is correct but statement II is incorrect (d) Both the statements I and II are incorrectGroup-13 IE anomaly, d-block contractionWhy IE falls sharply B→Al but not Al→Ga🟢 Exactbboth statements are correct
Concept Going down group 13 the ionisation enthalpy should fall steadily, because each step adds a shell. It does so from B to Al — and then stops falling. Between Al and Ga the whole 3d series is inserted, and the ten 3d electrons shield the outer 4p electron very poorly (d orbitals are diffuse and non-penetrating). The extra nuclear charge of +10 is therefore only partly cancelled, Zeff on the valence electron actually rises, and Ga ends up with an IE₁ marginally higher than Al. This is the "d-block contraction" or "scandide contraction" effect, and it is the whole point of the question.
Key data IE₁: B 801, Al 577, Ga 579, In 558, Tl 589 kJ mol⁻¹ · ΔIE(B→Al) = 801 − 577 = 224 kJ mol⁻¹ (a large drop) · ΔIE(Al→Ga) = 577 − 579 = −2 kJ mol⁻¹ (no drop at all; a slight rise) a decrease of 224 against a decrease of essentially zero is exactly what "much larger" means.
Solution
1. Statement I. B → Al: the valence electron moves from 2p to 3p, a genuine new shell with good s- and p-shielding, so IE collapses by 224 kJ mol⁻¹. Al → Ga: a new shell is added (3p → 4p) but ten 3d electrons are added too. Poor d-shielding ⇒ Zeff on the 4p electron is high enough to cancel the size increase, so IE does not decrease. Statement I is CORRECT.
2. Statement II. Ga has Z = 31, configuration [Ar]3d¹⁰4s²4p¹. The 3d sub-shell holds its full complement of 10 electrons. Statement II is CORRECT.
3. Both are true, so the answer is (b). Note that Statement II is also the cause of Statement I — the completely filled, poorly shielding 3d¹⁰ core is precisely why the expected drop from Al to Ga never happens. The paper only asks for truth values, but the causal link is worth seeing.
4. Eliminations: (a) and (d) both call Statement I incorrect — contradicted by the data (224 vs −2 kJ mol⁻¹). (c) calls Statement II incorrect — but 3d¹⁰ in Ga is a plain configuration fact.
 step        configuration change              Delta IE1 (kJ/mol)
 B  -> Al    2s2 2p1  -> 3s2 3p1                801 -> 577 = -224  (big drop)
 Al -> Ga    3s2 3p1  -> 3d10 4s2 4p1           577 -> 579 = +2    (no drop)
 cause: 3d10 shields poorly, Zeff on 4p stays high
 Statement I  : TRUE      Statement II : TRUE   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the group-13 IE₁ zig-zag as 801 → 577 → 579 → 558 → 589 ("big fall, then flat, small dip, rise"). The two rises (Al→Ga and In→Tl) are caused by the d-block contraction and the lanthanoid contraction respectively, and between them they generate half a dozen JEE questions per year. The trap here is a candidate who "knows" that IE always decreases down a group and therefore marks Statement I false; the second trap is treating Statement II as irrelevant trivia when it is actually the mechanism.
Answer: (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct.
51Given below are two statements: one is labelled as Assertion (A) and the other is labelled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): The first ionization enthalpy of 3d series elements is more than that of group 2 metals. Reason (R): In 3d series of elements successive filling of d-orbitals takes place. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below (JEE Main 2023, 31 Jan. Shift 2, Level-4) (a) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A) (b) (A) is true but (R) is false (c) (A) is false but (R) is true (d) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)IE of 3d series vs s-blockAssertion–Reason: 3d-series IE compared with group-2 metals🟢 Exactdboth true, but (R) is not the correct explanation
Concept An Assertion–Reason item has three separate decisions: is A true, is R true, and — only if both are true — does R cause A? Here A is the standard NCERT statement that d-block ionisation enthalpies lie above those of the s-block (and below the p-block) of the same period. R is a bald description of what happens along the 3d row. The examiner's point is that describing the filling of d orbitals is not the same as explaining why the electron is harder to remove — the explanation needs the extra idea of poor shielding by d electrons and the resulting rise in Zeff.
Key data Period-4 group-2: Ca 590 kJ mol⁻¹ vs 3d series: Sc 633, Ti 659, V 651, Cr 653, Mn 717, Fe 762, Co 760, Ni 737, Cu 745, Zn 906 kJ mol⁻¹ every 3d element of period 4 exceeds Ca, so the assertion holds when compared period-wise, which is how NCERT frames it.
Solution
1. Is (A) true? Yes. Along period 4, IE₁ jumps from Ca (590) to Sc (633) and keeps climbing to Zn (906). NCERT states it directly: the first ionisation enthalpies of d-block elements are higher than those of s-block elements and lower than those of p-block elements of the same period.
2. Is (R) true? Yes. Sc → Zn is by definition the successive filling of the 3d sub-shell: 3d¹4s² … 3d¹⁰4s².
3. Does (R) explain (A)? Not by itself. The mere act of adding electrons to d orbitals would, taken alone, lower IE by adding shielding electrons. The real cause is that d electrons shield very poorly (they are non-penetrating), so each added proton is only partly cancelled; Zeff felt by the 4s electron therefore increases across the series, and the 4s electron is held more tightly. R states the observation, not the mechanism ⇒ R is a true statement that is not the correct explanation.
4. Hence (d): both true, R not the explanation. Eliminations: (b) and (c) each declare one of the statements false, but both are demonstrably true; (a) would be right only if "filling of d-orbitals" were itself the causal mechanism, which it is not — poor screening is.
 element   Z    config          IE1 (kJ/mol)
 Ca       20    [Ar]4s2             590      <- group 2 reference
 Sc       21    [Ar]3d1 4s2         633
 Mn       25    [Ar]3d5 4s2         717
 Zn       30    [Ar]3d10 4s2        906
 A: 3d IE1 > group-2 IE1                TRUE
 R: successive filling of d orbitals    TRUE  (but only a description)
 real cause: poor screening by 3d ⇒ Zeff on 4s rises   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick In Assertion–Reason questions, test the Reason with the question "would this alone force the Assertion to be true?" A Reason that merely restates a definition or describes the region of the table almost always scores "true but not the explanation" (option d here). Note also the deliberate wording of A: compared against Be (899), most 3d metals have a lower IE₁ — the statement only survives if you compare within the same period (against Ca, 590), which is the NCERT convention.
Answer: (d) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A).
52The correct increasing order of the ionic radii is (JEE Main 2023, 31 Jan. Shift 1, Level-4) (a) Cl⁻ < Ca2+ < K⁺ < S2− (b) K⁺ < S2− < Ca2+ < Cl⁻ (c) S2− < Cl⁻ < Ca2+ < K⁺ (d) Ca2+ < K⁺ < Cl⁻ < S2−Isoelectronic ionic radiiIncreasing ionic radii of Cl⁻, Ca²⁺, K⁺ and S²⁻🟢 ExactdCa²⁺ < K⁺ < Cl⁻ < S²⁻
Concept All four ions carry 18 electrons — they are isoelectronic with argon. When the electron count is fixed, the electron–electron repulsion and the shielding are essentially the same for every species, so the only variable left is the nuclear charge Z. More protons pulling on the same 18 electrons means a tighter, smaller ion. Hence for an isoelectronic series, radius falls monotonically as Z rises, i.e. radius rises as the charge becomes more negative.
Key relation rion ∝ 1 / (Z/e⁻ ratio) = 1 / (Z ÷ 18) ⇒ for isoelectronic ions: larger Z ⇒ smaller radius equivalently, arrange by charge: highest positive charge is smallest, highest negative charge is largest.
Solution
1. Count electrons to confirm the series: S²⁻ (16 + 2 = 18), Cl⁻ (17 + 1 = 18), K⁺ (19 − 1 = 18), Ca²⁺ (20 − 2 = 18). All 18 ⇒ isoelectronic, all with the [Ne]3s²3p⁶ configuration.
2. List the nuclear charges in decreasing order: Ca (20) > K (19) > Cl (17) > S (16).
3. Radius is inversely ordered: Ca²⁺ < K⁺ < Cl⁻ < S²⁻. Literature values 100, 138, 181, 184 pm confirm it.
4. Equivalent one-line shortcut: order by charge from most positive to most negative — 2+, 1+, 1−, 2− — which gives the same chain without any arithmetic.
5. Eliminations: (a) puts Cl⁻ below Ca²⁺ (an anion cannot be smaller than a doubly charged cation of the same series); (b) scrambles the sequence entirely; (c) is the decreasing order — the reversal trap.
 ion     Z    electrons   Z/e-     ionic radius (pm)
 Ca2+   20       18       1.111          100      smallest
 K+     19       18       1.056          138
 Cl-    17       18       0.944          181
 S2-    16       18       0.889          184      largest
 increasing radius:  Ca2+ < K+ < Cl- < S2-      <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick The instant an isoelectronic set appears, stop thinking about periods and groups and write the ions in order of charge: 3+ < 2+ < 1+ < neutral < 1− < 2− < 3− in size. It works every time and takes two seconds. The distractors in this family are always (i) the exact reverse and (ii) a version in which one cation and one anion are swapped, so after choosing your chain check that every cation precedes every anion.
Answer: (d) Ca²⁺ < K⁺ < Cl⁻ < S²⁻
53Given below are two statements: one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: The energy required to form Mg2+ from Mg is much higher than that required to produce Mg⁺. Reason R: Mg2+ is small ion and carry more charge than Mg⁺. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below. (JEE Main 2023, 10 April Shift 2, Level-4) (a) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A (b) A is true but R is false (c) A is false but R is true (d) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of ASuccessive ionisation enthalpiesAssertion–Reason on the energy to form Mg²⁺ versus Mg⁺🟢 Exactdboth true and R correctly explains A
Concept Successive ionisation enthalpies always increase, and the reason is purely electrostatic: once an electron has left, the remaining electrons are held by an unchanged nuclear charge but there are fewer of them to repel one another and to shield. The ion contracts, the charge-to-size ratio jumps, and the next electron is bound far more tightly. For Mg the "energy required to form Mg²⁺ from Mg" is IE₁ + IE₂ measured against IE₁ alone — but the item is really comparing the second ionisation step with the first.
Key data Mg → Mg⁺ + e⁻, IE₁ = 738 kJ mol⁻¹ · Mg⁺ → Mg²⁺ + e⁻, IE₂ = 1451 kJ mol⁻¹ · IE₂/IE₁ ≈ 1.97 · r(Mg) = 160 pm, r(Mg⁺) ≈ 82 pm, r(Mg²⁺) = 72 pm the ion shrinks at every step while the +ve charge grows, so the attraction on the next electron rises sharply.
Solution
1. Assertion A. Total energy to reach Mg²⁺ = IE₁ + IE₂ = 738 + 1451 = 2189 kJ mol⁻¹, against 738 kJ mol⁻¹ to reach Mg⁺ only. Even step-for-step, IE₂ (1451) is roughly twice IE₁ (738). A is TRUE.
2. Reason R. Mg²⁺ (72 pm, charge +2) is both smaller and more highly charged than Mg⁺ (≈82 pm, charge +1). R is TRUE as a factual statement.
3. Is R the explanation? Yes. The electron being removed in the second step is pulled out of a species that is already positively charged and contracted; the electrostatic work needed to overcome that stronger attraction is precisely why IE₂ > IE₁. Removing an electron from a cation is always harder than from the neutral atom, and the harder the more charged and compact the cation is. Unlike Q51, here the Reason names the actual physical cause (charge density), not just a description.
4. Answer (d). Eliminations: (b) and (c) each falsify a true statement; (a) accepts both but denies the causal link, which would only be right if Mg⁺ and Mg²⁺ had the same size and charge — they do not.
 step                    energy (kJ/mol)   species left   radius(pm)  charge
 Mg  -> Mg+  + e-             738             Mg+            ~82        +1
 Mg+ -> Mg2+ + e-            1451             Mg2+            72        +2
 Mg  -> Mg2+ + 2e-           2189  (= 738 + 1451)
 smaller ion + larger charge  =>  stronger pull  =>  IE2 > IE1
 A TRUE, R TRUE, R explains A                     <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Read this item as "IE₂ > IE₁ — why?" and the answer writes itself. Remember the diagnostic jump rule too: for Mg the big leap comes at IE₃ (7733 kJ mol⁻¹, breaking the neon core), not at IE₂ — that jump is how you identify an element's group from a list of successive IEs. The trap in this question is the phrase "much higher": some candidates read it as demanding an order-of-magnitude jump, decide 1451 vs 738 is "only double" and wrongly mark A false.
Answer: (d) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A.
54The difference between electron gain enthalpies will be maximum between: (JEE Main 2023, 06 Apr Shift 1, Level-3) (a) Ne and F (b) Ar and F (c) Ne and Cl (d) Ar and ClElectron gain enthalpy — extremesWhich pair shows the largest gap in electron gain enthalpy🟢 ExactcNe and Cl
Concept Electron gain enthalpy ΔegH is the enthalpy change when a gaseous atom accepts an electron. Halogens release a great deal of energy (large negative ΔegH) because the incoming electron completes an ns²np⁶ octet. Noble gases must place the electron in the next shell against a completed octet, so energy has to be supplied — their ΔegH is positive and is the most positive of any element in that period. The biggest gap therefore pairs the most positive noble gas with the most negative halogen, and you must compare signed values, not magnitudes.
Key data ΔegH (kJ mol⁻¹): F = −328, Cl = −349, Ne = +116, Ar = +96 Cl is more negative than F because the small 2p sub-shell of F is already crowded, so the incoming electron suffers extra repulsion; Ne is more positive than Ar because the electron would have to enter the compact n = 3 shell of a very small atom.
Solution
1. Compute each gap as |ΔegH(noble gas) − ΔegH(halogen)|.
2. (a) Ne and F: |(+116) − (−328)| = 444 kJ mol⁻¹.
3. (b) Ar and F: |(+96) − (−328)| = 424 kJ mol⁻¹.
4. (c) Ne and Cl: |(+116) − (−349)| = 465 kJ mol⁻¹ — the largest.
5. (d) Ar and Cl: |(+96) − (−349)| = 445 kJ mol⁻¹.
6. Maximum difference = 465 kJ mol⁻¹ for Ne and Cl ⇒ option (c). Logic without arithmetic: pick the most positive of the two noble gases (Ne, +116) and the most negative of the two halogens (Cl, −349); those two extremes must give the widest spread.
 pair          values (kJ/mol)          |difference|
 Ne and F      +116  and  -328               444
 Ar and F       +96  and  -328               424
 Ne and Cl     +116  and  -349               465   <-- answer (c)
 Ar and Cl      +96  and  -349               445
 rule: most positive noble gas (Ne) with most negative halogen (Cl)
⚡ JEE Trick Fix two exceptions in memory and this whole family becomes free: Cl > F in the magnitude of ΔegH (−349 vs −328), and among noble gases Ne is the most positive (+116, larger than He's +48 and Ar's +96). The trap is students who "know" fluorine is the most electronegative element and therefore assume it must also have the most negative electron gain enthalpy — that reflex sends them to option (a).
Answer: (c) Ne and Cl — a difference of about 465 kJ mol⁻¹.
55Inert gases have positive electron gain enthalpy. Its correct order is (JEE Main 2023, 25 Jan. Shift 1, Level-1) (a) Xe < Kr < Ne < He (b) He < Ne < Kr < Xe (c) He < Xe < Kr < Ne (d) He < Kr < Xe < NePositive ΔegH of noble gasesOrdering the positive electron gain enthalpies of He, Ne, Kr and Xe🟢 ExactcHe < Xe < Kr < Ne
Concept A noble gas already has a closed ns²np⁶ shell, so an extra electron has to be forced into the next principal shell, which is entirely empty and well shielded from the nucleus. Energy must be supplied, so ΔegH is positive for every member of group 18. The size of that positive value measures how strongly the atom resists the extra electron: the smaller and more compact the atom, the greater the repulsion from the existing closed shell and the larger the positive ΔegH. That predicts a general decrease down the group — with helium as the single exception, because He has no p sub-shell at all and the incoming electron enters a 2s orbital which is relatively accessible.
Key data ΔegH (kJ mol⁻¹): He +48, Ne +116, Ar +96, Kr +96, Xe +77 Ne is the maximum; from Ne downwards the values fall steadily as the atoms grow; He is anomalously low because its added electron goes into 2s (1s² + e⁻ → 1s²2s¹) rather than into a fresh shell above a completed octet.
Solution
1. Write the four requested values: He +48, Ne +116, Kr +96, Xe +77.
2. Sort in increasing order: 48 (He) < 77 (Xe) < 96 (Kr) < 116 (Ne).
3. So the increasing chain is He < Xe < Kr < Ne, i.e. option (c).
4. Structural reading of the same result: within Ne, Kr, Xe the value simply falls with increasing size (Ne > Kr > Xe, since a bigger atom's outer shell repels the incoming electron less), and He is pulled out of that trend to the bottom by its 1s-only configuration.
5. Eliminations: (a) is the reversed chain of a wrong sequence and puts He at the top; (b) is the naive "increases down the group" guess; (d) swaps Kr and Xe, ignoring that ΔegH decreases from Kr (96) to Xe (77).
 element   Z     added electron enters     Delta_eg H (kJ/mol)
 He         2     2s                             +48    lowest
 Xe        54     6s                             +77
 Kr        36     5s                             +96
 Ne        10     3s                            +116    highest
 increasing:  He < Xe < Kr < Ne                 <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the single sentence "Ne has the most positive electron gain enthalpy of all elements; He is the odd one out at the bottom of group 18" and every noble-gas ΔegH question collapses. The designed trap is option (b), He < Ne < Kr < Xe, which is what you get by blindly applying "properties increase down a group" — for a positive ΔegH the trend actually runs the other way once you are past neon.
Answer: (c) He < Xe < Kr < Ne
56Match List-I with List-II. LIST-I (Atomic number): (A) 37, (B) 78, (C) 52, (D) 65. LIST-II (Block of periodic table): (i) p-block, (ii) d-block, (iii) f-block, (iv) s-block. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2023, 30 Jan. Shift 1, Level-1) (a) A-ii, B-iv, C-i, D-iii (b) A-i, B-iii, C-iv, D-ii (c) A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i (d) A-iv, B-ii, C-i, D-iiiBlocks of the periodic table from ZMatching atomic numbers 37, 78, 52 and 65 to their blocks🟢 ExactdA-iv, B-ii, C-i, D-iii
Concept The block of an element is decided by the sub-shell that receives the last electron in the Aufbau order 1s 2s 2p 3s 3p 4s 3d 4p 5s 4d 5p 6s 4f 5d 6p 7s 5f 6d. You do not need to write the whole configuration — you only need to know where each block begins and ends along the Z axis, because the blocks occupy contiguous stretches of atomic number. Learning those boundaries turns any "given Z, name the block" question into a look-up.
Key relation Period-5: Z = 37–38 s-block · 39–48 d-block (4d) · 49–54 p-block. Period-6: Z = 55–56 s-block · 57 (La) & 58–71 f-block (4f) · 72–80 d-block (5d) · 81–86 p-block the lanthanoid stretch 58–71 (Ce→Lu) is the one most often mis-remembered; anything in the 58–71 or 90–103 window is f-block.
Solution
1. (A) Z = 37 → Rb. Configuration [Kr]5s¹; last electron enters 5s ⇒ s-block ⇒ (iv).
2. (B) Z = 78 → Pt. [Xe]4f¹⁴5d⁹6s¹; the differentiating electron is in 5d ⇒ d-block ⇒ (ii). 78 lies inside the 72–80 window of the third transition series, so it is d-block regardless of the 5d⁹6s¹ irregularity.
3. (C) Z = 52 → Te. [Kr]4d¹⁰5s²5p⁴; last electron enters 5p ⇒ p-block ⇒ (i).
4. (D) Z = 65 → Tb. [Xe]4f⁹6s²; last electron enters 4f ⇒ f-block (a lanthanoid) ⇒ (iii).
5. Assembled pairing A-iv, B-ii, C-i, D-iii = option (d). Eliminations: (a) makes Rb d-block and Pt s-block — both impossible; (b) makes Rb p-block; (c) makes Pt f-block and Tb p-block. Only (d) survives, and in fact matching just (A) → s-block eliminates (a) and (b) at a glance.
 List-I   Z    element   configuration            last e- enters   block   List-II
 (A)     37    Rb        [Kr] 5s1                      5s          s        (iv)
 (B)     78    Pt        [Xe] 4f14 5d9 6s1             5d          d        (ii)
 (C)     52    Te        [Kr] 4d10 5s2 5p4             5p          p        (i)
 (D)     65    Tb        [Xe] 4f9 6s2                  4f          f        (iii)
 A-iv, B-ii, C-i, D-iii                                     <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not build configurations — memorise the four f-block/d-block windows: 21–30, 39–48, 72–80 and 104–112 are d-block; 58–71 and 90–103 are f-block; the first two columns of every period are s-block and the last six are p-block. Then Z = 65 is instantly a lanthanoid. In match-the-column items always match the entry you are most certain of first (here Rb) — a single confident pairing usually kills two of the four options.
Answer: (d) A-iv, B-ii, C-i, D-iii
57The correct order of electronegativity for given elements is (JEE Main 2023, 08 April Shift 1, Level-1) (a) P > Br > C > At (b) Br > P > At > C (c) Br > C > At > P (d) C > P > At > BrElectronegativity orderingRanking Br, C, At and P by electronegativity🟢 ExactcBr > C > At > P
Concept Electronegativity measures an atom's pull on a shared electron pair. It rises left→right across a period (Zeff grows, size shrinks) and falls down a group (the bonding electrons sit farther from the nucleus). Because the four elements here come from three different periods and three different groups, no single trend settles the order — you must fall back on the Pauling values, which is why the question is best solved by recalling the short table of common electronegativities rather than by reasoning about position.
Key data Pauling scale: F 4.0 > O 3.5 > N ≈ Cl 3.0 > Br 2.8–2.96 > C 2.55 > S 2.5 > I ≈ At 2.2 > P 2.19 > H 2.1 note the two near-ties that this question exploits: Br sits just below Cl, and At (≈2.2) is a shade above P (2.19).
Solution
1. Write the four values: Br = 2.96, C = 2.55, At ≈ 2.2, P = 2.19.
2. Br is the highest. It is a halogen — one electron short of an octet, with a large Zeff — and although it lies below Cl in group 17, it is still more electronegative than any non-halogen here.
3. C next. Carbon (2.55) beats both third-period P and sixth-period At because it is a small period-2 atom whose bonding electrons are close to the nucleus.
4. At vs P is the deciding comparison. At is a halogen (group 17), so despite being the heaviest element in the list its group position keeps it marginally above phosphorus, a group-15 element: 2.2 > 2.19.
5. Decreasing order: Br > C > At > P ⇒ option (c). Eliminations: (a) and (d) put P above Br or above C — no p-block group-15 element outranks a halogen or carbon; (b) places P above At, reversing the halogen-vs-pnictogen comparison.
 element   group / period    Pauling electronegativity
 Br        17 / 4                     2.96      highest
 C         14 / 2                     2.55
 At        17 / 6                     2.2
 P         15 / 3                     2.19      lowest
 decreasing order:  Br > C > At > P              <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Commit the mnemonic ladder F 4.0, O 3.5, N/Cl 3.0, Br 2.8, C 2.5, S 2.5, I 2.5, H 2.1, P 2.1 to memory — nine numbers that answer essentially every electronegativity ordering question in JEE. Two facts do the work here: C > P (period-2 beats period-3 in the same diagonal region) and At > P (halogen beats pnictogen even six periods down). Watch the direction of the inequality signs: this stem uses ">", so decreasing order is wanted.
Answer: (c) Br > C > At > P
58For elements B, C, N, Li, Be, O and F, the correct order of first ionisation enthalpy is (JEE Main 2023, 11 April Shift 1, Level-1) (a) Li < Be < B < C < O < N < F (b) B < Li < Be < C < N < O < F (c) Li < Be < B < C < N < O < F (d) Li < B < Be < C < O < N < FPeriod-2 IE₁ order with both anomaliesFirst ionisation enthalpy order for Li, Be, B, C, N, O and F🟢 ExactdLi < B < Be < C < O < N < F
Concept Ionisation enthalpy rises across period 2 because Zeff increases while the shell stays the same — but the rise is broken twice, and this single question tests both breaks at once. Break 1 (Be → B): boron's electron comes from a higher-energy, less penetrating 2p orbital, whereas beryllium's comes from a filled, stable 2s²; so IE falls. Break 2 (N → O): nitrogen's 2p³ is exactly half-filled and extra stable, while oxygen's 2p⁴ has one doubly occupied p orbital whose electron–electron repulsion helps the electron leave; so IE falls again. Every other step increases.
Key data IE₁ (kJ mol⁻¹): Li 520, Be 899, B 801, C 1086, N 1402, O 1314, F 1681 two dips — Be→B (899 → 801) and N→O (1402 → 1314) — both restored on the following step.
Solution
1. Sort the seven values in increasing order: 520 (Li) < 801 (B) < 899 (Be) < 1086 (C) < 1314 (O) < 1402 (N) < 1681 (F).
2. That is Li < B < Be < C < O < N < F ⇒ option (d).
3. Justify the two inversions: B < Be because removing 2p¹ (shielded by 2s², poorly penetrating) is easier than breaking into the filled 2s² of Be. O < N because N's 2p³ half-filled set is exchange-stabilised while O's 2p⁴ pays an inter-electronic repulsion penalty in the doubly occupied orbital.
4. Eliminations: (c) is the "no anomaly at all" answer — it has both Be < B and N < O, and so contains two errors. (a) fixes the N/O inversion but misses the Be/B one. (b) puts B below Li, which is impossible since IE increases across a period from lithium. Only (d) carries both anomalies.
 element   config        IE1 (kJ/mol)   comment
 Li        2s1               520        lowest, easily loses 2s1
 B         2s2 2p1           801        DIP: 2p is higher in energy than 2s
 Be        2s2               899        stable filled 2s2
 C         2s2 2p2          1086
 O         2s2 2p4          1314        DIP: pairing repulsion in one 2p orbital
 N         2s2 2p3          1402        half-filled, extra stable
 F         2s2 2p5          1681        highest
 increasing:  Li < B < Be < C < O < N < F        <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick For period 2, remember the shape rather than the numbers: "up, DOWN at B, up, up, DOWN at O, up". Whenever a period-2 or period-3 IE-ordering option shows an entirely smooth increase, it is almost certainly the distractor — the paper is testing whether you remember the Be/B and N/O reversals. The same pair of anomalies reappears in period 3 as Mg > Al and P > S.
Answer: (d) Li < B < Be < C < O < N < F
59Which of the following statements are not correct? (A) The electron gain enthalpy of F is more negative than that of Cl. (B) Ionization enthalpy decreases in a group of periodic table. (C) The electronegativity of an atom depends upon the atoms bonded to it. (D) Al2O3 and NO are examples of amphoteric oxides. Choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2023, 13 April Shift 1, Level-1) (a) A, B, C and D (b) A, B and D only (c) B and D only (d) A, C and D onlyMixed periodic-property statementsNeutral versus amphoteric oxides — NO, CO and N₂O🟡 SimilardA, C and D only
Concept A "which are NOT correct" item is graded on the whole set, so the fastest route is to classify the two statements you are certain about and let the option list do the rest. Two of these four are unambiguous: (A) reverses the famous F/Cl electron-gain-enthalpy anomaly, and (D) calls NO amphoteric when it is a textbook neutral oxide. Any correct answer must therefore contain both A and D, and must exclude any statement that is actually true.
Key data ΔegH: F = −328, Cl = −349 kJ mol⁻¹ ⇒ Cl is the more negative · IE₁ down group 1: Li 520 > Na 496 > K 419 > Rb 403 > Cs 376 ⇒ IE really does decrease down a group · oxide types: Al₂O₃, ZnO, BeO, PbO, SnO = amphoteric; CO, NO, N₂O, H₂O = neutral.
Solution
1. (A) NOT correct. Chlorine, not fluorine, has the more negative ΔegH (−349 vs −328). Fluorine's 2p sub-shell is so compact that the incoming electron meets heavy inter-electronic repulsion, which cancels much of the energy released. So A belongs in the answer.
2. (B) CORRECT. Down a group each successive element has an extra shell, so the valence electron is farther out and better shielded; IE₁ falls (520 → 376 kJ mol⁻¹ from Li to Cs). B is a true statement and must be excluded from the answer.
3. (D) NOT correct. Al₂O₃ is genuinely amphoteric (it dissolves in both HCl and NaOH), but NO is a neutral oxide — it forms neither an acid nor a salt with water, acids or bases. A statement that bundles a neutral oxide in with the amphoteric ones is false, so D belongs in the answer.
4. Selecting the option. The answer must contain A and D but not B. That kills (a) A,B,C,D and (b) A,B,D and (c) B,D — all three include B. The only survivor is (d) A, C and D only. The elimination is watertight regardless of how you judge (C): three of the four options contain the demonstrably true statement B, so (d) is forced.
5. About (C). The examiner treats "the electronegativity of an atom depends upon the atoms bonded to it" as not correct, reading electronegativity as a fixed property tabulated for each element. It is worth knowing that in NCERT's own words electronegativity is not a strictly invariant quantity — it varies with hybridisation and with the bonding partner (sp carbon is more electronegative than sp³ carbon). Fortunately you never have to adjudicate this: the presence of the true statement B in every other option decides the question.
 statement                                             verdict
 (A) Delta_eg H of F more negative than Cl             NOT CORRECT (Cl -349 < F -328)
 (B) ionisation enthalpy decreases down a group        CORRECT    (Li 520 -> Cs 376)
 (C) electronegativity depends on bonded atoms         taken as NOT CORRECT by the key
 (D) Al2O3 and NO are amphoteric oxides                NOT CORRECT (NO is neutral)
 options containing B are impossible  =>  only A, C, D remains  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick In multi-statement questions, hunt for the statement that is definitely true and delete every option containing it — that is usually faster and safer than adjudicating the debatable one. Here B is unarguably true and appears in three of the four options. Content traps to store: Cl beats F in electron gain enthalpy, and the four standard neutral oxides CO, NO, N₂O and H₂O, which examiners love to disguise as amphoteric.
Answer: (d) A, C and D only
60Given below are two statements. One is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: The first ionization enthalpy for oxygen is lower than that of nitrogen. Reason R: The four electrons in 2p orbitals of oxygen experience more electron-electron repulsion. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below. (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jun Shift 2, Level-4) (a) Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A (b) Both A and R are correct but R is NOT the correct explanation of A (c) A is correct but R is not correct (d) A is not correct but R is correctN vs O ionisation anomalyAssertion–Reason: why oxygen's first IE is below nitrogen's🟢 Exactaboth correct and R explains A
Concept Across period 2 the nuclear charge rises from N to O, so IE₁ "should" rise — yet it falls. The cause lies in the electron configurations: nitrogen is 1s²2s²2px¹2py¹2pz¹, an exactly half-filled p sub-shell with maximum exchange energy and no p–p pairing at all. Oxygen is 1s²2s²2px²2py¹2pz¹: the fourth 2p electron is forced to pair up in an already occupied orbital, where it suffers strong inter-electronic repulsion. Removing that crowded electron both relieves the repulsion and restores a stable half-filled 2p³ arrangement, so it comes off comparatively easily.
Key data IE₁(N) = 1402 kJ mol⁻¹ > IE₁(O) = 1314 kJ mol⁻¹ · N: 2p³ = half-filled, 3 exchange pairs, no paired p electrons · O: 2p⁴ = one doubly occupied orbital ⇒ extra repulsion · O⁺ becomes 2p³, a stable half-filled ion.
Solution
1. Assertion A. 1314 < 1402, so oxygen's first ionisation enthalpy is indeed lower than nitrogen's. A is CORRECT.
2. Reason R. Oxygen's 2p sub-shell holds four electrons, one orbital being doubly occupied; those four electrons in the compact 2p set do experience greater electron–electron repulsion than nitrogen's three unpaired ones. R is CORRECT.
3. Causal link. That very repulsion destabilises the fourth 2p electron and lowers the energy needed to pull it out — and the resulting O⁺ (2p³) is itself extra stable. So R is exactly why A happens. Answer (a).
4. Eliminations: (c) and (d) each mark a true statement false. (b) accepts both but severs the link — yet no other mechanism is on offer; Zeff alone would predict the opposite order, so the repulsion argument is the explanation, not a bystander.
 element   2p configuration            IE1 (kJ/mol)
 N         2px1 2py1 2pz1  (half-filled)   1402   no p-p pairing, max exchange energy
 O         2px2 2py1 2pz1                  1314   one paired orbital -> repulsion
 O -> O+ gives 2p3 (half-filled, stable) => easier removal
 A CORRECT, R CORRECT, R explains A                <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Every "IE anomaly" in the p-block is one of exactly two stories: s² → p¹ (Be > B, Mg > Al) or p³ → p⁴ (N > O, P > S). Identify which story a question is telling and the reason writes itself. The trap in this item is option (b): candidates accept both statements but hesitate over the link because they were taught "half-filled stability of N" rather than "pairing repulsion in O" — the two are the same argument seen from opposite ends, so R does explain A.
Answer: (a) Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A.
61The incorrect statement is (JEE Main 2022, 27 July Shift 1, Level-3) (a) The first ionization enthalpy of K is less than that of Na and Li (b) Xe does not have the lowest first ionization enthalpy in its group (c) The first ionization enthalpy of element with atomic number 37 is lower than that of the element with atomic number 38 (d) The first ionization enthalpy of Ga is higher than that of the d-block element with atomic number 30Ionisation enthalpy — spotting the false claimIdentifying the incorrect ionisation-enthalpy statement🟢 ExactdIE₁(Ga) is actually lower than IE₁(Zn)
Concept Four independent IE comparisons are offered and exactly one is false. Three of them test routine trends — down group 1, down group 18, across period 5 — while the fourth quietly compares a p-block element with the d-block element immediately before it. That last comparison is the one people get wrong, because they assume the d-block element (higher Z) must have the higher IE. In fact Zn's filled 3d¹⁰4s² configuration is unusually stable and gives it a high IE (906), while gallium's lone, loosely held 4p¹ electron comes off easily (579).
Key data Group 1: Li 520 > Na 496 > K 419 · Group 18: He 2372 > Ne 2081 > Ar 1521 > Kr 1351 > Xe 1170 > Rn 1037 · Rb (Z = 37) 403 < Sr (Z = 38) 549 · Zn (Z = 30) 906 > Ga (Z = 31) 579.
Solution
1. (a) CORRECT. K (419) < Na (496) < Li (520): ionisation enthalpy falls down group 1 as the valence s electron moves to higher shells. Nothing wrong here.
2. (b) CORRECT. Within group 18, IE₁ keeps falling below Xe: Rn is 1037 kJ mol⁻¹ against Xe's 1170. So Rn, not Xe, holds the group minimum — meaning the statement "Xe does not have the lowest" is true. This is a double-negative statement; read it slowly, because it is true precisely because radon exists below xenon.
3. (c) CORRECT. Z = 37 is Rb (403) and Z = 38 is Sr (549). Across a period IE rises, and a group-2 element with its filled ns² is harder to ionise than the group-1 element beside it. So Rb < Sr, as claimed.
4. (d) INCORRECT. The d-block element with Z = 30 is Zn (IE₁ = 906 kJ mol⁻¹); Ga has IE₁ = 579 kJ mol⁻¹. Ga is therefore lower, not higher. The statement inverts the truth, so (d) is the answer. Two reinforcing reasons: Zn's 3d¹⁰4s² closed configuration resists ionisation, while Ga loses a single 4p¹ electron and thereby reaches the same stable 3d¹⁰4s² arrangement.
 statement   comparison                          data (kJ/mol)          verdict
 (a)         K vs Na, Li                         419 < 496 < 520        TRUE
 (b)         Xe lowest in group 18?              Rn 1037 < Xe 1170      TRUE (Rn is lowest)
 (c)         Rb (37) vs Sr (38)                  403 < 549              TRUE
 (d)         Ga vs Zn (Z = 30)                   Ga 579 < Zn 906        FALSE  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Store the three "unexpectedly high IE" configurations — ns² (Be, Mg), np³ (N, P) and (n−1)d¹⁰ns² (Zn, Cd, Hg) — and the fourth option gives itself away instantly. Zn (906) actually exceeds every other 3d metal and even beats Ga and Ge. Also note the double negative in option (b): candidates who read it as "Xe has the lowest IE in its group" mark it false and lose the question, forgetting Rn sits one period below.
Answer: (d) — the first ionization enthalpy of Ga (579 kJ mol⁻¹) is lower, not higher, than that of Zn (906 kJ mol⁻¹).
62The first ionization enthalpy of Na, Mg and Si, respectively, are 496, 737 and 786 kJ mol⁻¹. The first ionization enthalpy (kJ mol⁻¹) of Al is (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jul Shift 1, Level-3) (a) 487 (b) 768 (c) 577 (d) 856Period-3 IE anomaly (Mg > Al)Estimating Al's first ionisation enthalpy from Na, Mg and Si🟢 Exactc577 kJ mol⁻¹
Concept The examiner gives you three of the four period-3 values and asks you to slot in the missing one. Blind interpolation between Mg (737) and Si (786) would give something around 760 — and that is the trap. Aluminium sits at the s² → p¹ break: its outermost electron occupies a 3p orbital that is higher in energy and less penetrating than magnesium's filled 3s², and it is shielded by that 3s² pair as well as by the neon core. So IE₁(Al) must be less than IE₁(Mg), not between Mg and Si. It must still exceed Na's 496, because Al has three more protons in the same shell.
Key relation Na 3s¹ (496) < Al 3s²3p¹ (577) < Mg 3s² (737) < Si 3s²3p² (786) the same anomaly as Be > B one period higher; removing a 3p¹ electron from Al leaves the stable filled 3s², which further favours ionisation.
Solution
1. Write the configurations: Na [Ne]3s¹, Mg [Ne]3s², Al [Ne]3s²3p¹, Si [Ne]3s²3p².
2. Al's electron leaves a 3p orbital, which lies above the filled 3s² of Mg in energy and is screened by it ⇒ IE₁(Al) < IE₁(Mg) = 737.
3. Al (Z = 13) has three more protons than Na (Z = 11) in the same third shell ⇒ IE₁(Al) > IE₁(Na) = 496.
4. So the value must lie strictly between 496 and 737. Test the options: 487 is below Na — impossible; 768 and 856 both exceed Mg — impossible. Only 577 fits, and it is the accepted literature value for aluminium.
5. Sanity check on the full run: 496 (Na) → 737 (Mg) → 577 (Al) → 786 (Si) → 1012 (P) → 999 (S) → 1256 (Cl), showing the two familiar dips at Al and at S.
 element   Z    configuration       IE1 (kJ/mol)
 Na       11    [Ne] 3s1                496
 Mg       12    [Ne] 3s2                737
 Al       13    [Ne] 3s2 3p1            577      <-- answer (c)   DIP below Mg
 Si       14    [Ne] 3s2 3p2            786
 constraint: 496 < IE1(Al) < 737  =>  only 577 qualifies
 (a) 487 < Na   (b) 768 > Mg   (d) 856 > Si   all rejected
⚡ JEE Trick Turn "estimate the missing IE" questions into an inequality-bracketing exercise: fix the upper and lower bounds from the trend plus the known anomaly, then read off the only option inside the bracket. Here the bracket 496 < x < 737 disposes of three options with no chemistry beyond "Mg > Al". The designed distractor is 768, the value you would pick by assuming a smooth increase from Mg to Si — the paper is checking whether you remember that the s²→p¹ dip exists in period 3 exactly as it does at Be→B.
Answer: (c) 577 kJ mol⁻¹
63The correct order of ionic size of N3−, Na+, F−, Mg2+ and O2− is: (a) Mg2+ < Na+ < F− < O2− < N3− (b) N3− < O2− < F− < Na+ < Mg2+ (c) F− < Na+ < O2− < Mg2+ < N3− (d) Na+ < F− < Mg2+ < O2− < N3− [JEE Main 2022 (27 Jun Shift 2), Level-2]Isoelectronic series — ionic radiiIncreasing ionic radii of Mg²⁺, Na⁺, F⁻, O²⁻, N³⁻ — the isoelectronic ladder🟢 ExactaMg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻ < O²⁻ < N³⁻
Concept The very first move on any "order of ionic size" question is to count the electrons. If the count is the same for every species you are in an isoelectronic series, and every trend you learned for neutral atoms (period, group, shell number) becomes irrelevant — the electron cloud is the same cloud in all five ions. What differs is the number of protons pulling on it. More protons compressing an identical 10-electron cloud means a smaller ion, so within an isoelectronic set size falls as atomic number rises. Equivalently, size rises with the electron-to-proton ratio.
Key relation for a fixed electron count, r ∝ 1 / Zeff and Zeff = Z − σ with σ constant σ depends only on how many electrons are doing the screening, and that number is frozen across an isoelectronic set — so Z alone drives the radius. · r increases with the ratio e⁻/p⁺ N³⁻ has 10/7 = 1.43, Mg²⁺ has 10/12 = 0.83; the higher the ratio, the looser the cloud.
Solution
1. Count electrons. N³⁻: 7 + 3 = 10. O²⁻: 8 + 2 = 10. F⁻: 9 + 1 = 10. Na⁺: 11 − 1 = 10. Mg²⁺: 12 − 2 = 10. All five are isoelectronic with neon, [He]2s²2p⁶.
2. Rank by nuclear charge: Mg (12) > Na (11) > F (9) > O (8) > N (7).
3. Invert that to get size, since more protons ⇒ tighter grip ⇒ smaller ion: Mg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻ < O²⁻ < N³⁻. Real crystal radii (pm) confirm it: 72, 102, 133, 140, 171.
4. Eliminate (b): it is the same sequence written backwards — the decreasing order dressed up with "<" signs. It is the single most-chosen wrong option on this question.Read the inequality signs before the elements; the setter never has to change a word to flip an option from right to wrong.
5. Eliminate (c): it puts F⁻ below Na⁺ and O²⁻ below Mg²⁺, i.e. it mixes anions and cations at random. Eliminate (d): Na⁺ < F⁻ is correct, but Mg²⁺ is placed above F⁻ when it must be the smallest species of all.
 ion     Z    electrons   e-/p+ ratio   radius/pm
 Mg2+    12      10          0.83          72     smallest
 Na+     11      10          0.91         102
 F-       9      10          1.11         133
 O2-      8      10          1.25         140
 N3-      7      10          1.43         171     largest
 increasing size: Mg2+ < Na+ < F- < O2- < N3-   <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Two-second method: strip the charge and read the atomic numbers backwards. Mg(12), Na(11), F(9), O(8), N(7) descending in Z = ascending in size, done — no radii to memorise. The trap set by every distractor here is direction, not chemistry: (b) is your answer reversed, and (c)/(d) are your answer with one species teleported. Always finish by sanity-checking the two ends — the most positive ion must be smallest and the most negative ion must be largest. If an option violates that, kill it without reading the middle.
Answer: (a) Mg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻ < O²⁻ < N³⁻
64Outermost electronic configurations of four elements A, B, C, D are given below: (A) 3s² (B) 3s²3p¹ (C) 3s²3p³ (D) 3s²3p⁴. The correct order of first ionization enthalpy for them is: (a) A < B < C < D (b) B < A < D < C (c) B < D < A < C (d) B < A < C < D [JEE Main 2022 (27 Jul Shift-2), Level-2]IE₁ anomalies in period 3Increasing order of first ionisation energy for Mg, Al, Si, P and S🟡 SimilarbB < A < D < C (Al < Mg < S < P)
Concept Ionisation enthalpy rises across a period because Zeff rises while the shell stays the same — but it does not rise monotonically. There are exactly two dips per p-block period, and both of them are planted in this question. The first dip is at group 13, where the electron leaving comes from a fresh, higher-energy, better-screened p sub-shell instead of a filled s². The second dip is at group 16, where the electron leaving is one of a pair sharing a single p orbital, so inter-electronic repulsion pays part of the ionisation cost. Identify the elements, then apply the two dips.
Key relation IE₁ ∝ Zeff / n² · dip 1: group 2 > group 13 ns² is a completed sub-shell and the np electron of group 13 lies higher in energy and is screened by that s² pair. · dip 2: group 15 > group 16 np³ is exactly half-filled (all spins parallel, maximum exchange energy); np⁴ has one doubly-occupied orbital whose electron-electron repulsion makes it easier to pull out.
Solution
1. Identify from the outermost configurations, all of which are 3s/3p ⇒ period 3. A = 3s² = Mg (group 2). B = 3s²3p¹ = Al (group 13). C = 3s²3p³ = P (group 15). D = 3s²3p⁴ = S (group 16).
2. Apply dip 1: Al (B) sits after Mg (A) in the period yet has the lower IE₁ — 577 against 737 kJ mol⁻¹. So B < A.
3. Both A and B are far below the two right-hand elements, because Zeff keeps climbing: P and S are both above 1000 kJ mol⁻¹.
4. Apply dip 2: S (D) is to the right of P (C) yet has the lower IE₁ — 1000 against 1012 kJ mol⁻¹. So D < C.P is 3s²3px¹3py¹3pz¹, half-filled and exchange-stabilised; S is 3s²3px²3py¹3pz¹ and the paired 3px electrons repel each other.
5. Assemble: B < A < D < C ⇒ option (b). Eliminate (a) A < B < C < D — the naive "IE just increases across a period", which misses both dips. Eliminate (d) B < A < C < D — it gets the Al/Mg dip right but forgets the P/S dip; this is the intended trap and the second-most-marked option. Eliminate (c) B < D < A < C — it would put sulfur (1000) below magnesium (737), which no trend supports.
 label  outer config   element   group   IE1 / kJ mol-1
 A      3s2            Mg         2         737
 B      3s2 3p1        Al        13         577   <-- dip 1 (fresh p electron)
 C      3s2 3p3        P         15        1012   half-filled, the maximum
 D      3s2 3p4        S         16        1000   <-- dip 2 (paired p electron)
 ascending: 577 < 737 < 1000 < 1012  =  B < A < D < C   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the period-3 IE₁ profile as a shape, not as numbers: up, DIP at 13, up, up, DIP at 16, up, up. Any option that presents a strictly increasing left-to-right order is wrong on sight, and any option that fixes only one dip is the deliberate half-credit trap. Note also how close P and S actually are (1012 vs 1000, a 1 % gap) while Mg and Al differ by 160 — the setter relies on you not knowing that the second dip is the shallow one and therefore "rounding it away".
Answer: (b) B < A < D < C, i.e. Al < Mg < S < P
65The correct decreasing order for metallic character is: (a) Na > Mg > Be > Si > P (b) P > Si > Be > Mg > Na (c) Si > P > Be > Na > Mg (d) Be > Na > Mg > Si > P [JEE Main 2022 (28 Jul Shift-2), Level-2]Metallic character trendArranging elements in decreasing order of metallic character🟡 SimilaraNa > Mg > Be > Si > P
Concept Metallic character is the tendency to lose electrons, so it tracks inversely with ionisation enthalpy and electronegativity. It therefore decreases left-to-right across a period (Zeff grows, the valence electrons are held harder) and increases down a group (a new shell puts the valence electrons further out and better screened). The trick with a mixed list like this one is that you must combine a group comparison with a period comparison — you cannot answer it from one trend alone.
Key relation metallic character ∝ 1 / IE₁ ∝ 1 / electronegativity ∝ atomic size · across a period → decreases · down a group → increases both statements are the same statement about Zeff/n²: across a period n is fixed and Zeff rises; down a group n rises faster than Zeff.
Solution
1. Locate every element. Na — period 3, group 1. Mg — period 3, group 2. Be — period 2, group 2. Si — period 3, group 14. P — period 3, group 15.
2. Na > Mg: same period, Na is further left, so Na loses its single 3s electron far more readily (IE₁ 496 vs 737).
3. Mg > Be: same group, Mg is one period lower, so its valence pair sits in n = 3 and is more shielded (IE₁ 737 vs 899). Going down a group always raises metallic character.
4. Be > Si: Be is a genuine metal (lustrous, conducts, forms Be²⁺-type bonding); Si is a metalloid sitting on the staircase and is a semiconductor. So the metal beats the metalloid.Be is the least metallic of the alkaline earths — it is famously diagonal-related to Al and forms covalent halides — but it is still on the metal side of the line, and Si is not.
5. Si > P: same period, Si is to the left, so Si is the metalloid and P is a full non-metal.
6. Chain it: Na > Mg > Be > Si > P ⇒ option (a). Eliminate (b) — that is exactly (a) reversed, i.e. the increasing order. Eliminate (c) — it opens with Si above P (true) but then ranks Na below Be and Mg last, which contradicts both trends. Eliminate (d) — it puts Be above Na, i.e. a period-2 group-2 element above a period-3 alkali metal, which is the opposite of both rules.
 element  period  group   IE1/kJ mol-1   classification
 Na         3       1         496        metal (most metallic here)
 Mg         3       2         737        metal
 Be         2       2         899        metal (least metallic metal)
 Si         3      14         786*       METALLOID
 P          3      15        1012        non-metal
 * Si's IE1 is numerically low, but metallic character is judged by
   electron-losing BEHAVIOUR, not by IE1 alone -- Si is a semiconductor.
 decreasing metallic character: Na > Mg > Be > Si > P   <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Draw the mental arrow: metallic character points down-and-left towards caesium, non-metallic points up-and-right towards fluorine. Rank the list by how close each element sits to the bottom-left corner and you are done. The trap in (a)/(b) is pure direction — "decreasing" vs "increasing" — so circle that word in the stem first. The subtler trap is Be vs Si: option (d) tempts you because Si's ionisation energy (786) is lower than Be's (899), so a numbers-only student ranks Si as "more metallic". Metallic character is about bonding and behaviour; on that test the staircase decides, and Si loses.
Answer: (a) Na > Mg > Be > Si > P
66The pair, in which ions are isoelectronic with Al3+ is: (a) Br− and Be2+ (b) Cl− and Li+ (c) S2− and K+ (d) O2− and Mg2+ [JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 1), Level-1]Isoelectronic pairsWhich pair of ions is isoelectronic with Al³⁺ — electron counting worked out🟢 Exact4 ⚠printed as "4" — read as (d) O²⁻ and Mg²⁺ (see note)
Concept "Isoelectronic" means identical total electron count — nothing more. It says nothing about charge, nothing about which period the parent atom came from, and nothing about size. The entire question is one subtraction per species: electrons = Z − (charge), where a negative charge adds. The setter's job is to build distractors whose two members are isoelectronic with each other but not with the target species, and that is exactly what happens in option (c).
Key relation n(e⁻) = Z − q q is the signed charge, so for O²⁻, q = −2 and n(e⁻) = 8 − (−2) = 10. · Al³⁺ : Z = 13, q = +3 ⇒ 10 electrons ⇒ [Ne] = 1s²2s²2p⁶
Solution
1. Fix the target: Al is Z = 13, so Al³⁺ carries 10 electrons and is isoelectronic with neon.
2. Option (a): Br⁻ = 35 + 1 = 36 (Kr); Be²⁺ = 4 − 2 = 2 (He). Neither is 10, and the two are wildly apart from each other. Rejected.
3. Option (b): Cl⁻ = 17 + 1 = 18 (Ar); Li⁺ = 3 − 1 = 2 (He). Rejected.
4. Option (c): S²⁻ = 16 + 2 = 18; K⁺ = 19 − 1 = 18. These two are isoelectronic — with argon, not with Al³⁺. This is the designed trap: a student who checks "are the two members isoelectronic?" instead of "are they isoelectronic with Al³⁺?" picks (c).Always re-read the stem after finding an internally consistent pair; the reference species is the whole point.
5. Option (d): O²⁻ = 8 + 2 = 10; Mg²⁺ = 12 − 2 = 10. Both match Al³⁺ exactly, all three being [Ne]. Correct.
 species   Z    charge   electrons   noble-gas core   = Al3+ (10 e-)?
 Al3+      13     +3        10           Ne            (target)
 Br-       35     -1        36           Kr             no
 Be2+       4     +2         2           He             no
 Cl-       17     -1        18           Ar             no
 Li+        3     +1         2           He             no
 S2-       16     -2        18           Ar             no  (trap: S2- = K+)
 K+        19     +1        18           Ar             no
 O2-        8     -2        10           Ne            YES
 Mg2+      12     +2        10           Ne            YES        <-- answer (d)
⚠ Note The printed answer key gives the digit "4" for this multiple-choice item rather than a letter. Counting the options from the top, option 4 is (d) O²⁻ and Mg²⁺ — the same species the derivation produces, so the chemistry is not in dispute. The key has simply slipped into the 1/2/3/4 numbering used by the original NTA paper while the book prints (a)/(b)/(c)/(d). Read "4" as "(d)". Flagged so that nobody hunts for a fifth, numerical option.
⚡ JEE Trick Write the noble-gas core beside each species instead of the raw count — He, Ne, Ar, Kr — and matching becomes visual rather than arithmetic. The single highest-yield fact for these questions is the 10-electron family: N³⁻, O²⁻, F⁻, Ne, Na⁺, Mg²⁺, Al³⁺, Si⁴⁺. Learn it once and questions 63 and 66 of this set both collapse to a glance. The trap to watch for is the pair that is isoelectronic with each other but not with the target (here S²⁻/K⁺, the 18-electron family) — it appears in almost every version of this question.
Answer: (d) O²⁻ and Mg²⁺ — key prints "4", i.e. the fourth option (flagged).
67What is the correct order of electron gain enthalpy of Cl, F, Te, Po? (a) F > Cl > Te > Po (b) Cl > F > Te > Po (c) Te > Po > Cl > F (d) Po > Te > F > Cl [JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 2), Level-1]Electron gain enthalpy orderCorrect order of electron gain enthalpies of Cl, F, Te and Po🟢 ExactbCl > F > Te > Po
Concept Electron gain enthalpy ΔegH is the enthalpy change when a gaseous atom accepts an electron; for these four elements it is exothermic, so the raw values are negative and the ranking is spoken in terms of how much energy is released. Two competing factors decide the magnitude: a larger effective nuclear charge attracts the incoming electron (raises the magnitude), while a small, already-crowded valence shell repels it (lowers the magnitude). Fluorine is the textbook casualty of the second factor — its 2p sub-shell is so compact that the extra electron enters a region of severe electron-electron repulsion, and Cl outdoes it. The other pair, Te and Po, is a plain group-16 comparison where size increases downward and the magnitude falls.
Key relation ΔegH : F −328, Cl −349, Br −325, I −295 kJ mol⁻¹ Cl, not F, is the maximum of group 17 — the single most examined anomaly in this chapter. · ΔegH : O −141, S −200, Se −195, Te −190, Po ≈ −183 kJ mol⁻¹ · group 17 magnitudes ≫ group 16 magnitudes a halogen gains one electron to reach a full octet; a chalcogen only reaches np⁵.
Solution
1. Split the list by group: Cl and F are halogens (group 17); Te and Po are chalcogens (group 16). Halogens are one electron short of a noble-gas octet, so their electron gain is much more exothermic — both halogens outrank both chalcogens immediately.
2. Inside group 17: Cl > F. Fluorine's 2p sub-shell is tiny (r ≈ 72 pm); the incoming electron is forced into a compact cloud and the repulsion it meets cancels a large part of the nuclear attraction. Chlorine's 3p shell is roomier, so it releases more energy (349 > 328 kJ mol⁻¹).This is not a Zeff effect — F has the larger Zeff. It is purely inter-electronic repulsion in a small shell.
3. Inside group 16: Te > Po. Here the normal group trend applies — as you descend, the added electron enters a shell further from the nucleus and is more screened, so less energy is released (Te ≈ 190, Po ≈ 183 kJ mol⁻¹).
4. Combine: Cl > F > Te > Po ⇒ option (b). Eliminate (a) F > Cl > Te > Po — correct except that it swaps the halogen pair; it is the answer a student gives who applies "electron affinity increases up a group" without knowing the F/Cl exception. Eliminate (c) and (d) — both place chalcogens above halogens, contradicting the octet argument, and (d) additionally reverses the group-16 pair.
 element  group   |dEgH| / kJ mol-1   comment
 Cl        17           349           MAXIMUM (roomy 3p shell)
 F         17           328           anomaly: small 2p, heavy e--e repulsion
 Te        16          ~190           chalcogen, only reaches np5
 Po        16          ~183           lowest: largest atom, best screened
 order of electron gain enthalpy: Cl > F > Te > Po      <-- answer (b)
Sign convention Because all four values are negative, "Cl > F" here means |ΔegH(Cl)| > |ΔegH(F)|, i.e. Cl releases more energy. If you insist on comparing the algebraic numbers then −349 < −328 and the printed order would read backwards. Every Indian board and JEE key states this order in terms of magnitude ("electron gain enthalpy with negative sign"), which is why (b) is correct as printed — but always check which convention an option is using before you answer.
⚡ JEE Trick Two facts settle almost every electron-gain-enthalpy ordering: Cl > F and S > O — the second-row element of each group loses to the third-row element because its valence shell is too small. Everything below those two behaves normally and falls down the group. Distractor design here is mechanical: option (a) is the correct answer with the F/Cl pair un-flipped, and (c)/(d) come from mixing groups. Watch out for one more variant of this question that supplies signed values instead of magnitudes — the correct letter then changes, and that is the whole point of the variant.
Answer: (b) Cl > F > Te > Po
68Which of the following is a metalloid? (a) Sc (b) Pb (c) Bi (d) Te [JEE Main 2022 (26 Jun Shift 2), Level-1]Metals, non-metals, metalloidsIs tellurium a metal, a non-metal or a metalloid?🟡 SimilardTe (tellurium)
Concept Metalloids (semi-metals) occupy the diagonal staircase that separates metals from non-metals in the p-block. They are lustrous like metals but brittle like non-metals, and — the defining physical signature — they are semiconductors: their electrical conductivity rises with temperature, whereas a true metal's conductivity falls with temperature. Because the boundary is a diagonal, a heavy p-block element can still be a full metal (Pb, Bi) while a lighter one in a group further right is a metalloid (Te).
Definition the six standard metalloids: B, Si, Ge, As, Sb, Te some books add Po and At as borderline; NCERT lists the six above, and the JEE key expects them. · metalloid ⇒ semiconductor: dσ/dT > 0 · metal ⇒ dσ/dT < 0
Solution
1. (a) Sc, Z = 21 — scandium, [Ar]3d¹4s². A d-block element; every d-block element is a metal. Rejected.
2. (b) Pb, Z = 82 — lead, group 14, period 6. Group 14 walks from non-metal (C) through metalloids (Si, Ge) to metals (Sn, Pb). Pb is at the bottom, so it is firmly a metal — soft, dense, malleable, a genuine conductor. Rejected.
3. (c) Bi, Z = 83 — bismuth, group 15, period 6. Group 15 runs N, P (non-metals) → As, Sb (metalloids) → Bi (metal). Bi is the classic "poor metal" with low conductivity, which is why it is the favourite distractor here, but it is on the metal side of the staircase. Rejected.Bi is diamagnetic and a bad conductor, so students "feel" it is a metalloid; the periodic-table position, not the conductivity value, is what the examiner is testing.
4. (d) Te, Z = 52 — tellurium, group 16, period 5. It sits directly on the staircase between Sb (metalloid) and I (non-metal). It is a brittle, silvery-white semiconductor used in CdTe photovoltaics and Bi₂Te₃ thermoelectrics. Metalloid — this is the answer.
 option  element   Z    block      position vs staircase   verdict
 (a)     Sc        21   d-block    far left                METAL
 (b)     Pb        82   p, gp 14   below the staircase     METAL
 (c)     Bi        83   p, gp 15   below the staircase     METAL (poor metal)
 (d)     Te        52   p, gp 16   ON the staircase        METALLOID   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the staircase as a six-element chant — "B–Si–Ge–As–Sb–Te" — and note that it steps one place to the right every time it drops a period. That single rule tells you why Ge (group 14, period 4) is a metalloid but Sn and Pb below it are metals, and why Te (group 16, period 5) is a metalloid while Se above it is a non-metal and Po below it is a metal. The trap in this question is Bi: it is the heaviest stable pnictogen, it conducts badly and it is brittle, so it behaves half-metalloid — but the staircase has already stepped past group 15 by period 6, so Bi is a metal.
Answer: (d) Te
69Element "E" belongs to the period 4 and group 16 of the periodic table. The valence shell electron configuration of the element, which is just above "E" in the group is: (a) 2s²2p⁴ (b) 3d¹⁰4s²4p⁴ (c) 3s²3p⁴ (d) 4d¹⁰5s²5p⁴ [JEE Main 2022 (28 Jun Shift 1), Level-1]Position ⇄ electronic configurationGroup 16 elements — positions, periods and valence configurations🟡 Similarc3s²3p⁴ (sulfur, the element above Se)
Concept Group and period translate directly into a configuration: the period number is the principal quantum number of the valence shell, and for a p-block element the group number minus 10 gives the number of valence electrons. "Just above in the group" means one period higher — same group, n reduced by one. The only subtlety is that from period 4 onwards the d sub-shell is filled first and appears in the written configuration, so the element above a period-4 p-block element looks structurally simpler, not just smaller.
Key relation p-block: group = 10 + (number of valence electrons) ⇒ group 16 ⇒ 6 valence electrons ⇒ ns²np⁴ · period number = n of the valence shell · "one place up the group" ⇒ n → n − 1, group unchanged
Solution
1. Identify E. Period 4, group 16 ⇒ valence shell n = 4, configuration 4s²4p⁴, full configuration [Ar]3d¹⁰4s²4p⁴. That is selenium, Z = 34.
2. Move one place up group 16: period 4 → period 3, same group. The group-16 column reads O (2), S (3), Se (4), Te (5), Po (6), so the element directly above Se is sulfur, Z = 16.
3. Write sulfur's valence shell: n = 3, six valence electrons ⇒ 3s²3p⁴. Full configuration 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁴ = [Ne]3s²3p⁴. Option (c).
4. Eliminate (a) 2s²2p⁴ — that is oxygen, which is two places above E, not one. The commonest wrong answer, chosen by students who jump to the head of the group."Just above" is a one-step instruction; O is the group head but S is Se's immediate neighbour.
5. Eliminate (b) 3d¹⁰4s²4p⁴ — that is E itself (Se). Eliminate (d) 4d¹⁰5s²5p⁴ — that is Te, the element below E; the option exists purely to catch a misread of "above" as "next in the list".
 group 16 column     period   valence configuration      relation to E
 O   (Z = 8)            2        2s2 2p4                 two above  -> option (a)
 S   (Z = 16)           3        3s2 3p4                 JUST ABOVE -> option (c)
 Se  (Z = 34)  = E      4        3d10 4s2 4p4            E itself   -> option (b)
 Te  (Z = 52)           5        4d10 5s2 5p4            just below -> option (d)
 element just above E = S, valence shell 3s2 3p4          <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Never hunt for the atomic number in these — build the configuration straight from the coordinates. Period gives n, group minus 10 gives the p-block electron count. Group 16, period 3 ⇒ 3s²3p⁴, one line, no counting to 34. Then check the distractor pattern, which is standard: this question offers you the element itself, the element below it, and the group head, and only one of the four is the true immediate neighbour. Also note that from period 4 downwards you must decide whether to include the filled d¹⁰ — it is part of the written configuration but not part of the valence shell, and options (b)/(d) exploit exactly that ambiguity.
Answer: (c) 3s²3p⁴ — sulfur
70The IUPAC nomenclature of an element with electronic configuration [Rn]5f146d17s2 is: (a) Unnilunium (b) Unnilbium (c) Unniltrium (d) Unnilquadium [JEE Main 2022 (25 Jul Shift-1), Level-1]IUPAC naming of Z > 100IUPAC name of the element with configuration [Rn]5f¹⁴6d¹7s²🟢 ExactcUnniltrium (Z = 103, lawrencium)
Concept For elements beyond Z = 100 IUPAC assigns a provisional systematic name built digit-by-digit from the atomic number, so that a name can exist before a discovery is confirmed and credited. The whole question is therefore two steps: convert the configuration into an atomic number by adding up electrons, then spell that number with the numerical roots. Nothing about the chemistry of the element is needed — the configuration is only a disguised way of writing "103".
Key relation Z = (electrons in the noble-gas core) + (electrons written after it) · roots: 0 nil, 1 un, 2 bi, 3 tri, 4 quad, 5 pent, 6 hex, 7 sept, 8 oct, 9 enn · name = root(d₁) + root(d₂) + root(d₃) + "ium" the digits are read left to right exactly as in the number, and a terminal "ium" is always appended; "bi"+"ium" and "tri"+"ium" keep both i's, while "enn"+"nil" drops one n.
Solution
1. Count the electrons. The core [Rn] is radon, Z = 86, so it contributes 86 electrons.
2. Add the electrons written outside the core: 5f14 → 14, 6d1 → 1, 7s2 → 2. Total added = 17.
3. Therefore Z = 86 + 17 = 103. That element is lawrencium, Lr, the last member of the actinoid series — consistent with 5f being complete and the next electron entering 6d.
4. Spell 103 with the roots: 1 = un, 0 = nil, 3 = tri, then "+ium" ⇒ Un + nil + tri + ium = Unniltrium, symbol Unt. Option (c).
5. Eliminate the neighbours, all of which are real systematic names of adjacent elements: (a) Unnilunium = 1-0-1 = 101 = mendelevium; (b) Unnilbium = 1-0-2 = 102 = nobelium; (d) Unnilquadium = 1-0-4 = 104 = rutherfordium.Every distractor is off by one or two in Z, so an arithmetic slip of a single electron lands you squarely on a wrong option — count the 5f¹⁴ carefully.
 contribution        electrons
 [Rn] core                86
 5f14                     14
 6d1                       1
 7s2                       2
 -------------------------------
 Z                       103   = lawrencium (Lr)

 digit   root      option check
   1     un        101 = Unnilunium   -> (a)  Md
   0     nil       102 = Unnilbium    -> (b)  No
   3     tri       103 = UNNILTRIUM   -> (c)  Lr   <-- answer (c)
                   104 = Unnilquadium -> (d)  Rf
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the ten roots with the mnemonic "nil–un–bi–tri–quad–pent–hex–sept–oct–enn" and remember that only three noble-gas cores ever appear in these questions: [Xe] = 54, [Rn] = 86, [Og] = 118. Once you know [Rn] = 86, every actinoid configuration converts in one addition. Reverse questions are just as common ("Unununnium belongs to which group?") — there you decode the name to 111, place it under Au in group 11, and answer without ever naming the element.
