The trap here is purely arithmetic: miscount 5f¹⁴ as 5f¹² or forget the 6d¹ and you land on a name that is also a genuine IUPAC name, so nothing looks wrong.
Answer: (c) Unniltrium (Z = 103)
71The first ionization enthalpies of Be, B, N and O follow the order: (a) B < Be < O < N (b) O < N < B < Be (c) Be < B < N < O (d) B < Be < N < O [JEE Main 2022 (25 Jul Shift 2), Level-1]Period-2 IE₁ anomaliesIncreasing order of first ionisation enthalpy for the second-period elements🟡 SimilaraB < Be < O < N
Concept Period 2 is where the two ionisation-enthalpy anomalies are largest and where examiners test them most often. Across the period Zeff rises steadily, so IE₁ should rise steadily — and it does, except at two places. At boron the electron removed is the lone 2p electron, which lies above the filled 2s² pair in energy and is screened by it, so B falls below Be. At oxygen the electron removed comes out of the one doubly-occupied 2p orbital, and the repulsion between that pair discounts the ionisation cost, so O falls below N. This question hands you exactly the four elements that straddle both anomalies, so both must be applied.
Key relation IE₁ / kJ mol⁻¹ : Li 520, Be 899, B 801, C 1086, N 1402, O 1314, F 1681, Ne 2081 · Be (2s²) > B (2s²2p¹) completed sub-shell versus a fresh, higher-energy, well-screened p electron. · N (2p³) > O (2p⁴) half-filled 2p³ has maximum exchange energy and no paired-electron repulsion; O's 2px² pair repel and one leaves easily.
Solution
1. Write the configurations. Be = 1s²2s²; B = 1s²2s²2p¹; N = 1s²2s²2p³; O = 1s²2s²2p⁴.
2. Anomaly 1 — B < Be. Removing B's single 2p electron costs 801 kJ mol⁻¹; breaking into Be's filled 2s² costs 899. Boron is therefore the lowest of the four, even though it lies further right.
3. Both B and Be are far below N and O, because two or three more protons have been added by the time you reach group 15/16 while the shell is still n = 2.
4. Anomaly 2 — O < N. Nitrogen's 2p³ is exactly half filled, all three spins parallel; oxygen must eject one of the paired 2px electrons. IE₁: N 1402 > O 1314.Removing that electron actually converts O⁺ into a half-filled 2p³ species, so the product is stabilised too — a double reason for the dip.
5. Assemble the increasing order: 801 < 899 < 1314 < 1402, i.e. B < Be < O < N ⇒ option (a).
6. Eliminate (c) Be < B < N < O — the naive monotonic increase, both anomalies ignored. Eliminate (d) B < Be < N < O — the half-right trap: it applies the Be/B dip and then forgets the N/O dip. Eliminate (b) O < N < B < Be — this is close to the decreasing order and additionally mis-ranks the pairs.
 element  config        IE1 / kJ mol-1   note
 B        2s2 2p1            801         LOWEST -- lone 2p electron
 Be       2s2                899         filled s sub-shell
 O        2s2 2p4           1314         paired 2p electron leaves easily
 N        2s2 2p3           1402         HIGHEST -- half-filled, exchange-stabilised
 increasing IE1:  B < Be < O < N                          <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Store period 2 as a zig-zag picture rather than eight numbers: Li up to Be, DOWN to B, up to C, up to N, DOWN to O, up to F, up to Ne. Two dips, at groups 13 and 16, in every p-block period — the identical pattern you used in question 64 for Mg/Al/P/S. Any option that shows a clean left-to-right increase is dead on sight. The discriminating option is always the one that fixes one dip only, and it is placed next to the correct answer so that the two look nearly identical; compare option (a) with option (d) here — they differ solely in the order of N and O.
Answer: (a) B < Be < O < N
72Given two statements below: Statement I: In Cl2 molecule the covalent radius is double of the atomic radius of chlorine. Statement II: Radius of anionic species is always greater than their parent atomic radius. Choose the most appropriate answer from options given below: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct. (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect. (c) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect. (d) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct. [JEE Main 2022 (26 Jul Shift-1), Level-1]Covalent radius vs atomic radiusCl–Cl bond length 1.98 Å — deriving the covalent radius of chlorine🟡 SimilardStatement I incorrect, Statement II correct
Concept For a homonuclear diatomic like Cl2 the covalent radius is defined as one half of the internuclear distance, and for such an element that same number is what we quote as the atomic radius. So covalent radius and atomic radius are equal, not in a 2 : 1 ratio; what is double the covalent radius is the bond length. Statement I inverts the definition. Statement II is the standard anion-size rule and is true for essentially every anion: adding electrons at constant nuclear charge lowers Zeff per electron and inflates the cloud.
Definition rcov = d(A–A) / 2 ⇒ d(A–A) = 2 rcov the bond length is double the radius — the statement has the two quantities the wrong way round. · Cl2: d = 198 pm ⇒ rcov(Cl) = 99 pm = atomic (covalent) radius of Cl · Zeff per electron falls when electrons are added at fixed Z ⇒ r(anion) > r(atom)
Solution
1. Statement I — INCORRECT. Experimentally the Cl–Cl internuclear distance in Cl2 is 198 pm (1.98 Å). Halving it gives rcov(Cl) = 99 pm, and 99 pm is precisely the value tabulated as chlorine's atomic radius. Hence covalent radius = atomic radius, and it is the bond length, not the atomic radius, that is double the covalent radius.The statement would only make sense if "atomic radius" secretly meant the van der Waals radius — and even then it fails, because rvdW(Cl) = 180 pm is larger than rcov, not half of it.
2. Statement II — CORRECT. An anion has the same nuclear charge as its parent atom but more electrons. Screening rises, the effective nuclear charge felt by each electron falls, and inter-electronic repulsion pushes the cloud outwards. Chlorine itself is the illustration: Cl 99 pm → Cl⁻ 181 pm, an 83 % expansion. Similarly O 66 → O²⁻ 140 pm and F 64 → F⁻ 133 pm.
3. Statement I false and Statement II true ⇒ option (d).
4. Eliminate (a) — requires I to be true. Eliminate (b) — requires II to be false, but the anion rule holds universally. Eliminate (c) — it is (d) with the two verdicts exchanged, and is the option chosen by anyone who accepts Statement I's definition at face value.
 quantity for chlorine                    value / pm   relation
 Cl-Cl internuclear distance d               198       = 2 x r_cov
 covalent radius r_cov = d/2                  99       = atomic radius of Cl
 van der Waals radius                        180
 ionic radius of Cl-                         181       > 99  (Statement II holds)

 Statement I : "r_cov = 2 x r_atomic"   -> FALSE (they are EQUAL; d = 2 r_cov)
 Statement II: "r_anion > r_parent atom" -> TRUE
 I incorrect, II correct                                 <-- answer (d)
⚠ Note The options as printed in this book are line-wrapped incorrectly: option (c) breaks off after "…but Statement II is", the trailing word "incorrect." has migrated onto the (d) line, and a stray "correct." is left dangling below. The intended NTA wording is (c) "Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect" and (d) "Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct". The answer letter is unaffected — (d) is still the option that says "I false, II true" — but read the option text from the original paper rather than from the printed block.
⚡ JEE Trick On any radius statement, immediately ask "which is double which?" The safe memory is the picture: two touching spheres, nuclei 198 pm apart, each sphere 99 pm — bond length = 2 × radius, never the reverse. Assertion-style items in this chapter almost always plant the error inside a definition rather than inside a trend, because a mis-stated definition still sounds right. Second rule for statement pairs: verify the statements in isolation and only then look at the options — reading the options first makes you rationalise whichever one you half-remember.
Answer: (d) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct.
73The metal that has very low melting point and its periodic position is closer to a metalloid is: (a) Al (b) Ga (c) Se (d) In [JEE Main 2022 (26 Jul Shift-2), Level-1]Anomalous properties of galliumThe metal with a very low melting point lying next to a metalloid🟢 ExactbGa (m.p. 302.9 K, next to the metalloid Ge)
Concept Two conditions must hold simultaneously: the species must be a metal, and it must sit adjacent to the metalloid staircase while melting abnormally low. Gallium is the standard NCERT example — it melts at 302.9 K (29.8 °C), low enough to liquefy in the palm of your hand, yet boils at 2676 K, giving it the largest liquid range of any element. The reason is structural: solid gallium is not a simple close-packed metal but an orthorhombic lattice built from Ga2 dimer-like pairs, so only weak inter-dimer forces have to be overcome to melt it, while full metallic bonding must still be broken to boil it. Its position, group 13 period 4, puts it immediately to the left of germanium — a metalloid.
Key relation m.p. / K : Ga 302.9, In 429.7, Al 933, Se 494 · Ga: group 13, period 4 — neighbour of Ge (metalloid) · Ga liquid range 302.9 → 2676 K, the widest of all elements low melting from the Ga₂-paired solid structure, high boiling from strong metallic bonding in the liquid.
Solution
1. (c) Se is out immediately — selenium is a non-metal (a chalcogen), so it fails the word "metal" in the stem no matter what its melting point is. This option is placed to catch students who scan for "low melting point" alone.
2. (a) Al — melts at 933 K, an ordinary structural metal, and its period-3 neighbour is Si, a metalloid; but 933 K is not "very low" by any standard. Rejected on the melting criterion.
3. (d) In — indium melts at 429.7 K (156.6 °C), genuinely low and the best distractor. But it is 127 K above gallium, and indium's period-5 neighbour on the right is Sn, which is a metal, not a metalloid. It fails the "closer to a metalloid" clause.The staircase steps right as it descends: Si (period 3), Ge (period 4), Sb/Te (periods 5). By period 5 the boundary has moved past group 14, so Sn is metallic.
4. (b) Ga — melts at 302.9 K, by far the lowest of the three metals listed, and sits directly beside Ge, a metalloid. Both clauses satisfied. Answer.
 option  element  metal?   m.p. / K   right-hand neighbour   both clauses?
 (a)     Al       yes        933      Si (metalloid)          m.p. too high
 (b)     Ga       yes        302.9    Ge (METALLOID)          YES   <-- answer (b)
 (c)     Se       NO         494      Br (non-metal)          not a metal
 (d)     In       yes        429.7    Sn (metal)              neighbour not a metalloid
⚡ JEE Trick Four elements are famous for melting below body temperature or near it — Cs (301.7 K), Ga (302.9 K), Fr, Hg (234 K) — and of these only gallium is a p-block metal sitting on the metalloid border, so "low melting + near a metalloid" is a fingerprint for Ga alone. Note the exam habit of hiding a non-metal (Se) among metals: read the noun in the stem ("the metal that…") before you compare any numbers, and one option disqualifies itself for free. If a question instead says "lowest melting metal", the answer is mercury; if it says "lowest melting solid metal at room temperature", it is caesium — the qualifier is always doing work.
Answer: (b) Ga
74In which of the following pairs, electron gain enthalpies of constituent elements are nearly the same or identical? (A) Rb and Cs (B) Na and K (C) Ar and Kr (D) I and At. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) A & B only (b) B & C only (c) A & C only (d) C & D only [JEE Main 2022 (28 Jul Shift-1), Level-1]Electron gain enthalpy — near-equal pairsPairs whose electron gain enthalpies are nearly the same — Rb/Cs, Na/K, Ar/Kr, I/At🟢 ExactcA & C only (Rb/Cs and Ar/Kr)
Concept Electron gain enthalpy falls in magnitude down a group because the incoming electron enters a shell that is further out and more screened — but the rate of that fall collapses towards the bottom of every group. By period 5 and 6 the added shells barely change how tightly the extra electron is bound, so consecutive heavy congeners end up with almost the same ΔegH. That is why Rb/Cs (periods 5 and 6) are nearly identical while Na/K (periods 3 and 4) still differ noticeably. Noble gases are the extreme case: all of them refuse the electron, which must go into a brand-new shell, so their ΔegH values are all large and positive and essentially the same number.
Key relation ΔegH / kJ mol⁻¹ : Li −60, Na −53, K −48, Rb −47, Cs −46 the alkali-metal series flattens out — the Na→K step is 5 units, the Rb→Cs step is barely 1. · ΔegH / kJ mol⁻¹ : He +48, Ne +116, Ar +96, Kr +96, Xe +77 · ΔegH / kJ mol⁻¹ : I −295, At ≈ −270
Solution
1. (A) Rb and Cs — −46.9 and −45.5 kJ mol⁻¹. A gap of about 1.4 units, i.e. ~3 %. Nearly the same. Both are huge atoms whose ns orbital is already so diffuse that one more shell hardly matters.
2. (B) Na and K — −52.8 and −48.4 kJ mol⁻¹. A gap of 4.4 units, about three times the Rb/Cs gap, because the 3s → 4s jump is a genuine size change. Not "nearly identical" relative to the other pairs on offer.
3. (C) Ar and Kr — both about +96 kJ mol⁻¹. Identical to the precision usually tabulated. A noble gas has a closed ns²np⁶ shell, so the extra electron must start a new shell against a fully screened nucleus; the energy penalty is nearly the same for every heavy noble gas. Nearly identical.
4. (D) I and At — −295 and about −270 kJ mol⁻¹, a gap of ~25 units. Large in absolute terms and roughly 9 %, the biggest discrepancy on the list. Not the same.Halogen ΔegH values are an order of magnitude bigger than alkali-metal ones, so even a modest percentage difference shows up as a large number — compare the pairs by percentage as well as by difference.
5. So the qualifying pairs are A and C ⇒ option (c). Eliminate (a), which keeps A but adds B; (b), which drops A for B; and (d), which keeps C but adds the worst pair, D.
 pair            dEgH values / kJ mol-1     difference    nearly identical?
 (A) Rb, Cs         -46.9  ,  -45.5            1.4            YES
 (B) Na, K          -52.8  ,  -48.4            4.4            no
 (C) Ar, Kr          +96   ,   +96            ~0              YES
 (D) I,  At          -295  ,  -270            ~25             no
 qualifying pairs = A and C only                  <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Two facts answer every question of this family. First, noble gases all have large positive ΔegH of similar size — any option containing a noble-gas pair as "nearly the same" is almost certainly in the answer. Second, differences shrink as you go down a group, so the lower of two congener pairs is the more equal one: Rb/Cs beats Na/K every time. Combine them and you can pick A and C without recalling one numerical value. The trap is (d): it correctly includes C, so it feels safe, and then attaches I/At, which students assume must be near-equal because At is "just below I" — but halogen values are large, so their differences are large too.
Answer: (c) A & C only — Rb/Cs and Ar/Kr
75Which of the following pair is not isoelectronic species? (Atomic numbers Sm = 62; Er = 68; Yb = 70; Lu = 71; Eu = 63; Tb = 65; Tm = 69) (a) Sm2+ and Er3+ (b) Yb2+ and Lu3+ (c) Eu2+ and Tb4+ (d) Tb2+ and Tm4+ [JEE Main 2022 (28 Jul Shift-2), Level-1]Isoelectronic lanthanoid ionsSpotting the species that is not isoelectronic — electron-counting method🟡 Similard ⚠Tb²⁺ (63 e⁻) and Tm⁴⁺ (65 e⁻) — but (a) also fails (see note)
Concept Nothing lanthanoid-specific is required: isoelectronic still means "same total electron count", and the atomic numbers are supplied precisely so that you never have to recall a 4f configuration. The examiner's design is that a +2 and a +3 ion from elements one apart, or a +2 and a +4 ion from elements two apart, come out equal — so you look for the pairs where the difference in atomic number does not match the difference in charge. That is the whole test, and it reduces to a single subtraction per option.
Key relation n(e⁻) = Z − q · a pair (Z₁, q₁) and (Z₂, q₂) is isoelectronic ⟺ Z₁ − q₁ = Z₂ − q₂ ⟺ Z₂ − Z₁ = q₂ − q₁ this shortcut lets you compare the gap in atomic number with the gap in charge and skip the arithmetic entirely.
Solution
1. (a) Sm²⁺ and Er³⁺. Sm²⁺ = 62 − 2 = 60; Er³⁺ = 68 − 3 = 65. ΔZ = 6 but Δq = 1, so they cannot match — 60 ≠ 65. NOT isoelectronic.
2. (b) Yb²⁺ and Lu³⁺. Yb²⁺ = 70 − 2 = 68; Lu³⁺ = 71 − 3 = 68. ΔZ = 1 = Δq. Equal — both are [Xe]4f¹⁴. Isoelectronic.
3. (c) Eu²⁺ and Tb⁴⁺. Eu²⁺ = 63 − 2 = 61; Tb⁴⁺ = 65 − 4 = 61. ΔZ = 2 = Δq. Equal — both are [Xe]4f⁷, which is exactly why Eu²⁺ and Tb⁴⁺ are the two anomalously stable non-trivalent lanthanoid ions.4f⁷ is half-filled; that stability is the reason Eu prefers +2 and Tb can reach +4 at all. Recognising this pair on sight saves the arithmetic.
4. (d) Tb²⁺ and Tm⁴⁺. Tb²⁺ = 65 − 2 = 63; Tm⁴⁺ = 69 − 4 = 65. ΔZ = 4 but Δq = 2. 63 ≠ 65. NOT isoelectronic — this is the keyed answer.
5. Both (b) and (c) are genuine isoelectronic pairs, so the answer must lie among (a) and (d) — and by the counts above, both of those fail. The key selects (d); see the note.
 option  ion 1     e-      ion 2     e-     equal?   verdict
 (a)     Sm2+   62-2=60    Er3+   68-3=65    NO      not isoelectronic  (!)
 (b)     Yb2+   70-2=68    Lu3+   71-3=68   yes      isoelectronic, [Xe]4f14
 (c)     Eu2+   63-2=61    Tb4+   65-4=61   yes      isoelectronic, [Xe]4f7
 (d)     Tb2+   65-2=63    Tm4+   69-4=65    NO      not isoelectronic  <-- key (d)
⚠ Note Re-derivation shows this item admits two correct answers. Option (d) is not isoelectronic (63 vs 65 electrons) and the key is right about that — but option (a) is not isoelectronic either (60 vs 65 electrons), and by a wider margin. Only (b) and (c) are genuine isoelectronic pairs, so a question asking for "the pair which is not isoelectronic" has no unique answer as printed. The answer is displayed as keyed, (d), and flagged. If you meet this item in a test, mark (d) to agree with the official key, but understand that (a) is equally defensible — the item would need an extra clause (for example "…and whose members differ by two units of charge") to become single-valued.
⚡ JEE Trick Do not subtract four times. Use the gap test: a pair is isoelectronic exactly when ΔZ = Δcharge. Scan the options — (b) has ΔZ 1 with Δq 1 ✓, (c) has ΔZ 2 with Δq 2 ✓, (a) has ΔZ 6 with Δq 1 ✗, (d) has ΔZ 4 with Δq 2 ✗ — and you have graded all four options in about five seconds. Bonus fact worth carrying into the f-block chapter: the stable non-trivalent lanthanoid ions are Ce⁴⁺ and Tb⁴⁺ (4f⁰-like and 4f⁷) and Eu²⁺ and Yb²⁺ (4f⁷ and 4f¹⁴) — empty, half-filled, full. Every pairing in this question is built from that list.
Answer: (d) Tb²⁺ and Tm⁴⁺ as keyed — flagged, because (a) Sm²⁺/Er³⁺ is equally not isoelectronic.
76The 1st, 2nd and the 3rd ionization enthalpies, I₁, I₂ and I₃, of four atoms with atomic numbers n, n+1, n+2 and n+3, where n < 10, are tabulated below. What is the value of n? — Ionization enthalpy (kJ/mol): atomic number n → I₁ 1681, I₂ 3374, I₃ 6050; n+1 → 2081, 3952, 6122; n+2 → 496, 4562, 6910; n+3 → 738, 1451, 7733. [JEE Adv. 2020 (Paper-2), Level-3]Successive IE jumps → group identificationJEE Advanced 2020 Paper-2 — finding n from the successive ionisation enthalpy table🟢 Exact9n = 9 (F, Ne, Na, Mg)
Concept Successive ionisation enthalpies always increase, but they increase smoothly within a shell and violently when the shell breaks. The size and position of that jump therefore fingerprint the group: a group-1 atom has one valence electron, so the enormous jump comes between I₁ and I₂; a group-2 atom has two, so the jump comes between I₂ and I₃. Read the jumps first and identify groups; only then use the constraint n < 10 to fix the actual atomic number. You never need to recall a single ionisation value — the ratios carry all the information.
Key relation I₁ < I₂ < I₃ < … always · a jump of ×4 or more marks the start of a new (inner) shell · huge I₂/I₁ ⇒ group 1 · huge I₃/I₂ ⇒ group 2 · very high I₁ with no early jump ⇒ noble gas or halogen
Solution
1. Compute the ratios row by row. Row n: 3374/1681 = 2.0 and 6050/3374 = 1.8 — smooth, no shell break in the first three removals. Row n+1: 1.9 and 1.5 — also smooth, and it has the highest I₁ of the whole table (2081).
2. Row n+2: I₁ = 496 is tiny, and I₂/I₁ = 4562/496 = 9.2 — a violent jump immediately after the first electron. That atom has exactly one valence electron ⇒ group 1.
3. Row n+3: I₁ = 738, I₂ = 1451 (ratio 2.0, smooth), then I₃/I₂ = 7733/1451 = 5.3 — the break comes after the second electron. That atom has exactly two valence electrons ⇒ group 2.
4. Rows n+2 and n+3 are consecutive group-1 and group-2 elements, so they begin a new period; row n+1, immediately before them and carrying the largest I₁ in the table, must be the noble gas that closes the previous period.
5. Apply the constraint n < 10. The only noble gas with atomic number below 11 that can occupy position n+1 is neon, Z = 10. Hence n + 1 = 10, giving n = 9.Helium would force n = 1, but then n+2 = 3 (Li, group 1 ✓) and n+3 = 4 (Be, group 2 ✓) as well — the numbers rule it out, since Li's I₁ is 520 and He's I₁ is 2372, and the table's row n (I₁ = 1681) would then have to be hydrogen, whose I₁ is 1312 and which has no I₂ at all. Neon is the only consistent fit.
6. Verify all four rows against real data: n = 9 → F (1681, 3374, 6050 ✓); n+1 = 10 → Ne (2081, 3952, 6122 ✓); n+2 = 11 → Na (496, 4562, 6910 ✓); n+3 = 12 → Mg (738, 1451, 7733 ✓). Every one of the twelve numbers matches. n = 9.
 row     I1     I2     I3     I2/I1   I3/I2   deduction        element (Z)
 n      1681   3374   6050    2.0     1.8     no early break   F   (9)
 n+1    2081   3952   6122    1.9     1.5     highest I1 ->    Ne  (10)
                                              noble gas
 n+2     496   4562   6910    9.2     1.5     jump at I2  ->   Na  (11)
                                              GROUP 1
 n+3     738   1451   7733    2.0     5.3     jump at I3  ->   Mg  (12)
                                              GROUP 2
 n + 1 = 10 (Ne)  =>  n = 9                                <-- answer  n = 9
⚡ JEE Trick Never read successive-IE tables as absolute numbers — divide adjacent entries and hunt for the ratio that exceeds about 4. Jump after I₁ ⇒ group 1; after I₂ ⇒ group 2; after I₃ ⇒ group 13, and so on: the electron count before the jump is the number of valence electrons. Here two rows announce their groups outright, the row before them must therefore be a noble gas, and n < 10 leaves only neon. The distractor pressure in a numerical-answer question like this is a one-off error — students identify Na correctly at n+2 and then report 11 instead of subtracting the offset. Write the offsets down explicitly (n+2 = 11 ⇒ n = 9) before you enter the number.
Answer: n = 9 (the four atoms are F, Ne, Na and Mg)
